### [1] [Scaling Point-in-Time Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11889)
*Bryan Kelly,Semyon Malamud,Johannes Schwab,Teng Andrea Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Training point-in-time language models up to 4B parameters on 1T chronologically filtered tokens narrows the performance gap with unrestricted models, enabling temporally valid backtesting and causal inference in finance and social sciences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs trained on unrestricted internet corpora inadvertently embed future information (lookahead bias), which compromises backtest validity and causal inference in time-sensitive domains like finance. Existing point-in-time models eliminate this leakage but significantly underperform their unconstrained counterparts. The authors aim to close this performance gap through scale while maintaining strict temporal validity.

Method: The authors train decoder-only transformers with up to 4 billion parameters on 1 trillion tokens from FineWeb, filtered chronologically so that each monthly checkpoint (spanning 2013–2024) only sees data available up to that date. They benchmark these models on commonsense reasoning and language understanding tasks, comparing against open-weight models like Gemma-3-4B and LLaMA-7B. Instruction fine-tuning is applied via LoRA for downstream usability. The full pipeline—dataset construction, training infrastructure, and evaluation code—is released.

Result: The point-in-time models approach the performance of leading open-weight models of comparable size trained on temporally unrestricted data, substantially narrowing the prior performance gap. However, a gap still remains on several tasks. LoRA-based instruction fine-tuning improves downstream usability.

Conclusion: Scaling point-in-time language models to 4B parameters with chronological data filtering effectively reduces the performance penalty associated with temporal validity constraints. The released pipeline enables reproducible, temporally rigorous language modeling for research applications requiring strict point-in-time guarantees.

Abstract: Large language models trained on unrestricted internet corpora inevitably embed information from the future, introducing lookahead bias that compromises the validity of backtests and causal inference in finance and the social sciences. Point-in-time language models--trained exclusively on text available up to each calendar date--eliminate this leakage by construction, but existing efforts typically produce models that lag substantially behind their unconstrained counterparts. We show that this performance gap can be substantially narrowed through scale. Training decoder-only transformers with up to 4 billion parameters on 1 trillion chronologically filtered tokens from FineWeb, we construct a sequence of monthly model checkpoints spanning 2013-2024. Across a range of common-sense reasoning and language understanding benchmarks, our models approach the performance of leading open-weight models of comparable size (e.g., Gemma-3-4B and LLaMA-7B) trained on temporally unrestricted data, although a performance gap remains on several tasks. Instruction fine-tuning via LoRA further improves downstream usability. We release the complete pipeline--including dataset construction, training infrastructure, and evaluation code--to enable reproducible point-in-time language modeling and to support research applications that require strict temporal validity.

</details>


### [2] [CANDI: Contextual Alignment for Niche Domains Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11891)
*Megha Chakraborty,Darssan L. Eswaramoorthi,Het Riteshkumar Shah,Madhur Thareja,Michelle A Ihetu,Harshul Raj Surana,Kaushik Roy,Amit Sheth*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CANDI-QA is a new benchmark dataset for evaluating LLMs on context-sensitive, domain-specific question answering in high-stakes fields like medicine and finance. It introduces two question types — Information Assistance and Applied Inference — and evaluates over ten models, also proposing MTSS-Net, a neuro-symbolic baseline that combines neural retrieval with rule-based reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional QA benchmarks fail to capture contextual grounding, user awareness, and nuanced domain understanding required in specialized fields such as medical diagnostics and financial advisory. There is a need for evaluation frameworks that test whether LLMs can deliver accurate, context-sensitive, and user-aligned answers in these high-stakes settings.

Method: The authors introduce CANDI-QA (Contextual Alignment for Niche Domains Question Answering), an expert-curated dataset with two structured categories: (1) Information Assistance Questions — direct factual queries requiring precise extraction, and (2) Applied Inference Questions — multi-hop reasoning tasks needing situational inference. Over ten language models (from compact open-source to proprietary systems) are evaluated. Additionally, MTSS-Net is proposed as a baseline: a lightweight neuro-symbolic framework combining neural retrieval with rule-based reasoning.

Result: Evaluation reveals profound challenges in achieving contextual alignment in niche domains. Current LLMs, without enhanced contextual or symbolic integration, exhibit significant limitations on both Information Assistance and Applied Inference question types, underscoring the difficulty of the benchmark.

Conclusion: CANDI-QA serves as a critical benchmark for advancing context-aware LMs, highlighting the gap between general-purpose capabilities and specialized domain requirements. The work encourages development of robust, trustworthy AI systems for high-stakes domains through better contextual and symbolic integration.

Abstract: The deployment of large language models (LLMs) in specialized domains like medical diagnostics and financial advisory necessitates evaluating capabilities beyond general knowledge. Traditional question-answering benchmarks often fail to capture the nuanced contextual grounding, user awareness, and domain understanding these fields require. To address this, we introduce CANDI-QA (Contextual Alignment for Niche Domains Question Answering), a novel dataset evaluating LLMs on delivering accurate, context-sensitive, and user-aligned answers in specialized settings. CANDI-QA features expert-curated question-answer pairs structured into two categories: (1) Information Assistance Questions, which are direct, factual queries requiring precise extraction, and (2) Applied Inference Questions, which are multi-hop reasoning tasks needing situational inference to generate actionable insights. We evaluate over ten diverse language models, from compact open-source to state-of-the-art proprietary systems. As a robust baseline, we present MTSS-Net, a lightweight neuro-symbolic framework combining neural retrieval with rule-based reasoning. Our findings highlight the profound challenges of achieving contextual alignment in niche domains, revealing the limitations of current LLMs without enhanced contextual or symbolic integration. Ultimately, CANDI-QA serves as a critical benchmark for advancing research in context-aware language models, stimulating the development of robust, trustworthy AI for high-stakes domains.

</details>


### [3] [G-SHARE: A Guideline-Based Structured Reasoning Framework for Human-Factor Event Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11892)
*Xingyu Xiao,Mao Du,Jiejuan Tong,Jingang Liang,Haitao Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: G-SHARE is a structured reasoning framework that encodes nuclear power plant human-factor event diagnosis guidelines into a multi-stage pipeline with evidence extraction, stepwise reasoning, and consistency repair, significantly outperforming one-shot LLMs and traditional ML baselines on real Chinese nuclear industry data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-factor event diagnosis in nuclear power plants currently relies on expert interpretation of narrative reports and guideline-based reasoning. Existing data-driven and one-shot LLM approaches lack structured reasoning, have poor alignment with formal diagnostic guidelines, and may produce logically inconsistent conclusions.

Method: The authors propose G-SHARE, which operationalizes the CNNP nine-step human-factor event diagnosis guideline into a three-stage pipeline: (1) evidence extraction from narrative reports, (2) stepwise diagnostic reasoning generating intermediate rationale, and (3) post-hoc consistency repair for logical validation of outputs. A real-world dataset was constructed from Chinese nuclear industry sources, with a gold-standard expert-annotated subset used for evaluation against one-shot prompting and traditional ML baselines.

Result: G-SHARE substantially outperforms both one-shot prompting and traditional machine learning baselines. The strongest G-SHARE variant achieved the best overall accuracy and macro-F1. Ablation studies demonstrate that structured reasoning and consistency enforcement are critical to robust diagnosis, particularly under weak prompting conditions.

Conclusion: Transforming expert diagnostic guidelines into auditable, structured reasoning workflows provides a practical pathway for intelligent human-factor analysis in safety-critical industries. The G-SHARE framework demonstrates that guideline-based multi-stage reasoning with consistency enforcement yields both higher accuracy and greater logical reliability than unstructured LLM approaches.

Abstract: Human-factor event diagnosis is essential for learning from operational events in nuclear power plants, yet its quality depends strongly on expert interpretation of narrative reports and guideline-based reasoning.Existing data-driven or one-shot large language model approaches often lack structured reasoning, have limited alignment with formal diagnostic guidelines, and may generate logically inconsistent conclusions. To address this issue, this study proposes G-SHARE, a guideline-based structured reasoning framework that operationalizes the CNNP nine-step human-factor event diagnosis guideline into a multi-stage diagnostic pipeline.The framework consists of evidence extraction, stepwise diagnostic reasoning, and post-hoc consistency repair, enabling explicit use of report evidence, intermediate rationale generation, and logical validation of diagnostic outputs. A dataset of real human-factor event reports was constructed from Chinese nuclear industry sources, and a gold-standard subset annotated by domain experts was used for evaluation. Results show that G-SHARE substantially outperforms one-shot prompting and traditional machine learning baselines, with the strongest version achieving the best overall accuracy and macro-F1. Ablation results further indicate that structured reasoning and consistency enforcement are critical to robust diagnosis, especially under weak prompting conditions. The findings demonstrate the value of transforming expert diagnostic guidelines into auditable reasoning workflows, providing a practical pathway for intelligent human-factor analysis in safety-critical industries.

</details>


### [4] [CityBehavEx: A Scalable and Empirically Validated LLM-Assisted Urban Simulation Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12086)
*Gustavo H. Santos,Aline Viana,Thiago H Silva*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CityBehavEx is an LLM-assisted urban simulation platform that scales to city-size populations (100K agents, 75 days, <1 hour on a consumer GPU) by combining classical mobility models with fine-tuned cross-encoders for semantic alignment, rather than invoking LLMs per action. It supports behavior inspection, empirical validation, and generates mobility patterns that better match real-world spatial, temporal, and semantic distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based multi-agent urban simulators are costly to scale and weakly validated against empirical data. There is a need for a platform that can simulate city-scale populations efficiently while producing mobility patterns that are verifiably realistic against ground-truth distributions.

Method: CityBehavEx replaces per-action LLM calls with a hybrid architecture: established human mobility models generate movement patterns, while fine-tuned cross-encoders estimate semantic alignment between agent profiles, schedules, and activity transitions. The platform provides an interactive interface for defining regions, launching experiments, inspecting trajectories, debugging behaviors, and validating outputs against real-world mobility, time-use, and semantic metrics.

Result: A case study demonstrated simulation of 100,000 agents over 75 days in under one hour on a single consumer GPU. The generated mobility patterns showed improved alignment with real-world spatial, temporal, and semantic distributions compared to prior LLM-based approaches.

Conclusion: By decoupling semantic reasoning from action generation via cross-encoders and classical mobility models, CityBehavEx achieves both scalability and empirical fidelity, making large-scale urban simulation practical and trustworthy.

Abstract: Recent LLM-based multi-agent urban simulators can generate semantically rich city routines, but they remain costly to scale and are often weakly validated against empirical mobility patterns. We present CityBehavEx, an interactive LLM-assisted urban simulation platform that scales to city-size populations, exposes agent behavior for inspection, supports empirical validation, and generates mobility patterns that better match real-world spatial, temporal, and semantic distributions. Instead of invoking large language models for every agent action, CityBehavEx combines established human mobility models with fine-tuned cross-encoders that estimate semantic alignment between agent profiles, schedules, and activity transitions. This design enables large-scale simulations, as demonstrated in a case study of 100,000 agents over 75 days in under one hour on a single consumer GPU. The platform allows users to define simulation regions, launch experiments, inspect trajectories and activity traces, debug unrealistic behaviors, and validate generated routines against real-world mobility, time-use, and semantic metrics.

</details>


### [5] [I'm Sorry, but I Can't Help with Braille: Revealing Accessibility Failures in State-of-the-Art LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11893)
*Abdullah Abdullah*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs fail at Korean-Braille translation due to poor tokenization and weak cross-modal alignment, while a fine-tuned small model (T5-small) dramatically outperforms them, revealing a systematic limitation of current LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs excel on many language tasks, but their ability to handle structurally constrained, accessibility-critical modalities like Braille is unexplored. The authors aim to assess whether multilingual, instruction-tuned LLMs can generalize to bidirectional Korean-Braille translation.

Method: The authors constructed a human-annotated Korean-Braille dataset and evaluated state-of-the-art LLMs in zero-shot and prompted settings. They then fine-tuned T5-small on the same data and compared performance using standard metrics: SacreBLEU, ChrF++, CER, BLEU, ROUGE-L, METEOR, and CIDEr.

Result: LLMs produced consistently poor, unstable outputs with substantial disagreement from human judgments. In contrast, supervised fine-tuning of T5-small yielded large, stable gains across all metrics, significantly outperforming zero-shot and prompted LLM baselines.

Conclusion: The findings expose a systematic limitation of current LLMs in Braille processing, attributed to missing Braille-aware tokenization and weak Korean-Braille alignment. Modest task-specific supervision proves highly effective, suggesting that dedicated training is essential for accessibility-critical, low-resource modalities.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) perform strongly on many language tasks, but their capability in structurally constrained, accessibility-critical modalities such as Braille remains unclear. We evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs on bidirectional Korean-Braille translation using a human-annotated dataset. Despite expectations that multilingual, instruction-tuned models can generalize to Braille via text representations, we find consistently poor, unstable outputs and substantial disagreement with human judgments. These results point to missing Braille-aware tokenization and weak alignment between Korean and Braille patterns. In contrast, supervised fine-tuning of a small model (T5-small) on the same data yields large and stable gains over zero-shot and prompted LLM baselines across standard metrics (SacreBLEU, ChrF++, CER, BLEU, ROUGE-L, METEOR, CIDEr). Our findings reveal a systematic limitation of current LLMs and demonstrate the effectiveness of modest task-specific supervision.

</details>


### [6] [Graph-Based Detection of Disinformation Narrative Diffusion between Russian and Ukrainian Telegram Channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11894)
*Yuliia Vistak,Viktoriia Makovska,Vera Schmitt,Veronika Solopova*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A graph-based framework combining weak supervision and propagation graph analysis to detect disinformation narratives on Telegram by clustering semantically related claims and modeling their diffusion across channels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detecting disinformation narratives on social media is hindered by massive amplification scale, rapid content evolution, and linguistic variability. Post-level analysis alone fails to capture coordinated narrative-level amplification patterns.

Method: The authors propose a two-part framework: (1) weak supervision to aggregate semantically related claims into narrative-level clusters, and (2) propagation graph analysis to model how these narratives diffuse across interconnected Telegram channels, enabling detection of coordinated amplification.

Result: The integration of textual signals with network structure yields a scalable method for detecting disinformation narratives, revealing propagation patterns within large-scale messaging environments that post-level analysis misses.

Conclusion: Combining weak supervision with propagation graph analysis provides an effective, scalable approach for identifying and analyzing disinformation narratives and their coordinated spread on platforms like Telegram.

Abstract: Detecting disinformation narratives on social media is challenging due to the scale of amplification, rapid evolution, and linguistic variability of online content. We propose a graph-based framework for identifying and analyzing disinformation narratives in Telegram ecosystems by combining weak supervision with propagation graph analysis. The approach aggregates semantically related claims into narrative-level clusters and models their diffusion across interconnected channels. This enables the detection of coordinated narrative amplification that is difficult to capture through post-level analysis alone. Our results demonstrate that integrating textual signals with network structure provides a scalable method for detecting disinformation narratives and offers insights into how they propagate within large-scale messaging environments.

</details>


### [7] [Fine-Tuned Multi-Agent Framework for Detecting OCEAN in Life Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12215)
*Rasiq Hussain,Darshil Italiya,Joshua Oltmanns,Mehak Gupta*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM framework with psychometrically-conditioned sub-agents (high/low/neutral trait perspectives) and a judge aggregator for robust OCEAN personality detection from text, reducing single-model bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single LLMs exhibit latent "personality-like" biases from pretraining, causing inconsistent personality inferences. A multi-perspective approach is needed to capture the latent, context-dependent, and subtly expressed nature of personality traits across long narratives.

Method: A fine-tuned multi-agent framework where sub-agents are conditioned via masked language modeling (MLM) and psychometric supervision to adopt high, low, or neutral perspectives for each OCEAN trait. A judge LLM aggregates and compares sub-agent outputs to produce final trait predictions, leveraging complementary perspectives to mitigate individual model biases.

Result: The framework was evaluated on a life narrative dataset through quantitative and qualitative experiments, including baselines, ablations, and inference quality analyses, demonstrating improved personality assessment performance.

Conclusion: The approach provides a scalable and interpretable method for text-based personality inference, demonstrating the value of multi-agent reasoning grounded in psychometric supervision.

Abstract: Accurately assessing personality from text is challenging because traits are latent, context-dependent, and often subtly expressed across long narratives. Large language models (LLMs) offer new opportunities by processing extensive textual contexts, but pretraining of these models can induce latent "personality-like" biases, making single-model inferences inconsistent. We propose a fine-tuned multi-agent framework for detecting OCEAN personality traits, in which sub-agents are conditioned to adopt high, low, or neutral perspectives for each trait through masked language modeling (MLM) and psychometric supervision. A judge LLM aggregates and compares sub-agent outputs to generate final trait predictions, capturing multiple complementary perspectives while mitigating individual model biases. We evaluate the framework on life narrative dataset through quantitative and qualitative experiments, including baselines, ablations, and inference quality analyses. Our approach offers a scalable and interpretable method for text-based personality inference, highlighting the benefits of multi-agent reasoning grounded in psychometric supervision.

</details>


### [8] [TAKE: Trajectory-Aware Knowledge Estimation for Text Dataset Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11898)
*Tri-Nhan Vo,Dang Nguyen,Sunil Gupta*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TAKE proposes a text dataset distillation framework using influence functions and Optimal Transport to compress corpora to 0.1% of original size while preserving downstream task accuracy, enabled by trajectory-aware knowledge scoring and synthetic prototype selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale text corpora impose substantial and cumulative costs across storage, training, fine-tuning, and continual learning, creating a need for principled, extreme dataset compression without sacrificing task fidelity.

Method: TAKE computes per-sample knowledge scores by convolving knowledge-based influence along the training trajectory (Trajectory-Aware Knowledge Estimation). These scores serve as sample weights in a discrete Optimal Transport objective that selects prototypes from a synthetically generated candidate pool, achieving extreme compression.

Result: Evaluated on text classification and natural language inference tasks at 0.1% compression (20 samples/class), TAKE demonstrates that data efficiency is achievable without sacrificing downstream task fidelity.

Conclusion: The approach provides a theoretically grounded framework for dataset distillation with broader implications for coreset construction and data-centric AI. Code is publicly released.

Abstract: Large-scale text corpora have become a quiet bottleneck in modern NLP, not just in storage, but in the accumulated cost of training, fine-tuning, and continual learning. We propose a text dataset distillation framework that reduces corpora to as little as 0.1% of their original size while preserving downstream task fidelity. We approach distillation through the lens of influence functions, which quantify each sample's contribution to the downstream objective, a natural and principled basis for selection. We introduce Trajectory-Aware Knowledge Estimation (TAKE), which convolves the knowledge-based influence along the training trajectory into a single per-sample knowledge score, capturing informative samples. These scores serve as sample weights within a discrete Optimal Transport objective, guiding prototype selection from a synthetically generated candidate pool. We evaluate TAKE on downstream accuracy across text classification and natural language inference tasks at extreme compression (0.1% or 20 samples/class), showing that data efficiency is achievable without sacrificing task fidelity. The approach is theoretically grounded, with broader implications for coreset construction and data-centric AI. We release our source code at https://github.com/votrinhan88/take.

</details>


### [9] [RCWT: Measuring Task-Budget Displacement from Coordination Content in LLM Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12216)
*Brenda Lelis,Rodrigo Cabral-Carvalho*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The Roundtable Context Window Test (RCWT) isolates how coordination content displaces task-relevant tokens within a fixed context budget, revealing a sharp performance cliff when residual reference evidence drops to a few hundred tokens—an effect attributable to task-budget displacement rather than semantic interference from coordination volume.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent and memory-augmented LLM systems pack coordination content (shared state, discussion, summaries, role instructions) into the same finite context window as task instructions and evidence. Under a fixed context budget, every token spent on coordination displaces tokens available for the task—yet this trade-off lacks a controlled measurement protocol. The authors aim to quantify this displacement effect systematically.

Method: The RCWT protocol varies coordination content volume while holding total context budget, position order, task family, and scoring constant. A context-dependent recall task at W=4096 tokens measures performance as coordination overhead increases, compressing residual reference evidence. An intact-task ablation expands total prompt length while keeping the full task/reference block present, distinguishing budget displacement from semantic interference.

Result: In the main RCWT, GPT-4.1-mini, Claude Haiku 4.5, and Gemini 2.5 Flash maintain near-baseline performance through moderate coordination overhead, then degrade sharply once residual reference evidence falls to a few hundred tokens. Window-scaling summaries align with a task-specific residual-budget interpretation, not a fixed percentage threshold. In the intact-task ablation, all models return every scored field correctly up to a 95% coordination ratio, confirming the cliff is driven by task-budget displacement, not coordination volume per se.

Conclusion: RCWT is a measurement primitive for context-allocation budgeting, quantifying the displacement cost of coordination content. The sharp degradation is best interpreted as evidence starvation (task-budget displacement) rather than semantic interference. RCWT is not a complete theory of multi-agent benefit or session-level coordination but provides a rigorous, falsifiable benchmark for context-window allocation decisions.

Abstract: Multi-agent and memory-augmented LLM systems often place coordination content, shared state, prior discussion, tool outputs, summaries, and role instructions, inside the same finite prompt used for the current task. This creates a practical allocation problem: every token spent on coordination is unavailable to task instructions or evidence when a call is assembled under a fixed context budget. We introduce the Roundtable Context Window Test (RCWT), a controlled protocol for measuring this task-budget displacement effect. RCWT varies coordination content while controlling total budget, position order, task family, and scoring. In the main context-dependent recall task at $W=4096$, three commercial models remain near baseline through moderate overhead and then degrade sharply once residual reference evidence falls to a few hundred tokens. Window-scaling summaries are consistent with a task-specific residual-budget interpretation rather than a fixed percentage threshold, but we treat this as descriptive evidence rather than a universal law. To test whether the fixed-budget cliff persists when task evidence remains intact, we add an intact-task ablation: the full task/reference block is kept present while coordination tokens increase by expanding total prompt length. In that setting, all tested calls return every scored field correctly across GPT-4.1-mini, Claude Haiku 4.5, and Gemini 2.5 Flash up to a 95\% coordination ratio. This ablation narrows the claim: the main RCWT cliff is best read as task-budget displacement, not as proof that coordination volume alone causes semantic interference in the original open-ended task. RCWT is therefore a measurement primitive for context-allocation budgeting, not a complete theory of multi-agent benefit or session-level coordination.

</details>


### [10] [Transforming LLMs into Efficient Cross-Encoders via Knowledge Distillation for RAG Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11933)
*Shreeya Dasa Lakshminath,Shubhan S*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Fine-tuned LLaMA 3 (8B) with LoRA and 4-bit quantization replaces cross-encoders in RAG reranking, achieving significant gains in answer relevancy (+14%), context precision (+16%), answer similarity (+19%), and correctness (+21%) while avoiding quadratic inference costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-encoders provide high reranking accuracy in RAG pipelines but incur quadratic inference costs (each query must be paired with every document), making real-time deployment impractical. The authors aim to replace cross-encoders with a more efficient reranker that maintains or improves accuracy without the quadratic complexity.

Method: A two-stage pipeline: (1) supervised fine-tuning of LLaMA 3 (8B) on a custom query-document relevance dataset using the Unsloth framework with LoRA adapters for parameter-efficient training, and (2) 4-bit post-training quantization for efficient inference. The fine-tuned model serves as a drop-in reranker within a dual-retriever RAG pipeline combining BM25 and dense vector search. Evaluation uses the RAGAS framework on a domain-specific QA benchmark.

Result: The LLaMA 3 reranker outperforms the cross-encoder baseline with gains of 14% in answer relevancy, 16% in context precision, 19% in answer similarity, and 21% in answer correctness, all while reducing inference overhead through 4-bit quantization.

Conclusion: Instruction-tuned LLMs can be effectively adapted into accurate and efficient rerankers, offering a viable alternative to cross-encoders without the quadratic inference complexity, making them suitable for real-time RAG deployments.

Abstract: Cross-encoders achieve high reranking accuracy in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines but impose quadratic inference costs that limit real-time deployment. We address this by fine-tuning LLaMA 3 (8B) as a drop-in reranker using a two-stage pipeline: supervised fine-tuning on a custom query-document relevance dataset via the Unsloth framework with LoRA adapters, followed by 4-bit quantization for efficient inference. The resulting model replaces the cross-encoder in a dual-retriever RAG pipeline combining BM25 and dense vector search. Evaluated on a domain-specific question-answering benchmark using the RAGAS framework, our fine-tuned LLaMA 3 reranker achieves gains of 14% in answer relevancy, 16% in context precision, 19% in answer similarity, and 21% in answer correctness over the cross-encoder baseline, while reducing inference overhead through 4-bit quantization. These results demonstrate that instruction-tuned LLMs can be adapted into accurate, efficient rerankers without the quadratic complexity of traditional cross-encoders.

</details>


### [11] [MAGE: Understanding Stability-Performance Trade-offs in Multi-component Prompt Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11944)
*Prateek Singh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces MAGE, a controlled framework for studying how components of iterative prompt optimization interact. It uncovers the Prompt Optimization Coupling Effect (POCE), where combining stochastic optimization signals in a reflective loop simultaneously boosts performance and amplifies variance. Key findings: failure-grounded reflection is essential, MAGE outperforms baselines on GSM8K-Hard, and increasing candidate diversity drives POCE. The effect is headroom-dependent and vanishes with strong base models; in low-data regimes, fixed prompts beat all reflective optimizers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While iterative prompt optimization methods proliferate, little is understood about how their internal components (memory, selection strategies, evaluation) interact when combined. Existing work analyzes components in isolation, missing emergent coupling effects that arise from closed-loop stochastic optimization. The authors aim to systematically characterize these interactions rather than propose a state-of-the-art optimizer.

Method: The authors build MAGE (Memory-Augmented Goal-directed Prompt Evolution), a modular framework integrating episodic memory, multi-objective Pareto selection, and adaptive evaluation. They conduct controlled ablation studies, manipulating components (reflection type, candidate pool size, selection criteria) and measuring both mean accuracy and variance across multiple seeds. Experiments span GPT-4o-mini and Llama 3.1 8B on GSM8K-Hard, with additional low-data regime tests (N_train=30).

Result: Three main results: (1) Failure-grounded reflection is necessary — score-only (OPRO) and abstract critique (Self-Refine) methods fail. (2) MAGE achieves 46.4% vs. GEPA's 34.0% on GSM8K-Hard (p=0.998), with comparable variance. (3) Expanding candidate pool from n=3 to n=5 improves mean accuracy by +21.6% but increases variance 3.7×, the clearest POCE signal. POCE is headroom-dependent: it disappears when the base model already performs well. In low-data settings, well-designed fixed prompts outperform all reflective optimizers.

Conclusion: Prompt optimization systems behave as coupled stochastic processes where components interact non-additively. Evaluation should therefore consider both performance and stability (variance), not just peak accuracy. The scaffold (fixed prompt engineering) can dominate optimizer choice in data-scarce regimes, and POCE's dependence on headroom suggests practical deployment strategies should account for base model capability.

Abstract: How do different components of iterative prompt optimization interact, and what happens when they are combined? We investigate this through MAGE (Memory-Augmented Goal-directed Prompt Evolution), a controlled analysis framework for studying component interaction in prompt optimization. MAGE is not proposed as a superior optimizer in absolute terms; it integrates episodic memory, multi-objective Pareto selection, and adaptive evaluation as a platform for controlled ablation. Our experiments uncover a previously unreported phenomenon, the Prompt Optimization Coupling Effect (POCE): when multiple stochastic optimization signals operate within a closed reflective loop, they interact in ways that simultaneously improve performance and amplify variance, behavior that cannot be predicted by analyzing components in isolation. Three main findings emerge. First, failure-grounded reflection is essential: methods relying only on scores (OPRO) or abstract critique (Self-Refine) fail to improve prompts. Second, MAGE achieves 46.4% versus GEPA's 34.0% on GSM8K-Hard (+12.4%, P(MAGE>GEPA)=0.998, 5 seeds on gpt-4o-mini), with comparable variance (7.3% vs. 7.0%). Third, increasing candidate diversity reveals the clearest POCE signal: expanding the candidate pool from n=3 to n=5 improves mean accuracy by +21.6% while increasing variance by 3.7x. We further validate on Llama 3.1 8B and show POCE is headroom-dependent: when the base model already achieves high accuracy, variance amplification disappears. Finally, in low-data regimes (Ntrain=30), well-designed fixed prompts outperform all reflective optimizers, indicating that scaffold choice dominates optimizer choice. Our results suggest prompt optimization systems behave as coupled stochastic processes and should be evaluated in terms of both performance and stability, not just peak accuracy.

</details>


### [12] [Belief-reality separation lives in routing over a shared value slot in language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11945)
*Oliver Steele,Jiangtao Wen,Yuxing Han*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Language models separate a character's belief from reality using two distinct mechanisms: a generic value slot that stores the attributed property, and a routing mechanism at the query position that selects whether to read from the belief frame or reality frame. The slot itself is untagged; the separation lives in dissociated routing subspaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand where and how in the internal computation of language models the separation between a character's belief and reality is implemented, moving beyond behavioral demonstrations to mechanistic interpretability.

Method: Mechanistic interpretability analysis across three architectures and five model families (identifying the emergence of belief-reality separation between 3B and 7B parameters). The authors use de-confounded stimuli to avoid theory-of-mind benchmark shortcuts, and employ subspace interventions to probe the causal roles of identified components: a value slot, routing subspaces, and two routes (asserted belief via direct binding; derived belief via visibility-gated lookback).

Result: The separation relies on two mechanisms: (1) a generic value slot that binds the attributed value and is untagged (intervening on it affects both belief and reality readouts equally); (2) a router at the query position with dissociated routing subspaces that select the frame without injecting donor values. Two routes fill the slot: asserted beliefs bind directly from text; derived beliefs arrive via a visibility-gated lookback. Only the derived route depends on described visibility. Subspaces trained on one route steer the other.

Conclusion: The belief-reality separation in language models is mechanistically grounded in a slot-and-router architecture, with the dissociation living in routing subspaces rather than in the slot itself. This format generalizes broadly; a companion paper shows the same mechanism applies across other non-actual contexts (counterfactual, fictional, temporal).

Abstract: Capable language models hold what a character believes apart from what is true: told "Anna believes the cup is blue; in reality it is red," they answer blue about Anna and red about the world. Where in the computation does that separation live? We show it rests on two separable mechanisms at two positions. A generic value slot binds the attributed value. A router at the query position selects which frame, the character's belief or reality, a query reads out. Two routes fill the slot: an asserted belief, whose value the text supplies, binds in directly; a derived belief, whose value must be inferred from what the character could see, arrives by a visibility-gated lookback. A subspace trained on either route steers the other, and only the derived route depends on described visibility. The slot itself carries no belief-reality tag: intervening on it moves a reality readout as strongly as a belief one. The separation lives instead in a dissociated pair of routing subspaces, which flip a query between frames without injecting the donor's value. These results hold across three architectures, on stimuli de-confounded against theory-of-mind-benchmark shortcuts; the behavior itself emerges between 3B and 7B across five model families. This paper develops the single belief-reality axis in depth; a companion paper shows the same slot-and-router format is shared across the other non-actual contexts a sentence can open (counterfactual, fictional, temporal).

</details>


### [13] [Hybrid Continual Learning for Low-Resource Australian Aboriginal Language Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11946)
*Pravina Mylvaganam,Ting Dang,Eliathamby Ambikairajah,Vidhyasaharan Sethu,Jingyao Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Two hybrid continual learning methods (Replay Augmented Elastic Weight Consolidation and Constraint Guided Knowledge Distillation) are proposed to adapt pretrained speech models for endangered Australian Aboriginal language identification, mitigating catastrophic forgetting under extreme data scarcity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Endangered Australian Aboriginal languages suffer from extreme data scarcity, limiting language identification performance. While transfer learning from high-resource languages helps, catastrophic forgetting occurs during adaptation. Continual learning can address this but remains difficult with very limited data.

Method: Two hybrid continual learning approaches: (1) Replay Augmented Elastic Weight Consolidation, combining rehearsal-based replay with EWC regularization; and (2) Constraint Guided Knowledge Distillation, which uses knowledge distillation with constraints to preserve prior knowledge. Both adapt pretrained speech models for AAL identification.

Result: Experiments on Warlpiri, Dalabon, and Dharawal demonstrate that both proposed methods outperform standard fine-tuning and existing continual learning baselines, achieving better adaptation to multiple AALs while retaining performance on previously learned high-resource languages.

Conclusion: Hybrid continual learning strategies effectively balance adaptation to low-resource Aboriginal languages with retention of high-resource language knowledge, offering a viable path for integrating endangered languages into speech technology systems.

Abstract: Language identification is an important step toward integrating endangered Australian Aboriginal languages (AALs) into speech technologies supporting language revitalisation and digital inclusion. However, extreme data scarcity limits model performance. Transfer learning from high-resource languages shows promise but often suffers from catastrophic forgetting when adapting to new languages. Continual learning (CL) can mitigate this issue, though it remains challenging with very limited data. To address this, we propose two hybrid continual learning methods: Replay Augmented Elastic Weight Consolidation and Constraint Guided Knowledge Distillation to adapt pretrained speech models for AAL identification while preserving previously learned knowledge. Experiments on Warlpiri, Dalabon and Dharawal show that the proposed methods outperform fine-tuning and existing CL baselines, improving adaptation to multiple AALs while maintaining performance on previously learnt high-resource languages.

</details>


### [14] [Evaluating Nonuniform Dependability Across Response Conditions: A Conditional Generalizability Framework Illustrated in Automated Essay Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11981)
*Yi Gui*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a conditional generalizability framework that treats automated scoring configurations as a universe of measurement conditions, evaluates design adequacy by comparing analytical D-study projections with empirical sweeps, and conditions reliability on entropy-defined response strata. Demonstrated with automated essay scoring, it reveals that dependability varies modestly but systematically across entropy levels, with higher-entropy responses requiring more crossed conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregate reliability estimates in generalizability theory (G-theory) mask heterogeneity in measurement-design burden across different response conditions. A single G-study or D-study can misrepresent a design's adequacy for specific strata. While recent G-theory extensions have addressed AI-generated item variants on the response side, no analogous framework exists for the scoring side, where AI-mediated scoring configurations introduce their own universe of variability that needs systematic treatment.

Method: The framework has three components: (1) treating automated scoring configurations (encoder architectures and scoring-head families) as a universe of admissible measurement conditions, not incidental modeling choices; (2) comparing analytical D-study projections with empirical configuration sweeps over a finite scoring pool to produce two estimands of design adequacy, whose agreement or divergence diagnoses the realized configuration universe; and (3) conditioning evidence on entropy-defined response strata, using entropy purely as an operational stratification variable. The approach is demonstrated on automated essay scoring of timed L2 writing.

Result: The realized design was dependable in aggregate (Phi ≈ 0.76). When re-estimated within entropy strata, dependability remained high but declined modestly and robustly: Phi = 0.88 (lowest entropy), 0.87 (medium entropy), and 0.84 (highest entropy). This gradient implies different decision-study requirements, with the highest-entropy stratum requiring the most crossed conditions to achieve comparable reliability.

Conclusion: The conditional generalizability framework provides a portable workflow for evaluating nonuniform dependability across response strata. It extends G-theory to the scoring side by formalizing AI-mediated scoring configurations as a universe of measurement conditions and demonstrates that entropy can serve as an effective operational stratification variable for identifying where designs are more or less adequate.

Abstract: Aggregate reliability estimates can obscure heterogeneity in measurement-design burden across response conditions, so a single G- or D-study may mischaracterize a design's adequacy for particular strata. This study introduces a conditional generalizability framework with three components. First, automated scoring configurations -- the encoder architectures and scoring-head families admissible within a fixed pipeline -- are treated as a universe of admissible measurement conditions rather than incidental modeling choices. Second, analytical D-study projections are compared with empirical configuration sweeps over a finite scoring pool, yielding two estimands of design adequacy whose agreement or divergence diagnoses the realized configuration universe. Third, evidence is conditioned on entropy-defined response strata, treating entropy as an operational stratification variable, not a construct claim about writing quality. Whereas recent generalizability-theory extensions address AI-generated item variants on the response side, this framework addresses the analogous scoring-side problem: AI-mediated scoring configurations. Demonstrated with automated essay scoring of timed L2 writing, the realized design was dependable in aggregate (Phi approx 0.76). Re-estimated within entropy strata, dependability stayed high but declined modestly and robustly (Phi = 0.88, 0.87, 0.84) -- a gradient implying different decision-study requirements, the highest-entropy stratum requiring the most crossed conditions. The framework offers a portable workflow for evaluating nonuniform dependability.

</details>


### [15] [Agentic systems for breast cancer treatment recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12051)
*Vinicius Anjos de Almeida,Nícolas Henrique Borges,Leonardo Vicenzi,Helena Kociolek,Sarah Miriã de Castro Rocha,Frederico Nassif Gomes,Júlia Cristina Ferreira Ribeiro,Lucas Emanuel Silva e Oliveira*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Evaluated agentic LLM systems on breast cancer treatment planning using 72 real cases; best model scored 0.594/1.0 with persistent clinically relevant failures, indicating current systems remain insufficient for unsupervised clinical use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly explored for clinical decision support, but their reliability in complex oncology treatment planning remains unclear. The study aims to rigorously evaluate whether agentic LLM systems can generate trustworthy breast cancer treatment recommendations across all disease stages.

Method: 72 real breast cancer cases (stages I–IV) were used with 1,147 case-specific rubrics generated via Asymmetric Information Rubric Generation (AIRG), where rubric generators had access to real clinical decisions that evaluated models did not. Seven pipelines were compared: single-LLM baselines, tool-augmented systems, and multi-agent architectures with fact-checking and autonomous subagent spawning. Oncologist-led error analysis was conducted.

Result: Best configuration (Claude Opus 4.8 with D&C+SA pipeline) achieved a global score of 0.594 ± 0.025. Tool use and agent autonomy had mixed effects. Performance varied by clinical domain and disease stage. Error analysis revealed incorrect/missing recommendations, flawed justifications, citation errors, outdated claims, and overconfidence.

Conclusion: Agentic LLM systems can generate clinically relevant breast cancer recommendations but remain insufficient for unsupervised clinical use due to persistent, clinically significant failures.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored for clinical decision support, but their reliability in complex oncology treatment planning remains unclear. We evaluated agentic LLM systems for breast cancer treatment recommendation generation using 72 real clinical cases across stages I to IV and 1,147 case-specific rubrics generated through Asymmetric Information Rubric Generation (AIRG), in which the rubric generator had access to real clinical decisions unavailable to the evaluated models. Seven pipelines were compared, including single-LLM baselines, tool-augmented systems, and multi-agent architectures with fact checking and autonomous subagent spawning. The best-performing configuration, Claude Opus 4.8 with the D&C+SA pipeline, achieved a global score of 0.594 $\pm$ 0.025. Tool use and increased agent autonomy had mixed effects, improving performance in some settings but degrading it in others. Performance varied by clinical domain and disease stage, and oncologist-led error analysis revealed persistent clinically relevant failures, including incorrect or missing recommendations, flawed justifications, citation errors, outdated claims, and overconfidence. These findings suggest that agentic LLM systems can generate clinically relevant breast cancer recommendations, but remain insufficient for unsupervised clinical use.

</details>


### [16] [Beyond Parallel Tracking: Interactive Multi-Feature Fusion Drives Semantic Reconstruction from Non-invasive Brain Recordings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12071)
*Boda Xiao,Xiran Xu,Songyi Li,Yujie Yan,Xihong Wu,Heping Cheng,Jing Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A multi-feature fusion framework combining static (W2V) and dynamic (GPT) semantic representations via interactive gating outperforms single-feature approaches for non-invasive neural semantic reconstruction, with non-linear cross-attention fusion achieving SOTA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-invasive neural semantic decoding suffers from a representational mismatch between semantic features and noisy neural signals. Prior methods use either static lexical or dynamic contextual representations in isolation, failing to capture how the human brain simultaneously integrates stable word attributes and dynamic contexts, leading to information loss.

Method: A multi-feature fusion framework that integrates static lexical representations (Word2Vec) with dynamic contextual representations (GPT) through an interactive gating mechanism. Two fusion approaches are benchmarked: linear Naive Concatenation and non-linear Multi-Head Cross-Attention. Performance is evaluated via semantic reconstruction and text generation experiments.

Result: A robust performance hierarchy emerged: Cross-Attention > Concatenation > GPT > W2V. The non-linear cross-attention fusion method achieved state-of-the-art performance, demonstrating that collaborative modulation between contextual and lexical features significantly outperforms isolated feature representations.

Conclusion: Simulating cooperative interaction between contextual information and core lexical attributes is critical for neural language decoding. The proposed multi-feature fusion framework with cross-attention offers a viable and effective non-invasive brain-to-text decoding method.

Abstract: Continuous semantic reconstruction from non-invasive neural recordings remains limited by the representational mismatch between semantic feature spaces and neural coding patterns, which severely impedes cross-modal alignment between high-noise neural signals and target semantic features. Prior semantic decoders have predominantly relied on static lexical representations or dynamic contextualized representations in isolation. This single-dimension approach inevitably leads to severe information loss, as it fails to account for the human brain's capacity to integrate stable word attributes and dynamic contexts simultaneously.To bridge this gap, this study introduces a multi-feature fusion framework for non-invasive semantic reconstruction, systematically benchmarking two integration approaches: linear Naive Concatenation and non-linear Multi-Head Cross-Attention. Within this framework, our approach complements static lexical representations (W2V) with dynamic contextual representations (GPT) via an interactive gating mechanism to facilitate cooperative processing during language comprehension.Evaluated through extensive semantic reconstruction and text generation experiments, our framework reveals a robust performance hierarchy: Cross-Att > Concat > GPT > W2V. Crucially, the non-linear cross-attention fusion method achieves state-of-the-art performance, demonstrating that neural language decoding benefits from simulating the collaborative modulation between contextual information and core lexical attributes rather than depending on isolated individual features, while also offering a viable non-invasive brain-to-text decoding method.

</details>


### [17] [The Capacity of Thought: Benchmarking Llama 3.2 in Semantic fMRI Neural Language Decoding and Improving the Huth Encoding-Model Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12079)
*Milos Suvakovic,Dom Marhoefer,Glenn Grant-Richards,Aidan Pinero*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper presents two fMRI-to-language decoding studies: (1) an improved ridge regression pipeline achieving modest gains (METEOR=0.149, BLEU-1=0.200), and (2) fMRIFlamingo, a deep cross-attention model mapping BOLD to Llama-3.2-1B that achieves 42.86% Top-1 ranking accuracy but fails a blind-control ablation — revealing that apparent decoding success is driven by the frozen language model prior, not neural signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decoding continuous language from fMRI is a central challenge for non-invasive BCIs. Prior work (Huth et al.) showed promising encoding/decoding results, but the authors question whether more powerful language models genuinely improve decoding or merely exploit strong linguistic priors that mask failure. The motivation is to push encoding performance and, critically, to test whether high-capacity LMs yield true neural-driven decoding through rigorous blind controls.

Method: Two investigations: (1) Replication and extension of Huth et al.'s ridge regression pipeline with expanded voxel selection (10K→15K), GPT-2 medium replacing GPT-1 as beam-search proposal model, and GPU-accelerated bootstrap training, evaluated on subject UTS03 across three held-out narratives. (2) fMRIFlamingo: a brain tokenizer + Perceiver Resampler maps BOLD activity to a frozen Llama-3.2-1B via trainable gated cross-attention layers, evaluated on a 1-in-100 ranking task. A blind-control ablation with zeroed fMRI inputs tests whether performance depends on neural signal or the language prior alone.

Result: Study 1: Mean METEOR=0.149, BLEU-1=0.200 (11% relative METEOR gain over replication baseline). Study 2: fMRIFlamingo achieves 42.86% Top-1 accuracy on 1-in-100 ranking (well above 1% chance), but the blind-control ablation with zeroed fMRI yields near-identical scores, demonstrating that the frozen Llama prior — not neural input — drives decoding performance.

Conclusion: Higher-capacity language models do not inherently improve fMRI decoding and can actively obscure failures. Rigorous blind-control evaluations are essential to distinguish genuine neural decoding from exploitation of strong language priors. The field must adopt such controls as standard practice to avoid overclaiming decoding performance.

Abstract: Decoding continuous language from fMRI signals remains a core challenge in non-invasive brain-computer interface research. We present two complementary investigations. First, we improve the Huth et al. ridge regression encoding pipeline through expanded voxel selection (10K->15K), substitution of GPT-2 medium for GPT-1 as the beam-search proposal model, and GPU-accelerated bootstrap training, achieving mean METEOR = 0.149 and BLEU-1 = 0.200 across three held-out narratives for subject UTS03 -- an 11% relative METEOR gain over our replication baseline. Second, we introduce fMRIFlamingo, which maps BOLD activity to a frozen Llama-3.2-1B with trainable gated cross-attention layers via a learned brain tokenizer and a Perceiver Resampler. Despite achieving 42.86% Top-1 accuracy on a 1-in-100 ranking task, well above chance, a blind control ablation with zeroed fMRI inputs yields near-identical scores, revealing that apparent decoding success is driven primarily by the frozen language prior rather than by neural input. These results demonstrate that high-capacity language models do not inherently improve fMRI decoding and can actively obscure failures without rigorous blind-control evaluation.

</details>


### [18] [Evaluating Health Misinformation in Low-Resource Languages: Integrating Small Language Models with a Culturally-Sensitive Responsible NLP Framework (Bangla as a Case Study)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12336)
*Farnaz Farid,Raihan Alam,Al Al-Areqi,Farhad Ahamed,Muhammad Hassan Khan,Sadia Hossain,Irena Veljanova,Anika Tabassum Binte Hossain*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes a culturally sensitive AI framework using Small Language Models (SLMs) for health misinformation detection in low-resource languages (Bangla), outperforming LLMs while incorporating Responsible NLP principles like cultural sensitivity, harm potential, and communication quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI's dual role in both spreading and combating misinformation disproportionately affects Culturally and Linguistically Diverse (CALD) communities, where non-English contexts and low socioeconomic status lead to data scarcity, poor AI model performance, and limited access to trustworthy health information due to neglected language nuances and traditions.

Method: Experiments evaluate various Small Language Models on a Bangla-translated health misinformation dataset for claim extraction (Phi-4 identified as best). A novel detection framework is then designed using Responsible NLP, integrating cultural sensitivity, potential for harm, and communication quality metrics to holistically evaluate misinformation in low-resource languages. A dashboard for medical professionals is also developed.

Result: Phi-4 achieves the optimal balance between precision and recall in health misinformation claim extraction. The proposed Responsible NLP-based framework addresses SLM limitations by incorporating multidimensional evaluation criteria beyond traditional accuracy metrics.

Conclusion: SLMs combined with a Responsible NLP framework provide a viable, culturally sensitive solution for health misinformation detection in low-resource languages, offering a practical tool for medical professionals and a path toward equitable AI-driven health information access for CALD communities.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) technologies, while serving as a foundational enabler for modern social media and digital health services, exert a bivalent effect by simultaneously acting as a combatant against and a spread vector for misinformation. A prevalent challenge in mitigating this issue arises in non-English contexts and low socioeconomic classes, where limited data hinders the training of AI models for effective detection. Consequently, culturally and linguistically diverse (CALD) communities struggle to access trustworthy health information through AI-driven tools. Current AI tools underperform due to a lack of training data and are largely unable to consider language nuances and traditions in non-English contexts. This research addresses these gaps by proposing a CALD-friendly AI-based health misinformation detector and providing a dashboard for medical professionals to analyse this misinformation, a critical step toward mitigating a growing concern among CALD populations. To this end, we conduct a series of experiments using a Bangla-translated health misinformation dataset to evaluate the performance of various Small Language Models (SLMs). SLMs are particularly relevant in this context given the frequent underperformance of Large Language Models (LLMs), which often stems from insufficient domain-specific knowledge and the prohibitive costs of resource-intensive fine-tuning. The results demonstrate that Phi-4 is the superior model, achieving an ideal balance between precision and recall in claim extraction. Then, to mitigate the limitations of SLMs, we design and test a novel health misinformation detection framework grounded in Responsible Natural Language Processing (NLP), which incorporates cultural sensitivity, potential for harm, and communication quality, thereby providing a holistic lens for evaluating misinformation in low-resource languages.

</details>


### [19] [Token Reduction Is Not Cost Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12161)
*Sarel Weinberger,Amir Hozez*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper challenges the common evaluation practice of measuring context-reduction layers by token removal alone, instead investigating when compression actually reduces billed cost without harming task success. Through a large-scale campaign of 2,908 provider-billed Claude Code runs, the authors find that (1) prompt-cache traffic dominates costs (~87%), (2) tool-output token reduction does not reliably predict billed-cost reduction, and (3) compression can harm task completion by removing critical information. They propose evaluating compression using success-adjusted billed cost rather than token reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of context-reduction layers (compressors, retrieval rankers, payload proxies) for API-based coding agents focuses on how much text they remove. The authors argue this metric is misleading because it does not capture whether token reduction actually translates to lower billed cost, nor whether it preserves task success. They aim to shift evaluation toward a more economically meaningful standard.

Method: A pre-registered, hash-frozen, paired experimental campaign of 2,908 provider-billed Claude Code runs (2,848 analyzed), spanning 103 tasks, seven repositories, and three models. The campaign compared a baseline against two generations of hook-based compression and an API-boundary proxy, within a broader program of ~5,500 billed executions. Cost composition was reconstructed and analyzed, including a dollar-weighted residual decomposition. A small single-shot study on SWE-bench-derived Go tasks assessed compression's effect on task completion accuracy.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Prompt-cache traffic dominated costs—cache creation and reads accounted for ~87% of reconstructed four-component cost (~80% of actual bill), with an 8.7% un-attributable residual that scaled with thinking effort on Haiku 4.5. (2) An arm removing 38% of estimated raw tool-output tokens had 6.8% higher paired cost (95% CI: +2.8% to +11.3%), and per-task token reduction correlated only weakly with cost change (Pearson r = 0.15, CI crossing zero). (3) Compression reduced SWE-bench patch application from 27/40 to 15/40 by corrupting verbatim edit anchors, yielding fewer solves at higher cost per solve.

Conclusion: Token reduction is an unreliable proxy for billed-cost savings in API-based coding agents because cached tokens dominate billing and compression can increase trajectory length or errors. The authors propose a layered evidence standard centered on success-adjusted billed cost, arguing that compression must be evaluated by whether it lowers actual cost without degrading task outcomes.

Abstract: Context-reduction layers for API-based coding agents, including command-output compressors, retrieval rankers, and payload-optimizing proxies, are usually evaluated by how much text they remove. We ask instead: when does reducing retrieved context or tool output lower the actual billed cost of a coding agent without reducing task success or lengthening its trajectory?
  Our primary evidence is a pre-specified, hash-frozen, paired campaign of 2,908 provider-billed Claude Code runs, of which 2,848 were analyzed, covering 103 tasks, seven repositories, and three models. The campaign compared a baseline with two generations of hook-based compression and an API-boundary proxy, within a broader measured program of roughly 5,500 billed executions.
  Three findings emerge. First, prompt-cache traffic dominated cost composition. Cache creation and reads accounted for about 87% of reconstructed four-component cost, or about 80% of the actual bill, with an 8.7% dollar-weighted residual that retained telemetry could not attribute. On Haiku 4.5, this residual scaled with thinking effort.
  Second, tool-output reduction did not reliably predict billed-cost reduction. An arm that removed 38% of estimated raw tool-output tokens had 6.8% higher paired cost (95% CI: +2.8% to +11.3%), while per-task reduction was only weakly associated with cost change (Pearson r = 0.15, CI crossing zero).
  Third, compression can harm task completion by removing action-critical evidence. In a small single-shot study on SWE-bench-derived Go tasks, compression reduced patch application from 27/40 to 15/40 by corrupting verbatim edit anchors, and the compressed grounded arm produced fewer solves at higher observed cost per solve.
  We propose a layered evidence standard centered on success-adjusted billed cost rather than token reduction alone.

</details>


### [20] [We Hebben Een Serieus Translatie: Modeling Intercomprehension as Probabilistic Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12169)
*Thomas Hikaru Clark,Edward Gibson,Roger Levy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Bayesian noisy-channel model of intercomprehension—the ability to partially understand an unfamiliar related language—using only an L1 language model and a form/symbolic noise model, then validates it against human behavioral data from three language pairs, showing better alignment than ablations and large-model zero-shot prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Intercomprehension (zero-shot partial understanding of a related L2 by an L1 speaker) is poorly understood computationally. Prior algorithmic models of noisy-channel inference haven't been applied to this real-world cross-language phenomenon, leaving open questions about how comprehenders make flexible inferences under wide uncertainty.

Method: A Bayesian noisy-channel framework: an L1-only language model scores latent hypotheses about L2 utterance translations, combined with a noise model that infers L2→L1 word mappings via either form-based (edit distance) similarity or handcrafted symbolic rules. The model is tested against a human behavioral experiment where English, Spanish, and Russian speakers infer meanings of utterances in Dutch, Italian, and Ukrainian, respectively. Comparisons include model ablations and zero-shot prompting of large language models.

Result: The full model aligns more closely with the distribution of human intercomprehension performance than ablated variants. It also compares favorably to zero-shot prompting of much larger models, despite using only L1 resources and a simple noise model.

Conclusion: The Bayesian noisy-channel framework provides a cognitively plausible computational account of intercomprehension, demonstrating that flexible cross-lingual inference can emerge from combining L1 prior knowledge with simple form- or rule-based noise models, without requiring bilingual training data or large-scale LMs.

Abstract: Intercomprehension refers to partial intelligibility of an unfamiliar language (L2) by a speaker of a related language (L1). How is this zero-shot cross-language comprehension possible? In this work, we extend past work on algorithmic models of noisy-channel inference to model intercomprehension in a Bayesian framework. The model uses an LM in L1 only for scoring latent hypotheses about the translations of observed L2 utterances, and a general-purpose noise model to infer a mapping between L2 and L1 words based on either form-based similarity or symbolic rules. We then conduct a human behavioral experiment, eliciting inferences for utterances in Dutch, Italian, and Ukrainian from speakers of English, Spanish, and Russian, respectively. Our full model shows a closer alignment to the distribution of human intercomprehension performance than ablations, and also compares favorably to zero-shot prompting of much larger models. These results provide a cognitively plausible computational model of intercomprehension, and highlight the flexible inferences made by comprehenders under wide uncertainty in real-world cross-language scenarios. We share our code publicly.

</details>


### [21] [Entropy in Semantic Memory Navigation in Blind and Sighted Individuals: The Effect of Visual Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12185)
*Felipe D. Toro-Hernández,Rodrigo Lagos,Sergio E. Chaigneau*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study investigated how visual experience shapes semantic memory by comparing blind and sighted individuals on a property listing task, using semantic entropy as a measure of retrieval predictability. Sighted participants showed higher entropy for abstract than concrete concepts, while blind participants did not; instead, blind individuals exhibited higher entropy for visually salient concrete concepts, suggesting visual experience fundamentally alters how semantic memory is organized and navigated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test embodied accounts of semantic memory by examining whether conceptual grounding requires visual experience, using congenitally blind populations as a critical test case.

Method: A property listing task was administered to congenitally blind and sighted participants for both concrete and abstract concepts. Semantic entropy — an embedding-based NLP metric capturing retrieval predictability — was computed, and generalized linear mixed models were used to analyze group differences in navigation patterns.

Result: Sighted individuals showed significantly higher semantic entropy for abstract compared to concrete concepts, while blind participants did not replicate this pattern. Instead, blind individuals demonstrated elevated entropy specifically for visually salient concrete concepts (e.g., penguin).

Conclusion: Visual experience plays a critical role in the organization and dynamic navigation of semantic memory, supporting embodied cognition frameworks and demonstrating that the absence of vision leads to qualitatively different conceptual retrieval structures.

Abstract: Embodied accounts of semantic memory highlight the role of sensorimotor systems in acquiring and storing knowledge. Congenitally blind populations offer a critical test bed for these assumptions, providing an opportunity to assess whether conceptual grounding requires visual experience. In this study, we assessed semantic memory navigation differences between blind and sighted individuals using a property listing task with concrete and abstract concepts. We computed semantic entropy, an embedding-based natural language processing metric that captures the predictability of retrieval. Generalized linear mixed models revealed distinct navigation patterns across groups: while sighted individuals showed higher entropy for abstract than concrete concepts, blind participants did not. Instead, blind individuals exhibited higher entropy for visually salient concrete concepts (e.g., penguin). These results underscore the role of visual experience in the organization and dynamic navigation of semantic memory.

</details>


### [22] [Comparing Semantic Navigation in Humans and Large Language Models using Natural Language Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12195)
*Gabriel Paris-Colombo,Rodrigo M. Cabral-Carvalho,Felipe D. Toro-Hernández*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Humans exhibit more variable and exploratory semantic search than LLMs across entropy, step size, and dispersion metrics; temperature tuning in LLMs only partially matches individual dimensions but fails to reproduce the full human profile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether large language models (LLMs) replicate human-like dynamics of semantic memory retrieval, conceptualized as navigation through conceptual space, and whether temperature tuning can align LLM behavior with human search patterns.

Method: The authors compared verbal fluency data from 82 human participants with outputs from three LLMs (GPT-4o, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Claude-Sonnet-4.5) across eight temperature settings. They applied trajectory-based NLP metrics to quantify three complementary dimensions: entropy (step size predictability), distance to next (successive semantic steps), and distance to centroid (global dispersion).

Result: Humans showed significantly higher entropy, larger semantic steps, and broader global dispersion than all LLMs. Temperature tuning produced only partial alignment: individual metrics matched at specific temperature settings, but no single configuration simultaneously reproduced the human profile across all three dimensions.

Conclusion: Human semantic search implements a distinctive balance between local exploitation and global exploration that current LLM architectures cannot fully reproduce, suggesting fundamental differences in how humans and LLMs navigate conceptual space.

Abstract: Semantic memory retrieval can be conceptualized as navigation through conceptual space. We compared semantic search dynamics between humans and three large language models (GPT-4o, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Claude-Sonnet-4.5) using verbal fluency data. By applying trajectory-based NLP metrics to the items generated by 82 human participants and LLM output across eight temperature settings, we quantified three complementary dimensions: entropy (step size predictability), distance to next (successive semantic steps), and distance to centroid (global dispersion). Humans exhibited higher entropy, larger semantic steps and broader dispersion than all LLMs, indicating more variable and exploratory search. Temperature tuning produced only partial alignments, as individual metrics matched between humans and LLMs at specific settings, but no configuration reproduced the complete human profile (in all dimensions). These findings suggest that human semantic search implements a distinctive balance between local exploitation and global exploration that current model architectures fail to reproduce.

</details>


### [23] [Fin-Analyst at FinMMEval 2026 Task 3: A Live Hybrid Trading Agent with LLM Specialists and Rule-Based Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12233)
*Mohotarema Rashid,Lingzi Hong,Junhua Ding,K. S. M. Tozammel Hossain*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Fin-Analyst is a hybrid trading agent combining an eight-specialist LLM pipeline with a meta-agent for Tesla (TSLA) and a rule-based voting system for Bitcoin (BTC). It ranked first on the FinMMEval 2026 leaderboard for TSLA (+13.51% return, Sharpe 4.10), while the BTC strategy broke even but outperformed a falling baseline. Key findings include the dominance of 8-K filings as signals and the brittleness of memoryless agents in sideways markets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based trading agents have shown promise in equity markets, but existing work focuses narrowly on US equities with little evidence from live deployment. The authors aim to fill this gap by building and evaluating a hybrid agent in a live competition setting (FinMMEval 2026 Task 3), covering both a single-stock equity (TSLA) and a cryptocurrency (BTC).

Method: Fin-Analyst uses a two-pronged architecture: (1) For TSLA, an eight-specialist LLM pipeline processes news, SEC filings, fundamentals, analyst forecasts, technical indicators, and social sentiment, with a Meta-Agent aggregating all signals into a final trading decision. (2) For BTC, a lightweight rule-based system uses a three-signal voting mechanism. Both operate in a live evaluation window, with ablation studies isolating which signal sources (particularly 8-K disclosures) drive performance. Error analysis examines memoryless agent failure modes.

Result: Fin-Analyst achieved first place on the official leaderboard for TSLA with a +13.51% return (+28.33 points over Buy-and-Hold), a Sharpe ratio of 4.10, and an 88% win rate. The BTC vote ended approximately flat but substantially outperformed a sharply declining baseline. Asset ranking reversed between interim and final evaluations, indicating that short live windows produce volatility-sensitive rankings. Ablation identified event-driven 8-K filings as the most influential TSLA signal. Error analysis revealed that memoryless agents repeat incorrect calls for consecutive days, and that fixed-threshold BTC rules lost money trading on noise in sideways markets while the LLM pipeline gained under similar conditions.

Conclusion: The hybrid architecture is effective for equity trading but the rule-based approach falters in sideways crypto markets. The authors advocate for a memory-aware, fully LLM-based successor for both asset classes, as memoryless agents are prone to persistent errors and fixed-threshold rules are vulnerable to noise.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) trading agents show promising performance in equity markets, yet remain narrowly focused on US equities with little evidence from live deployment. We present Fin-Analyst, a hybrid agent for FinMMEval 2026 Task 3: an eight-specialist LLM pipeline over news, SEC filings, fundamentals, analyst forecasts, technical indicators, and social sentiment, aggregated by a Meta-Agent for Tesla (TSLA), and a lightweight rule based three-signal vote for Bitcoin (BTC). On the final official leaderboard (accessed 2026-07-05), Fin-Analyst ranks first of all agents on TSLA with a +13.51% return, +28.33 points over Buy-and-Hold (Sharpe 4.10, 88% win rate), while the BTC vote ends flat yet well above a sharply falling baseline.
