Relative to the interim performance, the asset ranking reversed, indicating that short live windows yield volatility-sensitive rankings. Ablation identifies event-driven 8-K disclosures as the most influential TSLA signal. Error analysis shows that the memoryless agents repeat wrong calls for days at a time, and that the fixed-threshold BTC rules lost money by trading on noise in a sideways market while the LLM pipeline gained under similar conditions, motivating a memory-aware, LLM-based successor for both assets.

</details>


### [24] [FinResearchBench II: A Deep Research Benchmark with Consensus-Derived Gold Rubrics for Distinguishing Financial Report Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12252)
*Beidi Luan,Rui Sun,Sinuo Wang,Yan Gu,Chao Li,Zhenliang Xiong,Jing Li,Zuo Bai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a scalable, human-expert-free pipeline for generating and executing evaluation rubrics for financial deep research agents. Using 104 real user queries, it synthesizes 14,450 candidate rubrics from model-generated reports, validates that LLM judges can replace human experts (98.67% agreement on unanimous items), and applies consistency and distinguishability filters to produce 2,600 consensus-derived gold rubrics, enabling clear ranking differentiation across 10 systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale evaluation of deep research agents producing long-form financial reports is bottlenecked by the reliance on human experts to define and execute high-quality rubrics. Removing this dependency would enable scalable, automated benchmarking and system comparison.

Method: A pipeline is constructed with three stages: (1) Automatic rubric synthesis — 14,450 query-specific candidate rubrics are generated from model-produced reports based on 104 real-world financial queries. (2) Human-LLM alignment validation — three human experts and a three-LLM judge panel are compared on a sampled subset, showing sufficient consistency (98.67% label-level agreement on jointly unanimous items) to justify replacing humans. (3) Consensus-derived gold rubric filtering — a strict consistency filter (all three LLM judges unanimously agree on every report under a query) and a distinguishability filter (rubric must assign at least one majority-yes and one majority-no across systems) are applied, yielding 3,687 consistency-passed rubrics and a final set of 2,600 distinguishable gold rubrics.

Result: The filtering process retains 3,687 consistency-passed rubrics, with 2,600 passing the distinguishability filter to form the final gold set. Using these rubrics, 10 deep research systems are evaluated with clearly differentiated rankings, achieving item-level pass rates ranging from 58.58% to 22.23%. LLM judges achieve 98.67% label-level agreement with human experts on jointly unanimous items, validating the substitution.

Conclusion: The pipeline demonstrates that human experts can be removed from both rubric generation and execution without sacrificing evaluation quality. This enables scalable benchmark construction, automatic system comparison, and supports future work on evaluation-driven system improvement for financial deep research agents.

Abstract: Deep research agents are increasingly used to produce long-form financial reports, yet large-scale evaluation remains bottlenecked by the need for human experts to define and execute high-quality rubrics. We address this problem by proposing a scalable pipeline for generating high-quality rubrics without human experts in the final loop. We build a financial deep research benchmark from 104 real-world user queries and automatically synthesize 14,450 query-specific candidate rubrics from model-generated reports. To justify removing human experts from rubric execution, we compare rubric judgments from three human experts with those from a three-LLM judge panel on a sampled subset, and show that LLM-based evaluation is sufficiently consistent with human evaluation to replace it for large-scale rubric screening, including 98.67\% label-level agreement on jointly unanimous items. We then derive consensus-derived gold rubrics through two filters: a strict consistency filter, which keeps a rubric only if the three LLM judges unanimously agree on every report under the same query, and a distinguishability filter, which keeps a rubric only if it assigns at least one majority-yes and at least one majority-no label across the evaluated systems. This process retains 3,687 consistency-passed rubrics, of which 2,600 remain distinguishable and form the final set of consensus-derived gold rubrics. Using this final rubric set, we obtain clearly differentiated rankings across 10 deep research systems, with item-level pass rates ranging from 58.58\% to 22.23\%. More broadly, because the pipeline removes human-expert execution from rubric generation and evaluation, it is naturally scalable for benchmark evaluation, automatic system comparison, and future studies of evaluation-driven system improvement.

</details>


### [25] [Policy-Conditioned Constrained Decoding for Column-Level Access Control in Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12341)
*Ryoto Miyamoto,Xin Fan,Hayato Yamana*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PCC-SQL enforces column-use policies in Text-to-SQL by applying deterministic per-token logits masks during decoding, eliminating policy violations in a single pass while maintaining high coverage and low overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-SQL systems deployed across trust boundaries (between data providers and users) must balance policy compliance, answer coverage, and bounded cost. Existing refusal mechanisms rely on column-mention checks and stochastic enforcement, which are insufficient because compliance depends on how columns are semantically used (not just mentioned), and stochastic methods cannot deterministically guarantee zero violations.

Method: The authors formalize a column-use policy capturing three semantic roles: output, filter condition, and aggregation argument. These roles are aligned with grammar productions tracked by the decoder. The system, PCC-SQL, applies a per-token logits mask that deterministically prevents column-use violations on the supported SQL fragment during a single decoding pass, eliminating the need for post-hoc filtering or stochastic rejection.

Result: Across three benchmarks and three open-source models, PCC-SQL achieves 0% Leakage Rate (deterministic compliance) with Coverage up to 88.7% on Spider-CU. Token overhead remains within +10% of direct prompting. Semantic alignment is additionally evaluated via execution accuracy.

Conclusion: Deterministic, grammar-aligned logits masking during decoding is an effective and efficient approach to enforcing fine-grained column-use policies in Text-to-SQL, guaranteeing zero violations while preserving high query coverage at minimal cost.

Abstract: Text-to-SQL is increasingly deployed across trust boundaries between data providers and users. Such deployment must balance three competing requirements: policy compliance, answer coverage, and bounded cost. Existing approaches typically decide refusal based on which columns a query mentions and enforce it stochastically. Whether a query is compliant, however, depends not only on which columns appear but on how they are used, and stochastic enforcement cannot deterministically rule out violations. We formalize this requirement as a column-use policy over semantic use: output, filter condition, and aggregation argument. We integrate the policy by aligning each role with grammar productions tracked by the decoder. The resulting system, PCC-SQL, applies a per-token logits mask that deterministically eliminates single-query column-use violations on the supported SQL fragment in a single decoding pass. Across three benchmarks and three open-source models, PCC-SQL achieves 0% Leakage Rate and Coverage up to 88.7% on Spider-CU, while staying within +10% tokens of direct prompting. We additionally assess semantic alignment with execution accuracy.

</details>


### [26] [A Shared Subcircuit Lets LLMs Count Down Across Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12279)
*Jacob Dunefsky,Wes Gurnee,Emmanuel Ameisen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct uses a reusable "countdown subcircuit" that tracks remaining tokens until a target length, generalizing across diverse counting tasks and even across models via a shared geometric motif.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models can perform tasks requiring precise token counting (e.g., writing fixed-length sentences, ending sequences at correct positions), but the underlying mechanism enabling this capability is poorly understood. The authors aim to reverse-engineer how a frontier LLM internally represents and tracks countdown-style positional constraints.

Method: The authors isolate a countdown subcircuit in Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct using a controlled task (writing fixed-length sentences ending in a specified word). They analyze the geometry of the subcircuit's representations, compare them to a previously identified motif in another frontier LLM, and employ unsupervised probing on natural language data to discover additional tasks where the same subcircuit activates.

Result: A distinct countdown subcircuit was identified that compares current position to a goal length and estimates remaining time. Its representational geometry matches a motif previously found in a different frontier LLM on a separate task. Unsupervised probing revealed the subcircuit is reused across many tasks, including those where goal length is inferred from context rather than explicitly stated.

Conclusion: Reverse-engineering subcircuits enables understanding how behaviors generalize from a single example to many different tasks and even across different models, demonstrating that shared mechanistic motifs underlie ostensibly distinct capabilities in large language models.

Abstract: Writing a sentence of exactly twelve words; ending a DNA sequence at the right codon; formatting an ASCII table. These are all tasks that language models can do that requires tracking how many tokens remain before a target. In this work, we identify in Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct a general mechanism for performing these tasks: a "countdown subcircuit" that compares the current position to a goal length and estimates the time remaining until then. We first isolate a countdown subcircuit in a controlled setting, in which the model is tasked with writing a fixed-length sentence ending in a specified word. We then investigate the geometry of the representations used by the subcircuit, and find that the subcircuit uses an identical motif previously identified in a frontier LLM on a separate task, thus suggesting that this motif is shared across models. Finally, we use unsupervised probing on a natural language dataset to find a variety of other tasks where this subcircuit is used, including tasks where the goal length is inferred from context rather than explicitly stated. Our work suggests that reverse-engineering subcircuits allows us to understand how behaviors generalize from a single example to many different tasks and even models.

</details>


### [27] [LakeQuest: A Three-Domain Benchmark for Grounded Question Answering across Data Lakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12310)
*Michael Solodko,Steven Gong,Guangwei Yu,Satya Krishna Gorti,Jesse C. Cresswell,Victor Zhong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LakeQuest is a human-validated benchmark of 9,846 QA pairs spanning three domains (AI/ML metadata, retail banking, biomedical) that evaluates end-to-end retrieval-and-synthesis over realistic, heterogeneous data lakes. It reveals that strong retrieval does not guarantee correct reasoning, exposing failure modes in relation chaining, policy grounding, and cross-modal synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern QA systems perform well on clean, schema-aligned corpora, but real-world enterprise and scientific data lakes are heterogeneous, weakly structured, and noisy. Existing benchmarks abstract away the messy discovery process, failing to evaluate end-to-end retrieval-and-synthesis performance. LakeQuest bridges this gap by providing a realistic benchmark that tests systems on discovery, cross-file composition, and cross-modal reasoning.

Method: The authors construct LakeQuest, a human-validated benchmark with 9,846 QA pairs across three diverse domains: (1) AI/ML metadata, (2) retail banking ledgers, and (3) multimodal biomedical drug information. Each question is paired with exact, modality-aware evidence pointers. The benchmark isolates source discovery from cross-modal synthesis. Baseline evaluations are conducted using standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic tool-use methods.

Result: Baseline evaluations show that high-quality retrieval does not guarantee correct reasoning. Systems consistently fail at: (1) relation chaining in metadata graphs, (2) policy grounding in bank ledgers, and (3) joint tabular QA in biomedical contexts. These failures highlight a gap between retrieval success and faithful cross-file compositional reasoning.

Conclusion: LakeQuest exposes critical failure modes overlooked by existing benchmarks and demonstrates that robust discovery and faithful cross-file composition mechanisms are urgently needed for future agentic QA systems operating over real-world data lakes.

Abstract: While modern question answering (QA) systems excel on clean, schema-aligned corpora, real-world knowledge is rarely so neatly packaged. Answering questions over enterprise and scientific data lakes requires systems to navigate heterogeneous, weakly structured collections of tables, passages, and linked metadata. Current benchmarks abstract away this noisy discovery process, failing to evaluate end-to-end performance. To bridge this gap, we introduce LakeQuest, a human-validated benchmark of 9,846 QA pairs designed to evaluate the end-to-end retrieve-and-synthesize pipeline over realistic data lakes. LakeQuest spans three diverse domains (AI/ML metadata, retail banking, and multimodal biomedical drug information) and pairs every question with exact, modality-aware evidence pointers. By isolating source discovery from cross-modal synthesis, LakeQuest exposes critical failure modes in modern QA systems. Our baseline evaluations, including standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic tool-use methods, reveal that high-quality retrieval does not guarantee correct reasoning. Systems consistently struggle with relation chaining in metadata graphs, policy grounding in bank ledgers, and joint tabular QA in biomedical contexts, highlighting the need for robust discovery and faithful cross-file composition mechanisms in future agentic QA systems.

</details>


### [28] [QUBO-Optimized Evidence Selection for Retrieval-Augmented Question Answering with Unconventional Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12334)
*Rahul Singh,Madhav Vadlamani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper reformulates multi-hop evidence selection for RAG as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) problem, balancing relevance, coverage, complementarity, and redundancy. Evaluated on HotpotQA, the QUBO selector matches LLM-based set selectors in performance while offering a scalable, solver-compatible alternative that decouples combinatorial evidence selection from semantic answer generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard top-k ranking in RAG pipelines selects passages by individual relevance, which fails for multi-hop questions requiring complementary evidence across multiple information needs. LLM-based set selectors address this but are costly and hard to scale. The authors seek a principled, non-LLM approach to structured evidence subset selection.

Method: Evidence selection is cast as a QUBO problem. Given a question, candidate passages, and decomposed information requirements, the method constructs an energy function with terms for relevance, requirement coverage, support strength, redundancy, complementarity, and compactness. Low-energy binary solutions correspond to optimal evidence subsets, which are then fed to a downstream LLM solely for answer generation.

Result: On HotpotQA, the QUBO selector achieves competitive exact-match and token-F1 performance compared to LLM-based set selectors and outperforms non-LLM baselines (BM25, top-k relevance, MMR, hybrid lexical-semantic ranking, greedy coverage, random).

Conclusion: Multi-hop evidence selection can be effectively formulated as discrete optimization, enabling RAG pipelines where LLMs are reserved for semantic processing and answer generation while Ising/QUBO-compatible solvers handle structured context selection.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented question answering depends on selecting evidence passages that jointly support answer generation. However, many RAG pipelines rely on top-\(k\) ranking, where passages are selected mainly by individual relevance scores, even though multi-hop questions often require complementary evidence satisfying multiple information requirements. Recent LLM-based selectors address this by treating retrieval as set selection, but using an LLM for this intermediate stage can be costly and difficult to scale. In this work, we formulate evidence selection as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) problem. Given a question, candidate passages, and decomposed information requirements, our method constructs an energy function that balances relevance, requirement coverage, support strength, redundancy, complementarity, and compactness. Low-energy solutions correspond to compact evidence subsets that cover the needed requirements while avoiding unnecessary or repetitive context. The selected passages are then passed to a downstream language model for answer generation, separating combinatorial evidence selection from semantic answer generation. We evaluate the proposed QUBO selector on HotpotQA and compare it with LLM-based set selectors and non-LLM baselines including BM25, relevance top-\(k\), maximal marginal relevance, hybrid lexical--semantic ranking, greedy coverage, and random selection. The QUBO selector achieves competitive exact-match and token-F1 performance relative to LLM-based selectors while providing a solver-compatible formulation for structured evidence selection. These results suggest that multi-hop evidence selection can be cast as discrete optimization, opening a path toward RAG pipelines where LLMs are reserved for semantic processing and answer generation, while context selection is handled by Ising/QUBO-compatible solvers.

</details>


### [29] [Beyond Binary Detection: A Multi-Dimensional Taxonomy of Cancer Misinformation on Reddit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12383)
*Aria Pessianzadeh,Pooriya Jamie,Naima Sultana,Georgia Himmelstein,Yuliya Zektser,Patricia Ganz,Homa Hosseinmardi,Amir Ghasemian,Rezvaneh Rezapour*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a multi-dimensional taxonomy for cancer misinformation on Reddit, evaluates LLMs for scalable annotation, and finds that ~6% of cancer discussions contain misinformation with significant variation across communities and topics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cancer misinformation research is limited by narrow definitions, small-scale datasets, and binary labeling frameworks, hindering nuanced understanding of how misinformation manifests and spreads in online cancer communities.

Method: The authors developed a seven-dimension taxonomy (misinformation presence, information type, risk level, stance, topical focus, etc.) and used expert-annotated Reddit data across four cancer types (breast, lung, colon, prostate) to evaluate multiple LLMs under zero-shot and few-shot prompting for scalable misinformation classification.

Result: Cancer misinformation accounts for ~6% of Reddit cancer discussions, with substantial variation across communities and topics. Few-shot prompting significantly improves LLM classification performance, especially for nuanced taxonomy dimensions. Recurring misinformation narratives include unsupported treatments, distrust of conventional medicine, and misleading claims about diagnosis and screening.

Conclusion: The proposed taxonomy, annotated dataset, and LLM-based annotation framework establish a foundation for multi-dimensional modeling of online cancer misinformation, enabling more granular detection and analysis than binary approaches.

Abstract: Cancer-related discussions on social media provide an important space for information exchange and peer support, but also facilitate the spread of misinformation that may influence prevention, screening, and treatment decisions. Existing research on cancer misinformation often relies on narrow definitions, small-scale datasets, or binary labeling frameworks. We introduce a multi-dimensional taxonomy for characterizing cancer misinformation in Reddit discussions of breast, lung, colon, and prostate cancer. The taxonomy captures seven dimensions, including misinformation presence, information type, risk level, stance, and topical focus. Using expert-annotated data, we evaluate multiple large language models (LLMs) for scalable misinformation annotation and analyze cancer misinformation across Reddit communities. Our results show that cancer-related misinformation constitutes approximately 6\% of Reddit cancer discussions, with substantial variation across communities and misinformation topics. Few-shot prompting substantially improves classification performance, particularly for nuanced taxonomy dimensions. We additionally identify recurring misinformation narratives centered on unsupported treatments, distrust of conventional medicine, and misleading claims about diagnosis and screening. Our taxonomy, dataset, and findings provide a foundation for multi-dimensional modeling of online cancer misinformation.

</details>


### [30] [Ring-Zero: Scaling Zero RL to a Trillion Parameters for Emergent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12395)
*Xinyu Tang,Gangqiang Cao,Yurou Liu,Yuliang Zhan,Xiaochong Lan,Yifan Li,Yuchen Yan,Han Peng,Zican Dong,Zhenduo Zhang,Tianshu Wang,Xinyu Kong,Zujie Wen,Wayne Xin Zhao,Zhiqiang Zhang,Jun Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A training pipeline (Ring-2.5-1T-Zero) scales zero-RL to 1T parameters, revealing that scaling improves sample efficiency, follows a discovery-to-sharpening phase transition, and spontaneously elicits advanced reasoning behaviors—achieving competitive math benchmark results with structured, efficient chain-of-thought traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing zero-RL (reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, no human annotation) studies are limited to small models due to computational constraints. The authors aim to explore the unscaled frontier—specifically, how training dynamics and emergent reasoning capabilities manifest at the 1-trillion-parameter scale, where naive scaling introduces problems like poor readability, token redundancy, and lack of adaptive reasoning depth.

Method: The authors propose a stable, efficient training pipeline called Ring-2.5-1T-Zero, incorporating three key optimizations: (1) clipped importance sampling to stabilize policy updates, (2) training-inference ratio correction to mitigate distributional drift, and (3) mixed-precision control for computational efficiency. They also introduce a structured evaluation framework assessing CoT quality across three dimensions: comprehensibility, reproducibility, and efficiency.

Result: Three key findings emerge at 1T scale: (1) scaling significantly improves sample efficiency and raises performance ceilings; (2) training progresses through two sequential phases—an initial discovery phase followed by a sharpening phase; (3) the model spontaneously develops advanced cognitive behaviors (anthropomorphism, structured formatting, self-verification, parallel reasoning, context anxiety), making manual heuristics unnecessary. The model achieves competitive performance across seven mathematical benchmarks and produces structured, concise reasoning traces.

Conclusion: Scaling zero-RL to 1T parameters validates the "bitter lesson"—scale unlocks emergent cognitive capabilities that hand-crafted heuristics cannot match. The observed emergent phenomena and phase-transition dynamics provide the community with deeper insights into scaling behaviors at unprecedented model sizes.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards without human-annotated data, often referred to as zero RL, has emerged as a powerful paradigm for eliciting chain-of-thought reasoning. However, due to computational constraints, existing studies are largely restricted to small models, leaving the training dynamics and emergent capabilities at a large scale unexplored. To meaningfully explore this frontier, we aim to elicit high-quality reasoning behaviors from the model. However, we find that naive scaling often suffers from poor readability, token redundancy, and a lack of adaptive reasoning depth. To address these challenges, we present a stable and efficient training pipeline, incorporating algorithmic and system optimizations such as clipped importance sampling, training-inference ratio correction, and mixed-precision control. Our experiments offer three key findings that validate the "bitter lesson" of scaling: (1) scaling to 1T parameters significantly enhances sample efficiency and performance ceilings; (2) the training process progresses sequentially through an initial discovery phase followed by a sharpening phase; and (3) the model spontaneously develops advanced cognitive behaviors, including anthropomorphism, structured formatting, self-verification, parallel reasoning, and context anxiety, rendering hand-crafted heuristics redundant. Evaluated on seven mathematical benchmarks, Ring-2.5-1T-Zero achieves competitive performance. Additionally, to assess CoT quality beyond final-answer correctness, we propose a structured evaluation framework across three dimensions: comprehensibility, reproducibility, and efficiency, where our model demonstrates clear advantages in producing structured and concise reasoning traces. By sharing our observed emergent phenomena, we hope to provide the community with deeper insights into scaling behaviors, particularly at the 1-trillion scale.

</details>


### [31] [WikiSTAR: A System for Shedding Light on the Hidden History of Scientific Wikipedia Articles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12441)
*Omer Ehrlich,Nitzan Barzilay,Rona Aviram,Tom Hope*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: WikiSTAR is an interactive system that uses an LLM classifier with a multi-label taxonomy to identify scientifically meaningful edits in Wikipedia articles, enabling researchers to explore how scientific knowledge evolves across revision histories at multiple granularities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wikipedia's revision history is a valuable record of scientific knowledge evolution, but scientifically meaningful changes are buried under massive volumes of routine edits, making large-scale analysis impractical.

Method: WikiSTAR employs an LLM classifier trained with an expert-designed multi-label taxonomy to tag edit types (e.g., addition of technical terms, new research findings, narrative changes). An interactive interface then surfaces these tagged edits across multiple views, supporting analysis from aggregate trends down to individual revisions.

Result: A user study with domain experts from three fields demonstrated that WikiSTAR surfaced novel patterns and research questions, enabling analyses that were previously infeasible. The system, code, and a human-annotated benchmark are publicly released.

Conclusion: WikiSTAR successfully bridges the gap between Wikipedia's rich revision history and meaningful scientific analysis, providing a scalable, interactive tool for studying the dynamics of scientific knowledge on Wikipedia.

Abstract: Wikipedia plays a key role in shaping public understanding of science, and its openly accessible revision history is a unique record of how scientific knowledge evolves over time. Yet scientifically meaningful revisions are obscured by the sheer volume of routine edits, leaving each article's scientific history hidden. We present WikiSTAR (Scientific Tracking of Article Revisions), an interactive system for exploring scientifically meaningful changes across an article's revision history. Using an LLM classifier with an expert-designed multi-label taxonomy, WikiSTAR first tags edit types such as the addition of technical terms, new research findings, and changes in scientific narrative. Then, through interactive views, an article's full revision history can be traced at any granularity - from aggregate trends that reveal when and in which sections scientific content was added or refined, down to individual edits - showing how scientific knowledge develops at a scale previously impossible. In a user study, experts from three domains found that WikiSTAR surfaced new patterns and research questions and enabled previously impractical analyses. We release our system, code and a human-annotated benchmark.

</details>


### [32] [Language Identification with Succinct Machine-Independent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12443)
*Moses Charikar,Jon Kleinberg,Chirag Pabbaraju*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper addresses two open problems in the Gold-Angluin language identification framework augmented with computational traces: (1) whether positive identification results can be achieved with traces using only a small alphabet, and (2) whether traces can be defined directly from the language itself without requiring an underlying machine model. The authors establish positive results for both, showing that traces enabling identification in the limit can be constructed using a token alphabet linear in the size of the original language alphabet, independent of other language properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent work has shown that augmenting the Gold-Angluin model of language identification with computational traces (like commented code or chain-of-thought tokens) can yield positive learnability results. However, these existing results rely on traces derived from explicit automata-theoretic machine models that generate the language, and the trace token vocabularies are very large. The authors aim to overcome these limitations by investigating whether traces can be defined purely from the language itself with a small alphabet.

Method: The paper constructs computational traces directly from arbitrary collections of languages without requiring an underlying generative machine model. The construction uses a trace alphabet whose size is linear in the size of the alphabet over which the target languages are defined, making it independent of other language-specific properties. The authors then prove that these language-derived traces enable identification in the limit within the Gold-Angluin framework.

Result: The authors establish that for any arbitrary collection of languages, one can define computational traces that enable identification in the limit using an alphabet of tokens whose size is linear in the size of the original language alphabet. This result holds without any dependence on other properties of the languages and without requiring an explicit automata-theoretic machine model to generate the traces.

Conclusion: The positive results demonstrate that the benefits of computational traces for language identification do not inherently depend on large trace alphabets or on access to an underlying machine model. Traces can be defined purely from the languages themselves with compact alphabets, significantly broadening the applicability of the trace-augmented identification framework.

Abstract: Motivated by the power of large language models, there has been renewed interest in the Gold-Angluin model of language identification in the limit, with an eye toward variants of the model that might overcome the negative results for its original formulation. Recent papers on this question have proposed looking at computational traces and annotations of training strings as a source of additional power for a learner, reflecting empirical regularities such as the way that commented source code is easier to learn from than arbitrary source code, and text annotated with algorithmically generated chain-of-thought tokens can be easier to learn from than the raw text itself. This recent work has shown positive results for language identification in the presence of such computational traces, but the traces in these positive results come from explicit automata-theoretic machine models that generate the language, where the underlying vocabulary of tokens for the traces is very large. In this paper, we address two fundamental issues left open by this line of work: can we achieve positive results with traces that use only a small alphabet, and can we define traces directly from the language itself, without requiring an underlying machine model that generates it? We establish positive results for both of these questions: for an arbitrary collection of languages, we show how to define computational traces that enable identification in the limit, using an alphabet of tokens that is linear in the size of the alphabet that the languages are defined over, and independent of any other properties of the languages.

</details>


### [33] [Translation as a Computationally Efficient Bridge: Feasibility of English BERT for Low-Resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12612)
*Hielke Muizelaar,Giulia Rivetti,Marco Spruit,Marcel Haas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study systematically compares translation-based fine-tuning (translating non-English data to English and fine-tuning English BERT) against native-language BERT models across six NLP tasks and five languages. The translation-based approach matches or exceeds native performance in 53.3% of cases, excelling at syntax-oriented tasks and for languages typologically close to English, but underperforms on token-level and culturally nuanced tasks like NER and hate speech detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developing high-quality BERT models for non-English languages is constrained by limited annotated data and high computational costs. While translating data into English and leveraging existing English BERT models is a resource-efficient workaround, no prior study has structurally compared this translation-based fine-tuning strategy against native-language BERT performance across multiple tasks and languages.

Method: The authors evaluate translation-based fine-tuning across six NLP tasks — Sentiment Analysis, Hate Speech Detection, Question Answering, Named Entity Recognition (NER), Part-of-Speech (POS) Tagging, and Natural Language Inference (NLI) — using datasets translated from five languages: Bulgarian, Chinese, Dutch, Italian, and Russian. English BERT is fine-tuned on the translated data, and results are compared against models fine-tuned on native-language data.

Result: The translation-based approach was comparable or superior to native-language models in 53.3% of all settings. Gains were most frequent in Question Answering, POS Tagging, and NLI. Performance declines were common in NER and Hate Speech Detection. Languages typologically closer to English (e.g., Dutch) benefited more, while token-level and culturally nuanced tasks — particularly in Chinese — fared worse.

Conclusion: Translation-based fine-tuning is a scalable, resource-efficient, and empirically validated strategy for extending NLP to low-resource languages. It is most effective for tasks relying on syntactic or structural patterns and for languages similar to English. The approach advances linguistic inclusivity and sustainability in AI, though caution is warranted for token-level and culturally sensitive tasks.

Abstract: BERT models have revolutionised Natural Language Processing (NLP) through their ability to process unstructured text across diverse domains. However, developing high-quality BERT models for non-English languages remains challenging due to limited annotated data and high computational demands. Translating non-English data into English and fine-tuning existing English BERT models offers a resource-efficient alternative, yet few studies have structurally compared translation-based fine-tuning with native-language BERT performance across tasks and languages. This study provides such a comparison, evaluating the feasibility of translation-based fine-tuning across six NLP tasks: Sentiment Analysis, Hate Speech Detection, Question Answering, Named Entity Recognition, Part-of-Speech Tagging, and Natural Language Inference, using datasets translated from Bulgarian, Chinese, Dutch, Italian, and Russian. Across all settings, the translation-based approach was comparable or superior in 53.3 percent of cases. Gains were most frequent in Question Answering, Part-of-Speech Tagging, and Natural Language Inference, while performance declines were common in Named Entity Recognition and Hate Speech Detection. The results show that translation-based fine-tuning is most effective for tasks relying on syntactic or structural patterns and for languages typologically close to English, such as Dutch, but less effective for token-level or culturally nuanced tasks, particularly in Chinese. Overall, this study demonstrates that translation-based fine-tuning offers a scalable, resource-efficient, and empirically validated path for extending NLP to low-resource languages while advancing linguistic inclusivity and sustainability in artificial intelligence.

</details>


### [34] [KnowAct-GUIClaw: Know Deeply, Act Perfectly, Personal GUI Assistant with Self-Evolving Memory and Skill](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12625)
*Yunxin Li,Jinchao Li,Shibo Su,Zhenran Xu,Chenrui Zhao,Tongshu Bian,Xiaoman Liang,Meishan Zhang,Baotian Hu,Min Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: KnowAct-GUIClaw extends the OpenClaw agent framework with a Know-Route-Act-Reflect paradigm that unifies cognitive understanding and operational execution through experience-attributable memory and self-evolving skills, achieving state-of-the-art cross-platform GUI automation (64.1% on MobileWorld) with transferable improvements across base models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OpenClaw, despite being a leading agent framework, suffers from two critical limitations: (1) insufficient cross-platform GUI interaction support across diverse device ecosystems (Android, iOS, HarmonyOS, Windows), and (2) lack of a self-evolution mechanism to improve performance through continuous learning from execution experience and user interaction history.

Method: The authors propose the "Know Deeply, Act Perfectly" paradigm and implement it as KnowAct-GUIClaw, a Know-Route-Act-Reflect framework with: (1) a host agent that uses accumulated interaction experience for long-horizon task decomposition (Know); (2) a pluggable GUI subagent featuring an experience-attributable memory system (Know) and a self-evolving skill library (Act) for cross-platform migration and fast-path integration; and (3) continuous storage of user profiles and feedback to iteratively improve task decomposition accuracy and tool-calling precision.

Result: KnowAct-GUIClaw demonstrates superior efficiency, accuracy, and cross-platform adaptability on Android, iOS, HarmonyOS, and Windows. With open-source Kimi-2.6 models, GUIClaw achieves 64.1% on the long-horizon MobileWorld benchmark, surpassing all agentic frameworks and closed-source models including Seed-2.0-Pro and GPT-5.5. The knowledgeable memory and execution skills are transferable across diverse base models, yielding an 8.5% improvement with Kimi-2.6.

Conclusion: The Know-Route-Act-Reflect framework effectively resolves OpenClaw's cross-platform and self-evolution deficiencies, demonstrating that accumulated user interaction and task-running experience can directly enhance execution accuracy. The pluggable, experience-driven architecture enables both state-of-the-art benchmark performance and model-agnostic transferability of learned skills.

Abstract: OpenClaw has emerged as a leading agent framework for complex task automation, yet it faces insufficient cross-platform GUI interaction support and a well-built self-evolution mechanism. These flaws limit its adaptation to diverse device ecosystems and prevent performance improvements through continuous learning from execution experience. To resolve these issues, we propose the Know Deeply, Act Perfectly paradigm for personal assistants, which holds that accumulated user interaction and task-running experience directly improve execution accuracy and efficiency, unifying cognitive comprehension and operational execution. Based on this paradigm, we introduce KnowAct-GUIClaw, a novel Know-Route-Act-Reflect framework designed to address OpenClaw's GUI manipulation deficits and break through its cross-platform and recursive self-improvement constraints. First, the host agent leverages accumulated interaction experience and task-relevant knowledge for long-horizon task decomposition and allocation (Know). Second, a pluggable GUI subagent with an experience-attributable memory system (Know) and self-evolving skill library (Act), enabling seamless cross-platform migration and fast-path integration. Especially, this framework continuously stores user profiles and feedback to improve the accuracy of task decomposition and tool calls. Extensive experiments across Android, iOS, HarmonyOS and Windows show that KnowAct-GUIClaw achieves superior efficiency, accuracy and cross-platform adaptability. Especially, the GUIClaw with open-source Kimi-2.6 models achieves the best performance (64.1%) on the long-horizon MobileWorld benchmark, beating all agentical frameworks and closed-source agentical models, e.g., Seed-2.0-Pro and GPT-5.5. Additionally, the knowledgeable memory and execution skills supported by our framework are transferable across diverse base models, improving by 8.5% with Kimi-2.6.

</details>


### [35] [Can Induced Emotion Bias LLM Behaviors in Sequential Decision Making?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12631)
*Minh Khoi Ho,Zihao Zhu,Runchuan Zhu,Levina Li,Zhiwen Fan,Zhangyang Wang,Junyuan Hong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether induced emotions affect LLM decision-making using the Iowa Gambling Task. While LLMs can perceive emotions from context, induced emotion does not significantly bias their decisions on average—unlike humans. However, anger specifically reduces sensitivity to penalties and lowers early-stage exploration, revealing subtle, distinct emotional effects on LLM agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are deployed as autonomous agents in high-stakes domains, it is critical to understand whether contextual factors—specifically induced emotions—can modulate their sequential decision-making. Prior work has not clarified whether LLMs, despite being trained to perceive and resonate with emotions, are susceptible to emotion-driven biases in decision tasks.

Method: The authors use the Iowa Gambling Task (IGT), a classic psychological paradigm for decision-making under uncertainty, paired with an imagination-based emotion induction procedure. They first validate that LLMs can sense strong, distinguishable emotions from context and that LLM agents learn from sequential interactions at a human-like pace. They then test whether induced emotion (with a focus on anger) biases decision dynamics in the IGT.

Result: Induced emotion does not significantly bias LLM decision-making on average, contrasting with human behavior. However, anger has conditioned effects: it reduces LLM agents' sensitivity to penalties for bad decisions and, in early game stages, lowers exploration, locking agents into a narrow set of choices.

Conclusion: The effects of induced emotion on LLM decision-making are subtle yet distinct from human patterns. The study provides a validated paradigm (IGT + emotion induction) as a tool for future research on affective modulation of LLM agents, highlighting the importance of understanding emotion-conditioned behavior in autonomous AI systems.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents in high-stakes domains, understanding contextual factors that may modulate their decision-making becomes critical. While LLMs are trained to perceive and resonate with users' emotions, it remains unclear whether induced emotion can influence their sequential decision-making. We investigate this question using the Iowa Gambling Task (IGT), a classic psychological paradigm for studying decision-making under uncertainty, combined with an imagination-based emotion induction procedure. We first validate the feasibility of this paradigm by confirming that LLMs can sense strong, distinguishable emotions from context and that LLM agents can learn from sequential interactions in a human-like pace. With the validated setup, we find that, different from humans, induced emotion does not significantly bias the decision dynamics of LLM agents on average. However, the effects of anger are conditioned: inducing anger makes LLM agents less sensitive to penalties for bad decisions, and in early stages of the game, anger can lower exploration, locking decisions into a few choices early. These findings reveal the subtle yet distinct effects of induced emotion on LLM decision-making compared to human behavior, and provide a tool for future research on affective modulation of LLM agents.

</details>


### [36] [Segregate, Refine, Integrate: Decomposing Multimodal Fusion for Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12686)
*Alexios Filippakopoulos,Elias Kallioras,Nikolaos Xiros,Efthymios Georgiou,Alexandros Potamianos*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SeRIn is a multimodal fusion architecture that segregates modality-specific refinement from cross-modal interaction into separate pathways, deferring full integration to the final prediction step, achieving SOTA on CH-SIMS and CMU-MOSEI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal fusion methods entangle modality-specific signal refinement and cross-modal interaction within the same operations, creating a conflict between two competing objectives. The authors argue these should be architecturally decoupled.

Method: SeRIn (Segregate, Refine, Integrate) enforces a three-stage architectural prior: (1) modality-specific representations evolve along isolated pathways, each refined against its own encoder context; (2) a dedicated cross-modal pathway accumulates their joint evolution without contaminating unimodal streams; (3) full cross-modal interaction is deferred to a final prediction step. The approach is evaluated with ablation studies and gate analysis under visual corruption.

Result: SeRIn achieves state-of-the-art results on CH-SIMS and CMU-MOSEI benchmarks, improving all evaluation metrics on both datasets. Ablations confirm that structured interactions, not added capacity, drive the gains. Gate analysis under visual corruption reveals emergent modality reweighting without explicit supervision.

Conclusion: Enforcing a separation between unimodal refinement and cross-modal interaction as an architectural prior yields both performance improvements and emergent robustness properties, such as automatic modality reweighting under input corruption.

Abstract: Multimodal fusion must simultaneously refine modality-specific signals and model cross-modal interactions; two competing objectives typically entangled within the same operation. We propose \textbf{SeRIn} (\textbf{Se}gregate, \textbf{R}efine, \textbf{In}tegrate), a multimodal LM fusion scheme that enforces this separation as an architectural prior. Modality-specific representations evolve along isolated pathways, each refined against its respective encoder context, while a dedicated cross-modal pathway accumulates their joint evolution without contaminating unimodal streams. Full cross-modal interaction is deferred to a final prediction step - ablations confirm that structured interactions, not added capacity, drive the gains; gate analysis under visual corruption reveals emergent modality reweighting without explicit supervision. SeRIn achieves state-of-the-art results on CH-SIMS and CMU-MOSEI, improving all metrics on both benchmarks.

</details>


### [37] [From Critic to Confidence: PPO for Language-Based Quantitative Prediction with Confidence Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12687)
*Mehak Dhaliwal,Rasta Tadayon,Andong Hua,Haewon Jeong,Yao Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CARE-PPO is a reinforcement learning framework that jointly trains LLMs for quantitative prediction and reliable uncertainty estimation by repurposing the PPO critic as a confidence estimator, using a prediction-error-based reward function. It outperforms logit-based and verbalized baselines across healthcare and finance tasks, including under distribution shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can make quantitative predictions from unstructured text but suffer from hallucinations and overconfidence. Current methods lack reliable confidence signals that indicate when predictions can be trusted. The authors aim to develop a framework that jointly learns accurate numerical estimates and well-calibrated confidence scores.

Method: CARE-PPO (Confidence-Aligned Reward for Estimation with PPO) adapts the actor-critic PPO algorithm. It defines a reward function based on prediction error (e.g., negative absolute error), providing dense, error-aware feedback. The actor learns to minimize prediction error, while the critic learns a value function that naturally aligns with expected prediction quality. At inference, the critic is repurposed as a confidence estimator without additional overhead.

Result: Across healthcare and finance tasks with Qwen-3 models (4B, 8B), CARE-PPO achieves strong quantitative prediction performance and produces confidence estimates significantly better aligned with actual errors than logit-based and verbalized baselines. Gains persist under out-of-distribution settings including linguistic and domain shifts. It also reduces task-specific overfitting on general instruction-following prompts compared to supervised fine-tuning.

Conclusion: CARE-PPO effectively unifies quantitative prediction and uncertainty estimation within a single RL framework, leveraging the critic for reliable confidence signals. The approach generalizes better than supervised methods and maintains calibration under distribution shifts, offering a practical solution for trustworthy LLM-based numerical reasoning.

Abstract: LLMs can perform language-based quantitative prediction from unstructured inputs, but remain susceptible to hallucinations and overconfident errors, making it critical to know not only what a model predicts, but when its predictions can be trusted. We introduce CARE-PPO, a reinforcement learning framework that establishes a connection between loss prediction for uncertainty estimation and actor-critic PPO fine-tuning, enabling joint learning of accurate numerical estimates and reliable confidence signals in language-based quantitative prediction. CARE-PPO uses a Confidence-Aligned Reward for Estimation, defined as a function of prediction error, to provide dense error-aware feedback to the actor while inducing the critic to learn a value function aligned with prediction quality. During inference, we repurpose the critic as a confidence estimator. Across two real-world tasks in healthcare and finance and two Qwen-3 model scales (4B and 8B), CARE-PPO achieves strong quantitative prediction performance, while producing significantly better-aligned confidence estimates through the critic than logit-based and verbalized baselines. These gains persist under realistic out-of-distribution settings across domains, spanning linguistic and domain shifts. Finally, CARE-PPO reduces task-specific overfitting on general instruction-following prompts, consistent with the broader generalization advantages of RL fine-tuning over supervised approaches.

</details>


### [38] [Less Experts, Faster Decoding: Cost-Aware Speculative Decoding for Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12696)
*Jincheng Xie,Runheng Liu,Heyan Huang,Yawen Ling,Hanbin Dai,Yu Zheng,Wen Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EcoSpec is a cost-aware speculative decoding framework for MoE LLMs that selects draft tokens to minimize expert activation scattering, reducing memory traffic and achieving up to 1.62× speedup without modifying target-model verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard speculative decoding selects draft tokens by acceptance likelihood, but in MoE models this causes "expert scattering" — high-probability tokens route to disjoint experts, increasing expert-weight memory traffic and undermining speedup. The non-uniform memory-cost structure of MoE inference motivates a cost-aware alternative.

Method: EcoSpec incorporates a lightweight expert predictor and a dynamic expert buffer into draft-tree selection. It favors draft paths that jointly optimize high acceptance likelihood and expert reuse (i.e., experts already covered in the verification set), without altering the target-model verification rule.

Result: Evaluated on DeepSeek-V3.1 (671B), Qwen3-235B-A22B, and GPT-OSS-120B across reasoning, coding, QA, and dialogue benchmarks, EcoSpec consistently reduced active expert footprints and improved end-to-end decoding speed, achieving up to 1.62× speedup.

Conclusion: Accounting for expert activation cost is important for efficient speculative decoding in large-scale MoE models. EcoSpec demonstrates that cost-aware draft selection effectively mitigates expert scattering and yields practical decoding speedups.

Abstract: Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models have become an important approach for scaling Large Language Models (LLMs), but their inference efficiency depends strongly on expert activation patterns. Speculative decoding (SD) accelerates autoregressive generation by verifying multiple draft tokens in parallel, yet existing draft selection strategies primarily optimize acceptance likelihood. In large-scale MoE models, however, selecting draft tokens also determines the union of experts activated during verification. We observe that confidence-driven SD can introduce \textit{expert scattering}: high-probability draft tokens may route to disjoint experts, increasing expert-weight memory traffic and reducing the speedup from speculation. Motivated by this observation, we revisit draft-tree selection under the non-uniform memory-cost structure of MoE inference. We propose \textsc{EcoSpec}, a cost-aware speculative decoding framework that incorporates predicted marginal expert activation cost into draft selection. With a lightweight expert predictor and a dynamic expert buffer, \textsc{EcoSpec} favors draft paths that preserve high acceptance likelihood while reusing experts already covered by the current verification set, without modifying the target-model verification rule. We evaluate \textsc{EcoSpec} on three large-scale MoE models, including DeepSeek-V3.1 (671B), Qwen3-235B-A22B, and GPT-OSS-120B, across reasoning, coding, question-answering, and dialogue benchmarks. \textsc{EcoSpec} consistently reduces active expert footprints and improves end-to-end decoding speed, achieving up to $1.62\times$ speedup. These results show that accounting for expert activation cost is important for efficient speculative decoding in large-scale MoE models.

</details>


### [39] [Epistemic Stance Flexibility Probing: Measuring Prompt-Conditioned Register Shift in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12739)
*Binwen Liu,Yilin Ren*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ESFP is a benchmark that evaluates whether language models can distinguish between requests for external attribution ("what do experts believe?") vs. self-attribution ("what do you believe?") by shifting epistemic registers appropriately. Evaluated across 8 frontier models, epistemic flexibility proved largely orthogonal to general capability, with a 27B open-weight model matching proprietary systems and reasoning-optimized models showing no consistent advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks assess accuracy, instruction-following, or safety but do not measure whether models coherently shift between neutral attribution (reporting expert consensus) and stance expression (stating their own belief) when prompted differently about the same contested claim. A trustworthy conversational agent must make this distinction, motivating the need for a dedicated benchmark.

Method: The authors introduce ESFP, a behavioral benchmark comprising 104 controlled items across six epistemic categories and five phrasing templates. Evaluation proceeds along four dimensions: (1) lexical self-attribution (surface markers like "I think"), (2) representation-level responsiveness to role framing, (3) sentence-level stance content density via an LLM judge panel, and (4) cross-condition stance consistency. Bootstrap confidence intervals and weight-sensitivity analyses are provided at the item level.

Result: Epistemic flexibility is largely orthogonal to general model capability: a 27B open-weight model matched the strongest proprietary systems; one model family's flagship underperformed its lightweight counterpart; reasoning-optimized models did not consistently exhibit higher flexibility. Stance content density provided the strongest signal, while surface lexical markers (e.g., "I think") shifted substantially without corresponding changes in expressed stance.

Conclusion: ESFP measures a model's propensity to adapt epistemic stance under changing attribution conditions rather than general competence. The decoupling of lexical markers from actual stance content underscores the importance of multi-dimensional evaluation, and the benchmark offers a framework for assessing epistemic trustworthiness beyond existing metrics.

Abstract: A language model may be asked either what experts believe about a contested claim or what it believes about the claim itself. A trustworthy conversational agent should distinguish these two requests and respond in different epistemic registers: neutral attribution in the first case and stance expression in the second. Whether such a shift occurs-and whether it occurs coherently-is not directly assessed by existing benchmarks for accuracy, instruction following, or safety. We introduce ESFP, a behavioral benchmark that treats the contrast between externally attributed and self-attributed prompts as the fundamental unit of measurement. ESFP consists of 104 carefully controlled items spanning six epistemic categories and five phrasing templates, and evaluates model responses along four complementary dimensions: lexical self-attribution, representation-level responsiveness to role framing, sentence-level stance content density assessed by an LLM judge panel, and cross-condition stance consistency. Evaluating eight frontier models from five vendors, we find that epistemic flexibility is largely orthogonal to general model capability: a 27B open-weight model matches the strongest proprietary systems, the flagship model of one family underperforms its lightweight counterpart, and reasoning-optimized models do not consistently exhibit higher flexibility. Stance content density provides the strongest signal, while surface-level lexical markers such as 'I think' can change substantially without corresponding changes in expressed stance. We provide item-level bootstrap confidence intervals, weight-sensitivity analyses, and an explicit discussion of the interpretation limits of the composite score. ESFP measures a model's propensity to adapt its epistemic stance under changing attribution conditions, rather than a general competence measure.

</details>


### [40] [The One-Word Census: Answer-Choice Conformity Across 44 Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12796)
*Tapan Parikh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A minimal "One-Word Census" of 44 language models across 31 single-turn prompts reveals extreme convergence: models overwhelmingly pick the same answers (e.g., "serendipity" 41% of the time for "pick any word"), yet conformity varies systematically by model type, lineage, and generation — with persona-tuned models diverging most and flagship models converging most, though the latest Claude and GPT show a reversal that may signal strategic repositioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a lightweight, reproducible instrument for measuring answer-choice convergence across language models in open-ended categorical tasks, and to characterize the structure of that convergence — which models conform, which diverge, and how this patterns across lineages, generations, and tuning strategies.

Method: The One-Word Census: 31 single-turn prompts each naming a category with many valid one-word answers (e.g., "Name a tree."), administered four times per model to 44 models with no system prompt. Analysis uses exact-match on normalized tokens (no embeddings, no LLM judge), costing ~$1 per model. Convergence is quantified via answer-choice surprisal: the average negative log2 probability of a model's answers under the pooled (leave-one-out) distribution of all other models. Rankings are validated via leave-one-family-out correlations. Human category-production norms serve as a comparative baseline across 20 shared categories.

Result: Extreme convergence: in 7 of 31 categories, a single answer captures >80% of all responses. Conformity varies >4× across models and is structured: persona/community-tuned models diverge most; newest mainline flagships conform most (producing almost no unique answers). Within Claude, GPT, Qwen, and Grok lineages, conformity rises with each generation — but reverses for the latest Claude and GPT flagships. Model rankings are robust to roster composition (leave-one-family-out ρ = 0.985). Compared to humans on 18/20 shared categories, model responses are more concentrated.

Conclusion: The One-Word Census is a cheap, transparent, and robust instrument that exposes a deep structure in model convergence: conformity is not uniform but patterned by model lineage, generation, and tuning philosophy. The reversal in the latest top-tier models hints at deliberate differentiation strategies, and the stark contrast with human response diversity underscores how homogenized current LLM behavior has become.

Abstract: When a language model must pick one answer from a large space of equally valid options, which does it pick -- and how often is it the same answer every other model picks? Asked to "pick a word -- any word," 44 models chose "serendipity" 41% of the time. We characterize this convergence with a deliberately minimal instrument: 31 single-turn prompts, each naming a category with many valid one-word answers ("Name a tree."), asked four times per model with no system prompt. Analysis is exact-match on normalized tokens -- no embeddings, no judge -- at about a dollar per model. That models converge is well documented; our contribution is the instrument itself -- the One-Word Census -- and what it reveals about the structure of the convergence. We score each model by answer-choice surprisal: the average $-\log2$ probability of its answers under the pooled answers of all other models, leave-one-out. Convergence is extreme -- in 7 of 31 categories one answer takes over 80% of all answers -- yet conformity varies more than fourfold across models, and the variation is structured. Persona- and community-tuned models are the most divergent; the newest mainline flagships are the most conformist, producing almost no answer no other model gave. Within four lineages (Claude, GPT, Qwen, Grok) conformity rises with each generation -- but reverses for the latest flagship Claude and GPT models, a possible early signal of repositioning at the top tier. Rankings are robust to roster composition (leave-one-family-out rho = 0.985). Against human category-production norms, the field is more concentrated than people in 18 of 20 shared categories. All prompts, transcripts, and code are public.

</details>


### [41] [Knowledgeless Language Models: Suppressing Parametric Recall for Evidence-Grounded Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12831)
*Roi Cohen,Yvan Carré,Nick Lechtenbörger,Hendrik Droste,Lucas Kerschke,Russa Biswas,Gerard de Melo,Jan Buys*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Pretraining LLMs on entity-anonymized corpora (KLLMs) suppresses parametric recall and shifts models toward evidence-grounded reasoning, yielding 20–25% relative gains in retrieval-augmented settings with improved calibration and robustness to imperfect evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs encode extensive factual knowledge in their parameters, but this parametric knowledge can be outdated, incomplete, or conflict with provided context, leading to unreliable behavior. The authors investigate whether modifying the pretraining signal can systematically shift models from parametric recall toward evidence-based reasoning.

Method: The authors introduce Knowledge-Less Language Models (KLLMs), pretrained on corpora where named entities are anonymized to remove entity-linked factual supervision. They evaluate KLLMs at multiple scales against matched baselines on contextual QA, fact verification, hallucination detection, and retrieval-grounded settings with imperfect evidence.

Result: KLLMs consistently outperform baselines on contextual QA, fact verification, and hallucination detection. In retrieval-grounded settings with imperfect evidence, they achieve 20–25% relative gains, exhibit better calibration (improved ECE, Brier score, AUROC), and demonstrate more reliable abstention behavior.

Conclusion: Suppressing entity-linked supervision during pretraining induces an epistemic shift toward evidence-grounded reasoning. KLLMs provide a more evidence-sensitive base model that can complement retrieval-augmented and tool-based systems, suggesting pretraining-time control over knowledge acquisition as a viable strategy for improving reliability.

Abstract: Language models encode substantial factual knowledge in their parameters, which can lead to unreliable behavior when this knowledge is outdated, incomplete, or misaligned with the provided context. In this work, we study whether modifying the pretraining signal can systematically shift models away from parametric recall and toward evidence-grounded reasoning. We introduce Knowledge--''Less'' Language Models (KLLMs), a fundamentally different epistemic training paradigm for LLMs, which are pretrained on corpora in which named entities are anonymized, thereby removing a primary channel for entity-linked factual supervision. This intervention substantially reduces closed-book factual recall, while often improving performance on tasks where relevant information is provided as context. Across multiple model scales, KLLMs consistently outperform matched baselines on contextual question answering, fact verification, and hallucination detection benchmarks. Crucially, in retrieval-grounded settings with imperfect evidence, KLLMs show improved robustness and achieve up to 20--25\% relative gains over standard language models. They further exhibit better calibration, with improved ECE, Brier score, and AUROC, as well as more reliable abstention behavior. Our results demonstrate that suppressing entity-linked supervision during pretraining induces a shift in epistemic behavior: KLLMs rely less on parametric knowledge and more on external evidence, leading to improved reliability under realistic conditions. This suggests that pretraining-time control over knowledge acquisition can complement retrieval-augmented and tool-based systems by providing a more evidence-sensitive base model.

</details>


### [42] [Can LLMs Write Reliable Rubrics? A Meta-Evaluation for Experiment Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12835)
*Hanhua Hong,Yizhi Li,Jiaoyan Chen,Luu Gia Huy,Sophia Ananiadou,Jung-jae Kim,Chenghua Lin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic meta-evaluation of LLM-generated rubrics for evaluating paper reproduction by research agents. The authors reformulate rubrics into a checklist format, test four generation settings across two backbone models, and evaluate both intrinsically (semantic similarity) and extrinsically (score alignment with human gold standards). Augmented generation substantially improves downstream alignment — the best setting approaches the human baseline — though intrinsic gains are modest. LLM-generated rubrics tend to be overly fine-grained, high-score biased, and less domain-adaptive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rubric-based evaluation is promising for assessing open-ended LLM research agent outputs in paper reproduction tasks, but manually constructing paper-specific rubrics requires substantial expert effort, limiting benchmark scalability (e.g., PaperBench). Automating rubric generation with LLMs could solve this bottleneck, yet no systematic meta-evaluation exists to assess the quality and reliability of such generated rubrics.

Method: The authors reformulate rubrics into a checklist-style format and design four generation settings (varying levels of augmentation) across two backbone LLMs. They conduct a meta-evaluation along two axes: (1) intrinsic evaluation using semantic similarity between generated and ground-truth rubrics, and (2) extrinsic evaluation measuring score alignment — how well evaluations using generated rubrics correlate with those using human-crafted ground-truth rubrics. Further analyses examine granularity, scoring bias, and domain adaptability.

Result: Augmented generation settings substantially improve downstream evaluation alignment with human rubrics; the strongest augmented setting approaches the human baseline. However, intrinsic gains (semantic similarity) are more modest. Generated rubrics exhibit three key limitations: they are overly fine-grained, systematically biased toward high scores, and less adaptive to specific paper domains compared to human-authored rubrics.

Conclusion: LLM-generated rubrics, particularly under augmented settings, can serve as viable substitutes for human-crafted rubrics in paper reproduction evaluation, substantially reducing expert effort while maintaining reasonable alignment. However, persistent issues of over-granularity, positive score bias, and limited domain adaptation indicate that fully automated rubric generation still falls short of human quality, highlighting directions for future improvement.

Abstract: Rubric-based evaluation is a promising approach for assessing open-ended outputs from LLM-based research agents, particularly in paper reproduction, where direct paper-to-repository comparison is prone to hallucination. However, constructing paper-specific rubrics requires substantial expert effort, limiting the scalability of benchmarks such as PaperBench. In this work, we present, to our knowledge, the first systematic meta-evaluation of LLM-generated rubrics for paper reproduction. We reformulate rubrics into a checklist-style format and evaluate four generation settings across two backbone models. We meta-evaluate generated rubrics intrinsically by semantic similarity and extrinsically by score alignment with ground-truth rubrics. Our results show that the augmented settings substantially improves downstream evaluation alignment, with the strongest setting approaching the human baseline, while intrinsic gains are more modest. Further analyses reveal that LLM-generated rubrics are often overly fine-grained, biased toward high scores, and less adaptive to paper domains, highlighting both the affordances and limitations.

</details>


### [43] [Evaluating Large Language Models on Misconceptions in Multi-Turn Medical Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12884)
*Monica Munnangi,Saiph Savage*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ThReadMed-QA is a multi-turn medical dialogue dataset for evaluating whether LLMs can detect and correct patient misconceptions over conversation turns. Even frontier models show substantial degradation from ~85% correction accuracy on initial questions to ~50% within two follow-ups, primarily due to error propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical QA evaluation frameworks only assess single-turn interactions, ignoring how LLMs handle patient misconceptions that persist, emerge, or evolve across multi-turn conversations. Safe medical communication requires not just answering questions but also identifying and correcting false beliefs over time—a capability that remains unexamined.

Method: The authors introduce ThReadMed-QA, a dataset of 2,437 patient-physician conversation threads (8,204 QA pairs) sourced from AskDocs. They evaluate five LLMs using a rubric-based LLM-as-a-Judge framework that scores responses on misconception identification and correction. An oracle analysis replaces prior model outputs with ground-truth physician responses to isolate the effect of error propagation.

Result: Frontier models (GPT-5, Claude-Haiku) correct false presuppositions ~85% of the time on initial questions but drop to ~50% within two follow-up turns. Error propagation accounts for much of this degradation, but performance remains suboptimal even when models are given correct prior context.

Conclusion: LLMs demonstrate inconsistent and degrading misconception correction over multi-turn conversations, raising safety concerns for patient-facing applications. Evaluation frameworks must incorporate multi-turn dynamics to capture real-world clinical dialogue behavior.

Abstract: Patients seeking medical information often ask questions that embed incorrect assumptions or misconceptions. In such cases, safe medical communication requires not only answering the question, but identifying and correcting the underlying false belief. These interactions naturally unfold over multiple turns, a pattern now mirrored in interactions with LLMs. Yet current evaluation frameworks do not capture model behavior in these settings, where misconceptions can emerge, persist, or evolve over the course of a conversation. Whether LLMs can reliably correct such misconceptions over time remains largely unexamined. To study this, we introduce ThReadMed-QA, a multi-turn medical dialogue dataset of 2,437 patient-physician conversation threads comprising 8,204 question-answer pairs, derived from real patient interactions on AskDocs. This dataset enables systematic evaluation of whether models can detect and correct misconceptions under a multi-turn context. We evaluate five LLMs using a rubric-based LLM-as-a-Judge framework that scores responses based on their ability to identify and correct misconceptions. Our experiments reveal a consistent pattern: even frontier models that can address misconceptions in a single interaction degrade substantially over subsequent turns. GPT-5 and Claude-Haiku correct these false presuppositions around 85% on initial questions but drop to roughly 50% within two follow-ups. An oracle analysis replacing prior model outputs with physician responses shows that much of the degradation is driven by error propagation, while performance remains imperfect even under correct context. Even when models tend to correct misconceptions initially, their performance degrades substantially over later turns, leading to inconsistent and potentially unsafe guidance in patient-facing settings and highlighting the need for evaluation frameworks that capture multi-turn behavior.

</details>


### [44] [LLM Judges Can Be Too Generous When There Is No Reference Answer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12885)
*Chalamalasetti Kranti,Sowmya Vajjala*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM judges without reference answers tend to over-credit incorrect responses; adding a reference answer can flip decisions by up to 85%, and these reference-driven changes align with human judgment. The paper proposes a calibration methodology for reliable reference-free LLM evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges are increasingly used for open-ended response evaluation in no-reference settings, but their reliability in such setups is unclear. The authors aim to systematically investigate whether LLM judges can be trusted without ground-truth references.

Method: A two-stage pipeline: (a) calibration experiments assessing the judge model's task knowledge, and (b) sensitivity experiments measuring how the presence and prompt positioning of reference answers affect judge decisions. Experiments span three languages, with comparison to human annotations.

Result: LLM judges over-credit incorrect answers in reference-free settings. Introducing a reference answer flips correct/incorrect decisions by up to 85% in some settings, and these flips generally align with human judgments.

Conclusion: LLM judges require calibration with reference-aware evaluation before reliable reference-free deployment. The proposed methodology serves as a blueprint for calibrating LLM judges across other tasks.

Abstract: LLM judges are increasingly being used to evaluate open-ended model responses, often in no-reference settings where a ground-truth answer is unavailable. However, can they reliably assess in such evaluation setups? We explore this question in this paper through a two stage pipeline with a) calibration experiments that assess the judge model's knowledge of the task it is evaluating, and b) sensitivity experiments that assess how the judge model's performance is impacted by the presence and positioning of the reference answer in the prompt. Across experiments covering three languages, we show that the judge models we evaluated tend to over-credit incorrect answers in the absence of a reference answer, and adding reference answer information to the prompt flips the judge model's correct/incorrect decisions by as much as 85% in some experimental settings. Comparison with a subset of human annotations shows that these reference-driven changes generally align with human judgments. Our results emphasize the need for calibrating the LLM judges with a sample with reference-aware evaluation before using them in reference-free setups reliably, and our methodology provides a blueprint for researchers and practitioners in doing such calibration of LLM judges for other tasks.

</details>


### [45] [The Illusion of Robustness: Aggregate Accuracy Hides Prediction Flips under Task-Irrelevant Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12963)
*Yanzhe Zhang,Sanmi Koyejo,Diyi Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs show aggregate robustness to irrelevant context, but this masks per-example instability — even nonsense pseudo-words can flip individual predictions, revealing hidden tail risks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed with long, partially irrelevant context. While aggregate accuracy metrics suggest robustness, they may conceal critical per-example failures. The authors aim to investigate whether apparent contextual robustness holds up under per-instance scrutiny.
