Method: The authors prepend task-irrelevant context (including semantically meaningless pseudo-words formed by random character combinations) to benchmark questions and measure both aggregate accuracy changes and per-example prediction stability across multiple state-of-the-art LLMs, datasets, context types, context lengths, and inference-time compute budgets.

Result: Aggregate accuracy remains largely stable, but per-example predictions shift significantly. The effect is two-sided — some examples degrade while others improve — and the affected examples are model-specific. Instability is modulated by context type, context length, test-time compute, and model development stage.

Conclusion: Aggregate metrics hide context-induced tail risks that threaten reliability in high-stakes deployments. The findings motivate per-example reliability evaluation as a complement to standard aggregate benchmarking for language models.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) grow more capable, they are increasingly deployed in context-rich settings where task inputs are often accompanied by long, partially irrelevant context. In a controlled setting, we find that state-of-the-art models often appear robust to task-irrelevant context at the aggregate level: prepending it to benchmark questions causes little change in overall accuracy. This aggregate stability, however, masks significant per-example instability. Even semantically meaningless pseudo-words, formed by randomly combining characters, can markedly shift model predictions on a small fraction of examples, degrading performance on some while improving it on others. This two-sided effect holds consistently across a wide range of models and datasets, yet the affected examples are largely model-specific. We further show that this instability is modulated by context type, context length, test-time compute, and model development stage. Together, our findings reveal context-induced tail risks concealed by aggregate accuracy, motivating per-example reliability evaluation of language models.

</details>


### [46] [PalmClaw: A Native On-Device Agent Framework for Mobile Phones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13027)
*Hongru Cai,Yongqi Li,Ran Wei,Wenjie Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PalmClaw is an open-source mobile-native agent framework that exposes device capabilities as explicit tools with defined execution boundaries, achieving 11.5% relative improvement in task success and 94.9% reduction in completion time over GUI-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mobile LLM agents rely on GUI actions (tapping, swiping, typing) that produce long, interface-dependent action sequences, cannot directly access device capabilities, and suffer from poorly defined execution boundaries. Mobile devices are critical agent environments due to their ubiquity and access to user data, sensors, and apps, yet current solutions are desktop/server-based and lack native on-device operation.

Method: PalmClaw runs natively on mobile phones and implements on-device session management, memory, skills, tools, and an agent loop. The key design is abstracting device capabilities (sensors, apps, system functions) as structured "device tools" with explicit arguments, structured results, and clearly delineated execution boundaries, enabling direct and controlled invocation rather than indirect GUI manipulation.

Result: PalmClaw achieves an 11.5% relative improvement in task success rate and a 94.9% reduction in task completion time compared to the strongest baseline, with lower setup burden. Execution traces demonstrate how boundaries are applied in practice.

Conclusion: A mobile-native agent architecture with device capabilities exposed as explicit tools significantly outperforms GUI-based approaches in both effectiveness and efficiency, suggesting that structured tool abstraction is a superior paradigm for on-device mobile agents.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents have moved beyond generating responses to executing multi-step tasks by calling tools, observing the results, and iteratively deciding the next action. Most agent systems run on desktops or servers, which support tool use and task automation. Mobile devices are also important agent environments because they are widely accessible and contain users' data, sensors, and daily-use applications. Existing mobile agents mainly operate smartphones through graphical user interface (GUI) actions such as tapping, swiping, and typing, which often form long, interface-dependent sequences, cannot directly access device capabilities, and make execution boundaries difficult to define. We present \textbf{PalmClaw}, an open-source agent framework that runs natively on mobile phones and manages the sessions, memory, skills, tools, and agent loop directly on the device. PalmClaw exposes device capabilities as device tools with explicit arguments, structured results, and clearly defined execution boundaries. This design enables agents to use mobile capabilities directly while keeping each action explicit and controlled. Experiments show an 11.5\% relative improvement in task success and a 94.9\% reduction in completion time over the strongest baseline, with lower setup burden and traces illustrating how execution boundaries are applied. Code is available at https://github.com/ModalityDance/PalmClaw.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [47] [Explaining When PRF Fails: Participatory Auditing for Selective Query Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12098)
*Zeyan Liang,Graham McDonald,Iadh Ounis*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: PRF harms 25.6% of queries despite aggregate gains. The paper proposes a two-stage audit-then-automate framework: a participatory user audit reveals that avoiding harm is nearly twice as valuable as exploiting successful expansion, and LLM-based rerankers are repurposed to predict these user-derived labels automatically with inspectable evidence, making sPRF auditable and explainable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pseudo-Relevance Feedback (PRF) improves retrieval on average but causes query drift that degrades a substantial fraction of queries—an asymmetry masked by aggregate metrics. Existing Selective PRF (sPRF) methods rely on QPP derived from the same ranking statistics, thus inheriting the opacity rather than resolving it. The authors frame this as a core explainability problem in IR.

Method: A two-stage audit-then-automate framework. Stage 1: a participatory audit with 108 users across 43 TREC Deep Learning 2019 queries, measuring per-query benefit/harm and finding that avoiding harm is ~2× more valuable than exploiting expansions. Stage 2: repurposing LLM-based rerankers as system preference predictors that replicate user-derived labels automatically, grounded in inspectable document evidence, enabling scalable auditable decisions.

Result: Only 20.9% of queries benefit from PRF, while 25.6% suffer degraded user experience. Avoiding harm is nearly twice as valuable as exploiting successful expansion. The LLM-based predictors successfully replicate user-derived labels and provide inspectable document-level evidence for sPRF decisions.

Conclusion: The two-stage framework explains which queries PRF harms, why an sPRF decision is made, and how decisions can be inspected at scale, transforming an opaque retrieval component into an auditable, user-grounded one. This addresses both the effectiveness asymmetry and the explainability gap in PRF.

Abstract: Pseudo-Relevance Feedback (PRF) improves retrieval effectiveness on average, but harms a substantial fraction of queries through query drift, an asymmetry hidden by aggregate offline metrics. Existing Selective PRF (sPRF) approaches typically rely on Query Performance Prediction (QPP) methods derived from the same ranking statistics, and therefore inherit, rather than resolve, this opacity. We argue that this is a core explainability problem in IR, and propose a two-stage audit-then-automate framework. In Stage 1, a participatory audit with 108 users across 43 TREC Deep Learning 2019 queries shows that only 20.9% of queries benefit from PRF, while 25.6% suffer a degraded user experience, and that avoiding harm is nearly twice as valuable as exploiting successful expansion. In Stage 2, we repurpose LLM-based rerankers as system preference predictors that replicate these user-derived labels automatically, grounded in inspectable document evidence. Together, the two stages explain which queries PRF harms, why an sPRF decision is made, and how the decision can be inspected at scale, turning an opaque retrieval component into an auditable, user-grounded one.

</details>


### [48] [SHEAF: Self-profiled Hardness Estimation from Answer-set Flux for Predicting Query Hardness in Graph-based ANN Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12229)
*Dongfang Zhao*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: SHEAF is a novel per-query hardness estimator for ANN search that predicts the minimum beam-width needed by measuring how much a query's top-k answer set changes (flux) between two shallow probe widths, outperforming LID and other baselines without requiring ground truth at query time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In graph-based ANN search, a fixed beam-width for all queries is suboptimal because query hardness varies dramatically (e.g., >32× difference on SIFT1M). Existing proxies like LID are static and geometric, weakly correlating with the minimum sufficient beam. A cheap, accurate per-query hardness predictor would enable adaptive beam-width allocation, improving throughput without sacrificing recall.

Method: The authors propose SHEAF (Self-profiled Hardness Estimation from Answer-set Flux), which quantifies query hardness as the degree of change in the top-k answer set between two shallow probe widths. They design a self-profiling estimator that converts this flux into a per-query beam predictor. They also introduce a fixed-probe evaluation protocol that scores each hardness measure against the observed minimum sufficient beam across all queries.

Result: SHEAF predicts per-query beam better than five baseline measures (including LID) on CAGRA and HNSW indexes across four diverse datasets, achieving up to 1.55× improvement in held-out correlation on both GPU and CPU, using only two shallow probe searches and no query-time ground truth.

Conclusion: SHEAF provides an effective, low-cost, self-profiling approach for per-query beam prediction in ANN search, enabling adaptive resource allocation that can significantly improve overall system efficiency across different index types and hardware platforms.

Abstract: Graph-based approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) search is usually governed by a beam-width parameter that trades recall for throughput and is fixed for the whole workload. Yet, queries may not be equally hard: for example, on the widely used data set SIFT1M, the beam that a query needs to reach 95\% recall varies by more than $32\times$. Therefore, serving each query at its own width would help if the system could tell, cheaply and in advance, how hard it is. The prevailing proxy for this difficulty is called local intrinsic dimensionality (LID); however, LID is static and geometric, which makes it only weakly predict the minimum beam.
  This paper presents a new measure, namely Self-profiled Hardness Estimation from Answer-set Flux (SHEAF), which represents a query's hardness as how much its own top-$k$ answer set changes between two shallow probe widths. We design a self-profiling estimator that turns this flux into a deployable per-query beam predictor; furthermore, we develop a fixed-probe evaluation protocol that scores each measure over all queries with an observed minimum sufficient beam. On popular ANN indexes such as CAGRA and HNSW across four diverse data sets, SHEAF predicts the per-query beam better than five baseline measures on both GPU and CPU by up to $1.55\times$ in held-out correlation, using only two shallow probe searches and no query-time ground truth.

</details>


### [49] [Not Only NTP: Extending Training Signal Coverage for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12277)
*Changhao Li,Shuli Wang,Junwei Yin,Senjie Kou,Yinqiu Huang,Chi Wang,Yinhua Zhu,Haitao Wang,Xingxing Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: NTP suffers from temporal locality (no long-range supervision) and spatial locality (no cross-domain gradient pathways). The authors propose NONTP with two auxiliary objectives — TCL for temporal contrastive learning and TDL for trans-domain learning — both discarded at inference, achieving significant improvements on industrial and public benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Next-Token Prediction (NTP) has two inherent structural limitations: (1) temporal locality — it only optimizes single-step prediction, lacking supervised pressure for learning long-range behavioral structure; (2) spatial locality — in multi-domain sequences, target item embeddings receive gradients only from the immediately preceding hidden state, with no explicit gradient pathway from cross-domain context. These limitations hinder the model's ability to capture longer-range dependencies and cross-domain relationships.

Method: The authors propose NONTP, which extends NTP's signal coverage via two auxiliary objectives. TCL (Temporal Contrastive Learning) employs a BYOL-style exponential moving average (EMA) teacher network with InfoNCE loss to align hidden states against K-step future trajectory representations in the representation space. TDL (Trans-Domain Learning) mean-pools cross-domain hidden states and feeds them through the shared prediction head, creating a second gradient pathway without introducing additional parameters. Both auxiliary objectives are discarded at inference time, resulting in zero overhead.

Result: On a four-domain Meituan industrial dataset (full ranking), NONTP achieves HR@10 improvements of +34.3% over NTP and +18.3% over MBGR. On the public Amazon Movie-Book-CDs benchmark, HR@10 improves by +2.8% and NDCG@10 by +3.7%. Online A/B tests confirm CTR gains of +1.8% and GMV gains of +2.1% (both p < 0.01). Ablation studies confirm each component contributes independently, with gradient conflict identified as a direction for future work.

Conclusion: NONTP effectively addresses the temporal and spatial locality limitations of NTP through two complementary auxiliary objectives (TCL and TDL) that are training-only and incur zero inference overhead. The method demonstrates strong empirical gains across both industrial and public benchmarks, with online A/B testing validating real-world impact. Gradient conflict between the objectives remains an open area for future investigation.

Abstract: Next-Token Prediction (NTP) carries two structural training signal limitations. First, NTP optimizes for single-step prediction only, placing no supervised pressure on learning longer-range behavioral structure -- we term this \textbf{temporal locality}. Second, in multi-domain sequences, each target item embedding receives gradient updates exclusively from the immediately preceding hidden state, with no explicit gradient pathway from cross-domain context -- we term this \textbf{spatial locality}.
  We propose \textbf{NONTP}, extending NTP's signal coverage along both dimensions through two auxiliary objectives. \textbf{TCL (Temporal Contrastive Learning)} uses a BYOL-style EMA teacher with InfoNCE to align hidden states against a $K$-step future trajectory in representation space. \textbf{TDL (Trans-Domain Learning)} mean-pools cross-domain hidden states and predicts through the shared prediction head, opening a second gradient pathway with no additional parameters. Both are discarded at inference: zero overhead.
  On a four-domain Meituan industrial dataset (full ranking), NONTP achieves HR@10 +34.3\% over NTP and +18.3\% over MBGR. On the public Amazon Movie-Book-CDs benchmark, HR@10 +2.8\% and NDCG@10 +3.7\%. Online A/B tests confirm CTR +1.8\% and GMV +2.1\% (both $p < 0.01$). Ablation studies confirm each component contributes independently, with gradient conflict analyzed as a direction for future work.

</details>


### [50] [SlimPer: Make Personalization Model Slim and Smart](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12281)
*Siqi Wang,Xianjie Chen,Shaofeng Deng,Albert Chen,Romil Shah,Jiawei Huang,Zhaoqin Wang,Zhang Zhang,Yiqun Liu,Meilei Jiang,Anish Dubey,Moyan Mei,Tongxin Wang,Nathan Berrebbi,Misael Manjarres,Armand Sauzay,Shardul Kothapalli,Aryaman Vinchhi,Kevin Johnstone,Juheon Lee,Gufan Yin,Ziheng Huang,Justin Lin,Mert Terzihan,Yilin Qi,Cynthia Yang,Colin Peppler,Qi Ding,Ruohan Sun,Ge Song,Litao Deng,Parichay Kapoor,Matt Ma,Huihui Cheng,Jiyuan Zhang,Yanli Zhao,Yiping Han,Fangqiu Han,Ning Yao,Arun Singh,Jordan Edwards,Zhengyu Su,Abhishek Kumar,Guangdeng Liao,Ankit Asthana*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: SlimPer reformulates personalized ranking as iterative refinement of a compact knowledge base, decoupling model depth from user history length to achieve O(N) per-layer cost with fixed-size intermediate representations, deployed successfully on Instagram Reels and Feed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer architectures used in industrial recommendation systems are designed for generative autoregressive tasks, requiring large intermediate tensors that scale with sequence length. However, recommendation systems only produce a single set of relevance scores per <user, item> pair without token-level supervision, making this design wasteful. There is a need for an architecture that decouples model depth from user history length to enable deeper relevance modeling without proportional compute/memory growth.

Method: SlimPer introduces a compact, unified <user, item> knowledge base that is iteratively refined across layers. At each layer, the model: (1) selectively queries raw multi-modal user-side tokens, (2) computes explicit relevance matching scores, and (3) refines the knowledge base. This operates at O(N) per-layer cost with a fixed-size intermediate representation. A request-only optimization shares a single copy of user-side tokens across all candidate items to further reduce memory. The architecture unifies sparse, dense, and sequence features within a single backbone and provides interpretability via its attention mechanism.

Result: Deployed on Instagram Reels and Feed, SlimPer achieved measurable improvements in user engagement while streamlining the overall system and enabling effective modeling of 10k+ fine-grained user history events.

Conclusion: SlimPer demonstrates that by aligning architecture design with the fundamental nature of recommendation tasks (single relevance prediction rather than token-level generation), significant efficiency gains and engagement improvements are achievable. The decoupling of model depth from sequence length enables deeper models to handle very long user histories (10k+ events) without proportional resource scaling.

Abstract: Transformer-style architectures are increasingly adopted for industrial recommendation systems, yet they inherit a design premise misaligned with the task: generative models rely on per-token autoregressive prediction, which justifies maintaining large intermediate tensors that scale with sequence length. In contrast, recommendation systems produce a single set of relevance scores for each <user, item> pair without token-level supervision. Leveraging this observation, we propose SlimPer, which reformulates personalized ranking as iterative refinement of a compact, unified <user, item> knowledge base. At each layer, the model selectively queries raw multi-modal user-side tokens, computes explicit relevance matching scores, and refines the knowledge base, all in O(N) per-layer cost with a fixed-size intermediate representation. As a result, model depth is decoupled from user history length, enabling deeper relevance understanding without proportional growth in compute or memory; request-only optimization further trims memory by sharing a single copy of user-side tokens across all candidate items. SlimPer unifies sparse, dense, and sequence features within a single backbone and provides inherent interpretability through its attention mechanism. Deployed on Instagram Reels and Feed, SlimPer yields measurable improvements in user engagement while streamlining the overall system and enabling effective modeling of 10k+ fine-grained user history events.

</details>


### [51] [MESH: Scaling Up Retrieval with Heterogeneous Content Unification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12392)
*Jiaxing Qu,Yilin Chen,Junpeng Hou,Jinfeng Rao,Olafur Gudmundsson,Sai Xiao,Huizhong Duan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: MESH is a unified retrieval framework that addresses the "Scaling Bias of Heterogeneity" — where model scaling benefits large, popular content but neglects fresh/long-tail items — via a modularized architecture with gated bias correction. It achieves a 14× improvement in power-law scaling exponent for fresh items, yielding +5.5% fresh-item repins, +55% funnel efficiency, and +0.46% user retention on Pinterest's billion-scale Related Pins platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern large-scale retrieval systems rely on a fragmented collection of specialized models to serve diverse content tiers (e.g., fresh, long-tail, popular). This operational complexity stems from the Scaling Bias of Heterogeneity: as model capacity increases, performance gains disproportionately favor high-frequency, popular content while sparse and fresh items see diminishing returns. The authors seek a unified framework that scales equitably across all content tiers, eliminating the need for a "zoo" of retrieval models.

Method: MESH employs a modularized architecture that partitions the feature space into independent domains, enforcing a structural inductive bias. This separation reduces interference between sparse-item signals and high-frequency engagement features. A gated bias correction mechanism is integrated to further mitigate distributional skew. The architecture creates a protected gradient path for sparse content, enabling more equitable scaling behavior. An asynchronous serving strategy is also introduced to maintain production throughput.

Result: Empirically, MESH delivers a 14× improvement in the power-law scaling exponent for fresh items. In online A/B tests on Pinterest's Related Pins (billion-scale item-to-item recommendation): +5.5% lift in fresh-item repins, +55% improvement in funnel efficiency, +0.46% user retention gain, and 2.87× improvement in system throughput via asynchronous serving.

Conclusion: MESH demonstrates that a unified retrieval architecture can overcome the Scaling Bias of Heterogeneity, enabling consolidated, ecosystem-aware retrieval backbones that scale effectively across all content tiers — from popular to fresh and long-tail — without sacrificing production efficiency.

Abstract: Optimizing large-scale retrieval hinges on the ability to efficiently surface candidates across diverse content tiers. However, to capture segments such as fresh and long-tail content, modern systems typically resort to a fragmented "zoo" of specialized retrieval models. This operational complexity is attributed to a fundamental challenge in heterogeneous retrieval systems, the Scaling Bias of Heterogeneity, where model capacity gains do not apply equally across diverse content tiers. To bridge this gap, we propose MESH as a unified retrieval scaling framework that mitigates this bias through a modularized architecture integrated with gated bias correction. By partitioning the feature space into independent domains, MESH enforces a structural inductive bias that reduces interference between sparse-item signals and high-frequency engagement features. This protected gradient path leads to improved scaling behavior for sparse content, empirically validated by a 14 times improvement in the power-law scaling exponent for fresh items. In online evaluations on Pinterest's Related Pins platform, a billion scale item-to-item recommendation system, these improvements translate into a +5.5% lift in fresh-item repins, alongside with 55% improvement in funnel efficiency and +0.46% improvement in user retention. Finally, our asynchronous serving strategy ensures production viability by delivering a 2.87 times improvement in system throughput. Our findings suggest MESH as a promising paradigm for consolidating fragmented retrieval infrastructures into more scalable and ecosystem-aware backbones.

</details>


### [52] [Where Reasoning Matters: Rethinking Latent Reasoning in Semantic ID-based Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12425)
*Shangxin Yang,Min Gao,Zongwei Wang,Junliang Yu*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper investigates how to optimally allocate latent reasoning (refinement) steps across semantic ID token positions in generative recommendation. Finding that earlier positions carry more information gain, the authors propose IBA, a framework that learns to distribute a fixed computational budget unevenly—more steps to high-IG positions, fewer to low-IG ones—yielding better accuracy-efficiency trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent reasoning improves semantic ID-based generative recommendation by adding hidden-state computation before each token prediction. However, it is unclear whether every token position should receive the same number of refinement steps. The authors hypothesize that positions differ in how much uncertainty they resolve about the target item, and that allocating refinement steps according to this differential informativeness could yield better performance under a fixed computational budget.

Method: The authors introduce position-wise information gain (IG) to quantify how much each semantic ID position reduces uncertainty about the target item. Observing that earlier positions exhibit higher IG, they propose IBA (Information-Gain Budget Allocation), which treats latent refinement steps as a constrained resource and learns to allocate them across positions—assigning more steps to high-IG positions and fewer to low-IG ones—via a learned allocation strategy.

Result: Experiments on multiple public datasets demonstrate that IBA consistently outperforms strong generative recommendation baselines, achieving a superior accuracy–computation trade-off compared to both uniform (fixed) allocation and poorly matched allocation strategies.

Conclusion: Position-wise information gain varies significantly across semantic ID tokens, with earlier positions contributing more to item disambiguation. Leveraging this insight, IBA's learned non-uniform allocation of latent reasoning steps provides both accuracy gains and computational efficiency, suggesting that budget-aware, position-sensitive refinement is a promising direction for generative recommendation.

Abstract: Semantic ID-based generative recommendation predicts an item by generating a short sequence of semantic ID tokens, where each token is produced autoregressively. Latent reasoning has recently been introduced to improve this process through additional hidden-state computation before each token decision. This raises a practical question: when one item is represented by a sequence of semantic ID tokens, should each token receive the same fixed number of latent refinement steps, or should these steps be allocated more effectively across positions? We study this question through position-wise information-gain (IG), which measures how much each semantic ID position reduces the uncertainty of the target item. We observe that earlier semantic ID positions usually provide higher information-gain, while later positions contribute less additional information. We further analyze that applying more refinement to high-IG positions tends to bring larger expected benefits. Based on this observation, we propose IBA, an Information-Gain Budget Allocation framework for semantic ID-based generative recommendation. IBA treats latent refinement steps as a limited computational resource and learns how to allocate them across semantic ID positions, assigning more refinement to informative positions and less to positions with smaller contribution. Experiments on multiple public datasets show that IBA consistently improves strong generative recommendation baselines and achieves a better accuracy--computation trade-off than fixed or poorly matched step allocations.

</details>


### [53] [Cheaper is Better: A Discount-Aware Network for Conversion Rate Prediction in E-commerce Recommendation System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12578)
*Ruocong Tang,Yang Huang,Xing Fang,Chenyi Yan,Chuike Sun,Jing Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: DANet introduces discount-rate awareness into CVR prediction via Fourier-based time-frequency analysis, distribution debiasing, and a supervised regression auxiliary task, achieving significant offline and online gains on Alibaba's Tmall platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CVR prediction models focus heavily on data sparsity, sample selection bias, and delayed feedback, but largely overlook the impact of item discount rate — a key factor shaping both pricing strategy and user purchase behavior. This gap motivates explicitly modeling the relationship between discount rates and conversion.

Method: DANet comprises three components: (1) a time-frequency transformation module using Fourier transform to extract frequency spectra and capture long-term discount rate trends; (2) a distribution de-bias module that corrects user-specific discount rate biases from varied purchase combinations, promotions, and periodic promotion-cycle deviations on e-commerce platforms; (3) a supervised regression auxiliary task with explicit discount labels to improve value-accuracy and learn effective discount-rate representations.

Result: On real-world datasets, DANet achieves +1.61% offline AUC improvement. In online A/B testing, it yields +3.63% on pCVR and +2.23% on GMV. The model is deployed on Alibaba Tmall APP.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling item discount rates via DANet substantially improves CVR prediction, addressing a neglected but critical dimension in e-commerce recommendation. The strong offline and online results, plus successful industrial deployment, validate the practical value of discount-aware modeling.

Abstract: Post-click conversion rate (CVR) is a crucial element in online recommendation systems, which addresses significant challenges such as data sparsity (DS), sample selection bias (SSB), and delayed feedback. However, the impact of item discount rate-a key factor influencing both pricing and user purchasing behavior, has received limited attention. In this paper, we introduce the Discount-Aware Network (DANet) to model the relationship between item discount rates and CVR. DANet comprises three main components: 1) a time-frequency transformation module that utilizes Fourier transform to derive the frequency spectrum and capture the long-term discount rate trends of items; 2) a distribution de-bias module designed to mitigate the biases in user-specific discount rates caused by various purchase combinations and promotional activities, as well as periodic deviations linked to different promotion periods on e-commerce platforms; and 3) a supervised regression auxiliary task that establishes the explicit item discount labels to enhance the model's performance in terms of value accuracy, facilitating an effective representation of item discount rates. Experimental results on real datasets demonstrate the superiority of DANet, with offline AUC improving by 1.61%, and online A/B test also shows that DANet achieves impressive gains of 3.63% on pCVR and 2.23% on GMV. DANet has been successfully deployed on Alibaba Tmall APP. The code is available at https://github.com/tangrc/DANet.

</details>


### [54] [Learning to Forget: Satiation-Aware Long-Sequence Transducers for Mitigating Post-Purchase Redundancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12714)
*Yipin Dai,Ruocong Tang,Xing Fang,Yang Huang,Jing Wang,Zhentao Song,He Guo*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: SAM proposes a framework that models the lifecycle of user interests in sequential recommendation, distinguishing between interest continuation and interest exit after purchase. It uses dual-path cross-attention, adaptive satiation gating, and a self-supervised TTNP task to suppress post-purchase redundancy, achieving a 60%+ reduction in Post-Purchase Repeat Rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sequential recommenders treat all user interactions as positive signals, failing to recognize that a purchase often terminates a specific intent (Interest Exit) rather than indicating continued preference. This Action-Intent Asymmetry causes severe post-purchase redundancy, where models keep recommending already-satisfied items.

Method: SAM is an end-to-end framework with three components: (1) Dual-path Cross-Attention — one path retroactively suppresses historical clicks tied to fulfilled intents, the other retrieves personalized replenishment rhythms from long-term purchase history; (2) Adaptive Satiation Gating Unit (ASGU) — produces a time-sensitive soft mask that inhibits satisfied interests post-purchase and gradually re-awakens them as the predicted repurchase cycle nears; (3) Self-supervised Time-to-Next-Purchase (TTNP) auxiliary task — learns latent product lifecycles without manual labels.

Result: Extensive offline experiments on industrial datasets and online A/B testing show that SAM reduces the Post-Purchase Repeat Rate (PPRR) by over 60%.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling the satiation and re-awakening lifecycle of user interests effectively addresses Action-Intent Asymmetry, substantially mitigating post-purchase redundancy in sequential recommendation. The SAM framework achieves strong practical impact as validated by both offline and online experiments.

Abstract: Sequential recommendation models predominantly interpret user interactions as positive signals for preference accumulation. However, in e-commerce scenarios, a purchase action often signifies the termination of a specific intent ("Interest Exit") rather than its continuation. Existing models overlook this distinction, suffering from Action-Intent Asymmetry, which leads to severe post-purchase redundancy. In this paper, we propose the Satiation-Aware Mechanism (SAM), an end-to-end framework designed to explicitly model the lifecycle of user interests. SAM incorporates three key components: (1) A Dual-path Cross-Attention architecture that retroactively suppresses historical clicks associated with a fulfilled intent while simultaneously retrieving personalized replenishment rhythms from long-term purchase history; (2) An Adaptive Satiation Gating Unit (ASGU) that generates a time-sensitive soft mask to inhibit satisfied interests immediately after purchase and gradually "re-awaken" them as the predicted repurchase cycle approaches; and (3) A self-supervised Time-to-Next-Purchase (TTNP) auxiliary task to learn latent product lifecycles without manual annotation. Extensive offline experiments on industrial datasets and online A/B testing demonstrate that SAM significantly reduces the Post-Purchase Repeat Rate (PPRR) by over 60%.

</details>


### [55] [RecRec: Latent Interests Recursive Reasoning for Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12945)
*Wenhao Deng,Junchen Fu,Hanwen Du,Alexandros Karatzoglou,Ioannis Arapakis,Hangjun Guo,Kaiwen Zheng,Yongxin Ni,Joemon M. Jose*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: RecRec decouples reasoning from prediction in sequential recommendation using a Context Compressor and Recursive Reasoner, trained with simple supervised learning. By operating in a separate latent space with multi-vector interest representations, it overcomes the single d-dimensional state bottleneck of prior RL-based approaches, enabling flexible inference-time reasoning depth and achieving SOTA results on four datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sequential recommenders use a single forward pass, and recent latent reasoning methods that iterate before prediction are bottlenecked by a fixed d-dimensional state that couples reasoning and prediction, limiting depth and requiring complex RL pipelines. The core question is how to properly structure the reasoning process for recommendation.

Method: RecRec has two components trained in two supervised stages: (1) A Context Compressor distills backbone hidden states into a small set of latent interests, regularized by an Interest Diversity Regularizer to ensure distinct aspects are captured. (2) A Recursive Reasoner refines these interests in a separate intermediate latent space via recursive computation. Deep supervision allows inference-time reasoning depth to be freely adjusted without retraining. No RL is used.

Result: RecRec outperforms state-of-the-art reasoning-enhanced methods on four real-world datasets. On three of four datasets, performance gains extend beyond the training-time reasoning depth, demonstrating flexible depth scaling at inference.

Conclusion: A decoupled, multi-vector reasoning architecture frees latent reasoning from the single-state bottleneck, and reasoning-state structure is a promising design axis for future exploration in sequential recommendation.

Abstract: Sequential recommender systems rely on a single forward pass to encode user interaction histories and predict the next item. Increasing inference-time computation through latent reasoning, with the model proceeding step by step before the final prediction, has been recently explored in sequential recommendation with promising results. However, how to structure the reasoning process for sequential recommendation remains an open question. Existing approaches couple reasoning and prediction in a single $d$-dimensional state, limiting reasoning depth and often relying on multi-stage pipelines with reinforcement learning (RL). We propose RecRec (Recursive Reasoning for Recommendation), an RL-free framework that decouples reasoning from prediction, overcoming the fixed $d$-dimensional state bottleneck of prior methods. RecRec consists of a Context Compressor and a Recursive Reasoner, trained in two simple supervised stages. The Context Compressor distills the backbone's hidden states into a small set of latent interests, with an Interest Diversity Regularizer encouraging each interest to capture a distinct aspect of user behavior. The Recursive Reasoner then refines these interests by reasoning in a separate intermediate latent space. Deep supervision lets the reasoning depth be freely adjusted at inference without retraining. On four real-world datasets, RecRec outperforms state-of-the-art reasoning-enhanced methods, and on three of four datasets, gains extend past the training-time depth. Our findings point to a decoupled, multi-vector recipe that unleashes latent reasoning from the single-state bottleneck of prior methods, suggesting reasoning-state structure as a design axis to explore further in sequential recommendation.

</details>


### [56] [ViHoRec: A Quality-Controlled Vietnamese Hotel Recommendation Dataset and Cold-Start Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12946)
*Minh Hoang Nguyen*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ViHoRec is a publicly released, quality-controlled Vietnamese hotel recommendation dataset with 18,267 interactions, featuring a reproducible construction pipeline, privacy-preserving pseudonyms, and a cold-start benchmark that reveals sharp degradation of learned models on short-history users.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vietnamese recommender-system research lacks a public, well-documented hotel interaction dataset. Building one is challenging due to cross-platform hotel name reconciliation, the need for reproducible quality auditing, and the tension between privacy preservation and realistic cold-start benchmarking.

Method: The authors crawled interactions from Booking.com, Traveloka, and Ivivu (18,267 interactions, 6,832 users, 560 hotels). They designed a reproducible construction pipeline incorporating cross-platform entity resolution and quantitative quality control. Privacy is preserved via HMAC-based pseudonyms. A temporal leave-last-one-out split is used for cold-start evaluation, with data-centric ablations and dependency-free baselines (e.g., BPR-MF, UserKNN).

Result: On the public split, BPR-MF Recall@10 drops from 0.120 to 0.065 for users with short histories, confirming severe cold-start degradation. UserKNN remains the strongest method overall, establishing ViHoRec as a sparse, cold-start-dominated testbed for low-resource recommendation.

Conclusion: ViHoRec fills a critical gap in Vietnamese recommendation research by providing a rigorous, reproducible, and privacy-compliant dataset and benchmark. The cold-start-centric design highlights the limitations of current learned models and encourages future work on low-resource, sparse recommendation scenarios.

Abstract: Recommender-system research for Vietnamese remains limited by the absence of a public, well-documented hotel interaction resource. Building such a resource is challenging for three reasons: cross-platform hotel names must be reconciled before interactions are comparable; quality must be audited with reproducible metrics rather than ad hoc cleaning; and public release must preserve privacy while remaining benchmarkable under realistic cold-start conditions. We introduce ViHoRec, a quality-controlled Vietnamese hotel recommendation dataset of 18{,}267 interactions between 6{,}832 users and 560 hotels, crawled from Booking.com, Traveloka, and Ivivu. Our contributions are: (i) a reproducible construction pipeline with cross-platform entity resolution and quantitative quality control; (ii) a privacy-preserving release with HMAC pseudonyms; and (iii) a public cold-start benchmark with temporal leave-last-one-out split, data-centric ablations, and dependency-free baselines. On the public split, learned models degrade sharply for users with short histories (BPR-MF Recall@10: 0.065 vs. 0.120), while UserKNN remains strongest overall, establishing ViHoRec as a sparse, cold-start-dominated testbed for low-resource recommendation. All data are publicly available at https://github.com/MinhNguyenDS/ViHoRec.

</details>


### [57] [FAIR GraphRAG: A Retrieval-Augmented Generation Approach for Semantic Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11464)
*Marlena Flüh,Soo-Yon Kim,Carolin Victoria Schneider,Sandra Geisler*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: FAIR GraphRAG integrates FAIR Digital Objects (FDOs) as nodes in a graph-based RAG system, co-designed with physicians for biomedical applications, and demonstrates improved QA accuracy, coverage, and explainability on RNA-sequencing data in gastroenterology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-based RAG approaches (e.g., GraphRAG) lack structured FAIRification of underlying knowledge resources, limiting their effectiveness for domain-specific information retrieval — particularly in complex fields like medicine where FAIR principles (Findability, Accessibility, Interoperability, Reusability) are critical.

Method: The authors propose FAIR GraphRAG, where each graph node is a FAIR Digital Object (FDO) containing core data, metadata, persistent identifiers, and semantic links. LLMs are leveraged for schema construction and automated extraction of content and metadata. The framework was co-designed by physicians and computer scientists, and evaluated on a biomedical dataset of RNA-sequencing data in gastroenterology.

Result: FAIR GraphRAG significantly improves question answering accuracy, coverage, and explainability, particularly for complex queries involving metadata and ontology links, while ensuring adherence to FAIR principles.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates the feasibility of combining FAIR data practices with graph-based retrieval and suggests broader applicability to other specialized domains such as education and business.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) addresses the limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) when providing responses to domain-specific questions. Graph-based RAG approaches, such as GraphRAG, enhance retrieval by capturing semantic relationships within knowledge graphs (KGs). While the FAIR principles (Findability, Accessibility, Interoperability, and Reusability) are becoming prevalent for scientific data management, especially in complex domains such as medicine, existing RAG approaches lack a structured FAIRification of the underlying knowledge resources. This lack limits their potential for FAIR information retrieval in these domains. To address this gap, we introduce FAIR GraphRAG, a novel framework that integrates FAIR Digital Objects (FDOs) as the fundamental units of a graph-based retrieval system. Each graph node represents an FDO that incorporates core data, metadata, persistent identifiers, and semantic links. We leverage LLMs to support schema construction and automated extraction of content and metadata from data sources. The framework was co-designed by physicians and computer scientists to ensure technical and clinical relevance. We apply FAIR GraphRAG to a biomedical dataset in gastroenterology, demonstrating its applicability to RNA-sequencing data. Beyond ensuring adherence to the FAIR principles, FAIR GraphRAG significantly improves question answering accuracy, coverage, and explainability, particularly for complex queries involving metadata and ontology links. This work shows the feasibility of combining FAIR data practices with graph-based retrieval techniques. We see potential for applying our approach to other specialized fields such as education and business.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [58] [OmniPMNet: Bridging discrete and gridded PM10 forecasts via omni-query neural processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11896)
*Shuangshuang He,Shuo Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OmniPM-Net is a ConvCNP-based fusion model that combines GNN station forecasts with CAMS gridded forecasts to produce unified PM10 predictions at both stations and grid cells. It matches station-level GNN accuracy, reduces CAMS error by 30%, and excels during high-concentration dust events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PM10 forecasting requires both station-scale accuracy and continuous spatial fields, especially during severe dust storms. Existing approaches are fragmented: CTMs (like CAMS) produce gridded outputs but suffer local biases, while GNNs achieve strong station-level accuracy at short lead times but cannot generate gridded forecasts. No single method currently satisfies both requirements simultaneously.

Method: OmniPM-Net uses a ConvCNP backbone to fuse two heterogeneous forecast sources. A terrain-aware Gaussian set convolution lifts irregular GNN station forecasts onto a regular grid. A multi-scale Spatial Source Attention (SSA) module then blends these with CAMS gridded forecasts. A shared omni-query readout decodes the unified representation into consistent PM10 predictions at either monitoring stations or arbitrary grid cells over a 108-hour horizon.

Result: Evaluated on 1,618 air-quality monitoring stations across China for the full year 2024: (1) station-level MAE of 21.14 µg/m³, matching the GNN baseline (22.00) and reducing CAMS MAE by 30%; (2) 90th-percentile MAE reduced by 9% vs. GNN and 25% vs. CAMS; (3) improved categorical detection skill during dust episodes with better spatial trajectory tracking.

Conclusion: OmniPM-Net successfully bridges the gap between discrete station-level GNN forecasts and continuous gridded CTM forecasts, achieving strong accuracy across both modalities. Its largest gains appear in the high-concentration tail and during dust storm events, making it a practical solution for operational PM10 forecasting that requires both local precision and spatial coverage.

Abstract: Forecasting particulate matter (PM10) requires both station-scale accuracy and continuous spatial fields, especially during severe dust storms. Chemical transport models (CTMs) provide gridded forecasts but retain local biases, whereas graph neural networks (GNNs) track monitoring sites well at short lead times but do not produce gridded outputs. Here we present OmniPM-Net, a Convolutional Conditional Neural Process (ConvCNP)-based fusion model that reconciles these two forecast types within a shared spatial representation. A terrain-aware Gaussian set convolution lifts irregular GNN station forecasts onto a regular grid, where a multi-scale Spatial Source Attention (SSA) module blends them with Copernicus Atmosphere Monitoring Service (CAMS) forecasts; a shared omni-query readout then decodes this representation into consistent PM10 predictions at either stations or grid cells over a 108 h horizon. Evaluated across 1,618 air-quality monitoring stations throughout China over the full year of 2024, OmniPM-Net matches the station-level accuracy of the stronger GNN baseline (mean absolute error 21.14 versus 22.00 ug/m3) and reduces the CAMS mean absolute error by 30%, while simultaneously delivering the gridded fields that the discrete GNN cannot. Its clearest gains are in the high-concentration tail, where the 90th-percentile MAE falls by 9% relative to the GNN and 25% relative to CAMS, and during dust episodes, where it improves categorical detection skill while tracking the evolving spatial trajectory.

</details>


### [59] [Semidirect Fourier Delta Attention: Phase-Controlled Delta Memory with Constructive Chunk-WY Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11897)
*Tiantian Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SFDA introduces block-rotational Fourier control (phase-controlled decay) into linear attention, replacing Kimi Delta Attention's real diagonal decay. The paper proves a constructive chunk-WY factorization for the resulting state transition products, yielding exact affine chunk transfers with bounded rank growth. Numerical verification shows SFDA learns cyclic memory on state-tracking tasks where KDA fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear attention compresses the KV cache into a fixed recurrent state, but this compression limits exact state tracking and long-context memory, particularly for tasks requiring cyclic or periodic memory patterns. Existing approaches like Kimi Delta Attention use real diagonal decay, which lacks the phase structure needed for such memory.

Method: The authors propose Semidirect Fourier Delta Attention (SFDA), which generalizes Kimi Delta Attention by replacing real diagonal decay with a block-rotational Fourier-controlled state update: S_t = (I - β_t k_t k_t^*) Λ_t S_{t-1} + β_t k_t v_t^*, where Λ_t = diag(α_t ⊙ e^{iθ_t}) incorporates complex phase rotations. The main theoretical contribution is a constructive chunk-WY factorization proving that products A_t = Λ_t - u_t r_t^* can be expressed as A_t⋯A_1 = Γ_t - Y_t M_t W_t^*, with rank growth bounded within fixed chunks. This yields exact affine chunk transfer, formal stability and complexity bounds, and a compact characterization of phase-plus-low-rank memory.

Result: The algebra is verified numerically. In toy state-tracking experiments, SFDA successfully learns cyclic memory patterns, whereas the phase-disabled KDA baseline remains near chance performance.

Conclusion: Phase-controlled rotational dynamics enable linear attention models to learn cyclic memory, overcoming a key limitation of real diagonal decay. Fused kernels and large-scale language model comparisons are deferred to future work.

Abstract: Linear attention replaces softmax attention's growing KV cache with a fixed recurrent state, but this compression limits exact state tracking and long-context memory. We introduce \emph{Semidirect Fourier Delta Attention} (SFDA), a phase-controlled generalization of Kimi Delta Attention that replaces real diagonal decay with block-rotational Fourier control: \[ S_t=(I-β_t k_tk_t^*)Λ_tS_{t-1}+β_tk_tv_t^*, \qquad Λ_t=\diag(α_t\odot e^{iθ_t}). \] Our main result is a constructive chunk-WY factorization for products \(A_t=Λ_t-u_tr_t^*\), giving \[ A_t\cdots A_1=Γ_t-Y_tM_tW_t^* \] with rank growth bounded inside fixed chunks. This yields an exact affine chunk transfer, formal stability and complexity bounds, and a compact characterization of phase-plus-low-rank memory. We verify the algebra numerically and show in toy state-tracking experiments that SFDA learns cyclic memory where the phase-disabled KDA baseline remains near chance. Fused kernels and large-scale language-model comparisons are left to future work.

</details>


### [60] [Repairing Shape-Prior Shortcuts in Long-Range Single-Shot Fringe Projection Profilometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11928)
*Adam Haroon,Cody Fleming,Beiwen Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PhiCalNet eliminates the shape-prior shortcut in single-shot fringe projection profilometry by architecturally enforcing a wrapped-phase representation and fixed differentiable calibration layer, reducing object MAE 3.3× (from 14.54 mm to 4.46 mm) and enabling the first pixel-wise conformal uncertainty quantification for FPP, which localizes error at phase wrap discontinuities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing single-shot FPP networks that regress depth directly learn a shape-prior shortcut—recovering depth from object boundaries rather than fringe phase. This shortcut persists regardless of additional data or model capacity because neither changes the hypothesis space. The authors aim to eliminate this shortcut architecturally rather than through loss penalties.

Method: PhiCalNet replaces direct depth regression with a two-stage architecture: (1) the network outputs a wrapped-phase representation (sin φ, cos φ), and (2) a fixed, differentiable calibration layer maps phase to depth. Since single-shot phase mapping is non-injective without fringe order, fringe order is provided as auxiliary input (with sensitivity analysis confirming tolerance to realistic decoding errors). A physics-informed neural network (PINN) baseline using the same physics as a soft penalty shows no gain, isolating the architectural choice. A three-frame extension is also tested. Uncertainty is quantified via pixel-wise conformal prediction, using snapshot disagreement to reject unreliable pixels.

Result: On a photorealistic synthetic benchmark (15,600 fringe images, 50 objects at 1.5–2.1 m), the UNet baseline plateaus at 14.54 mm MAE. PhiCalNet achieves 4.46 mm MAE (3.3× reduction), with residual error confined to 0.103% of pixels at the ±π wrap discontinuity. The three-frame extension reaches 1.16 mm MAE. Interpretability analysis shows phase is the most decodable internal feature. Conformal uncertainty quantification localizes error at the same discontinuity; rejecting the top 5% of pixels by snapshot disagreement reduces RMSE by 64% (vs. 3.5% for baseline).

Conclusion: Architecturally constraining the network to output wrapped phase rather than direct depth eliminates the shape-prior shortcut, as a PINN with equivalent physics as a soft penalty fails to achieve comparable gains. The phase representation simultaneously improves accuracy, interpretability, and enables effective uncertainty quantification, with errors concentrated at predictable phase wrap discontinuities where selective pixel rejection dramatically improves RMSE.

Abstract: Single-shot fringe projection profilometry (FPP) networks that regress depth directly can exploit a shape-prior shortcut, recovering depth from object boundaries rather than from fringe phase. On a photorealistic synthetic benchmark (15,600 fringe images, 50 objects at 1.5-2.1 m standoff), the best such UNet baseline plateaus at 14.54 mm object mean absolute error (MAE), and neither more data nor more capacity removes the shortcut, because neither changes the hypothesis space the optimizer searches. We introduce PhiCalNet, which outputs a wrapped-phase representation $(\sinφ, \cosφ)$ and maps it to depth through a fixed differentiable calibration layer, removing the shape-prior solution architecturally rather than by a loss penalty. Because the single-shot mapping is non-injective without fringe order, PhiCalNet takes the fringe order as auxiliary input, an assumption a sensitivity analysis shows tolerates realistic decoding error; a physics-informed (PINN) baseline with the same physics as a soft penalty yields no gain, isolating the architectural choice as the operative factor. PhiCalNet reduces object MAE 3.3x to 4.46 mm, its residual confined to 0.103% of pixels at the $\pmπ$ wrap discontinuity, and a three-frame extension reaches 1.16 mm. Two checks agree: interpretability makes phase the most decodable internal feature, and pixel-wise conformal uncertainty quantification, to our knowledge the first for FPP, localizes error at the same discontinuity, where rejecting the top 5% of pixels by snapshot disagreement cuts root-mean-square error by 64% versus 3.5% for the baseline.

</details>


### [61] [Qubit-Efficient Quantum Search for Hyperdimensional Decomposition via Logarithmic Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11936)
*Sanggeon Yun,Hyunwoo Oh,Ryozo Masukawa,Raheeb Hassan,Mohsen Imani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a qubit-efficient quantum framework for hypervector decomposition in HDC that encodes hypervectors using O(log D) qubits instead of O(D), while preserving O(√(N^F)) search complexity, achieving up to 2000× qubit reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hypervector decomposition in HDC requires searching over N^F candidate tuples, which is computationally prohibitive classically. While existing quantum approaches offer quadratic speedup, they rely on qubit-inefficient O(D)-qubit representations that are impractical for high-dimensional hypervectors.

Method: The framework introduces: (1) logarithmic hypervector encoding that compresses D-dimensional hypervectors into O(log D) qubits using amplitude encoding, (2) a logarithmic binding encoding for representing bound hypervector combinations, (3) a reversible hypervector lookup operator for circuit-level manipulation, and (4) a modified Dürr-Høyer search procedure adapted to the compressed representation while maintaining O(√(N^F)) complexity.

Result: Experimental validation demonstrates correct similarity computation and accurate decomposition in executable regimes. The method achieves up to 2,000× fewer qubits compared to baselines using explicit D-qubit hypervector encodings, with significantly improved qubit scaling.

Conclusion: The proposed logarithmic encoding scheme makes quantum HDC decomposition significantly more resource-efficient, paving the way for practical quantum-accelerated hypervector decomposition on near-term quantum devices.

Abstract: Hyperdimensional Computing (HDC) represents symbols using high-dimensional hypervectors of dimension $D$. In hypervector decomposition, the objective is to recover $F$ constituent hypervectors, each drawn from a codebook of size $N$, from a bound target hypervector. This requires searching over $N^F$ candidate tuples, making the task computationally prohibitive at scale. Recent quantum approach provides a quadratic search advantage, but typically rely on qubit-inefficient $O(D)$-qubit hypervector representations. We propose a qubit-efficient quantum framework for HDC decomposition that reduces the representation cost to $O(\log D)$. The framework introduces logarithmic hypervector and binding encodings, together with a reversible hypervector lookup operator for circuit-level manipulation of dense hypervectors. Combined with a modified Dürr-Høyer search procedure, the method preserves $O(\sqrt{N^F})$ search complexity while substantially reducing qubit usage. Experimental results validate correct similarity computation, accurate decomposition in executable regimes, and significantly improved qubit scaling over baselines based on explicit $D$-qubit hypervector encodings, achieving up to $2{,}000\times$ fewer qubits.

</details>


### [62] [PFAdapter: Hierarchical LoRA Decomposition for Personalized Federated MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12111)
*Jing Liu,Kun Yang,Yan Wang,Dingkang Yang,Xiaoshuai Hao,Wei Zhang,Yang Liu,Wei Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PFAdapter is a communication-efficient federated fine-tuning framework for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) that uses hierarchical LoRA decomposition to separate adapter parameters into global-shared (query/key) and local-private (value/output) components, with orthogonality regularization and selective aggregation cutting communication costs by ~50% while improving accuracy by 2.4–4.8% across edge intelligence tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems at the network edge require MLLMs fine-tuned via federated learning to preserve data privacy. However, conventional federated protocols use uniform parameter aggregation that conflates domain-invariant features with client-specific nuances, leading to suboptimal personalization and high communication overhead. There is a need for a framework that balances global knowledge sharing with local adaptation under heterogeneous network conditions.

Method: PFAdapter introduces three key mechanisms: (1) hierarchical LoRA decomposition that splits adapter parameters into global-shared components (query and key projections for universal multimodal semantics) and local-private components (value and output projections for edge-specific adaptation); (2) Frobenius-norm-based orthogonality regularization to enforce strict separation between global and local components, preventing redundant feature learning; and (3) selective aggregation protocols that synchronize only global-shared components across the federated network, preserving local expertise while reducing communication costs by nearly 50%.

Result: Evaluated on VQA-RAD, SLAKE, Hateful Memes, and CrisisMMD datasets, PFAdapter consistently outperformed state-of-the-art baselines with accuracy improvements of 2.4% to 4.8% across diverse edge intelligence tasks, while simultaneously reducing communication overhead by approximately 50%.

Conclusion: PFAdapter establishes an efficient and practical solution for deploying agentic AI in resource-constrained communication networks by effectively decoupling global multimodal semantics from local adaptation, thereby achieving both superior personalization and significant communication savings.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems are reshaping communications and networking by deploying autonomous intelligent agents capable of collaborative learning while maintaining data privacy at network edges. Within distributed network environments, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) serve as cognitive engines for edge devices, yet federated fine-tuning faces substantial challenges in balancing global knowledge aggregation with local adaptation under heterogeneous network conditions. Conventional federated protocols typically rely on uniform parameter aggregation, which conflates domain-invariant features with client-specific nuances, thereby resulting in suboptimal personalization and excessive communication overhead. To address these challenges, we propose PFAdapter, a communication-efficient framework introducing hierarchical LoRA decomposition to explicitly separate adapter parameters into global-shared and local-private components. Query and key projections are assigned to global synchronization for capturing universal multimodal semantics across the network, while value and output projections remain localized for edge-specific adaptation. Additionally, orthogonality regularization based on the Frobenius norm enforces strict separation between these components, preventing redundant feature learning. Selective aggregation protocols synchronize only global-shared components across the federated network, preserving local expertise and reducing communication costs by nearly 50%. Extensive experiments on VQA-RAD, SLAKE, Hateful Memes, and CrisisMMD datasets demonstrate that PFAdapter consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving accuracy improvements ranging from 2.4% to 4.8% across diverse edge intelligence tasks. Consequently, our framework establishes an efficient solution for agentic AI deployment in resource-constrained communication networks.

</details>


### [63] [Mirror Horizon: Viable Path Entropy as a Measure of Bounded Reflection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11937)
*Tiantian Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mirror Theory operationalizes intelligence as the capacity to sustain coherent continuations under repeated reflection. The paper introduces Viable Path Entropy (VPE), a budget-constrained measure of verified continuation diversity, and validates it on GSM8K reasoning tasks, showing that Mirror Horizon — not parameter count — best captures accessible capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional evaluation metrics like one-shot accuracy or pass@k fail to capture the structural depth of an intelligent system's capabilities. Mirror Theory argues that a system should be assessed by the coherent continuations it can sustain under iterative self-reflection, not merely by what it initially represents. The paper seeks to operationalize this philosophical claim into a measurable, computationally tractable framework.

Method: The authors define Viable Path Entropy (VPE) as a finite-budget measure combining two components: (1) the probability of reaching a viable continuation, and (2) the entropy of verified continuation modes among successful rollouts. The theoretical scaffold includes four constructs — intuition (local underdetermination), taste (invariant-selecting pressure), reflection (taste-guided resolution), and geometry (learned structure stabilizing future reflection). Experiments instantiate this on the GSM8K benchmark using Qwen2.5-Instruct models (1.5B, 3B) with 32 rollouts per problem and two reflection horizons, varying token budgets from 96 to 160.

Result: Increasing the token budget from 96 to 160 substantially expanded verified reachability, reduced zero-reachability, increased verified-mode entropy, and improved smoothed VPE across both model sizes. At 160 tokens, Qwen2.5-1.5B achieved the strongest Mirror Horizon, outperforming the larger Qwen2.5-3B, demonstrating that Mirror Horizon reflects accessible verified continuation capacity rather than raw parameter count.

Conclusion: Mirror Theory is validated as a measure-level account of capability: intelligence is better characterized by the structure of viable continuations made reachable under bounded reflection than by one-shot accuracy or pass@k. The framework provides a novel, operational lens for evaluating and comparing model reasoning capacities.

Abstract: Mirror Theory proposes that an intelligent system should be studied not only by what it represents, but by what coherent continuations it can sustain under repeated reflection. We make this claim operational through \emph{viable path entropy} (VPE), a finite-budget measure of verified continuation capacity. Given a mirror state, a rollout protocol, a verifier, and a mode map, VPE decomposes bounded capability into two parts: the probability of reaching a viable continuation and the diversity of verified continuation modes reached among successful rollouts. This paper restores the full theoretical scaffold behind the measure: intuition as local underdetermining constraint, taste as invariant-selecting pressure, reflection as taste-guided resolution of underdetermination, and geometry as the learned structure that makes future reflection stable. We then instantiate the theory in language-model reasoning experiments on GSM8K. Across Qwen2.5-Instruct models, 32 sampled rollouts per problem, and two reflection horizons, increasing the token budget from 96 to 160 substantially expands verified reachability, reduces zero-reachability, increases verified-mode entropy, and improves smoothed VPE. At 160 tokens, Qwen2.5-1.5B realizes the strongest mirror horizon among the tested models, even though Qwen2.5-3B has more parameters. This shows that mirror horizon is not parameter count, but accessible verified continuation capacity under a bounded reflection protocol. The result supports Mirror Theory as a measure-level account: capability is the structure of viable continuations made reachable, not merely one-shot accuracy or pass@k.

</details>


### [64] [Mathematics of Data Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11938)
*Afonso S. Bandeira,Amit Singer,Thomas Strohmer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive textbook covering the mathematical and statistical foundations underpinning modern data science, spanning linear algebra, probability, optimization, and their applications to machine learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a rigorous mathematical framework for understanding the core principles, algorithms, and theoretical guarantees in data science, bridging the gap between applied techniques and their theoretical underpinnings.

Method: The book systematically develops mathematical tools across multiple domains: linear algebra (SVD, PCA, random projections), probability and statistics (concentration of measure, Gaussian analysis, matrix concentration inequalities), optimization theory, graph theory (Laplacians, diffusion maps), and compressive sensing/low-rank recovery. These are presented in the context of canonical data science tasks such as regression, classification, clustering, dimensionality reduction, and deep learning.

Result: The text synthesizes a broad spectrum of mathematical results — including high-dimensional phenomena, spectral methods, regularization theory, community detection guarantees, and the large-sample behavior of graph Laplacians — into a unified pedagogical framework for data science.

Conclusion: A solid mathematical foundation is indispensable for both the principled application and the continued advancement of data science methods, and this text serves as a comprehensive reference for that foundation.

Abstract: This book is about the mathematical foundations of data science.
  1. Introduction
  2. Curses, Blessings, and Surprises in High Dimensions
  3. Singular Value Decomposition and Principal Component Analysis
  4. Linear Regression and Regularization
  5. Graphs, Networks, and Clustering
  6. Nonlinear Dimension Reduction and Diffusion Maps
  7. Linear Dimension Reduction via Random Projections
  8. Optimization for Data Science
  9. Classification
  10. A Mathematical Introduction  to Deep Learning
  11. Large Sample Limit of Graph Laplacians
  12. Community
  13. Concentration of Measure and Gaussian Analysis
  14. Matrix Concentration Inequalities
  15. Compressive Sensing and Sparsity
  16. Low-Rank Matrix Recovery

</details>


### [65] [CARE-LoRA: Compressed Activation REconstruction for Memory-Efficient LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11940)
*Gengyu Zhang,Haiyin Ran,Zhengbao He,Yuhang Liu,Hanling Tian,Zhehao Huang,Xiaolin Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CARE-LoRA proposes a data-aware compressed activation reconstruction method that replaces full input activations with low-rank compressed activations during LoRA fine-tuning, using a lightweight reconstruction matrix to recover gradients during backpropagation. This significantly reduces memory footprint while maintaining or improving performance compared to standard LoRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With LoRA drastically reducing trainable parameters, the activations stored for backpropagation have become the dominant memory bottleneck during fine-tuning of large pre-trained models under limited memory budgets.

Method: CARE-LoRA exploits LoRA's inherent projection structure: it replaces full input activations with low-rank compressed activations naturally produced by the LoRA branch (A·x). During the forward pass, it computes a lightweight reconstruction matrix with negligible overhead. During backpropagation, this reconstruction matrix is used to recover the gradient signal for the LoRA matrices, keeping them fully trainable without needing to store full activations.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse models and downstream tasks show that CARE-LoRA substantially reduces overall memory footprint while achieving competitive or even superior performance compared to standard LoRA and representative LoRA variants.

Conclusion: CARE-LoRA effectively addresses the activation memory bottleneck in LoRA fine-tuning, enabling memory-efficient adaptation of large pre-trained models without sacrificing performance.

Abstract: As the scale of large pre-trained models continues to grow, fine-tuning them under limited memory budgets has become increasingly challenging. Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), currently one of the most widely adopted parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods, mitigates this challenge by optimizing only low-rank adaptation matrices, thereby greatly reducing the number of trainable parameters. With the parameter overhead substantially reduced, the activations retained for backpropagation have emerged as the primary remaining memory bottleneck during LoRA fine-tuning. To address this, we propose CARE-LoRA, a data-aware Compressed Activation REconstruction framework. By exploiting the inherent projection structure of LoRA, CARE-LoRA replaces the full input activation with the low-rank compressed activation naturally produced by the LoRA branch. It further computes a lightweight reconstruction matrix during the forward pass with negligible additional computation cost, which is used during backpropagation to reconstruct the gradient signal, thereby keeping LoRA matrices fully trainable. Extensive experiments across diverse models and downstream tasks demonstrate that, while substantially reducing the overall memory footprint, CARE-LoRA achieves competitive or even superior performance compared with standard LoRA and representative LoRA variants. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/fishandyu/CARE-LoRA .

</details>


### [66] [How Query Visibility Changes KV-Cache Compression Rankings: A Matched-Budget Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11942)
*Daming Luo,Christy Liang,Junyu Xuan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KV-cache compression methods are typically evaluated query-aware, but real deployment requires query-agnostic compression. This paper audits six published methods against three baselines under both protocols, finding that query visibility dramatically changes rankings — most methods lose their advantage when questions aren't seen during compression, with only KeyDiff consistently beating trivial baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The economic rationale for KV-cache compression is reuse: compress a document once and answer many future queries. However, existing methods are predominantly evaluated under a query-aware protocol where the question is known before compression. The authors aim to expose the gap between these two evaluation protocols and determine which methods genuinely work when deployed in realistic, query-agnostic settings.

Method: The authors conduct a matched-budget audit: six published KV-cache compression methods (including SnapKV, KeyDiff, and four others sharing a common attention backend) are benchmarked against three trivial baselines (e.g., "keep the start and recent window") across three open 7-9B models. Two evaluation protocols are compared — query-aware and query-agnostic — with everything held fixed (model, compression ratio, instances, decoding) except the scoring rule. The evaluation spans 144,300 paired runs on RULER-8192 and 40,800 on LongBench, with 50,000-resample paired bootstrap for statistical rigor.

Result: Three key findings emerge: (1) Query visibility changes method rankings. Under the agnostic protocol, only KeyDiff beats the best-of-3 trivial baseline consistently (31 of 36 cells). SnapKV, the most widely deployed method, underperforms the "keep start + recent window" baseline by -0.066 on average. (2) The per-method performance drop between protocols is ordered by how visible the query is in each method's scoring signal — SnapKV drops most (Δ=+0.198, query sits in its 64-token observation window) while KeyDiff drops least (Δ=+0.011, its score contains no query term), as legible from source code inspection.

Conclusion: The query-aware evaluation protocol systematically inflates the apparent performance of most KV-cache compression methods. Under realistic query-agnostic deployment, most methods fail to beat trivial baselines. The degree of inflation is directly proportional to how much each method's scoring mechanism exploits query visibility, which can be diagnosed by inspecting source code. KeyDiff stands as the notable exception — its query-agnostic design yields consistent, though modest, gains.

Abstract: KV-cache compression methods are predominantly evaluated with the query appended to the context before compression -- a query-aware protocol. Yet the economic case for a compressed KV cache is reuse: compress a document once, answer many future questions against it. In that deployment, compression must happen query-agnostic -- before any question is seen. We present a matched-budget audit of six published compression methods against three trivial baselines on three open 7-9B models (144,300 paired evaluations on RULER-8192; 40,800 on LongBench; 50,000-resample paired bootstrap throughout). Everything is held fixed -- model, compression ratio, instances, decoding -- except the scoring rule. Three findings. (1) Query visibility changes the rankings: under the agnostic protocol, of the five audited methods that share a common attention backend, only KeyDiff beats a best-of-3 trivial baseline consistently (31 of 36 cells), and the most widely deployed method, SnapKV, loses to "keep the start and the recent window" on average (-0.066). (2) The per-method drop between the two protocols is ordered consistently with how visible the question is to each method's scoring signal, legible in its source code: from Delta=+0.198 for SnapKV (the question sits inside its 64-token observation window) down to Delta=+0.011 for KeyDiff (its score contains no query term at all).

</details>


### [67] [BattVAE-GP: Generative Modeling of Long-Horizon Battery Degradation with Uncertainty Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11943)
*Raghvender Raghvender,Mahdi Abid,Ferran Brosa Planella,Charles Delacourt,Arnaud Demortière*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A VAE-GP hybrid framework (BattVAE-GP) encodes battery degradation trajectories into a 2D latent space and uses a sparse multitask Gaussian process to interpolate degradation dynamics across unseen C-rates, providing uncertainty-aware SOH predictions with computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon physics-based battery degradation simulations (e.g., DFN/P2D models) are computationally expensive, limiting dense exploration of operating conditions over extended cycle life. There is a need for efficient surrogate models that can interpolate degradation behavior across unseen charging rates while providing uncertainty estimates.
