Method: Cycle-resolved degradation data from DFN/P2D simulations (PyBaMM) are transformed into capacity-aligned voltage and derivative features, then encoded via a Variational Autoencoder (VAE) into a 2D latent space organized by cycle progression and charging protocol. A sparse multitask Gaussian process (GP) is trained on this latent space using cycle number and C-rate as inputs, enabling continuous interpolation with posterior uncertainty. During evaluation, latent trajectories at unseen C-rates are decoded through the frozen VAE decoder to reconstruct voltage-capacity evolution, and Monte Carlo propagation through an auxiliary latent-to-SOH predictor yields uncertainty-aware SOH estimates.

Result: Under protocol-level holdout, the latent-space GP accurately recovers unseen C-rate degradation trajectories with uncertainty behavior consistent with training data support. At unseen interior C-rates, the model generates latent trajectories coherently positioned between neighboring simulated protocols. Decoded outputs yield smooth voltage-capacity evolution, and the framework provides calibrated uncertainty-aware SOH predictions.

Conclusion: The BattVAE-GP framework offers a computationally efficient, uncertainty-aware surrogate for long-horizon battery degradation modeling and provides a structured basis for extending health prediction to richer operating conditions and simulation-experiment fusion.

Abstract: Long-horizon physics-based simulations of battery degradation provide mechanistic insight but remain computationally expensive, limiting their use for dense exploration of operating conditions over extended cycle life. Here, we propose a hybrid physics-probabilistic learning framework for surrogate modeling of lithium-ion battery degradation trajectories at unseen charging rates. Cycle-resolved degradation data generated with a DFN/P2D electrochemical model in PyBaMM are first transformed into capacity-aligned voltage and derivative features and encoded using a Variational Autoencoder (VAE). The resulting two-dimensional latent space organizes degradation trajectories according to both cycle progression and charging protocol. A sparse multitask Gaussian process (GP) is then trained in this latent space using cycle number and C-rate as input variables, providing continuous interpolation of latent degradation dynamics together with posterior uncertainty estimates. Under protocol-level holdout evaluation, the latent-space GP accurately recovers unseen C-rate trajectories and exhibits uncertainty behavior consistent with the support of the training data. When queried at unseen interior C-rates, the model generates latent trajectories that remain coherently positioned between neighboring simulated protocols. Decoding the GP-predicted latent states through the frozen VAE decoder yields smooth voltage-capacity evolution, while Monte Carlo propagation of the GP latent posterior through an auxiliary latent to State of Health (SOH) predictor provides uncertainty-aware SOH estimates. The proposed BattVAE-GP framework therefore offers a computationally efficient and uncertainty-aware surrogate for long-horizon degradation modeling, providing a structured basis for extending battery health prediction toward richer operating conditions and future simulation-experiment fusion.

</details>


### [68] [Generalized Distribution-Free Semi-Supervised Learning with Risk Rewrite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11947)
*Yushi Hirose,Hiroo Irobe,Takafumi Kanamori*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper generalizes PNU learning to a broader framework for semi-supervised learning that uses linear combinations of component risks to construct unbiased risk estimators, extends to multiclass classification, and demonstrates that minimizing estimator variance (via derived minimum achievable variance) directly improves generalization performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard SSL methods depend on distributional assumptions that fail in practice. PNU learning offers a distribution-free alternative but is limited to binary classification, and its variance optimality is unestablished. The authors aim to generalize PNU to multiclass settings, resolve the variance optimality question, and connect variance reduction to generalization guarantees.

Method: A generalized framework that constructs unbiased risk estimators as linear combinations of component risks (positive, negative, unlabeled). The authors derive the minimum achievable variance of such estimators, establish a generalization bound linking variance reduction to learning performance, and propose two practical SSL algorithms based on these theoretical results.

Result: The proposed estimator achieves lower variance than PNU learning in asymmetric loss scenarios. The two derived practical methods match or outperform existing SSL approaches on both binary and multiclass benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: The generalized linear-combination framework successfully extends distribution-free SSL to multiclass classification, resolves PNU's variance optimality question, and demonstrates both theoretically and empirically that variance reduction yields improved generalization performance.

Abstract: Typical semi-supervised learning (SSL) methods rely on distributional assumptions, and their performance degrades when these are violated. While PNU learning, a risk rewriting method, offers a distribution-free alternative, it is restricted to binary classification and its variance optimality remains unclear. In this paper, we propose a generalized framework that constructs unbiased risk estimators using linear combinations of component risks, subsuming PNU learning and extending to multiclass classification. We derive the minimum achievable variance, demonstrating our estimator can attain lower variance than PNU in asymmetric loss scenarios. Furthermore, we establish a generalization bound directly linking this variance reduction to improved learning performance. Based on these theoretical insights, we introduce two practical SSL methods that empirically match or outperform existing approaches on binary and multiclass benchmarks.

</details>


### [69] [Scale-Aware Attention for Scarce Neural Data: An RG-Flow Transformer on Sleep-EDF EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11950)
*Dibakar Sigdel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether a transformer with explicit renormalization-group inductive bias (RG-Flow Transformer) outperforms a vanilla transformer on EEG sleep staging. While both models perform equivalently on classification accuracy, RG-Flow uniquely recovers the spectral exponent β out-of-sample, offering superior interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG brain field potentials exhibit scale-free 1/f^β spectra where the exponent β tracks cortical state and sleep depth. The authors hypothesize that a transformer with an explicit renormalization-group inductive bias — mirroring the scale-invariant structure of EEG — would have an advantage over vanilla transformers, particularly in scarce-data regimes where inductive biases matter most.

Method: The authors propose the RG-Flow Transformer, which augments standard self-attention with: (1) a scale-aware stream with a learnable anomalous dimension γ, (2) block-spin coarse-graining operations, and (3) an entropy-gated synchronization bridge between scales. They benchmark this against a parameter-matched vanilla transformer and a hierarchy-only ablation on the PhysioNet Sleep-EDF corpus using 5-class AASM sleep staging with strict leave-one-subject-out cross-validation. They also sweep per-subject data budgets to test for a scarce-data crossover and evaluate whether the learned γ tracks measured spectral exponent β out-of-sample.

Result: RG-Flow (77.3%) and vanilla transformer (77.0%) are statistically indistinguishable on 5-class sleep staging (paired p=0.294) across 5 subjects and 5 seeds. The predicted scarce-data crossover does not materialize — vanilla is numerically ahead at every data-limited budget. However, RG-Flow successfully recovers the continuous spectral exponent out-of-sample (β-recovery R² = 0.416), a capability the vanilla architecture cannot replicate.

Conclusion: The explicit RG inductive bias does not improve classification performance on real EEG data, even in scarce-data settings, but it does confer interpretability: RG-Flow learns a quantity (γ) that correlates with the physiologically meaningful spectral exponent β. This suggests that RG-inspired architectures may be valuable not for predictive gains but for building models whose internal representations align with known biophysical structure.

Abstract: Brain field potentials are scale-free: their power spectra follow a $1/f^β$ law whose aperiodic exponent $β$ tracks cortical state, and sleep depth in particular is a shift in $β$. We ask whether a transformer endowed with an explicit renormalization-group (RG) inductive bias -- the RG-Flow Transformer, which couples ordinary self-attention to a scale-aware stream with a learnable anomalous dimension $γ$, block-spin coarse-graining, and an entropy-gated synchronization bridge -- has an advantage over a parameter-matched vanilla transformer on \emph{real, scarce} EEG. Using the PhysioNet Sleep-EDF corpus with a strict leakage-free by-subject hold-out, we (i) benchmark RG-Flow against a param-matched vanilla transformer and a hierarchy-only ablation on 5-class AASM sleep staging, (ii) sweep the per-subject data budget to look for the inductive-bias crossover predicted when data are scarce, and (iii) test whether RG-Flow's learned $γ$ tracks the measured spectral exponent $β$ out-of-sample -- a quantity the vanilla model does not possess. Across $5$ subjects and $5$ seeds under leave-one-subject-out cross-validation, RG-Flow and the vanilla transformer are statistically indistinguishable on 5-class staging (77.3\% vs 77.0\% accuracy; paired $p=0.294$), and the predicted scarce-data crossover does not appear: vanilla is numerically ahead at every data-limited budget. What does separate the models is interpretability -- RG-Flow recovers the continuous spectral exponent out-of-sample ($β$-recovery $R^2 = 0.416$), a capability the vanilla architecture has no analogue for.

</details>


### [70] [Graph-Constrained Policy Learning for Extreme Clinical Code Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11954)
*Amritpal Singh,Sebastian Torres,Khawar Shakeel,Syed Ahmad Chan Bukhari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph-constrained traversal policy reformulates ICD coding as a hierarchical decision process over a pruned code tree, where a single language model descends level-by-level selecting child nodes until reaching billable leaf codes. The method outperforms flat multi-label baselines on MIMIC-IV, especially on rare codes, with supervised fine-tuning proving superior to reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing clinical code prediction systems treat the task as flat multi-label classification, scoring codes independently. This provides limited training signal for rare labels in the large (15,761-code), sparse, deeply hierarchical ICD-10-CM space and does not leverage or guarantee structural validity of the code hierarchy.

Method: The authors propose a graph-constrained traversal policy that formulates ICD prediction as a finite-horizon decision process over a pruned code hierarchy. A single language model descends the graph level by level, selecting valid child nodes at each step until billable leaf codes are reached. This converts extreme multi-label prediction into sparse, hierarchy-aware subset decisions with guaranteed structural validity. Variants include supervised fine-tuning (SFT-1+) and GRPO reinforcement learning, with a controlled factorial study evaluating architecture, training algorithm, and data budget.

Result: On MIMIC-IV, SFT-1+ achieves 0.709 micro-F1 on a 50-code subset and 0.527 micro-F1 on the full 15,761-code space, outperforming CAML, LAAT, and PLM-ICD. In the full setting, it improves over the strongest flat baseline by 0.044 micro-F1 and 0.157 macro-F1. A shared policy matches a three-specialist cascade while avoiding context-window overflow (28-32% of full-space notes). Increasing supervised trajectory data consistently improves performance; GRPO reinforcement learning provides no benefit over supervised continuation with matched data.

Conclusion: Simple graph-constrained policy learning with supervised fine-tuning outperforms more complex flat, cascaded, and reinforcement-learning alternatives for extreme clinical code prediction, particularly mitigating the rare-code bottleneck through hierarchical decomposition.

Abstract: Clinical code prediction maps unstructured discharge summaries to ICD-10-CM leaf codes in a large, sparse, and deeply hierarchical label space. Most systems treat the task as flat multi-label classification, scoring codes independently and providing limited training signal for rare labels. We propose a graph-constrained traversal policy that formulates ICD prediction as a finite-horizon decision process over a pruned code hierarchy. A single language model descends the graph level by level, selecting valid child nodes until billable leaf codes are reached. This converts extreme multi-label prediction into sparse, hierarchy-aware subset decisions while guaranteeing structurally valid outputs.
  On MIMIC-IV discharge summaries, our best supervised policy, SFT-1+, achieves 0.709 micro-F1 on a curated 50-code subset and 0.527 micro-F1 on the full 15,761-code space, outperforming flat baselines including CAML, LAAT, and PLM-ICD. In the full setting, SFT-1+ improves over the strongest flat baseline by 0.044 micro-F1 and 0.157 macro-F1, suggesting that graph-constrained decomposition mitigates the rare-code bottleneck. A controlled factorial study evaluates architecture, training algorithm, and data budget. Across both scales, one shared policy matches a three-specialist cascade while avoiding its context-window overflow on 28-32% of full-space test notes. Increasing supervised trajectory data is the only intervention that consistently improves performance, while GRPO reinforcement learning provides no benefit over supervised continuation with matched data. These results show that simple graph-constrained policy learning can outperform more complex flat, cascaded, and reinforcement-learning alternatives for extreme clinical code prediction.

</details>


### [71] [Scalable Optimal Transport Algorithm for Network Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11952)
*Elaheh Hassani,Durga Mandarapu,Qi Yu,Hanghang Tong,Ariful Azad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FastAlign is a scalable framework for optimal transport-based network alignment that preserves the original OT formulation while reinterpreting its computation as recurring mixed sparse-dense operations, achieving up to 9.45x speedup on CPU and 32.54x on GPU without sacrificing alignment quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art network alignment methods achieve high accuracy by repeatedly constructing and updating dense matrices, which sacrifices scalability. There is a need to address this scalability limitation without compromising alignment accuracy.

Method: FastAlign preserves the original optimal transport (OT) formulation but reinterprets its computation as a set of recurring mixed sparse-dense operations. It combines sparsity-aware graph computation with domain-specific kernel fusion, including a custom SpMM (Sparse Matrix-Matrix Multiplication) kernel.

Result: FastAlign achieves alignment quality comparable to state-of-the-art OT-based methods while reducing end-to-end runtime by up to 3.89x–9.45x on CPU and 2.24x–32.54x on GPU.

Conclusion: Sparsity-aware computation and kernel fusion can dramatically improve the scalability of OT-based network alignment without any loss in alignment accuracy, making FastAlign a practical solution for large-scale network alignment tasks.

Abstract: Network alignment identifies node correspondences across different networks and is a fundamental primitive in many data science applications, including social network analysis, fraud detection, and knowledge graph integration. However, state-of-the-art network alignment methods often achieve high accuracy by repeatedly constructing and updating dense matrices, sacrificing scalability in the process. To address this scalability limitation without compromising alignment accuracy, we present FastAlign, a scalable, sparsity-aware framework for optimal transport-based network alignment. Rather than introducing a new alignment model, FastAlign preserves the original OT formulation and reinterprets its computation as a set of recurring mixed sparse-dense operations. FastAlign combines sparsity-aware graph computation with domain-specific kernel fusion, including a custom SpMM kernel. Our results show that FastAlign achieves alignment quality comparable to state-of-the-art OT-based methods while substantially reducing end-to-end runtime up to 3.89x-9.45x on CPU and 2.24x-32.54x on GPU.

</details>


### [72] [When Does Reward Teach State? A Hidden-Automaton Instrument and the Group-Language Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11953)
*Jim Allchin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a white-box framework using deterministic finite automata (DFA) to separately measure an RL agent's reward achievement and latent state recovery, revealing that high reward does not imply true task understanding. The authors identify three controllable axes (optimizer strength, task structure, observation informativeness) that govern whether agents learn latent state or merely exploit reward-correlated shortcuts, and introduce the distinction between perception gaps and planning gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL evaluation cannot distinguish whether a high-reward agent has genuinely learned the task's latent structure or merely discovered a reward-correlated shortcut, because the "true state" is typically undefined. The authors aim to make this question rigorously answerable by providing a framework where the true latent state is known exactly.

Method: The authors formalize tasks as hidden deterministic finite automata (DFA), where the agent observes symbol streams and intermittently chooses the next symbol, receiving a single sparse terminal reward for acceptance. Knowing the DFA provides the optimal return (normalized score) and exact latent state at every step, enabling direct probing of the agent's internal representation. They systematically vary three axes: (1) optimizer strength (comparing weak on-policy RL vs. PPO+GAE), (2) task structure (using permutation/group-language structure as a computationally identifiable warning sign), and (3) observation informativeness (varying how much symbols reveal about state). Linear probes are used to assess whether latent state is recoverable from the agent's representations.

Result: Key findings: (1) Weak on-policy RL yields chance-level state probing despite reward success; PPO+GAE partially recovers state with high seed variance. (2) Permutation structure is predictable from the transition function before training and flags perception gaps with 0.86 precision (89/103) on held-out automata, in one direction only. (3) Observation informativeness proportionally governs state recovery. The framework distinguishes perception gaps (latent state not linearly recoverable from representations) from planning gaps (state recoverable but not behaviorally used) — a distinction reward-only evaluation cannot make.

Conclusion: High reward is not evidence of task understanding. Whether an RL agent recovers latent state is predictable in advance from task structure and training configuration. The DFA-based white-box framework enables rigorous, interpretable evaluation of representation learning in RL, decomposing performance into separately measurable quantities and revealing fundamental dissociations between reward success and state recovery.

Abstract: Does a reinforcement-learning agent that earns high reward represent its task's latent state, or only a reward-correlated shortcut? The question is usually unanswerable: the "true state" is undefined. We make it exactly answerable with a white-box instrument: express the task as a hidden deterministic finite automaton (DFA), let the agent observe a symbol stream and intermittently choose the next symbol under partial control, and grant one sparse terminal reward for acceptance. Knowing the automaton gives two things for free: the optimal return (so reward becomes an interpretable normalized score) and the exact latent state at every step (so we can probe the agent's representation without ever showing it). Reward success and latent-state learning thus become separately measured quantities whose coupling is governed by three controllable axes. Optimizer strength: under weak on-policy RL the agent earns reward with the state probe at chance for every architecture, tempting the conclusion that sparse RL cannot install latent state; a pre-registered control overturns it -- PPO+GAE recovers the state, but only partially and with high seed variance. Task structure: permutation (group-language) structure is a warning sign computable from the transition function before any training, and held out on 153 capacity-controlled fresh automata it flags perception gaps at precision 0.86 (89 of 103), in one direction only. Observation informativeness: a label-free auxiliary is vacuous when observations carry no state and recovers it in proportion to how much they reveal. The payoff is a distinction reward-only evaluation cannot make: a perception gap (latent state not linearly recoverable, though representable) versus a planning gap (state recoverable but unused). High reward is thus not evidence of task understanding; whether an agent recovers latent state is predictable in advance.

</details>


### [73] [Exact and Certified Data Shapley for Weighted k-Nearest-Neighbor Regression and Soft-Label Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11956)
*Zongye Lyu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents the first pseudo-polynomial-time exact algorithm for weighted KNN regression Data Shapley, resolving a previously open problem where the only exact method was exponential O(N^K) brute force. It provides a counting dynamic program, a certified FPTAS, complexity lower bounds, and demonstrates that exact values match Monte-Carlo estimates statistically but offer determinism and certified error bounds that Monte-Carlo cannot guarantee.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data Shapley is the standard method for quantifying training data value, and its KNN specialization is widely deployed in practice (pyDVL, OpenDataVal). However, while exact algorithms exist for unweighted KNN and weighted KNN classification, weighted KNN regression and soft-label prediction have resisted polynomial-time solutions due to the ratio structure of the regression prediction (a quotient of two coalition-dependent sums), which breaks the additive, threshold, and duplication properties leveraged by prior algorithms. This gap motivates the work.

Method: The authors develop a counting dynamic program that operates over a joint integer state (sum of weights, sum of weights × labels) at fixed lattice precision, yielding pseudo-polynomial runtime (polynomial in N and K). They discretize continuous weights/targets and use DP to count coalitions yielding each (numerator, denominator) pair, then aggregate to compute exact Shapley values. They also provide a certified FPTAS via rounding and scaling, with per-value error certificates. Theoretical contributions include an Omega(D_w) output-size lower bound and access-model hardness results. The method extends to weighted soft-label multi-class settings.

Result: The exact algorithm was verified against brute-force enumeration on 12,716 adversarial instances with zero mismatch. The FPTAS error certificate was never violated across 86,400 checks. In downstream mislabel detection, exact values were statistically equivalent to Monte-Carlo Data Shapley (dataset-level TOST, n=8, p < 10⁻⁴). However, Monte-Carlo failed to reproduce the exact top-10% ranking at any budget tested, up to 3,000 permutations (~1.28×10⁶ utility evaluations).

Conclusion: Exact weighted KNN regression Data Shapley is now tractable via pseudo-polynomial DP. The primary value of exactness lies not in outperforming Monte-Carlo statistically (they are equivalent on downstream tasks) but in providing determinism, certified error bounds, and a ground-truth reference for auditing approximate estimators — capabilities Monte-Carlo fundamentally cannot offer.

Abstract: Data Shapley is the standard principled answer to which training points are worth what, and its k-nearest-neighbor (KNN) specialization is the version deployed in practice: the exact estimator shipped by toolkits such as pyDVL and OpenDataVal. Exact algorithms are known for unweighted KNN and for weighted KNN classification, but weighted KNN regression and soft-label prediction have resisted: the only exact method is an O(N^K) brute force, exponential in neighborhood size K. The obstruction: the weighted regression prediction is a ratio of two coalition-dependent sums, whose normalization denominator breaks the additive, threshold, and duplication structures the prior polynomial algorithms rely on. We close this gap. We give (i) the first pseudo-polynomial-time exact algorithm (polynomial in N and K at fixed lattice precision) for weighted KNN-regression Data Shapley, a counting dynamic program over the joint integer state (sum of w, sum of w*y), verified against exhaustive enumeration with zero mismatch on 12,716 adversarial instances; (ii) a certified FPTAS for continuous weights and targets, with a machine-checkable per-value error certificate never violated across 86,400 checks; (iii) a complexity landscape, including an unconditional Omega(D_w) output-size lower bound and access-model hardness results; and (iv) a weighted soft-label multi-class extension. We release an open-source, CPU-only library and the first exact weighted-regression Data Shapley ground truth. On downstream mislabel detection our exact values are statistically equivalent to Monte-Carlo Data Shapley (dataset-level TOST, n=8, p<10^-4), the pre-registered outcome; the value of exactness is instead determinism, a certified error bound, and an exact reference for auditing estimators: Monte-Carlo did not reproduce the exact top-10% ranking at any budget tested, up to 3,000 permutations (~1.28e6 utility evaluations).

</details>


### [74] [Constructed Reality, Contested Priors: Decoupling and the Architecture of Cognitive Relapse Under the Free Energy Principle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11958)
*MD Ibrahim Hossain Ridoy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A computational study using a VAE + recurrent predictor demonstrates that a predictive system can undergo "ontological inversion," where its default generative behavior adopts a synthetic target domain while representational accuracy remains high. However, at intermediate domain-mixing ratios, a "cognitive relapse" phenomenon emerges — the system partially reverts to baseline behavior despite continued training — revealing that resistance to reality-adoption is a structural property distinct from learning capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under the free energy principle, organisms maintain a generative model and act to minimize surprise. The authors ask whether a synthetic environment could become so internally consistent that the system's inference machinery adopts it as its default hypothesis, permanently displacing the original environment — a state termed "ontological inversion." Since testing this in biological nervous systems is both unethical and infeasible, they establish a computational proxy to investigate the underlying dynamics.

Method: The authors employ a convolutional variational autoencoder (VAE) paired with a recurrent latent predictor, where the evidence lower bound (ELBO) is mathematically equivalent (up to sign) to variational free energy. The network is trained first on a baseline visual domain, then on a mixed stream where a swept rehearsal ratio r controls the proportion of baseline content persisting during transition to a target domain. Two metrics are tracked: representational capacity (what the latent space discriminates) and default behavior (what the system generates when unconstrained). The experiment spans 90 runs across a full sweep of r.

Result: Representational accuracy remains near ceiling (0.97–0.998) regardless of r, while default behavior varies dramatically with r, spanning nearly the system's entire behavioral range. This demonstrates a decoupling of learning from acceptance. Critically, at intermediate r values, default output initially rises toward the target domain but then partially reverts toward baseline despite unchanged training — a phenomenon termed "cognitive relapse." This resistance to reality-adoption is not explained by learning speed but constitutes a structural property with distinct failure modes.

Conclusion: The paper offers a computational existence proof that cognitive resistance to ontological inversion is a structural property, not merely a function of learning rate or capacity. The decoupling of representational accuracy from default behavior, and the discovery of cognitive relapse, reveal distinct failure modes in predictive systems when transitioning between environments. The authors frame this as an initial demonstration without further claims.

Abstract: Under the free energy principle, a predictive system does not observe reality directly; it maintains a generative model of the world and experiences that model's best current hypothesis. Can a synthetic environment be made consistent enough that a predictive system's own inference machinery adopts it as this default hypothesis, permanently displacing the environment that first shaped it? We call this state ontological inversion. Because inducing and monitoring such a transition in a nervous system is neither ethical nor technically feasible, we study the underlying computational problem through a controlled proxy: a convolutional variational autoencoder paired with a recurrent latent predictor, whose evidence lower bound objective is mathematically identical, up to sign, to variational free energy itself. The network is trained first on a baseline visual domain, then on a mixed stream in which a swept rehearsal ratio r controls how much baseline content persists during transition to a target domain. Representational capacity, what the latent space can discriminate, is tracked separately from default behavior, what the system generates when left unconstrained. Across a full sweep of 90 runs, the two diverge sharply: representational accuracy stays near ceiling, 0.97 to 0.998, regardless of r, while default behavior spans nearly the system's entire range depending on r alone, a decoupling of learning from acceptance. More strikingly, at intermediate r the system's default output rises toward the target domain, then partially reverts toward the baseline while training continues unchanged, a structural failure we term cognitive relapse. Resistance to reality-adoption is not reducible to learning speed; it is a structural property with its own distinct failure modes, established here as a computational existence proof and nothing further.

</details>


### [75] [Evaluating Reliability in Machine Learning Models for Early Chronic Kidney Disease Prediction: A Systematic Review of Data Leakage and Predictor Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11963)
*Mashrul Hossain,Nafesa Kibria,Fahim Shahriar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic review of CKD prediction studies using interpretable ML reveals that methodological flaws—particularly data leakage—inflate reported performance by ~15%. The paper introduces a leakage taxonomy and scoring framework, finding that >80% of predictors lack cross-study reproducibility, suggesting reported gains often stem from methodological artifacts rather than genuine predictive power.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CKD prediction studies using ML report inconsistent and potentially misleading results due to poor methodological rigor. Key concerns include data leakage, limited temporal patient records, and inconsistent clinical indicators. There is no organized evaluation framework addressing these methodological issues, motivating a systematic assessment of reliability across studies.

Method: The authors conducted a systematic literature review across major academic databases, selecting 19 relevant studies on interpretable ML for CKD prediction. They developed (1) a structured taxonomy of information leakage, (2) a quantitative leakage scoring framework to evaluate methodological reliability, and (3) a cross-study feature stability analysis to assess predictor reproducibility across studies.

Result: High-leakage studies reported an average accuracy of 95.48% vs. 80.2% for leakage-free studies—a ~15.28% inflation. Over 80% of predictive features lacked cross-study reproducibility, with only a small subset consistently emerging as reliable predictors.

Conclusion: Many reported performance improvements in CKD prediction literature are attributable to methodological limitations (especially data leakage) rather than genuine predictive capability. The field requires stricter methodological standards and leakage-aware evaluation protocols.

Abstract: The early detection of Chronic Kidney Disease using machine learning has attracted significant interest in healthcare-related computer science. Despite rapid advancements in this field, many reported studies remain inconsistent and potentially misleading. A significant drawback is the lack of organized evaluation regarding methodological concerns. Key issues include data leakage, limited access to temporal patient records and inconsistency in reported clinical indicators. This research offers a systematic literature review of existing CKD prediction studies using interpretable machine learning techniques, where nineteen relevant studies were selected via systematic searches across major academic databases. To assess methodological reliability, this study introduces a structured taxonomy of information leakage and a quantitative leakage scoring framework to systematically evaluate reliability across CKD prediction studies. The analysis reveals a strong relationship between leakage and inflated performance. Here, High leakage-studies report an average accuracy of 95.48%, compared to 80.2% for leakage-free studies, reflecting an increase of approximately 15.28%. Furthermore, a cross-study feature stability analysis shows that only a small subset of predictors is consistently reproducible, with over 80% lacking reliability. Overall, the findings suggest that many reported performance improvements stem from methodological limitations rather than true predictive capability.

</details>


### [76] [LIDAR-AD: A Decoder-Free Latent-Interaction Dreamer with Action-Residual Chains for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11964)
*Yongzhi Liu,Yang Xiao,Zhong Cao,Zeng Kang,Sunan Zhang,Zhaozhi Dong,Guojun Yu,Weichao Zhuang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LIDAR-AD proposes a decoder-free world model for autonomous driving that replaces observation reconstruction with redundancy-reduced latent alignment and models vehicle control via residual action updates, outperforming existing world-model baselines in both simulated and real-world-derived driving scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing latent world models for autonomous driving suffer from a mismatch: multi-source observations contain control-irrelevant redundancy, while reliable driving decisions depend on risk-relevant relations, future dynamics, and continuous action adjustments. Reconstruction-based objectives and absolute action modeling are suboptimal for learning decision-relevant latent dynamics.

Method: LIDAR-AD introduces two key designs: (1) decoder-free redundancy-reduced latent alignment that encourages compact representations of risk-relevant relations instead of reconstructing observations; (2) residual action modeling with a latent-tanh parameterization and residual-action sequence contrastive learning that aligns multi-step residual-driven rollouts with future latent states. The method preserves interior action reachability while representing smooth long-horizon control as compact local updates.

Result: LIDAR-AD consistently outperforms world-model baselines across diverse simulated driving scenarios, achieving the highest reward and best success rate among learning-based methods. Evaluations on nuPlan-derived log-reconstructed scenarios further demonstrate strong transferability under real-world traffic layouts.

Conclusion: The combination of redundancy-reduced latent alignment and residual action chains significantly improves risk-aware state abstraction, continuous-control modeling, and long-horizon dynamics prediction for autonomous driving world models, with demonstrated generalization to real-world traffic conditions.

Abstract: Autonomous driving requires long-horizon closedloop decision making in dynamic traffic environments. Latent world models offer an effective framework for this problem by enabling imagination-based decision making in compact latent spaces. However, multi-source observations contain controlirrelevant redundancy, whereas reliable driving decisions rely on risk-relevant relations, future dynamics, and continuous action adjustments. This mismatch makes observation reconstruction and absolute action modeling suboptimal for learning decisionrelevant latent dynamics. We propose LIDAR-AD, a decoderfree Latent-Interaction Dreamer with Action-Residual Chains for autonomous driving. LIDAR-AD replaces observation reconstruction with redundancy-reduced latent alignment, encouraging compact representations of risk-relevant relations in multi-source driving inputs. It further models vehicle control as residual action updates and uses residual-action sequence contrastive learning to align multi-step residual-driven rollouts with future latent states. A deterministic analysis shows that the latent-tanh residual parameterization preserves interior action reachability while representing smooth long-horizon control as compact local updates. Together, these designs improve risk-aware state abstraction, continuous-control modeling, and long-horizon dynamics prediction. Extensive experiments across diverse simulated driving scenarios demonstrate that LIDAR-AD consistently outperforms world-model baselines, achieving the highest reward and the best success rate among learning-based methods. Evaluations on nuPlan-derived log-reconstructed scenarios further demonstrate the transferability of LIDAR-AD under real-world traffic layouts.

</details>


### [77] [Beyond Coordinate Gauge: An Audited Protocol for Detecting Donor-Specific Functional Fingerprints after Neural Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11967)
*Truong Xuan Khanh,Phan Thanh Duc*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Independently trained neural networks lack a shared coordinate frame, complicating cross-network comparison. Using aligned MNIST networks exhibiting Neural Collapse, the authors demonstrate that donor-specific functional fingerprints remain detectable after alignment, establishing detectability (but not transplantability or causal persistence) with rigorous leakage control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural Collapse implies networks converge toward shared low-dimensional geometry, raising the question of whether trajectory-specific functional variation survives this convergence. The paper aims to disentangle three distinct claims—detectability, transplantability, and causal persistence—and rigorously test the first in a controlled setting.

Method: Five independently trained networks on MNIST reconstructing Neural Collapse were used. An affine-correct alignment mapping was applied to map donor heads into recipient coordinates. Donor-recipient pair identification was tested after recipient-level baseline correction, with an exact permutation test and a leakage audit to control for spurious signal.

Result: All 20 ordered donor-recipient pairs were correctly identified (exact permutation p = 0.0083), robust to the leakage audit. This establishes detectability of donor-specific functional fingerprints under the test conditions.

Conclusion: Detectability is established in this controlled MNIST setting, but transplantability and causal persistence remain open questions. The study provides a methodological framework combining alignment, ambiguity diagnostics, and leakage control; generalization beyond this setting is not yet demonstrated.

Abstract: Independently trained neural networks have no shared neuron-index reference frame, so comparing them requires accounting for coordinate freedom. Neural Collapse sharpens this problem: networks converge toward a shared, low-dimensional geometry, raising the question of whether trajectory-specific functional variation remains distinguishable after convergence. We distinguish three claims - detectability, transplantability, and causal persistence - and address the first. Using five independently trained networks reconstructing Neural Collapse on MNIST, we apply a verified affine-correct alignment mapping donor heads into recipient coordinates. Donor-specific functional fingerprints remain distinguishable after recipient-level baseline correction: all 20 ordered donor-recipient pairs are correctly identified, with an exact permutation p=0.0083, robust to a leakage audit. These findings establish detectability under the test used here, but not transplantability or causal persistence. The study shows how alignment, ambiguity diagnostics, and leakage control combine to test cross-network variation in a controlled setting; whether this generalizes beyond it is open.

</details>


### [78] [Self-Evolving In-Context Learning for Direct Pilot-to-Beamformer Design in MU-MISO Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11970)
*Yubo Zhang,Xiaodong Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel in-context learning framework for MU-MISO beamforming that adapts to multiple channel models without retraining, using curriculum learning, self-evolving context datasets, and mismatch-aware extensions to outperform existing methods including WMMSE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing beamforming methods require retraining when channel models change and struggle with model mismatches. The authors aim to develop a unified framework that can rapidly adapt to diverse channel conditions without gradient updates while maintaining high sum-rate performance.

Method: The proposed ICL framework combines an ICL-Transformer backbone with pilot encoder-decoder and beamformer encoder-decoder networks. Three innovations are introduced: (a) curriculum learning transitioning from supervised LMMSE imitation to unsupervised sum-rate maximization; (b) a self-evolving mechanism that dynamically expands context datasets during training; and (c) a mismatch-aware extension that handles channel mismatches without explicit calibration. Model-specific context datasets enable multi-model handling without retraining.

Result: Ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of the in-context architecture and training strategies. Simulations show the scheme rapidly adapts to both seen and unseen channel models without gradient updates, mitigates mismatch issues via context construction, and consistently outperforms WMMSE and recent Transformer-based baselines under pilot-based settings.

Conclusion: The proposed ICL-based beamforming framework achieves robust, retraining-free adaptation across diverse channel models, surpassing conventional benchmarks. The combination of curriculum learning, self-evolving contexts, and mismatch-aware design proves effective for practical deployment in dynamic wireless environments.

Abstract: We develop an enhanced in-context learning (ICL) framework to improve the performance of pilot-based beamforming in multi-user multiple-input single-output (MU-MISO) systems. The proposed scheme integrates the ICL-Transformer backbone with the pilot encoder-decoder network (EDN) and the beamformer EDN. A crucial feature of our ICL network is that it can handle multiple channel models without retraining, enabled by the construction of model-specific context datasets. To improve convergence and robustness, we introduce three key innovations: (a) a curriculum learning (CL) strategy that smoothly transitions from supervised LMMSE-labeled imitation to unsupervised sum-rate maximization, (b) a self-evolving mechanism that dynamically expands and refines the context datasets for all channel models during CL-based training, and (c) a mismatch-aware extension that incorporates several mismatches into the general ICL framework and bypasses explicit channel calibrations. Ablation studies validate the effectiveness of the in-context architecture and enhanced training strategies. Simulation results over diverse communication environments show that the proposed scheme is able to rapidly adapt to both seen and unseen channel models without gradient-based parameter updates, and can mitigate the mismatch issues via intelligent context constructions. Furthermore, our scheme consistently outperforms the existing beamforming schemes under pilot-based settings, including the WMMSE benchmark and the recent Transformer-based methods.

</details>


### [79] [Learning to Discretize: Diffusion-Based Adaptive Mesh with Spectral Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11974)
*Zixuan Shen,Bingchuan Wang,Zhi Wang,Yong Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes learning spatial discretization (grids) for neural PDE solvers via a two-stage diffusion framework: Stage 1 generates r-adaptive mesh displacements conditioned on observed dynamics, and Stage 2 predicts solution evolution from the learned mesh. The key insight is that mesh allocation should be a learnable, regime-dependent generative problem rather than a fixed heuristic, yielding competitive performance especially when fixed grids are insufficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional neural PDE surrogates operate on pre-chosen grids, which implicitly fix the allocation of modeling capacity across space, resolution, and spectral bandwidth before any operator is applied. The authors argue this hidden design choice should itself be learnable, reframing the problem from "can a surrogate learn operators on a given mesh?" to "can a surrogate learn where resolution should exist before predicting field evolution?"

Method: A two-stage diffusion framework: Stage 1 learns an r-adaptive displacement mesh conditioned on observed dynamics using a diffusion model regularized by physics-aware proxy channels, geometric validity constraints, and local spectral concentration. Stage 2 predicts solution evolution from the mesh-informed representation. Adaptation remains physically interpretable and numerically legal through these constraints.

Result: Across five PDE regimes, diffusion-based learned discretization is competitive with adaptive-mesh and reduced-order baselines, with particularly strong gains in regimes where fixed or handcrafted allocation is insufficient. The approach learns regime-dependent allocation behaviors that reflect different spatial and spectral structures.

Conclusion: There is no universal optimal mesh rule; discretization should be learned in a regime-dependent manner. This reframes adaptive meshing for neural PDE solvers from a solver-specific heuristic into a generative representation-learning problem.

Abstract: Most neural partial differential equation (PDE) surrogates learn how fields evolve after a grid has already been chosen. However, before any operator is applied, the grid has already determined how modeling capacity is allocated across space, resolution, and spectral bandwidth. We argue that this hidden design choice should itself be learnable, leading to a question different from standard operator learning: can a surrogate learn where resolution should exist before predicting field evolution? We formulate adaptive discretization as a physics-constrained conditional generation problem over valid mesh displacements. The success of diffusion models in PDE field prediction suggests their potential for learning adaptive discretizations under similar structured constraints. This leads to a two-stage diffusion framework: Stage 1 learns an r-adaptive displacement mesh conditioned on the observed dynamics, while Stage 2 predicts the solution evolution from the mesh-informed representation. The mesh generator is regularized by physics-aware proxy channels, geometric validity constraints, and local spectral concentration so that adaptation remains physically interpretable and numerically legal. Across five PDE regimes, the results show that diffusion-based learned discretization is competitive with adaptive-mesh and reduced-order baselines, with particularly strong gains in regimes where fixed or handcrafted allocation is insufficient. The main conclusion is not that there exists a universal optimal mesh rule, but that discretization should be learned in a regime-dependent manner: different spatial and spectral structures favor different allocation behaviors. This reframes adaptive meshing for neural PDE solvers from a solver-specific heuristic into a generative representation-learning problem.

</details>


### [80] [Signal-Guided Optimization for Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11975)
*Xujia Li,Dan Li,Jian Lou,Wenjie Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GSUO introduces task-specific, fine-grained guidance signals for machine unlearning, addressing the limitations of uniform global strategies. It improves unlearning precision by calibrating the process based on sample memorization strength, outperforming 14 baselines across both random-subset and class-wise forgetting tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current machine unlearning methods use global, coarse-grained interventions that lack precise, differentiable guidance. Since samples have varying memorization strengths from original training, uniform strategies cause over-unlearning (harming utility) for some samples and under-unlearning (leaving exploitable residual information) for others.

Method: GSUO (Guidance-Signal-aware Unlearning Optimization) is a framework that designs task-specific, fine-grained guidance signals to steer the unlearning process. It supports both random-subset forgetting and class-wise forgetting tasks, providing differentiable guidance tailored to each unlearning scenario.

Result: Extensive experiments show GSUO outperforms 14 baselines in both unlearning effectiveness and generalization, while achieving high efficiency and significant speedups.

Conclusion: GSUO validates that fine-grained, guidance-signal-aware optimization is effective for reliable machine unlearning, overcoming the utility-privacy trade-off limitations inherent in coarse-grained approaches.

Abstract: Current machine unlearning methods predominantly rely on global, coarse-grained intervention strategies. They lack precise pilot signals to guide the unlearning process and fail to provide differentiable guidance across different unlearning tasks. Due to the varying memorization strengths of samples during original training, such a uniform strategy leads to two problems: some samples are over-unlearned, which harms model utility; while others are under-unlearned, leaving residual information that can be exploited by privacy attacks. In this paper, we propose GSUO, a guidance-signal-aware unlearning optimization framework that designs task-specific fine-grained guidance signals to steer the unlearning process and is applicable to both random-subset and class-wise forgetting tasks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GSUO outperforms 14 baselines in terms of both unlearning effectiveness and generalization, while achieving high efficiency and significant speedups, validating its effectiveness for reliable machine unlearning.

</details>


### [81] [LiteTopK: Exploiting the Curse of Dimensionality for a Fused Indexer-TopK Kernel in Long-Context Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11976)
*Ziqi Yin,Jianyang Gao,Peiqi Yin,Jiangneng Li,Gao Cong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LITETOPK is a fused Indexer-TopK kernel that leverages the curse of dimensionality (distance concentration in high-dimensional spaces) to estimate score ranges via sampling, partition candidates into bins online, and maintain a tight approximate threshold. This reduces global memory traffic, synchronization costs, and memory overhead while preserving exact TopK correctness, achieving 1.2x acceleration of GLM 5.2's prefill stage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GPU-based Indexer-TopK kernels (e.g., DeepSeek Sparse Attention) suffer from three key inefficiencies: excessive global memory traffic, costly synchronization, and prohibitive memory overhead. These bottlenecks limit performance in sparse attention for LLMs and vector retrieval in recommendation systems and vector databases.

Method: LITETOPK exploits distance concentration in high-dimensional spaces — where vector distances fall within a narrow range — through a three-stage approach: (1) sampling a small data subset to estimate query-data score ranges, (2) using these estimates to dynamically partition candidate results into bins during online processing, and (3) maintaining a tight approximate threshold that filters out unpromising candidates early, writing back only promising ones to reduce I/O and memory footprint. The method guarantees exact TopK correctness despite these approximations.

Result: LITETOPK accelerates the prefill stage of GLM 5.2 by 1.2× in real-world deployment scenarios while simultaneously reducing memory overhead compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: By exploiting the curse of dimensionality for score range estimation and adaptive thresholding, LITETOPK successfully addresses the memory bandwidth and synchronization bottlenecks in Indexer-TopK operations, delivering both performance gains and memory savings in production LLM deployments.

Abstract: Indexer-TopK, the operation to compute the scores and select the top-k candidates, is widely used by sparse attention kernels in large language models and vector retrieval in recommendation systems and vector databases. However, existing GPU-based Indexer-TopK kernels like DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA) remain inefficient due to excessive global memory traffic, costly synchronization, and prohibitive memory overhead. In this work, we exploit the curse of dimensionality in high-dimensional spaces, where distances between high-dimensional vectors tend to concentrate within a narrow range, to design LITETOPK, a novel and efficient fused Indexer-TopK kernel. LITETOPK first samples a small subset of data to estimate query-data score ranges, then uses these estimates to partition candidate results into bins online. This organization allows the LITETOPK kernel to maintain a tight approximate threshold, write back only promising candidates, reduce unnecessary I/O, substantially lower memory overhead, and still preserve exact Top-k correctness. Experimental results show that LITETOPK accelerates the prefill stage of GLM 5.2 by 1.2x in real-world deployment scenarios while incurring lower memory overhead.

</details>


### [82] [Gene Expression-Informed Jointly Controlled Generative Modeling for Precision Molecular Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11978)
*Hang Yuan,Chen Li,Wenjun Ma,Tadahiko Murata,Yuncheng Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JoPMol is a jointly controlled molecular generative model that unifies gene expression profiles, textual structural descriptions, and numerical chemical properties to generate optimized drug candidates under multiple conditions, outperforming state-of-the-art methods in precision molecular design tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precision molecular design requires simultaneous control over multiple conditions — biological relevance (cellular functional states under disease/perturbation), structural intentions (molecular design strategies), and property optimization. Existing methods fail to integrate these heterogeneous conditioning signals (gene expression, text, numerical properties) within a single unified framework.

Method: JoPMol integrates three conditioning modalities within a unified modeling framework: (1) gene expression profiles encoding biological states, (2) textual descriptions capturing molecular structure information, and (3) numerical values representing chemical properties. This enables coordinated generation and optimization of candidate molecules under joint condition control.

Result: JoPMol outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple evaluation metrics. It demonstrates strong generalization in both transfer tasks and biologically grounded simulation scenarios.

Conclusion: JoPMol effectively enables precision molecular design through joint condition control, validating the value of unifying heterogeneous biological and chemical signals for personalized drug candidate discovery. The code is publicly available.

Abstract: Precision molecular design aims to discover personalized drug candidates through joint control of multiple conditions, such as biological relevance and molecular design strategies. Biological relevance reflects cellular functional states under disease or perturbation conditions, while molecular design strategies provide complementary guidance in terms of structural intentions and property optimization. In this study, we propose JoPMol, a jointly controlled precision molecular generative model that integrates biological states encoded by gene expression profiles with molecular structure information expressed in text, and chemical properties quantified by numerical values within a unified modeling framework. This formulation enables coordinated generation and optimization of candidate molecules under joint condition control. Experimental results show that JoPMol outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple evaluation metrics. Moreover, JoPMol demonstrates strong generalization ability in both transfer tasks and biologically grounded simulation scenarios, validating its effectiveness for precision molecular design. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/hala-yh/JoPMol.

</details>


### [83] [Sparse Inter-Layer Dependencies of Transformer FFN Neurons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11990)
*Johannes Knittel,Hanspeter Pfister*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FFN neurons in Transformers can be explained by sparse subsets of upstream activations; a training-free attribution method reveals that small numbers of preceding neurons and attention outputs suffice to preserve downstream activations, with model perplexity remaining stable even when sparsity masks propagate through all layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FFN blocks dominate Transformer parameters and computation, yet their internal structure is poorly understood due to the additive superposition in the residual stream. The authors seek to understand whether individual FFN neuron activations can be decomposed into contributions from sparse upstream signals.

Method: The authors propose a training-free attribution method that estimates, for each target FFN neuron, the relative influence of every upstream neuron activation and attention output. They then mask all but the most influential upstream inputs (replacing masked inputs with their dataset-average values) and measure how well the target neuron's activation is preserved. This is extended to simultaneous masking across all layers to assess propagated effects on model perplexity.

Result: Small subsets of preceding activations and attention outputs preserve neuron activations with high fidelity. Effective sparsity increases further when accounting for inherent activation sparsity of upstream layers. When neuron-specific masks are applied simultaneously in all layers, model perplexity remains largely unchanged at moderate sparsity levels.

Conclusion: Despite dense parameterization, FFNs exhibit sparse and structured inter-layer dependencies at the neuron level. The proposed method offers a practical, scalable tool for circuit-level interpretability and identifies candidate sparse pathways relevant to efficient inference.

Abstract: Feedforward network (FFN) blocks account for a large fraction of the parameters and computation in Transformer architectures, yet their internal structure remains difficult to interpret due to the additive superposition induced by the residual stream. We examine whether the activation of an FFN neuron can be explained by a sparse set of preceding neuron activations and attention outputs. We introduce a training-free attribution method that estimates the relative influence of upstream neurons and attention outputs on a target neuron's activation. Empirically, across models and layers, we find that small subsets of preceding activations and attention outputs suffice to preserve neuron activations with high fidelity when all remaining inputs are masked with their average values. Effective sparsity is even greater when accounting for the inherent activation sparsity of upstream layers. Moreover, applying the neuron-specific masks in all layers simultaneously, such that the induced deviations propagate through the network, leaves model perplexity largely unchanged at moderate sparsity levels. These results demonstrate that, despite dense parameterization, FFNs exhibit sparse and structured inter-layer dependencies at the neuron level. Our method provides a practical, scalable tool for circuit-level interpretability and identifies candidate sparse pathways with potential implications for efficient inference.

</details>


### [84] [Evidence-Grounded Verified Agentic Reasoning: A Path Toward Eliminating LLM Hallucination in Empirical Inference via Tool-Attested Kernel Proofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12650)
*Junyu Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EG-VAR introduces a Lean 4-based tool-calling architecture where the Lean kernel serves as the sole authority for minting Verified claims. By structurally binding outputs to attested tool calls and kernel-checked proofs, the system eliminates unsupported claims entirely, achieving perfect source-faithfulness on numerical reasoning benchmarks while making residual errors and abstentions explicitly auditable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs equipped with tool access can still produce outputs unsupported by evidence and inferences that fail formal scrutiny. Current tool-calling frameworks lack governance guarantees: accepted outputs need not descend from attested evidence, and deductions need not be logically valid. This undermines trust in high-stakes empirical reasoning settings where claims must be auditable and verifiably grounded.

Method: EG-VAR is a Lean 4-based architecture with three core mechanisms: (1) The Lean kernel is the sole minter of Verified claims via tool-attestation axioms and declared source lifts, ensuring every verified output structurally descends from attested tool calls (Theorem 3.1) and kernel-checked valid inference (Theorem 3.2). (2) Residual outputs that cannot be verified are honest Abstain with replayable audit trails. (3) The LLM acts as a deployment-time formalizer, translating natural language problems into formal specifications. The system is evaluated on TableBench numerical reasoning (n=120) and counterfactual stress tests across 5 domains and 2 models.

Result: On TableBench numerical reasoning (n=120), EG-VAR achieves 120/120 perfect score versus 95% for a same-tool baseline lacking formal verification. On counterfactual stress tests (5 domains × 2 models), EG-VAR maintains 100% source-faithfulness while the same-tool baseline drops to 80-90% (no-tool baseline: 50-80%). Residual semantic-formalization error rates are 3.3% on Claude Sonnet and 1.7% on Claude Opus.

Conclusion: EG-VAR demonstrates a viable technical-governance interface for high-stakes empirical claims. The formal sidecar architecture makes target propositions, source scope, evidence boundaries, proof obligations, and abstention conditions fully auditable. It eliminates unsupported Verified outputs today while converting formalization errors, source-authority disputes, ambiguities, and abstentions into explicit audit targets. The authors propose that typed sidecars in datasets, APIs, and public records can amortize formalization overhead into reusable infrastructure over time.

Abstract: Tool access alone does not make LLM empirical reasoning governable: accepted outputs need not descend from attested evidence, and accepted deductions need not hold up under formal scrutiny. We present EG-VAR (Evidence-Grounded Verified Agentic Reasoning), a Lean 4-based tool-calling architecture in which the Lean kernel is the sole minter of Verified claims via tool-attestation axioms and declared source lifts. Every verified output structurally descends from an attested tool call (Thm. 3.1) and a kernel-checked chain of valid inference (Thm. 3.2); residual outputs are honest Abstain with a replayable audit trail. On a subcollection of TableBench numerical reasoning (n=120), EG-VAR attains 120/120 versus a 95% same-tool baseline; on counterfactual stress tests (5 domains x 2 models), EG-VAR stays 100% source-faithful while same-tool drops to 80-90% (no-tool 50-80%). With the LLM as deployment-time formalizer, residual semantic-formalization error is 3.3% on Sonnet and 1.7% on Opus. We position EG-VAR as a technical-governance interface for high-stakes empirical claims: a formal sidecar makes the target proposition, source scope, evidence boundary, proof obligation, and abstention condition auditable, eliminating unsupported Verified outputs today while turning formalization errors, lift and source-authority disputes, ambiguities, and abstentions into explicit audit targets. Over time, typed sidecars in datasets, APIs, public records, and AI-generated documents can amortize this formalization burden into reusable infrastructure.

</details>


### [85] [Mitigating The Effect of Class Imbalance in Data with Hierarchical and Dependable Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11994)
*Bipin Chhetri,Deepika Giri,Avishek Kadel,Rabin Kumar Karki,Akbar Siami Namin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hierarchy-aware RoBERTa outperforms SMOTE/ADASYN oversampling for imbalanced CWE vulnerability classification, achieving 0.76 weighted F1; synthetic interpolation violates taxonomic constraints and degrades deep learning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CWE vulnerability classification suffers from extreme class imbalance and strong hierarchical dependencies among categories. While SMOTE and ADASYN are common remedies for imbalance, their effectiveness in hierarchical CWE text classification is unexplored, and the authors hypothesize that synthetic oversampling may violate parent-child taxonomic constraints.

Method: A Hierarchy-Aware RoBERTa framework is proposed, incorporating CWE structural information via learnable parent-class embeddings to preserve taxonomic consistency. The method is compared against classical ML models and deep learning baselines (including BERT) with and without SMOTE/ADASYN augmentation, evaluated on a CWE Research Concept dataset using weighted F1-score.

Result: Hierarchy-Aware RoBERTa achieves a weighted F1-score of 0.76 without data augmentation, surpassing all baselines. SMOTE/ADASYN provide only marginal gains for classical ML models while consistently degrading deep learning architectures. Minority class performance improves notably, e.g., the "Class" category F1-score rises from 0.40 (BERT) to 0.60.

Conclusion: Hierarchy-aware representation learning offers a more principled and effective alternative to oversampling techniques for structured vulnerability classification, directly addressing taxonomic dependencies rather than relying on synthetic interpolation that disrupts hierarchical consistency.

Abstract: Classifying cybersecurity vulnerabilities using the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) taxonomy is challenging due to extreme class imbalance and strong hierarchical dependencies among weakness categories. Although oversampling techniques such as Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE) and Adaptive Synthetic Sampling (ADASYN) are widely adopted to mitigate class imbalance, their effectiveness for hierarchical CWE text classification remains largely unexplored. This paper proposes a Hierarchy-Aware RoBERTa framework that explicitly incorporates CWE structural information through learnable parent-class embeddings, preserving taxonomic consistency. Our experiments demonstrate that synthetic interpolation in high-dimensional embedding spaces violates the inherent parent-child constraints of the CWE hierarchy, offering only marginal benefits for classical ML models while consistently degrading deep learning architectures. Evaluated on a CWE Research Concept dataset, the proposed model achieves a weighted F1-score of 0.76 without data augmentation, outperforming all baselines with notable gains on minority classes, including the Class category whose F1-score improved from 0.40 to 0.60 over the BERT baseline. Our results suggest that hierarchy-aware representation learning is a more principled alternative to oversampling for structured vulnerability classification.

</details>


### [86] [Are we Merging the Right Models? Impact of Expert Training Duration on Model Merging for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11997)
*Nikita Kozodoi,Zainab Afolabi,Jack Butler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges the standard practice of merging multi-task models at their optimal validation loss, showing that optimal training duration depends on the merging method: simple averaging degrades with overfitting, while sparsification-based methods benefit from longer training. A bias-variance decomposition explains this, drawing parallels to random forests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard practice in multi-task model merging assumes that merging experts at their individual validation-optimal checkpoints yields the best merged model. The authors question whether this is universally true, hypothesizing that optimal training duration may interact with the choice of merging method—an underexplored relationship.

Method: The authors fine-tune models (Qwen 3.5 at 0.8B, 2B, 4B scales) on five domains (Math, Code, Instruction Following, Multilingual, Safety), saving checkpoints from 25% to 500% of optimal training steps. Five merging methods are evaluated at each duration. A bias-variance decomposition analysis is applied to formally characterize the interaction between training duration and merging strategy.

Result: A strong method-dependent pattern emerges: simple weight averaging suffers significantly when experts are overfitted, whereas sparsification-based merging methods (e.g., TIES-Merging, DARE) achieve peak performance well beyond the individual models' validation optima. The bias-variance analysis reveals that methods like averaging benefit from high-variance learners (analogous to random forests), explaining why longer training harms them.

Conclusion: Training duration and merging method should be jointly selected rather than treated independently. The conventional "merge at validation optimum" rule only holds for certain methods, and practitioners should tune both hyperparameters together for optimal multi-task model merging.

Abstract: Multi-task model merging combines separately trained expert models into a single model that handles all tasks without co-training. Standard practice merges experts at their optimal validation loss. We challenge this convention by systematically studying how training duration of domain experts affects the quality of the merged model. We fine-tune experts on five domains (Math, Code, Instruction Following, Multilingual, and Safety) across three model sizes (Qwen 3.5 0.8B, 2B, and 4B), saving checkpoints from 25\% to 500\% of the optimal training steps and evaluating five merging methods at each duration. Our findings reveal a striking method-dependent pattern: simple averaging degrades sharply with overfitting, while sparsification-based methods achieve their best performance well past the validation optimum. We formalize this through bias-variance decomposition analysis, drawing a parallel to random forests where averaging benefits from high-variance individual learners. These results suggest that training duration and merging method should be chosen jointly rather than independently.

</details>


### [87] [Institutional Equity Holdings Prediction Using Node Affinities of Dynamic Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12067)
*Emad Izadifar,Zahed Rahmati*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces NAVIS, a temporal graph machine learning model for predicting institutional portfolio allocations from SEC 13F filings, framing the problem as node affinity prediction on a bipartite manager-security graph. NAVIS achieves state-of-the-art NDCG of 0.9127, significantly outperforming dynamic graph baselines, though a simple EMA baseline (0.8882) surpasses most competitors, revealing the strong persistence in institutional holdings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting institutional portfolio allocations from 13F filings is challenging due to disclosure lags, reporting noise, and strong temporal persistence. No benchmark previously existed for applying temporal graph machine learning to this task, leaving a gap in reproducible evaluation frameworks for holdings prediction.

Method: The authors frame holdings prediction as node affinity prediction on a discrete-time temporal bipartite graph of managers and securities (99 managers, S&P 500 securities, 48 quarters, 2013–2025). They propose NAVIS (Node Affinity prediction using Virtual State) and benchmark it against multiple Temporal Graph Benchmark (TGB) dynamic graph models, both with and without domain-specific node features, plus heuristic baselines (EMA, Persistent Forecast).

Result: NAVIS achieves test NDCG of 0.9127 (0.9121 without features), outperforming all dynamic graph models and heuristics. EMA baseline scores 0.8882, surpassing all dynamic graph models except NAVIS. Domain-specific node features yield marginal gains (<1.2%), indicating temporal and structural signals already capture most predictable information.

Conclusion: The work establishes the first reproducible benchmark for temporal graph ML in institutional holdings prediction, demonstrating that NAVIS advances the state of the art while revealing that institutional portfolio persistence makes even simple methods surprisingly competitive.

Abstract: Institutional equity holdings disclosed in SEC Form 13F filings provide a rich temporal record of portfolio decisions by large investment managers. However, forecasting future allocations and modeling future demand remains challenging due to disclosure lags, reporting noise, and strong persistence in institutional behavior. We introduce the first benchmark for these tasks using temporal graph machine learning, framing holdings prediction as node affinity prediction -- i.e., forecasting portfolio weights -- on a discrete-time temporal bipartite graph of managers and securities extracted from preprocessed filings. On a sampled dataset comprising 99 managers and the S\&P 500 index (503 securities, 209,351 temporal edges across 48 quarters from 2013--2025), Node Affinity prediction model using Virtual State (NAVIS) achieves a state-of-the-art test Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (NDCG) of 0.9127 with features (0.9121 without), outperforming all dynamic graph representation learning competitors by a substantial margin, and outperforming all heuristic methods. Remarkably, a simple Exponential Moving Average baseline achieves 0.8882, surpassing all dynamic graph models except NAVIS and all heuristics except Persistent Forecast (0.8891), highlighting the strong smoothness and persistence of institutional portfolios. Domain-specific node features provide only marginal gains (<1.2\%), indicating that temporal and structural signals in the 13F ownership graph already capture most of the predictable information. By benchmarking a suite of Temporal Graph Benchmark (TGB) models under the node affinity prediction setting, both with and without features, on real-world 13F data, this work provides a reproducible foundation for temporal graph machine learning in holdings prediction and portfolio allocation.

</details>


### [88] [Sparse Autoencoders for Interpretable Out-of-Distribution Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12094)
*Ayush Karmacharya,Luke Luschwitz,Lucia Romero,Yanan Niu,Joseph Campbell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a post-hoc OOD detection method using sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to extract interpretable features from intermediate network layers, scoring samples via cosine similarity between their sparse feature activations and ID class means, achieving SOTA performance with interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing OOD detection methods focus on final-layer outputs but overlook hierarchical information in intermediate layers. Neural networks also suffer from overconfidence on OOD inputs, necessitating more reliable detection approaches that leverage richer internal representations.

Method: The method applies sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to learn interpretable, sparse features from intermediate-layer activations of a pretrained network. It computes an OOD score based on the cosine similarity between a test sample's sparse feature activations and the mean sparse activation vectors of in-distribution classes. Low similarity indicates OOD.

Result: The proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard OOD detection benchmarks.

Conclusion: Leveraging sparse features from intermediate layers via SAEs not only improves OOD detection accuracy but also provides interpretable insights into how distribution shifts affect learned representations.

Abstract: Reliable detection of out-of-distribution (OOD) samples is crucial for the safe deployment of machine learning models. Neural networks often produce overconfident predictions for inputs that deviate from their training data, leading to significant degradation in performance. While many OOD detection methods focus on the final output layer, they neglect the rich hierarchical information present in intermediate network layers. This paper introduces a novel approach that leverages sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to learn interpretable features from these intermediate activations. We find that in-distribution (ID) and OOD data activate distinct sets of these sparse features. We propose a new OOD score derived from the cosine similarity between the sparse feature activations of a test sample and the mean activations of ID classes.
