Our post-hoc detection method not only achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard OOD detection benchmarks, but yields interpretable insights into how distribution shift affects learned representations.

</details>


### [89] [Continual Learning with Elastic Regularization and Synthetic Replay for Federated MLLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12112)
*Jing Liu,Chenxuanyin Zou,Jiayang Ren,Gaoyun Fang,Chengfang Li,Yan Wang,Zhenchao Ma,Bo Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCMM is a federated continual learning framework for Multimodal LLMs that prevents catastrophic forgetting across three levels: modality-aware elastic weight consolidation (parameter), local generative replay (data), and task-similarity-aware gradient aggregation (aggregation). It outperforms baselines on accuracy and backward transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated fine-tuning of MLLMs faces catastrophic forgetting when adapting to sequential tasks in distributed, privacy-sensitive environments. This is critical for safety-sensitive autonomous systems (e.g., content moderation) where retaining prior knowledge is essential. Existing approaches lack holistic, modality-aware mechanisms to prevent forgetting across visual, linguistic, and cross-modal representations simultaneously.

Method: FedCMM introduces three complementary forgetting safeguards: (1) Parameter-level: modality-aware elastic weight consolidation computing separate Fisher information matrices for vision encoder, language backbone, and cross-modal projector for granular, asymmetry-aware protection. (2) Data-level: a lightweight local generative replay module synthesizes raw-data-free embedding-level multimodal replay tuples without raw data sharing. (3) Aggregation-level: task-similarity-aware gradient aggregation filters and reweights client updates via gradient cosine similarity to suppress conflicting directions and stabilize global learning.

Result: Extensive experiments on two benchmarks show FedCMM consistently outperforms recent baselines on both accuracy and backward transfer metrics, validating the effectiveness of holistic, modality-aware optimization for evolutive adaptation in heterogeneous networked AI deployments.

Conclusion: Holistic, modality-aware continual learning safeguards embedded into the federated optimization loop enable robust, privacy-sensitive adaptation of MLLMs in dynamic distributed environments, effectively mitigating catastrophic forgetting across modalities and supporting reliable deployment in safety-critical applications.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across distributed networks enables privacy-sensitive adaptation to evolving data streams, yet a fundamental obstacle prevents robust deployment in dynamic environments: catastrophic forgetting, wherein sequential task updates erase previously acquired knowledge across visual, linguistic, and cross-modal representations. Addressing this challenge is especially critical for autonomous networked AI operating in safety-sensitive domains, such as content moderation, where reliable retention of prior knowledge underpins system integrity. To overcome this, we propose Federated Continual Multimodal Learning (FedCMM), a framework that embeds continual-learning safeguards into the federated optimization loop at three complementary levels. At the parameter level, modality-aware elastic weight consolidation computes separate Fisher information matrices for the vision encoder, language backbone, and cross-modal projector, providing granular, asymmetry-aware protection against modality-specific forgetting. At the data level, each client trains a lightweight local generative replay module to synthesize raw-data-free embedding-level multimodal replay tuples without any raw data sharing. At the aggregation level, Task-similarity-aware gradient aggregation autonomously filters and reweights client updates by gradient cosine similarity, suppressing conflicting directions and stabilizing the global learning trajectory. Extensive experiments on two benchmarks demonstrate that FedCMM consistently outperforms recent baselines on accuracy and backward transfer, confirming that holistic, modality-aware optimization enables robust evolutive adaptation across heterogeneous networked AI deployments.

</details>


### [90] [An Agentic AI Scientific Community for Automated Neural Operator Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12122)
*Luis Loo,Ulisses Braga-Neto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An agentic AI scientific community of virtual labs, each with LLM planner, worker, and reviewer agents, autonomously discovers hybrid neural operator architectures across five PDE problems, demonstrating that LLM agency is essential for maintaining architectural diversity and that no single architecture universally dominates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural operator design typically relies on human expertise and manual architecture search. The authors aim to automate this discovery process using a multi-agent framework that mimics scientific communities, exploring whether autonomous, LLM-driven collaboration can discover high-performing, parameter-efficient neural operator architectures without human intervention.

Method: An AI scientific community consisting of multiple virtual labs operating under a citation-based economy of influence. Each lab contains three agents: an LLM planner (proposes architectures), a numerical worker (trains/evaluates), and an LLM reviewer (peer review). Labs share a vocabulary of five building blocks: DeepONet, Fourier, Transformer, wavelet, and residual CNN operators. Highly cited labs spawn new labs following their direction; poorly performing labs are replaced. Evaluated on five problems (piecewise regression, linear advection 1D, Burgers 1D, Navier-Stokes 2D, Darcy flow 2D) with three repetitions each, totaling 9,623 logged LLM calls. An ablation replaces LLM agents with rule-based alternatives.

Result: The community discovered high-accuracy, low-parameter-count neural operator architectures. LLM planners hybridized building blocks in 99.8% of decisions, consistently producing multi-family hybrids. The rule-based ablation caused the community to collapse to non-hybridized single-family stacks, confirming that LLM agency preserves architectural diversity. Results support a no-free-lunch theorem for neural operators: no single architecture universally wins across all problems.

Conclusion: Autonomous LLM-driven scientific communities can effectively discover hybrid neural operator architectures. LLM agency is critical for maintaining diversity and avoiding premature convergence to suboptimal single-family designs. The findings advocate for hybrid architectures and caution against one-size-fits-all neural operator solutions.

Abstract: We present an agentic approach to autonomous neural operator discovery based on an AI scientific community, which consists of a swarm of virtual laboratories that interact under a citation-based economy of influence. Highly-cited labs found new labs that follow their research direction and replace non-performing labs. Each virtual lab contains three agents: an LLM planner that proposes an architecture, a numerical worker that trains and measures it, and an LLM reviewer that participates in cross-lab peer review. All labs share a common vocabulary consisting of DeepONet (branch-trunk), Fourier, Transformer (attention), wavelet, and residual convolutional neural operator building blocks. We evaluate the neural operator AI scientific community on five problems, namely piecewise regression, the linear advection and Burgers 1D PDEs, and the Navier-Stokes and Darcy flow 2D PDEs, while repeating the simulation three times for each problem. The results show that the neural operator AI scientific community is capable of discovering high-accuracy, low-parameter-count neural operator architectures. All 9,623 LLM calls are logged and audited, which reveals that the virtual lab LLM planners choose to hybridize in 99.8% of their logged decisions, consistently returning multi-family hybrids. Moreover, we conducted an ablation study by replacing the LLM agents in each lab by rule-based alternatives, which caused the scientific community to collapse to non-hybridized single-family stacks in several cases, showing that LLM agency is needed to preserve diversity. The results suggest a no-free-lunch theorem for neural operators: there is no universal winner. The code, configurations, and the complete LLM transcripts are released at https://github.com/luislootx/AI-SC.

</details>


### [91] [From Geometric Recovery to Causal Validation: A Reproducible Audit of Sparse Autoencoder Features, from Superposition Geometry to Causal Inertness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12166)
*Mohamed Abdessalem Bal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that high cosine similarity between SAE decoder atoms and ground-truth features is an unreliable proxy for true feature recovery. Through causal audits (ablation and steering), up to 77% of high-similarity matches in degraded SAEs and 9% in well-trained ones are causally inert — the matched atom never fires when the feature is present. The authors provide sae-causal-audit, a model-agnostic auditing tool, decompose inertness into structural and competitive subtypes, and reveal dissociations between read-inertness and write-inertness via antipodal pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current SAE evaluation relies on correlational recovery metrics (cosine similarity between decoder atoms and ground-truth directions), which conflates decoder-geometry alignment with actual encoder-activation behavior. The authors argue this conflated metric masks whether recovered features are genuinely functionally meaningful, motivating a causal audit framework that probes whether matched atoms actually respond to feature presence and causally influence model behavior.

Method: The authors developed sae-causal-audit, a deterministic, model-agnostic pipeline. They reproduced the superposition phase diagram (Elhage et al., 2022) and the TopK-vs-L1 comparison (Gao et al., 2024). The core method subjects every recovered feature to two causal interventions: (1) ablation — removing the matched atom's influence and measuring downstream effect, and (2) steering — amplifying the atom to test behavioral sensitivity. Features passing a cosine ≥0.90 recovery bar are tested for causal efficacy. Inertness is decomposed into structural inertness (antipodal-pair geometry in good SAEs), competitive inertness (TopK pathology in degraded SAEs), and further into read-inertness (unmonitorable) vs. write-inertness (steerable). The audit was applied to both synthetic settings and a production SAE.

Result: Key findings: (1) Up to 77% of features meeting cosine ≥0.90 in degraded SAEs and 9% in well-trained SAEs are causally inert, including matches at cosine ~1.000. (2) Inertness decomposes into structural inertness (antipodal-pair geometry, present even in good SAEs) and competitive inertness (a TopK-specific pathology). (3) Read- and write-inertness can dissociate completely: five antipodal pairs are unmonitorable (zero ablation effect) yet steerable through the same atom, with steering specificities of 143–310. (4) On a production SAE, 14% of features are inert, with an atom-collision signal where a handful of atoms serve as nearest matches for dozens of unrelated concepts. (5) The authors identify a convergence artifact at high sparsity and a diffuse sharing regime at extreme overcompleteness in the superposition phase diagram.

Conclusion: Cosine similarity between decoder atoms and ground-truth directions is an insufficient criterion for evaluating SAE feature recovery; causal auditing reveals widespread inert features even at near-perfect cosine matches. The authors recommend adopting causal benchmarks as standard practice, report reproducibility as a stack of claims with explicit scopes (since byte-exact reproducibility is structurally unavailable), and provide sae-causal-audit as a reusable instrument for systematic SAE evaluation.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are the standard for decomposing superposed neural representations into interpretable features, and evaluation relies predominantly on correlational recovery metrics -- cosine similarity between ground-truth directions and decoder atoms. We show this conflates two distinct claims: decoder-geometry alignment and encoder-activation behavior. We reproduce the superposition phase diagram of Elhage et al. (2022), identifying a convergence artifact at high sparsity and an under-described diffuse sharing regime at extreme overcompleteness. We reproduce the TopK-versus-L1 comparison of Gao et al. (2024), with direct evidence of L1 shrinkage. Our central result is causal: subjecting every recovered feature to ablation and steering, we find up to 77% of features passing a recovery bar (cosine >= 0.90) in a degraded SAE -- and 9% in a well-trained one -- are causally inert: the matched atom never fires when the feature is present, including matches at cosine ~1.000. We package the method as sae-causal-audit, a model-agnostic instrument with a deterministic pipeline. Re-auditing refines the finding: inertness decomposes by cause into structural inertness (antipodal-pair geometry, present in good SAEs) and competitive inertness (a TopK pathology of degraded SAEs), and by direction into read- and write-inertness, which five antipodal pairs dissociate completely -- unmonitorable yet steerable through the same atom, with steering specificities of 143-310 attached to zero ablation effects. We document why byte-exact reproducibility is unavailable by construction, and propose reporting it as a stack of claims with explicit scopes. Applying the instrument to a production SAE reproduces the pattern at small scale (14% inert) and surfaces an atom-collision signal: a handful of atoms recur as the nearest match for dozens of unrelated concepts, replicated across three batches.

</details>


### [92] [Forgetful Attention: A Trainable Support-Vector Memory with Certified Selection and Exact Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12204)
*Vishwajith Ramesh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SV-Attention introduces a max-margin attention mechanism where token weights are support coefficients of a one-class SVM, enabling certified output-preserving eviction (tokens with zero weight) and exact, reversible deletion via an incremental solver. It achieves strong rare-item recall, surgical forgetting, and improved language modeling over sliding-window baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time attention memories cannot provably guarantee that removing a token leaves model outputs unchanged, nor can they exactly delete a token's influence. The authors aim to create an attention mechanism with formal certificates for output-preserving eviction and exact unlearning, motivated by applications in privacy (patient-record deletion), retrieval, and efficient memory management.

Method: SV-Attention frames attention as a one-class SVM with a fixed box parameter C. The active-set partition naturally assigns zero weight to reserve tokens, certifying that eviction does not alter output. A reversible incremental solver enables exact token deletion, recovering the identical state that retraining-without-that-token would produce, by reusing a maintained KKT inverse matrix in a custom backward pass. Training uses a separate stabilized batched approximation without the exact-deletion certificate.

Result: In fp64, decrement and refit recover identical partitions under unique optima; decision functions match to median deviation ~10⁻⁹ (10⁻¹³ on learned keys). Worst-case (~10⁻²) is confined to ill-conditioned duplicates. Certified selection achieves 0.86 vs. 0.32 rare-item recall and 0.80 vs. 0.05 deterioration hours on MIMIC-IV streams. On enwik8, a hybrid model reaches 2.178 BPC vs. 2.383 for a matched sliding-window Transformer (8.6% improvement, p=0.001 across seven seeds). TinyStories results are directionally positive but not significant (p=0.057). Training throughput reaches 9,125 tokens/s on a 3.22M-parameter model, 35.8× slower than MPS softmax.

Conclusion: SV-Attention provides the first attention mechanism with formal, verifiable certificates for output-preserving token eviction and exact influence deletion. It demonstrates practical utility in rare-item recall, surgical forgetting, and language modeling, though training speed remains substantially slower than standard softmax attention.

Abstract: Attention can be viewed as an online learner over context, yet existing test-time memories cannot certify that dropping a token leaves outputs unchanged or delete its influence outright. We introduce Support Vector Attention (SV-Attention), a max-margin memory whose weights are support coefficients of a one-class SVM with fixed box parameter C. Its active-set partition gives reserve tokens exactly zero weight, certifying output-preserving eviction; a reversible incremental solver deletes a token to recover the state produced by retraining without it under the same C. In fp64 experiments, decrement and refit recover identical partitions whenever the optimum is unique, and their decision functions match to a median deviation of about 10^-9 (10^-13 on learned keys); the 10^-2 worst case is confined to ill-conditioned duplicates and remains below coefficient decay in every regime. The exact path reuses the maintained KKT inverse in a custom backward. Training uses a separate stabilized batched approximation and does not carry the exact-deletion certificate; it reaches 9,125 tokens/s on a 3.22M-parameter model, while remaining 35.8 times slower than an MPS softmax reference. At matched budgets, certified selection reaches 0.86 vs. 0.32 rare-item recall and retains 0.80 vs. 0.05 deterioration hours on real MIMIC-IV streams. We also demonstrate surgical forgetting, exact editing, patient-record deletion, and a forgettable retrieval memory over real sentence embeddings. On enwik8, the hybrid obtains 2.178 BPC vs. 2.383 for a matched-state sliding-window Transformer across seven seeds (8.6% paired improvement, p=0.001); a three-seed TinyStories result is directionally positive but not significant (p=0.057).

</details>


### [93] [Speculate with Memory: Lossless Acceleration for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12236)
*Yu Li,Qinyuan Ye,Prafulla Kumar Choubey,Jiaxin Zhang,Chien-Sheng Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes memory-augmented speculative execution for LLM agents, where three online memory systems (contrastive transition table, episodic memory, confusion tracker) learn from past trajectories to improve prediction accuracy by 19-39% on action prediction and up to 2.5× on observation prediction, with zero added wall-clock cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speculators for LLM agents are stateless—they discard all information between tasks, preventing prediction quality from improving with experience. The authors identify this as a missed opportunity, since agent trajectories contain reusable patterns that could enhance future speculation.

Method: Three online memory systems augment the speculator: (1) a contrastive transition table that tracks action-sequence statistics using contrastive learning to distinguish productive from unproductive action transitions; (2) an episodic memory that retrieves contextually similar past trajectory segments to inform current predictions; and (3) a confusion tracker that identifies and suppresses recurring prediction errors. Memory accumulates continuously across tasks without retraining. Speculation runs during environment idle time, preserving losslessness.

Result: Memory-augmented speculation achieves 19-39% relative accuracy improvement on action prediction and up to 2.5× gains on observation prediction tasks with repetitive action spaces. Improvements grow continuously as memory accumulates and generalize across speculator models of varying cost. All gains come at zero added wall-clock cost with identical actor trajectories.

Conclusion: Online memory is a practical, lossless mechanism to substantially improve LLM agent speculation, with benefits that compound over time and generalize across model scales.

Abstract: Speculative execution accelerates LLM agents by using a smaller, cheaper model to predict and pre-launch the next step while the environment is idle. However, existing speculators are stateless and discard all information between tasks, preventing prediction quality from improving with experience. We equip the speculator with three online memory systems that learn from past agent trajectories: a contrastive transition table tracking action-sequence statistics, an episodic memory retrieving contextually similar segments, and a confusion tracker suppressing recurring errors. We evaluate this approach on six benchmarks spanning three speculation types: action prediction, observation prediction, and chained prediction. Memory-augmented speculation yields a 19--39\% relative accuracy improvement on action prediction and up to a $2.5\times$ increase on observation prediction tasks with repetitive action spaces. These gains grow continuously as memory accumulates and generalize across speculator models of varying cost. All speculation is lossless because it runs during idle time at zero added wall-clock cost, and the actor's trajectory is identical to non-speculative execution.

</details>


### [94] [Cluster-Weighted EDMD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12243)
*Lorenzo Tomaz,Judd Rosenblatt,Flavio Kicis,Thomas B. Jones,Diogo Schwerz de Lucena*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CW-EDMD introduces a soft clustering approach to EDMD that jointly learns data partitions and local Koopman operators via an EM objective, achieving dramatic improvements in prediction accuracy across multiple nonlinear dynamical systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A single global EDMD operator fails to capture heterogeneous dynamics across different regions of state space. The authors aim to replace one global linear Koopman approximation with multiple local operators, each specialized to a region where linear dynamics hold well.

Method: CW-EDMD employs an Expectation-Maximization framework that jointly learns: (1) a soft partition of phase space into clusters, and (2) a per-cluster EDMD operator. Crucially, the assignment of each data transition to clusters depends on both geometric proximity and prediction residuals, encouraging clusters to form where local Koopman models are accurate rather than merely where data are dense.

Result: Evaluated on Lorenz, damped pendulum, and Duffing systems across 36 configurations and 10 seeds (288 paired comparisons), CW-EDMD significantly reduced prediction error in 258/288 cases, with only 4 increases and 26 ties. Median one-step error reductions were 57× on pendulum, 2.7× on Duffing, and 12× on Lorenz.

Conclusion: CW-EDMD substantially outperforms global EDMD of matched degree by enabling region-specific Koopman approximations. The residual-guided soft assignment is key to this improvement, as it allocates model capacity where linear dynamics are most valid.

Abstract: Extended Dynamic Mode Decomposition (EDMD) approximates Koopman operators from data, but a single global operator is inefficient when different state-space regions exhibit distinct local dynamics. We introduce Cluster-Weighted EDMD (CW-EDMD), which jointly learns a soft phase-space partition and a per-cluster EDMD operator. Its Expectation-Maximization (EM) objective assigns each transition based on both geometric proximity and prediction residuals, so clusters specialize where local Koopman models are accurate rather than where the data are dense. On Lorenz, damped pendulum, and Duffing systems, across 36 configurations and 10 seeds, CW-EDMD improves matched-degree EDMD in one-step and 5s-rollout prediction. Across 288 paired comparisons, there are significant error reductions in 258 cases, increases in 4, and no differences in 26. Median one-step error reductions are 57x, 2.7x, and 12x on pendulum, Duffing, and Lorenz, respectively.

</details>


### [95] [Proximity Features: Privacy-Compliant Cold-Start Personalization at Airbnb](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12246)
*Wei Jiang,Bin Xu,Hui Gao,Bharathi Thangamani,Weiwei Guo,Sundar Srinivasavaradhan,Tracy Yu,Huiji Gao,Michael Kinoti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Airbnb introduces Proximity Features, a privacy-compliant system that groups ~1,000 geographically nearby users via geo-IP and adaptive clustering to generate aggregated behavioral signals, enabling personalization for users without individual history. Deployed in production, it yields significant booking lifts, especially for users with absent or stale history.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In two-sided marketplaces with infrequent, high-consideration purchases (like Airbnb), a large fraction of users — particularly logged-out, first-time visitors, and those arriving via paid channels — lack sufficient individual history for effective personalization. Privacy regulations and third-party cookie deprecation further restrict identifier-based tracking, creating a cold-start problem that traditional user-level features cannot address.

Method: Proximity Features uses geo-IP data to map users to approximate locations, then applies an adaptive clustering algorithm to form groups of roughly 1,000 nearby users. Aggregated user-level behavioral signals (e.g., booking patterns, destination preferences) are computed within each cluster. At inference time, a user is mapped to their cluster and served the corresponding aggregated features — no persistent individual identifier is required. The entire pipeline operates on consented, aggregated data within consent-gated privacy controls.

Result: Online A/B experiments show statistically significant lifts in bookings. The largest improvements are observed among users with absent or stale individual history. The system is deployed across multiple surfaces including marketing landing pages and destination recommendations, with email integration underway.

Conclusion: Geographic proximity-based user grouping provides an effective, privacy-by-design solution for cold-start personalization in two-sided marketplaces. The approach is production-viable, privacy-compliant, and delivers measurable business impact where traditional user-level features fail.

Abstract: Personalization in two-sided marketplaces relies heavily on user-level features, yet for platforms with infrequent, high-consideration purchases, a large fraction of users lack sufficient history for effective recommendation, spanning both paid and organic channels. At Airbnb, a substantial share of search requests comes from logged-out or first-time users, with this challenge especially pronounced on paid-channel landing pages, leaving traditional user-level features unavailable for a large fraction of traffic. Privacy regulations and increasing restrictions on third-party cookies further limit identifier-based tracking for non-essential use cases. This paper introduces Proximity Features, a privacy-compliant feature system that groups users by geographic proximity using geo-IP data and an adaptive clustering algorithm, producing aggregated user-level signals for groups of approximately 1,000 nearby users without requiring a persistent individual identifier at inference time. Privacy is preserved by design: the pipeline operates on consented, aggregated data only within consent-gated privacy controls.
  The system is deployed in production at Airbnb, serving multiple surfaces including marketing landing pages and destination recommendation, with engagement emails integration under way. Online A/B experiments demonstrate statistically significant lifts in bookings, with the largest gains observed among users with absent or stale history.

</details>


### [96] [Understanding Structured Health Data through Interaction-Aware Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12255)
*Ji Hwan Park,Ying Ding,Tianjin Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper explores using a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with interaction-aware routing to predict post-stroke rigidity from structured health records. While performance improvements are modest, routing attribution analysis reveals that different views (representations) of the data carry systematically different importance, highlighting that view construction — not just model architecture — is critical for interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether interaction-aware mixture-of-experts can improve post-stroke rigidity prediction and, more importantly, to understand how different multi-level views of structured health records contribute to model decisions, thereby advancing interpretable clinical ML beyond raw predictive performance.

Method: An Interaction-aware Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework applied to multi-level structured health records. Multiple "views" (representations at different granularities or feature subsets) are constructed from the same underlying data. The MoE router learns to weight these views per sample, and routing attribution techniques are used to quantify and analyze the systematic importance differences across views.

Result: The MoE approach yields only minimal performance gains over baselines for post-stroke rigidity prediction. However, routing attribution analysis demonstrates that different views exhibit systematic and interpretable differences in importance, validating that view construction is a key lever for model interpretability.

Conclusion: View construction is a critical design choice for interpretability in clinical prediction models. Even when performance gains are negligible, interaction-aware MoE routing can surface meaningful structure in how different data representations contribute to predictions, offering a pathway to more transparent clinical decision support.

Abstract: We study interaction-aware mixture-of-experts for post-stroke rigidity prediction using multi-level views of structured health records. Despite minimal performance gains, routing attribution reveals systematic importance differences across views, underscoring view construction as key to interpretability.

</details>


### [97] [From Many to Meaningful: Feature-Guided Zero-Shot Chronic Kidney Disease Screening Using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12260)
*Muhammad Ashad Kabir,Sirajam Munira*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates zero-shot LLMs for early CKD screening using a minimal set of clinically meaningful, community-accessible features. A feature-guided framework selects compact variables via ML analysis, serializes tabular records into text prompts, and evaluates four LLMs (LLaMA-3, Qwen-3, Mistral, GPT-4o-mini) across three international datasets. The selected feature subset consistently and significantly improved balanced accuracy over full feature sets, achieving screening-viable performance without any dataset-specific training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ML-based CKD screening methods are impractical for community and resource-limited settings due to their reliance on large labeled datasets, costly pathology tests, high-dimensional clinical features, and poor robustness to population/distributional shifts. The authors aim to explore whether LLMs can overcome these barriers through zero-shot inference, eliminating the need for training data and complex feature collection.

Method: A feature-guided zero-shot framework was proposed: (1) ML-based feature selection identifies a compact, clinically relevant subset of readily available community-based variables; (2) tabular patient records are serialized into standardized text prompts; (3) four LLMs (LLaMA-3, Qwen-3, Mistral, GPT-4o-mini) perform zero-shot CKD screening inference using both full and reduced feature sets; (4) evaluation is conducted on three heterogeneous CKD datasets from three countries to assess generalizability.

Result: The selected feature subset yielded consistent and statistically significant improvements in balanced accuracy and probability estimates across all models and datasets compared to the full feature set, achieving performance levels suitable for screening purposes.

Conclusion: LLMs can support clinically meaningful, training-free CKD screening using minimal community-accessible patient features, offering a practical complement to conventional ML methods in real-world, resource-constrained screening contexts.

Abstract: Early screening of chronic kidney disease (CKD) is essential for preventing irreversible progression; however, many machine learning (ML)-based screening methods remain difficult to deploy in community and resource-limited screening settings due to their reliance on large labeled datasets, resource-intensive pathology tests, or high-dimensional clinical features, and limited robustness to population and distributional shifts. This study examines the feasibility of using large language models (LLMs) for early-stage CKD screening in a zero-shot setting, without dataset-specific training. We propose a feature-guided zero-shot framework that evaluates LLM performance using a selected set of clinically meaningful, readily available community-based features, rather than exhaustive clinical inputs. Feature selection was guided by ML-based analysis to identify a compact, clinically relevant subset of variables. Tabular patient records were subsequently serialized into text using standardized prompt templates to enable zero-shot inference. The zero-shot performance of four LLMs (LLaMA-3, Qwen-3, Mistral, and GPT-4o-mini) was evaluated using both the full feature set and the selected subset. Generalizability was assessed across three heterogeneous CKD datasets spanning three countries. Across models and datasets, the selected feature set yielded consistent and statistically significant improvements in balanced accuracy and probability estimates, achieving performance levels suitable for screening purposes. These findings suggest that LLMs can support clinically meaningful, training-free CKD screening using minimal community-accessible patient features, offering a practical complement to conventional ML methods in real-world screening contexts.

</details>


### [98] [Saturation Makes Quantization Error Additive: A Coverage Model with a Certificate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12266)
*Joshua Hill*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges the premise that per-layer or pairwise sensitivity measurements can accurately reconstruct quantization loss for 4-bit deployment. Through Boolean cube analysis, it finds that 85-93% of loss variance is explained by per-layer effects alone, and proposes a coverage model that ranks configurations with <2% misordering. The model yields two efficient predictors requiring only L+1 parameters, achieving state-of-the-art allocation for models from 30B to 355B parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mixed-precision quantization methods like HAWQ and CoopQ assume that loss from quantizing layer sets can be reconstructed from isolated per-layer or pairwise sensitivities. The authors test whether this premise holds at aggressive 4-bit weight-and-activation precisions now being deployed, where interactions between layers may violate the additive assumption.

Method: The authors model quantization loss f(S) as a set function on the Boolean cube, decomposing it via two changes of basis (Fourier-Walsh/Hadamard transform and Shapley-type analysis). They measure the variance explained by per-layer vs. higher-order interaction terms. They then propose the coverage model f(S)=c(1−∏(1−a_i)), a monotone submodular function parameterized by L break-rates, and derive both an additive first-order predictor and the coverage predictor itself, each with L+1 parameters. Parseval's identity is used to certify the additive model's MSE against unexplained variance.

Result: 85–93% of loss variance is explained by per-layer effects alone. The coverage model reproduces the measured variance profile to within a few percent. The ranking of configurations is preserved with at most 2% pair misordering. As allocators at matched memory budgets, both predictors achieve lowest KL divergence compared to baselines on 30B–355B parameter models. Below 4-bit, allocations from these models continue solving code and reasoning tasks where gradient-sensitivity-based allocations fail to produce terminating generations.

Conclusion: The additive premise largely holds at 4-bit, but a multiplicative coverage model provides a better structural fit for quantization loss. The proposed framework not only yields practical, parameter-efficient allocators but also provides a rigorous certificate (via Parseval) of how well any additive model can perform, establishing a principled foundation for mixed-precision allocation in large language models.

Abstract: Mixed-precision quantization must decide which parts of a model to keep at higher precision. A common premise, shared by sensitivity-based methods such as HAWQ and CoopQ, is that the loss from quantizing a set of layers can be reconstructed from per-layer or pairwise sensitivities measured in isolation. We test this premise at the 4-bit weight-and-activation precisions now being deployed, treating the change in loss $f(S)$ from quantizing a layer set $S$ as a set function on the Boolean cube and analyzing it through two classical changes of basis. This analysis yields two findings. First, across configurations drawn from the deployment distribution, 85--93\% of the variance of $f$ is explained by per-layer effects alone. Second, a monotone transform of a sum of per-layer terms reproduces $f$'s ranking of configurations, misordering at most 2\% of pairs.
  We propose the coverage model $f(S)=c\bigl(1-\prod_{i\in S}(1-a_i)\bigr)$, which reproduces the measured variance profile of $f$ to within a few percent from its $L$ fitted break-rates. This structure supports two predictors of a configuration's loss, each with $L+1$ parameters. The additive model is the optimal first-order predictor. By Parseval's identity its mean-squared error equals the variance of $f$ left unexplained by per-layer effects, which we measure on full lattices, estimate out of sample at full-network scale, and report with every result as a certificate of how well any additive model can do. The coverage model itself is the second predictor.
  As allocators at matched memory, they attain the lowest KL divergence among the compared allocators on models from 30B to 355B parameters. Below four bits, the resulting allocations continue to solve code and reasoning tasks at budgets where allocations from gradient sensitivities no longer produce terminating generations.

</details>


### [99] [Track, Rank, Crack: Epistemic Working Memory Scales Multi-Hop Reasoning in Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12267)
*Ning Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Language agents degrade on long reasoning chains due to "context dilution," where key discoveries get buried in the context window. SLEUTH addresses this with structured epistemic working memory (Confirmed Facts, Active Hypotheses, Open Questions). It outperforms baselines by increasing margins as task difficulty grows (+5 to +11 points), and further gains come from a commitment trigger that only works when structured state exists, isolating epistemic organization—not raw model capability—as the key to scaling multi-hop reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language agents that chain reasoning with tool use perform poorly on long reasoning chains, even when individual steps are easy. The authors diagnose the root cause as "context dilution": the agent's investigative state (confirmed findings, suspicions, open questions) exists only implicitly in a growing context window, where early discoveries become buried under later retrievals. There is a need for an explicit, structured mechanism to maintain epistemic state throughout multi-hop reasoning.

Method: SLEUTH introduces structured epistemic working memory with three components: (1) Confirmed Facts grounded to specific sources, (2) Active Hypotheses ranked by supporting evidence, and (3) Open Questions that directly drive the agent's next action. The agent explicitly updates this state after each reasoning step. The paper also introduces a lightweight "commitment trigger" that prompts the agent to finalize an answer when evidence is sufficient, preventing unnecessary verification. Additionally, they test protocol adherence enforcement on weaker models to isolate the effect of structured reasoning organization.

Result: Across five multi-hop benchmarks and five baselines, SLEUTH's advantage scales with difficulty: +5 points on HotpotQA, +11 on 4-hop chains, surpassing Reflexion without requiring multiple episodes. The commitment trigger yields further gains but only when combined with structured state—applied to an unstructured agent, it produces no improvement. Enforcing protocol adherence on a weaker model recovers up to +19 points on the hardest problems, demonstrating that reasoning organization, not raw model capability, is the active ingredient.

Conclusion: How an agent organizes its reasoning—specifically, maintaining explicit structured epistemic state—is more critical than raw model capability for scaling multi-hop reasoning. The commitment trigger's dependence on structured state isolates this as a necessary condition for effective decision-making about evidence sufficiency.

Abstract: Language agents that interleave reasoning and tool use degrade sharply as reasoning chains lengthen, even when each individual step is easy. We trace this to context dilution: an agent's investigative state (what it has confirmed, what it suspects, and what it still needs) lives only implicitly in a growing context window, where early discoveries are buried under later retrievals. We introduce SLEUTH, which makes this state explicit and actionable through a structured epistemic working memory: the agent maintains Confirmed Facts grounded to sources, Active Hypotheses ranked by evidence, and Open Questions that directly drive its next action. Across five multi-hop benchmarks and five established baselines, SLEUTH's advantage grows with difficulty, from +5 points on HotpotQA to +11 on 4-hop chains, surpassing Reflexion without multiple episodes. Analyzing where the remaining gap lies, we identify the evidence sufficiency problem: agents often find the answer but fail to commit, exhausting their budget on needless verification. A lightweight commitment trigger fixes this, but only when the agent already maintains structured state: the identical trigger applied to an unstructured agent yields no improvement, isolating organized epistemic state as the necessary condition for effective commitment. Finally, enforcing protocol adherence on a weaker model recovers up to +19 points on the hardest problems, showing that how an agent organizes its reasoning, not raw model capability, is the active ingredient for scaling multi-hop reasoning.

</details>


### [100] [Quantum Port-Hamiltonian Neural Networks: Learning Conservative and Dissipative Dynamics via Measurement-Induced Nonlinearity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12269)
*Dibakar Sigdel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Quantum Port-Hamiltonian Neural Networks (Q-pHNNs), which leverage parameterized quantum circuits to learn classical dynamics while preserving structure (conservation, passivity) by design. Uses Isomorphic Hamiltonian Mapping: unitary gates for conservative dynamics and mid-circuit measurements with feedforward for dissipation. Demonstrates low energy drift, guaranteed energy monotonicity, and unsupervised damping-coefficient identification on pendulum and harmonic oscillator benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical physics-informed neural networks often enforce conservation laws and dissipative structure via penalty terms, which are soft constraints that can be violated. The authors aim to hard-code these structural properties (symplecticity, passivity, energy monotonicity) directly into the architecture of quantum circuits, yielding strictly structure-preserving learned dynamics.

Method: Proposes the Isomorphic Hamiltonian Mapping (IHM): the skew-symmetric interconnection matrix J maps to unitary gate evolution (conservative part), and the positive-semidefinite dissipation matrix R maps to Measurement-Induced NonLinearity (MINL) via mid-circuit measurement and classical feedforward (dissipative part). Four architectures are instantiated: (1) Quantum HNN for conservative energy manifolds using Parameter-Shift Rule to extract Hamilton's equations; (2) Q-pHNN with Born-rule measurement for dissipation; (3) Q-pHNN jointly learning energy ansatz and damping coefficient; (4) topology-entangled Quantum Graph Neural Network for N-node coupled-phasor networks.

Result: On nonlinear pendulum and damped harmonic oscillator: (i) 1.35% relative energy drift with symplectic integrator and scale correction; (ii) 100% energy monotonicity achieved by the MINL circuit; (iii) 12.1% error in damping-coefficient identification from vector-field snapshots without direct supervision on the damping coefficient.

Conclusion: The Q-pHNN framework successfully enforces conservation and passivity by construction in quantum circuits for learning classical dynamics, avoiding soft penalty constraints. Results demonstrate structural guarantees with competitive accuracy, suggesting quantum circuits as viable structure-preserving priors for physics-informed learning.

Abstract: We introduce Quantum Port-Hamiltonian Neural Networks (Q-pHNNs), a family of parameterised quantum circuits that learn classical dynamics in a structure-preserving manner. The framework relies on the Isomorphic Hamiltonian Mapping (IHM): the skew-symmetric interconnection matrix $\mathbf{J}$ corresponds to unitary gate evolution, and the positive-semidefinite dissipation matrix $\mathbf{R}$ corresponds to Measurement-Induced NonLinearity (MINL) realised via mid-circuit measurement and classical feedforward. This ensures conservation and passivity are enforced by construction rather than penalty terms. We instantiate the IHM in four architectures: (1) a Quantum HNN that learns conservative energy manifolds and extracts Hamilton's equations exactly via the Parameter-Shift Rule; (2) a Q-pHNN using Born-rule measurement for dissipation; (3) a Q-pHNN jointly learning the energy ansatz and damping coefficient; and (4) a topology-entangled Quantum Graph Neural Network for $N$-node coupled-phasor networks. Experiments on the nonlinear pendulum and damped harmonic oscillator demonstrate: (i)~$1.35\%$ relative energy drift with a symplectic integrator and scale correction; (ii)~$100\%$ energy monotonicity for the MINL circuit; and (iii)~$12.1\%$ error in damping-coefficient identification from vector-field snapshots with no direct supervision on the damping coefficient.

</details>


### [101] [A hybrid analytical-PINN model for subsurface simulation of geothermal heat exchangers in heterogeneous underground](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12271)
*Moke Rao,Thomas Hamacher,Smajil Halilovic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-informed neural network (PINN) framework is developed for heterogeneous soil thermal problems with borehole heat exchangers (BHEs). Singularities are removed via analytical line source models, explicit gradient thermal conductivity enables parametrized learning, and superposition yields a universal corrector. The method decomposes the temperature field into a homogeneous approximation and a learned heterogeneous correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modeling soil heterogeneity in BHE thermal problems is challenging due to singular delta-function sources and spatially varying thermal conductivity. Standard numerical methods struggle with singularity treatment and parametric variation. The goal is to develop an efficient, parametric, and singularity-free neural network approach that can serve as a universal corrector for heterogeneous effects.

Method: The temperature change is decomposed into a homogeneous analytical (line source) solution and a correction term governed by a diffusion/advection-diffusion PDE with homogeneous initial conditions. The delta singularity is thus removed analytically. Linearly varying thermal conductivity models heterogeneity, with its gradient explicitly formulated for physics-informed loss. A PINN is trained as a universal corrector for a single borehole with unit heat extraction rate, using adaptively selected training points across sampled conductivity parameters. A location indicator function for the source is included as an input feature. Superposition principles extend the corrector to multiple BHEs.

Result: Numerical tests based on three different analytical models demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. The location indicator function improves the network's ability to capture local information. The learned correction functions as an efficient universal corrector via superposition.

Conclusion: The proposed parametric PINN framework effectively handles heterogeneous soil thermal problems with BHEs by removing singularities analytically, enabling parametric learning through explicit conductivity gradients, and providing a reusable universal corrector. The approach shows promise for efficient thermal analysis of borehole fields in heterogeneous ground.

Abstract: In this paper, a parametric physics-informed neural network for solving the heterogeneous soil thermal problem with borehole heat exchangers (BHEs) as singular sources is developed. There are three novel features in the present framework; namely, (i) the singularity is naturally removed by using analytical line source models; (ii) using the explicit formulation for gradient thermal conductivity enables physics-informed learning of the parametrization featuring the conductivity; (iii) the learned correction is utilized as an efficient universal corrector via superposition principles. We first introduce the decomposition of the temperature change and transform the approximation of the entire heterogeneous response to the correction compensating the difference between the practical solution and idealized homogeneous approximation. In such a way, the delta function singularity is excluded and the bulk heat transfer is captured for the sake of facilitating the effective training of the neural network. The original problem is then reformulated as a governing correction diffusion or advection-diffusion equation subject to a homogeneous initial condition. The linearly varying thermal conductivity is used to model the soil heterogeneity. We propose a physics-informed neural network to approximate a universal corrector with respect to a single borehole with unit heat extraction rate. As a result, the network is trained by minimizing the physics-informed and data-anchored loss function that is evaluated for sampled conductivity parameters on adaptively selected training points. In addition, we include the location indicator function regarding the source as a feature input of network and find that it helps the network to process the local information. We perform numerical tests to exhibit the effectiveness of the proposed method based on three different analytical models.

</details>


### [102] [Gradient Flow Dynamics and Implicit Bias of Diagonal Linear Networks under Infinitesimal Initialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12332)
*Jiajie Zhao,Jianxing Wang,Junjie Yang,Zhiwei Bai,Yaoyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes gradient flow dynamics in diagonal linear networks under infinitesimal initialization, generalizing prior work to deep networks and a broader two-layer class. It shows training trajectories are captured by a proposed algorithm that converges to a modified ℓ₁-norm minimization solution, establishing that the implicit bias corresponds to a modified ℓ₁ norm. The authors identify the Structural Invariant Manifold (SIM) as the key geometric structure governing these dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the theoretical understanding of implicit bias in diagonal linear networks beyond the shallow case, characterizing how gradient flow under infinitesimal initialization induces sparsity-inducing regularization in both deep and broader two-layer architectures.

Method: The authors employ gradient flow analysis on diagonal linear networks, propose Algorithm 1 to equivalently characterize training trajectories, prove convergence to a modified ℓ₁-norm minimization problem, and leverage the Structural Invariant Manifold (SIM) framework to explain the geometric underpinnings of the dynamics.

Result: The training dynamics of both deep diagonal linear networks and the broader two-layer class are equivalently described by Algorithm 1, which converges to a modified ℓ₁-norm minimization solution. This establishes that the implicit bias in the infinitesimal initialization regime is a modified ℓ₁ norm for both architectures.

Conclusion: The implicit bias of diagonal linear networks under infinitesimal initialization is a modified ℓ₁ norm, generalizing previous results. The Structural Invariant Manifold provides the geometric mechanism underlying these dynamics, unifying the analysis across network depths and widths.

Abstract: We study the gradient flow dynamics of diagonal linear networks for regression tasks under infinitesimal initialization. Extending Theorem 1 from Pesme & Flammarion (2023), we generalize the analysis to both deep diagonal linear networks and a broader class of two-layer diagonal linear networks (as defined in Definition 4.1). Specifically, we demonstrate that the training trajectories of these models can be equivalently characterized by the proposed Algorithm 1. We further prove that this algorithm converges to the solution of a modified $ \mathcal{l}_1 $ norm minimization problem. As a result, we establish that the implicit bias of both network architectures corresponds to a modified $ \mathcal{l}_1 $ norm in the regime of infinitesimal initialization. Additionally, we provide insights into the underlying mechanisms governing these dynamics by identifying the Structural Invariant Manifold (SIM) (Zhao et al., 2026) as the key geometric structure that shapes the learning process.

</details>


### [103] [Generating Developable 3D Molecules via Pocket-Conditioned Diffusion and Property-Aware Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12349)
*Ruoxi Gao,Jiangweizhi Peng,Ziqi Chen,Frazier N. Baker,David C. Kombo,John L. Kane,Andrew A. Scholte,Yi Li,Matthew J. LaMarche,Luigi I. Iconaru,Hans-Peter Biemann,Mingyi Hong,Xia Ning*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: conDitar-dev is a conditional diffusion framework for structure-based drug design that generates ligands with strong binding affinity and favorable ADMET properties. It uses multi-scale pocket representations, a pocket-conditioned diffusion model, and a generation-time optimization module. The method outperforms baselines, and experimentally validated molecules showed μM-level binding to PD-L1 and CSF1R, with hit expansion yielding 200 nM selective CSF1R inhibitors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Drug discovery is costly and slow. Existing diffusion-based SBDD methods have three key limitations: (1) they tightly couple pocket and ligand representation learning, (2) they model interactions only at the atom level, and (3) they optimize primarily for binding affinity while neglecting ADMET and other developability properties. There is a need for a framework that simultaneously achieves strong binding and favorable drug-like properties.

Method: conDitar-dev comprises three modules: (1) msPRL — a pretrained multi-scale pocket representation learning module that captures hierarchical pocket features; (2) conDitar — a pocket-conditioned diffusion model guided by msPRL representations for ligand generation; and (3) paOPT — a generation-time optimization method that steers ligand generation toward favorable ADMET properties without post hoc filtering. The framework was benchmarked on a newly curated set of human disease targets and validated experimentally against PD-L1 and CSF1R.

Result: conDitar achieved an average binding score of -8.85 kcal/mol on the benchmark, outperforming state-of-the-art SBDD baselines. With paOPT, conDitar-dev improved ADMET property performance by up to 73% over conDitar alone. Experimentally, two conDitar-dev-generated PD-L1 molecules showed SPR-derived KD values of 3.49 and 3.75 μM. Hit expansion identified selective CSF1R inhibitors with IC50 as low as 200 nM and revealed drug repurposing opportunities.

Conclusion: conDitar-dev effectively bridges the gap between computational molecular generation and experimental validation by jointly optimizing binding affinity and developability. The successful identification of active compounds against PD-L1 and CSF1R demonstrates its practical utility in real-world drug discovery campaigns, including hit identification and drug repurposing.

Abstract: Drug discovery and development is time-consuming and resource-intensive, motivating computational approaches such as diffusion models for de novo drug design. Many such models follow the structure-based drug design (SBDD) paradigm, generating molecules to fit a target binding pocket. However, existing diffusion-based SBDD methods typically couple pocket and ligand representation learning, model interactions only at the atom level, and prioritize binding affinity over other developability properties. Here, we introduce conDitar-dev, a conditional diffusion-based SBDD framework for generating ligands with strong binding affinities and favorable ADMET properties. It consists of three modules: msPRL, a pretrained multi-scale pocket representation learning module; conDitar, a pocket-conditioned diffusion model guided by msPRL representations; and paOPT, a generation-time method for optimizing ligand developability. On a newly curated benchmark of human disease targets, conDitar outperforms state-of-the-art SBDD baselines, achieving an average binding score of -8.85 kcal/mol. Across five ADMET properties, conDitar-dev improves performance by up to 73% over conDitar. To further validate the abilities of conDitar-dev to generate developable molecules, we have applied it to two validated druggable targets: programmed death-ligand 1 (PD-L1) and colony-stimulating factor 1 receptor (CSF1R) proteins. Top-ranked generatively designed molecules and their analogs have been experimentally synthesized and biologically tested. Two molecules generated directly by conDitar-dev for PD-L1 exhibited SPR-derived $K_D$ values of 3.49 and 3.75 $μ$M, respectively. Hit expansion based on conDitar-dev-designed molecules identified selective CSF1R inhibitors with IC$_{50}$ values as low as 200 nM, while also uncovering opportunities for drug repositioning.

</details>


### [104] [Reducing information dependency does not cause training data privacy. Adversarially non-robust features do](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12354)
*Rasmus Torp,Shailen K. Smith,Adam Breuer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper challenges the assumption that rote memorization causes training data exposure in image reconstruction attacks. It shows that adversarial robustness, not memorization, drives reconstruction vulnerability, introducing Anti Adversarial Training (AT-AT) that intentionally learns non-robust features to achieve both better privacy defense and higher accuracy, revealing a privacy-robustness tradeoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevailing view holds that information dependency (rote memorization) is the primary driver of training data exposure under Model Inversion Attacks (MIAs). The authors challenge this by presenting three counterintuitive empirical findings: (1) defenses that reduce MIA success do not lower standard measures of information dependency (HSIC); (2) models that maximally memorize training data remain MIA-robust; and (3) models trained with 97% of pixels withheld—where privacy guarantees are theoretically strong—can still be reconstructed. These contradictions motivate a deeper investigation into what actually causes reconstruction vulnerability.

Method: The authors first present three empirical observations contradicting the memorization-driven exposure hypothesis. They then provide causal evidence linking MIA vulnerability to adversarial robustness, specifically showing that privacy under MIA stems from "non-robust" features (generalizable but imperceptible and unstable). They demonstrate that recent MIA defenses inadvertently shift models toward non-robust features. To establish causality, they introduce Anti Adversarial Training (AT-AT), a training regime that deliberately learns non-robust features, achieving superior reconstruction defense while maintaining higher accuracy compared to state-of-the-art defenses.

Result: Three key findings: (1) MIA defenses do not reduce HSIC; (2) maximally memorizing models resist MIA; (3) models trained on only 3% of pixels remain highly vulnerable to MIA. AT-AT outperforms existing defenses in both privacy protection and accuracy, establishing a causal link between non-robust features and reconstruction defense, and revealing a new privacy-robustness tradeoff.

Conclusion: Training data exposure under MIAs is not driven by rote memorization but by adversarial robustness. Non-robust features are the causal mechanism behind reconstruction vulnerability, and intentionally learning them via AT-AT yields superior privacy-accuracy tradeoffs. This revises the foundational understanding of training data exposure and uncovers a previously unrecognized privacy-robustness tradeoff.

Abstract: In this paper, we challenge the prevailing view that information dependency (including rote memorization) drives training data exposure to image reconstruction attacks. We show that extensive exposure can persist without rote memorization and is instead caused by a tunable connection to adversarial robustness. We begin by presenting three surprising results: (1) recent defenses that inhibit reconstruction by Model Inversion Attacks (MIAs), which evaluate leakage under an idealized attacker, do not reduce standard measures of information dependency (HSIC); (2) models that maximally memorize their training datasets remain robust to MIA reconstruction; and (3) models trained without seeing 97% of the training pixels, where recent information-theoretic bounds give arbitrarily strong privacy guarantees under standard assumptions, can still be devastatingly reconstructed by MIA.
  To explain these findings, we provide causal evidence that privacy under MIA arises from what the adversarial examples literature calls ``non-robust'' features (generalizable but imperceptible and unstable features). We further show that recent MIA defenses obtain their privacy improvements by unintentionally shifting models toward such features. To establish this causal relationship, we introduce Anti Adversarial Training (AT-AT), a training regime that intentionally learns non-robust features to obtain both superior reconstruction defense and higher accuracy than state-of-the-art defenses. Our results revise the prevailing understanding of training data exposure and reveal a new privacy-robustness tradeoff.

</details>


### [105] [Same Loss, Same Noise, Opposite Schedules: Noise Structure and Optimizer Normalization Jointly Determine Whether Learning-Rate Cooldown Helps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12360)
*Subham Singh,Ashutosh Mishra,Subha Raut*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a theoretical account of when the cooldown phase of a warmup-stable-decay (WSD) learning-rate schedule reduces final training loss. The key insight: cooldown helps for normalized optimizers (like signSGD/normalized SGD) under multiplicative noise by escaping a noise floor of order η², but does nothing for plain SGD, which self-anneals. The authors derive closed-form stationary laws, extend results to momentum and heavy-tailed noise, and validate with simulations and a real classification task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The WSD learning-rate schedule is now standard in large-model pretraining, yet the cooldown phase's effectiveness is inconsistent — it helps in some settings and is irrelevant in others. No existing theory explains this empirical dichotomy. The paper aims to provide a provable, mechanistic account of when and why cooldown matters.

Method: The authors analyze stochastic gradient methods on strongly convex objectives with multiplicative (gradient-proportional) noise. They derive that plain SGD contracts geometrically at constant LR (no cooldown benefit). For signSGD, they solve the stationary distribution on a quadratic exactly in closed form, revealing an η² noise floor. They extend the floor to normalized SGD in d>1 via a scale-invariance argument, prove a local dissociation under (L₀,L₁)-smoothness, and establish robustness to momentum and heavy-tailed noise. Predictions are validated via simulation and a real classification task with directly measured gradient noise.

Result: Plain SGD with multiplicative noise contracts geometrically and needs no cooldown. Sign-based and normalized methods settle on a noise floor of order η²; the floor constant for signSGD is obtained in closed form. Additive noise reinstates a floor for all methods. The mechanism is elementary: SGD steps shrink with the gradient (self-annealing), while normalized steps maintain unit scale. The noise-regime diagnostic on a real classification task confirms theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: Whether cooldown helps is determined by two properties: gradient noise structure (multiplicative vs. additive) and whether the optimizer normalizes its updates. For normalized optimizers under multiplicative noise, cooldown is necessary to escape the noise floor; for plain SGD, it is redundant. The interior cooldown fraction used in practice lies outside stationary landscape-and-noise geometry, suggesting further theoretical work is needed to fully characterize optimal cooldown scheduling.

Abstract: The cooldown phase of a warmup-stable-decay (WSD) learning-rate schedule, now a default in large-model pretraining, lowers the final training loss in some settings and does nothing in others. We give a provable account of which case obtains, and it turns on two properties together: the structure of the gradient noise and whether the optimizer normalizes its update. On a strongly convex objective with multiplicative (gradient-proportional) noise, stochastic gradient descent contracts geometrically at a constant learning rate, so cooldown has nothing to improve. Under the same objective and noise, sign-based and normalized methods, the standard surrogates for adaptive optimizers, settle on a noise floor of order $η^2$ and reach the minimizer only as the learning rate is driven to zero; any additive noise then reinstates a floor for every method. The mechanism is elementary: an SGD step shrinks in proportion to the gradient and so anneals itself, whereas a normalized step keeps unit scale and cannot. We solve the signSGD stationary law on the quadratic exactly and obtain the floor constant in closed form, prove a local form of the dissociation under $(L_0,L_1)$-smoothness, extend the floor to normalized SGD in dimension d>1 by a scale-invariance argument, and establish robustness to momentum and heavy-tailed noise. Simulation confirms every prediction, and we demonstrate the resulting noise-regime diagnostic on a real classification task with directly measured gradient noise. The mechanism explains whether cooldown helps; the interior cooldown fraction used at scale lies outside stationary landscape-and-noise geometry.

</details>


### [106] [SinAE: A Single-Architecture Flow-Matching Autoencoder for Cross-Domain Atomic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12380)
*Yuxuan Ren,Fan Yang,Jianhua Yao,Yatao Bian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SinAE is a single-architecture flow-matching autoencoder that unifies molecular, crystal, and protein generation using a vanilla Transformer with no domain-specific operators, achieving near-lossless reconstruction via an iterative decoder and enabling cross-domain transfer through a shared atomic latent space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative pipelines for small molecules, crystals, and proteins remain fragmented with domain-specific architectures (graph, equivariant, frame-based). Cross-domain training could alleviate per-domain data scarcity, but no prior latent autoencoder is simultaneously lossless and architecturally general across all three domains. Handling heterogeneous structural priors across domains in 3D coordinate space presents a fundamental challenge.

Method: SinAE employs a vanilla Transformer encoder-decoder architecture without any equivariant, graph, or domain-specific operators. The key innovation is shifting the reconstruction burden from the encoder to an iterative flow-matching decoder, which progressively refines outputs. A single per-token latent representation is fed into a standard Diffusion Transformer prior for generation across all three domains.

Result: SinAE achieves near-lossless reconstruction across molecules, crystals, and proteins, reducing reconstruction errors by orders of magnitude compared to prior latent baselines. The Diffusion Transformer prior attains strong performance on generation benchmarks for all three domains. Joint training on molecules and crystals strictly improves both domains, providing direct evidence of cross-domain transfer through the shared atomic latent representation.

Conclusion: A single, simple Transformer architecture without domain-specific inductive biases can serve as a unified generative framework across molecules, crystals, and proteins. Cross-domain training is not only feasible but mutually beneficial, suggesting that shared atomic latent representations enable effective transfer learning across previously siloed scientific domains.

Abstract: Small molecules, crystals, and proteins all reduce to atoms in 3D space, yet their generative pipelines remain fragmented across domains, each with its Small molecules, crystals, and proteins all reduce to atoms in 3D space, yet their generative pipelines remain fragmented across domains, each with its own graph, equivariant, or frame-based architecture. Cross-domain training would mitigate per-domain data scarcity, but direct generation in 3D coordinate space cannot easily handle the heterogeneous structural priors of all three domains, and no prior latent autoencoder is simultaneously lossless and architecturally general across all three. We introduce SinAE, a single-architecture flow-matching autoencoder for molecules, crystals, and proteins, with vanilla Transformer encoder and decoder and no equivariant, graph, or domain-specific operators. Rather than requiring the encoder to capture fine-grained geometry, SinAE shifts the reconstruction burden into an iterative flow-matching decoder, achieving near-lossless reconstruction across domains and reducing reconstruction errors by orders of magnitude relative to prior latent baselines. The same per-token latent supports a standard Diffusion Transformer prior that reaches strong performance on molecular, crystal, and protein generation benchmarks. Joint molecule--crystal training strictly improves both domains, providing direct evidence of cross-domain transfer through a shared atomic latent. Code is available at https://github.com/BlueWhaleLab/SinAE .

</details>


### [107] [Differentiable Clone-Structured Causal Graphs for End-to-End Cognitive Map Learning from Image Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12382)
*Arash Nikzad,Sasan Sarbishegi,Ali Dasmeh,Muhammad Asif,Parsa Gharavi,Erik Husom,Sagar Sen,Andrew B. Lehr,Olivier Penacchio,Ana Clemente,Tristan M. Stöber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces gradCSCG, a fully differentiable reformulation of the Clone-Structured Causal Graph (CSCG) algorithm, coupled with a VQ-VAE perceptual front-end to enable end-to-end learning of structured cognitive maps directly from raw image sequences, without requiring a predefined discrete alphabet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CSCG is a normative hippocampus model that learns interpretable maps from aliased observations, but it requires a predefined discrete symbol alphabet and uses expectation-maximization, making it incompatible with gradient-based neural network training and incapable of end-to-end processing of raw sensory data like images.

Method: The authors reformulate CSCG as gradCSCG, a single differentiable module, and combine it with a learned VQ-VAE perceptual front-end. A soft emission forward pass enables gradients from the map-learning objective to propagate into perception. Loss-balancing mechanisms are introduced to prevent module collapse during joint training. The pipeline is evaluated on symbolic grid-worlds and MNIST image sequences across four heavily aliased environments.

Result: gradCSCG reproduces CSCG's results on symbolic grid worlds, recovering room topology from heavily aliased observations. On MNIST image sequences—where each visit to a location yields a newly sampled digit image—the end-to-end pipeline successfully uncovers the underlying adjacency graph with high edge precision and recall, directly from visual input.

Conclusion: gradCSCG serves as a proof of principle that CSCG can function as a composable building block within deep learning architectures, enabling end-to-end learning of structured cognitive maps from raw perceptual streams.

Abstract: How can an agent build a structured map of its world from nothing but an ongoing sequence of raw sensory input and its own movements, especially when natural variation means exact sensory patterns rarely repeat? The Clone-Structured Causal Graph algorithm (CSCG), a normative hippocampus model, shows how an interpretable map can be learned from aliased observations. However, CSCG requires a predefined discrete alphabet, and its expectation-maximization formulation is not easily combined with existing neural network modules, preventing the end-to-end processing of raw image sequences. We remove this barrier by reformulating CSCG as a single, fully differentiable module, gradCSCG, and coupling it to a learned vector-quantized variational autoencoder (VQ-VAE) perceptual front-end. A soft emission forward pass allows the map-learning objective to flow back into perception, while a set of loss-balancing mechanisms mitigates module collapse during joint training. We demonstrate, first, that gradient training reproduces CSCG's results on original symbolic grid worlds by recovering room topology from heavily aliased observations. Second, we show that map recovery remains robust on MNIST image sequences, where each visit to a location yields a newly sampled image of its assigned digit. Across four heavily aliased environments, the end-to-end pipeline successfully uncovers the underlying adjacency graph with high edge precision and recall, directly from visual input. This work provides a proof of principle that CSCG can serve as a composable building block in a deep learning architecture.

</details>


### [108] [ReDiTT: Retrieval Augmented Conditional Diffusion Transformers for Asynchronous Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12391)
*Saiyue Lyu,Zhitian Zhang,Ruizhi Deng,Thibaut Durand*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReDiTT is a retrieval-augmented diffusion transformer that operates in latent space for asynchronous time series prediction, using structurally similar retrieved sequences as conditional guidance to improve next-event and long-horizon forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Asynchronous time series prediction faces inherent uncertainty in forecasting future inter-event times and event types. Existing methods struggle with long-horizon stability and sample diversity. The authors aim to address these limitations by providing global structural guidance during generation.

Method: ReDiTT operates in three stages: (1) an encoder maps asynchronous event sequences into a latent space; (2) a retrieval module fetches structurally similar latent sequences from a memory bank; (3) a conditional diffusion transformer denoises latent representations using cross-attention to incorporate the retrieved sequences as reference conditions. Retrieval occurs during both training and inference, providing global temporal guidance that stabilizes the generative process.

Result: Experiments on seven real-world datasets show state-of-the-art performance on both next-event prediction and long-horizon forecasting tasks, with improved sample diversity and forecasting stability.

Conclusion: Retrieval-augmented conditioning in latent diffusion models effectively captures global temporal structures, yielding significant improvements in asynchronous time series forecasting. The approach is general and the code is publicly released.

Abstract: We present a diffusion based model for asynchronous time series prediction, where the goal is to predict the next inter event time and event type. To address the inherent uncertainty of future events, we introduce ReDiTT, a retrieval augmented conditional diffusion transformer that operates in latent space. ReDiTT retrieves structurally similar latent sequences from a memory bank during both training and inference and incorporates them as reference conditions through cross attention. This retrieval based conditioning allows the model to attend to relevant temporal dynamics and provides global structural guidance for generation. As a result, ReDiTT stabilizes long horizon forecasting and improves sample diversity. Experiments on seven real world datasets demonstrate state of the art performance on next event prediction and long horizon forecasting. Our code is available at https://github.com/BorealisAI/ReDiTT.

</details>


### [109] [Mechanical Analysis of Parachute Suspension Line Deployment with Binding Tapes Using PINN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12409)
*Xiang Zhao,Ronghui Quan,Yaqi Xiao,Junlin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) for rapidly predicting dynamic tension at arbitrary positions along parachute suspension lines during the extraction and straightening phase, outperforming traditional ODE integration methods in efficiency and accuracy, and investigates how binding tape parameters regulate line tension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing numerical integration methods for calculating suspension line tension during parachute deployment cannot rapidly acquire tension values at arbitrary positions along the lines, creating a need for a faster, more flexible prediction approach for this critical ultra-short dynamic process.
