Method: A Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) framework is developed that embeds the governing physics of suspension line extraction and straightening into the neural network training process, enabling efficient tension prediction at arbitrary line positions. Comparative validation is conducted against flight test data and conventional numerical results. Additionally, the regulatory law of binding tape parameters on dynamic tension is systematically investigated.

Result: The proposed PINN algorithm outperforms traditional numerical integration methods in both computational efficiency and numerical accuracy. The reliability and effectiveness of the PINN framework are verified through comparative validations against flight test data and conventional numerical results.

Conclusion: The PINN-based approach provides a superior alternative to traditional ODE integration for parachute suspension line tension analysis, offering rapid, accurate predictions at arbitrary positions and revealing the regulatory effects of binding tape parameters on dynamic tension during the critical extraction and straightening phase.

Abstract: Parachutes are widely utilized in aviation, aerospace and lifesaving missions. As the initial stage of parachute deployment, suspension line extraction and straightening directly determines the smooth implementation of subsequent inflation procedures. This ultra-short process involves intricate dynamic load variations. Most existing studies adopt numerical integration of ordinary differential equations to calculate line tension, yet this method fails to rapidly acquire tension values at arbitrary positions along suspension lines. This paper develops a physics-informed neural network (PINN) algorithm for tension prediction during line extraction and straightening, which outperforms traditional integration methods in both computational efficiency and numerical accuracy. Furthermore, the regulatory law of binding tape parameters on line dynamic tension is investigated. Comparative validations against flight test data and conventional numerical results verify the reliability and effectiveness of the proposed PINN framework.

</details>


### [110] [PolarBM: Complex-valued Boltzmann Machine for Modeling Audio Signals in Polar and Log-polar Coordinates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12417)
*Toru Nakashika,Kohei Yatabe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces PolarBM and LogPolarBM, novel Boltzmann machines that natively handle complex-valued data in polar coordinates by modeling the amplitude-phase dependency. LogPolarBM's conditional distribution generalizes several well-known distributions. Restricted variants (RBMs) outperform conventional models, including deep neural networks, on audio signal modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex-valued data (e.g., audio spectra) inherently couples amplitude and phase, yet conventional ML methods treat them as real-valued, discarding this structural relationship. Existing approaches lack a principled probabilistic framework that preserves the natural polar-coordinate dependency.

Method: The authors define probability density functions over complex variables in polar form where phase explicitly depends on amplitude. PolarBM operates on linear amplitude; LogPolarBM extends this to logarithmic amplitude, yielding a power-weighted noncentral complex Gaussian (PW-NCCG) distribution whose marginal amplitude subsumes Rice, Nakagami, and noncentral chi distributions. Restricted variants (PolarRBM, LogPolarRBM) are introduced for practical use.

Result: The proposed RBMs achieve superior modeling accuracy compared to conventional models, including deep neural networks, by explicitly capturing amplitude-phase dependency in audio signals.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling the amplitude-phase relationship yields significant gains in complex-domain probabilistic modeling. The framework extends beyond audio to wireless communications, quantum mechanics, and other fields involving complex-valued data.

Abstract: Although vast amounts of data, such as audio signal spectra, are naturally represented using complex numbers, conventional machine learning methods often simplify complex-domain problems by employing frameworks designed for real-valued variables. While this simplification offers computational benefits, it discards structural information regarding the inherent relationship between amplitude and phase. In this paper, we propose a novel Boltzmann machine (BM), named PolarBM, capable of naturally handling complex-valued variables in the polar coordinate (i.e., an amplitude-phase representation). PolarBM defines a probability density function for complex variables in which the phase explicitly depends on the amplitude, thereby capturing the physically important relationships of complex-valued signals. Furthermore, to process audio signals in accordance with human auditory perception, we propose LogPolarBM, which models amplitude on a logarithmic scale. This extension yields a flexible conditional probability density function, a power-weighted noncentral complex Gaussian (PW-NCCG) distribution, whose marginal amplitude distribution encompasses the Rice, Nakagami, and noncentral chi distributions as special cases. For practical applications, we also introduce the restricted variants of these proposed models: PolarRBM and LogPolarRBM. Experimental results demonstrate that by explicitly modeling the dependency between amplitude and phase, the proposed RBMs achieve superior modeling accuracy compared to conventional models, including deep neural networks. Although our experiments focus on audio signals, the utility of the proposed BMs is not limited to audio applications; their potential extends widely across various fields of science and engineering that involve complex-valued data, such as wireless communications and quantum mechanics.

</details>


### [111] [Fisher Rank Inflation: A Spectral Signature of Memorization under Label Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12438)
*Satwik Bathula,Anand A. Joshi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep networks exhibit a spectral signature called Fisher Rank Inflation during label-noise memorization: the effective rank of the per-example gradient scatter matrix transiently expands when fitting corrupted labels, then contracts. This enables detecting corrupted examples and the structure-to-memorization transition before test degradation appears.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding when and how neural networks transition from learning clean structure to memorizing corrupted labels is critical for robust training under label noise. Prior work observed this temporal separation but lacked a quantitative, spectral diagnostic that connects gradient geometry to memorization dynamics and enables corrupted-example identification.

Method: The authors analyze the centered scatter matrix of per-example last-layer gradients (the Fisher-gradient matrix). They define effective rank via spectral entropy and track its trajectory during training. A first-order leave-one-out attribution formula is derived to quantify each example's contribution to rank inflation. Predictions are validated on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and CIFAR-10N across SmallCNN, ResNet18, and Vision Transformers under varying synthetic corruption rates.

Result: Fisher effective rank exhibits a consistent inflation--collapse trajectory aligned with memorization. At peak rank, corrupted examples dominate the highest rank-contributing samples (top-100 noisy fractions: 69.2%–96.2% synthetic; 94.4%±1.9% on CIFAR-10N). First-order attribution matches exact leave-one-out contributions in CNNs and remains enriched in ViTs. Peak effective rank grows monotonically with corruption severity (28.88±1.95 clean → 97.09±1.78 at 60% corruption). Rank inflation often precedes observable test degradation.

Conclusion: Fisher Rank Inflation serves as a robust spectral signature of the structure-learning-to-memorization transition, connecting corrupted-example enrichment, corruption severity, and gradient spectral dynamics. It offers a practical tool for detecting label noise and potentially guiding early stopping or noise-robust training interventions.

Abstract: Deep networks trained with label noise often learn clean structure before memorizing corrupted labels. We show that this transition leaves a spectral signature in the centered scatter of per-example last-layer gradients. Its effective rank transiently expands during memorization and contracts after corrupted labels are fit. We call this phenomenon Fisher Rank Inflation. Corrupted labels increase effective rank by injecting spectral mass into low-energy or previously unused eigendirections, increasing the entropy of the gradient spectrum. We derive a first-order leave-one-out attribution formula, identify conditions under which corrupted examples contribute more strongly than clean examples, and explain why attribution signals weaken once the normalized Fisher-gradient spectrum stabilizes. We test these predictions on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and CIFAR-10N using SmallCNN, ResNet18, and Vision Transformers. Across settings, Fisher effective rank exhibits a consistent inflation--collapse trajectory aligned with memorization. At peak-rank checkpoints, corrupted examples are enriched among the highest rank-contributing samples, with top-100 noisy fractions from \(69.2\%\) to \(96.2\%\) across five-seed synthetic-corruption experiments and \(94.4\%\pm1.9\%\) on CIFAR-10N. First-order spectral attribution closely matches exact leave-one-out contributions in convolutional models and remains enriched in the Vision Transformer. Peak effective rank increases monotonically with corruption severity, from \(28.88\pm1.95\) under clean training to \(97.09\pm1.78\) at \(60\%\) corruption. In several settings, the retrospectively identified onset of rank inflation precedes observable test degradation. These results establish Fisher Rank Inflation as a spectral signature connecting corrupted-example enrichment, corruption severity, and the transition from structure learning to memorization.

</details>


### [112] [The Computational Basis of Confidence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12447)
*Dharshan Kumaran,Viorica Patraucean,Maks Ovsanikov,Petar Veličković,Nathaniel Daw*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether answer logits in multimodal language models reflect a latent decision variable (normative, Bayesian-like confidence) or merely a heuristic preference signal. Using Statistical Decision Confidence (SDC), a framework from computational neuroscience, the authors test qualitative signatures predicted by SDC. Across perceptual and memory tasks, answer-logit differences satisfy SDC signatures (including the folded-X pattern), indicating they behave as monotonic readouts of a latent decision variable. In complex visual reasoning, these signatures break down, delineating the framework's boundary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing confidence evaluation in language models focuses on calibration and correctness prediction, but overlooks a deeper question: what does the confidence signal fundamentally represent? The paper aims to distinguish whether answer logits encode a normative latent decision variable or a non-Bayesian heuristic preference, which has implications for trustworthy deployment.

Method: The authors adopt Statistical Decision Confidence (SDC), a normative framework from computational neuroscience that models confidence as the probability of being correct given a latent decision variable and decision time. They treat the answer-logit difference (LD) as a candidate readout of this latent variable. Across three perceptual discrimination tasks, one memory task, three multimodal non-reasoning models, and one reasoning model, they test qualitative signatures predicted by SDC: (1) the diagnostic correct/error folded-X pattern, (2) mean confidence increases with stimulus strength, and (3) confidence predicts correctness conditional on the decision variable. They also probe boundary conditions using complex visual reasoning tasks lacking explicit normative process models.

Result: In perceptual and memory tasks, LD satisfied all SDC signatures, including the folded-X pattern, confirming that answer logits behave as monotonic readouts of a latent decision variable. In complex visual reasoning, LD still predicted correctness beyond objective task difficulty, but the full geometric SDC signatures were absent, marking the current boundary of the framework when explicit normative process models are unavailable.

Conclusion: Answer logits in multimodal language models can behave as readouts of a latent decision variable under conditions where normative process models apply, but this breaks down in complex reasoning. SDC provides a unifying computational framework for studying confidence across biological and artificial intelligence.

Abstract: Reliable confidence -- the probability that a model's own answer is correct -- is essential for the trustworthy deployment of language models. Existing work has largely evaluated confidence by how well it predicts correctness and whether it is calibrated, leaving open a more fundamental question: what does the confidence signal itself represent? Answer logits may reflect a latent decision variable sufficient to compute normative confidence, or instead a heuristic preference signal that combines the available evidence in a non-Bayesian manner. We address this using statistical decision confidence (SDC), a normative framework from computational neuroscience. Treating the answer-logit difference (LD) as a candidate readout of the latent decision variable, we test the qualitative signatures predicted by SDC. Across three perceptual discrimination tasks and a memory-based decision task, spanning three multimodal non-reasoning models and one reasoning model, LD satisfied these signatures -- including the diagnostic correct/error folded-X pattern -- showing that, in these settings, answer logits behave as monotonic readouts of a latent decision variable rather than heuristic preference scores. In complex visual reasoning, LD continued to predict correctness beyond objective task difficulty, but the full geometric signatures of SDC were absent, illustrating the current boundary of the framework when explicit normative process models are unavailable. These results provide a computational account of confidence in multimodal language models, delineate when answer logits behave as readouts of a latent decision variable, and establish SDC as a unifying framework for studying confidence across biological and artificial intelligence.

</details>


### [113] [Exploring Zero-Shot Foundation Models for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12454)
*Martin Uray,Saverio Messineo,Roland Kwitt,Stefan Huber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether a univariate forecasting foundation model (TimesFM) can perform zero-shot multivariate time series anomaly detection on the SWaT benchmark. Two strategies—forecaster with thresholded errors and embedder with outlier detectors—both fail to match baselines. The FM's strong temporal modeling yields low errors even during anomalies, making them indistinguishable from normal behavior. However, error peaks at anomaly boundaries suggest promise for change-point detection instead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional MTSAD methods require costly, application-specific models that are hard to scale. Foundation Models offer strong zero-shot generalization for univariate forecasting, raising the question of whether they can address MTSAD without task-specific training, thereby reducing cost and improving scalability.

Method: The authors evaluate TimesFM (a univariate forecasting FM) on the SWaT industrial benchmark in two zero-shot setups: (1) per-feature forecaster using thresholded prediction errors for anomaly scoring, and (2) feature extractor where intermediate FM representations feed standard unsupervised outlier detectors. Both are compared against established baselines.

Result: Neither setup is competitive with baselines. Embeddings show only partial separation between normal and anomalous segments. The FM's strong temporal dynamics capture yields low prediction error even within fully anomalous windows, making persistent anomalies indistinguishable from normal behavior. However, errors peak reliably at anomaly boundaries, indicating sensitivity to distribution changes.

Conclusion: Naive zero-shot application of univariate forecasting FMs is unsuitable for MTSAD due to their excessive effectiveness at modeling temporal dynamics. However, the observed error peaks at anomaly boundaries suggest these models are promising for change-point detection instead.

Abstract: Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection (MTSAD) is essential for reliability and safety in domains such as industrial process monitoring and financial risk management, yet conventional approaches rely on application-specific models that are costly to train and hard to scale. Foundation Models (FMs), pre-trained on broad data with strong zero-shot generalization, have recently become available for univariate time series forecasting, raising the question of whether they can address MTSAD without task-specific training. We investigate the zero-shot application of a univariate forecasting FM, TimesFM, to industrial MTSAD on the Secure Water Treatment (SWaT) benchmark, evaluating two strategies: treating the FM as a per-feature forecaster with thresholded prediction errors, and as an embedder whose intermediate representations feed standard outlier detectors. Neither of our proposed setups is competitive with established baselines; embeddings reveal only partial separation between normal and anomalous segments, insufficient for reliable detection. The cause is that the FM is too effective at capturing temporal dynamics, yielding low error even within fully anomalous windows, so persistent anomalies become indistinguishable from normal behavior. However, these observations yield valuable insights: the error peaks at anomaly boundaries, indicating FMs reliably detect distribution changes. We conclude that the proposed naive zero-shot FMs are unsuitable for MTSAD but promising for change-point detection.

</details>


### [114] [Sample Efficient Generative Optimization for Molecular Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12488)
*Sarina Kopf,Cristina Nevado,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SEGO is a sample-efficient Bayesian optimization framework that couples a probabilistic surrogate model with a generative model to adaptively propose and evaluate molecular candidates, achieving state-of-the-art performance on the PMO benchmark using only one-tenth the oracle calls of competing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular optimization across drug discovery, materials design, and catalysis requires exploring vast chemical spaces, but high-fidelity oracles (e.g., experimental assays, docking simulations) are expensive. Existing methods lack sufficient sample efficiency, limiting their practical applicability in campaigns driven by direct experimental feedback where evaluation budgets are severely constrained.

Method: SEGO (Sample Efficient Generative Optimization) integrates four components in a Bayesian optimization loop: (1) a probabilistic surrogate model that learns where promising candidates lie in chemical space, (2) a generative model steered to propose molecules in high-promise regions, (3) an acquisition function that selects the most promising candidate for evaluation, and (4) the resulting oracle evaluation used to refine the surrogate and anchor the generator with real reward signals.

Result: On the Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark, SEGO achieves state-of-the-art performance using only 10% of the oracle calls required by other methods. On a multiparameter docking task, it identifies 10 hits in approximately half the oracle calls needed by existing approaches.

Conclusion: SEGO substantially advances sample efficiency in molecular optimization, bringing the field closer to enabling optimization campaigns that operate under direct experimental feedback with realistic evaluation budgets.

Abstract: Molecular optimization in drug discovery, materials design, and catalysis requires searching vast chemical spaces under tight evaluation budgets, since high-fidelity oracles and experimental measurements are costly. The practical impact of an optimization method therefore hinges on its sample efficiency: how few evaluations it needs to find strong candidates. We introduce Sample Efficient Generative Optimization (SEGO), a framework for Bayesian optimization on adaptively generated molecules. In SEGO, a probabilistic surrogate model forms a hypothesis about where hits lie in chemical space, a generative model is steered to propose candidates in that region, the most promising candidate is selected via an acquisition function, and the resulting oracle call is used both to sharpen the surrogate and to anchor the generator in real reward. SEGO attains state-of-the-art performance on the practical molecular optimization (PMO) benchmark using only one tenth of the oracle calls consumed by other methods, and on a multiparameter docking task it reaches ten hits in roughly half the oracle calls of existing approaches. These gains move molecular optimization closer to campaigns driven by direct experimental feedback.

</details>


### [115] [A JoLT for the KV Cache: Near-Lossless KV Cache Compression via Joint Tucker and JL-Residual Allocation for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12550)
*Rahul Krishnan,Volker Schulz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JoLT compresses the KV cache using a partial Tucker decomposition on the token and feature axes combined with a JL-rotated low-bit residual, achieving near-lossless 2-3x compression under a byte budget allocated via Lagrangian dual optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The KV cache is the dominant memory bottleneck in transformer inference, scaling with batch size, context length, and depth. Existing methods either apply low-rank factorization to 2D slices (per-head or cross-layer) or quantize entries uniformly, but none exploit the fact that the KV cache is a third-order tensor (heads × tokens × features) whose axes carry different amounts of redundancy.

Method: JoLT applies a partial Tucker decomposition that compresses only the token and feature axes while preserving head and layer dimensions. Truncation energy is recovered via a Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) rotated low-bit residual. A single Lagrangian dual jointly allocates Tucker ranks and residual bit-widths per layer group, separately for keys and values, under a global byte budget. A randomized-SVD variant, FlashJoLT, accelerates compression-time computation.

Result: JoLT achieves 2-3x near-lossless compression: perplexity, GSM8K accuracy, and RULER needle-in-a-haystack retrieval remain within statistical noise of uncompressed baselines on Mistral-7B-v0.3 (GQA) and LLaMA-2-13B (MHA). At 2x, relative Frobenius error is 0.009 (keys) and 0.006 (values), roughly 10× lower than cross-layer SVD and 4-bit quantization. FlashJoLT provides a 5-13× compression-time speedup at matched quality.

Conclusion: Treating the KV cache as a third-order tensor and applying partial Tucker decomposition with JL-residual correction yields substantially better compression-quality tradeoffs than prior 2D low-rank or uniform quantization methods, while remaining practical via the FlashJoLT speedup.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache has become the dominant memory cost of transformer inference. It grows with batch size, context length, and depth, and at long context it, rather than the model weights, sets the ceiling on throughput. Two families of methods reduce it. Low-rank methods factor two-dimensional slices of the cache, either per-head matrices or cross-layer feature blocks, and quantization methods lower the bit-width of every entry. Neither family exploits the fact that the cache at a layer is naturally a third-order tensor whose three axes, the heads, the tokens, and the features, carry very different amounts of redundancy. We take this tensor view directly. Our method, JoLT, applies a partial Tucker decomposition that compresses only the token and feature axes while leaving the head and layer axes intact, and then restores the energy that truncation discards with a Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) rotated low-bit residual. A single Lagrangian dual allocates the Tucker ranks and the residual bit-widths together, per layer group and separately for keys and values, under one byte budget. The result is a near-lossless 2-3x compression: perplexity, GSM8K accuracy, and RULER needle-in-a-haystack retrieval all stay at or within statistical noise of the uncompressed baseline on both a grouped-query-attention model (Mistral-7B-v0.3) and a multi-head-attention model (LLaMA-2-13B). At 2x, JoLT reconstructs the cache to relative Frobenius error 0.009 (K) and 0.006 (V) on both architectures, roughly an order of magnitude below cross-layer SVD and 4-bit quantization. A randomized-SVD variant, FlashJoLT, delivers a 5-13x compression-time speedup at matched quality.

</details>


### [116] [Adversarial Attacks on Online Handwriting using Salience-based Temporal Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12500)
*Yataro Tamura,Brian Kenji Iwana,Jiseok Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new adversarial attack for online handwriting recognition that inserts/deletes trajectory points at salient time steps instead of adding spatial noise, achieving superior black-box transferability while preserving visual stroke quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing adversarial attacks designed for image inputs rely on additive spatial perturbations, which produce high-frequency jitter and unnatural stroke artifacts when applied to the time-series trajectory data of online handwriting. There is a need for attacks tailored to the temporal structure of handwriting sequences.

Method: The authors propose salience-guided temporal editing: gradient-based temporal activation mapping identifies time steps most influential for the original class prediction, then adversarial examples are crafted by inserting or deleting pen-trajectory points specifically at those salient time steps, rather than adding noise. This preserves shape continuity and smoothness.

Result: Evaluated on Unipen and CASIA-OLHWDB under white-box and one-shot black-box settings. Conventional image-based attacks achieve strong white-box performance but poor cross-model transferability. The proposed temporal editing attack achieves stronger one-shot black-box transferability while maintaining the visual structure of handwriting.

Conclusion: Temporal editing constitutes a relevant and potent threat model for online handwriting recognition, especially in one-shot black-box transfer scenarios, highlighting a vulnerability distinct from traditional spatial perturbation attacks.

Abstract: Deep learning models for online handwriting recognition have been shown effective and are increasingly deployed in practical applications. However, their vulnerability to adversarial attacks is still a challenge. Existing adversarial methods are predominantly designed for image-based inputs and typically rely on additive spatial perturbations. When applied to online handwriting, which is inherently represented as a time series of pen trajectories, such perturbations often introduce high-frequency jitter and visibly unnatural stroke artifacts. In this work, we propose a novel adversarial attack framework for online handwriting recognition based on salience-guided temporal editing. Instead of adding noise, the proposed method generates adversarial examples by inserting and deleting points at time steps selected according to temporal salience, preserving the shape and smoothness of the original handwriting. Temporal salience is estimated using gradient-based activation mapping, which guides edits toward time steps that strongly support the original class prediction. We evaluate the proposed approach on the Unipen and CASIA-OLHWDB datasets under both white-box and one-shot black-box attack settings. Experimental results demonstrate that while conventional image-based attacks achieve strong white-box performance, they exhibit poor transferability across models. In contrast, the proposed temporal editing attack achieves stronger one-shot black-box transferability while preserving the visual structure of the handwriting. These results indicate that temporal editing is a relevant threat model for online handwriting recognition, particularly in one-shot black-box transfer settings.

</details>


### [117] [What Does Goodness Measure? A Likelihood-Ratio Account of Forward-Forward Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12501)
*Paolo Giannitrapani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper provides a probabilistic framework showing that the Forward-Forward algorithm's heuristic choices — squared goodness and inter-layer normalization — are optimal under explicit generative models. It generalizes goodness to anisotropic (Mahalanobis) and heavy-tailed settings, and diagnoses failure modes like depth collapse under unit-norm normalization and scale-inflation shortcuts in pairwise objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Forward-Forward algorithm uses a squared-activation "goodness" and inter-layer normalization as heuristic design choices. This paper aims to provide a principled probabilistic foundation for these heuristics, showing they emerge naturally from explicit generative assumptions rather than being arbitrary.

Method: The authors frame FF training as a likelihood-ratio test between two zero-mean populations differing in scale. Under this model: (1) squared goodness emerges as the sufficient statistic; (2) the FF threshold corresponds to the decision boundary. They extend this to anisotropic populations (yielding Mahalanobis goodness), heavy-tailed populations (yielding saturating statistics with divisive normalization and posterior precision), and analyze inter-layer normalization via the lens of preserving per-coordinate energy while removing length. They also identify and address a scale-inflation shortcut in pairwise objectives using whitened goodness.

Result: Key findings include: (1) squared goodness is the sufficient statistic of a scale-based likelihood-ratio test, not a heuristic; (2) Mahalanobis goodness generalizes to anisotropic data; (3) heavy-tailed models produce saturating statistics where the slope acts as posterior precision — a form of divisive normalization — with bounded evidence; (4) inter-layer normalization must remove vector length while preserving per-coordinate energy, and violating this causes "depth collapse" under unit-norm normalization; (5) whitened goodness eliminates a scale-inflation shortcut in the pairwise objective.

Conclusion: The Forward-Forward algorithm's design choices have a rigorous probabilistic interpretation as likelihood-ratio testing under a scale-difference generative model. This framework not only justifies existing heuristics but also suggests principled generalizations (Mahalanobis, heavy-tailed) and remedies for failure modes (depth collapse, scale inflation), unifying FF learning under a coherent statistical lens.

Abstract: The Forward-Forward (FF) algorithm trains each layer locally, so that a scalar goodness - the sum of squared activations - is high on real inputs and low on contrastive ones, with activations normalized between layers. Both choices are usually treated as heuristics. Under an explicit generative model they are not: the squared goodness is the sufficient statistic of a likelihood-ratio test between two zero-mean populations differing in scale, and the FF threshold is its boundary. It generalizes: anisotropic populations yield a Mahalanobis goodness, the plain square being its isotropic case; heavy-tailed populations yield a saturating statistic whose slope is a posterior precision - divisive normalization - with bounded evidence and an advantage only under aggregation. The same lens characterizes the inter-layer normalization: it must remove the length while preserving per-coordinate energy, explaining a depth collapse we observe under unit-norm normalization; and the pairwise objective admits a scale-inflation shortcut that a whitened goodness removes.

</details>


### [118] [Extractable Memorization From First Principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12649)
*A. Feder Cooper,Marika Swanberg,Jamie Hayes,Lea Duesterwald,Christopher De Sa,Daniel E. Ho,Mark A. Lemley,Percy Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies validity issues in prior work on extractable memorization in LLMs—overstating extraction via short sequences or dismissing it because models reproduce non-training text. It proposes matched comparisons against non-training baselines, formalized via conformal tests (for populations) and census calibration (for single documents), enabling rigorous, calibrated memorization claims with controlled false positive rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on extractable memorization suffers from two validity problems: (1) overstating extraction by using sequences too short to distinguish memorization from ordinary predictability, and (2) dismissing extraction as unreliable because models also reproduce text they weren't trained on. Both fail to establish a valid baseline for what constitutes evidence of memorization. A rigorous framework is needed to determine when a training sequence's generation probability is sufficiently high—relative to comparable non-training sequences—to warrant a memorization claim.

Method: The authors introduce matched comparisons, where generation probabilities of training sequences are measured against those of comparable non-training sequences that cannot have been memorized. This is formalized in two ways: (1) a conformal test that calibrates a probability threshold to a chosen false positive rate when training and non-training sequences are sampled from defined populations, and (2) a census method that calibrates against a matched non-training document when the object is a single document (e.g., a book). They apply these methods to Wikipedia data with OLMo 2 32B and to books with Llama 3.1 70B.

Result: Using matched comparisons, the authors show that OLMo 2 32B reproduces non-training 10-token suffixes approximately 24% as often as training ones, indicating that a substantial share of apparent "extraction" is actually false positives due to predictability. For Llama 3.1 70B on books, calibrated thresholds are as low as 1e-27, meaning valid memorization claims can be made for sequences that no realistic sampling budget could extract. These findings demonstrate that prior setups have significant validity issues.

Conclusion: The paper refines the definition of "extractable memorization" to require both a valid memorization claim (established via matched comparison against non-training baselines) and near-certain generation within a realistic sampling budget. This framework enables rigorous, calibrated memorization claims and exposes the validity shortcomings of prior extraction studies.

Abstract: Recent work on extractable memorization in LLMs suffers from two contrasting validity problems. Some studies overstate extraction, e.g., relying on sequences too short to distinguish memorization from predictability. Others imply that extraction is unreliable evidence of memorization, since models can also reproduce real-world text they weren't explicitly trained on. In different ways, both overlook what makes a valid extraction claim: the model must generate a training sequence with high enough probability to indicate memorization. To determine what's high enough, one has to perform a matched comparison: measuring the generation probabilities of both the training sequences of interest and comparable non-training sequences. Because non-training sequences cannot have been memorized, their probabilities provide a baseline for predictability; a training sequence exceeding this baseline provides evidence of memorization. We formalize matched comparisons in two ways: (1) a conformal test that calibrates a threshold to a chosen FPR when training and non-training sequences are sampled from populations, and (2) a census that calibrates against a matched non-training document when the object is a single document (e.g., a book). We show that matched comparisons enable rigorous, calibrated memorization claims, and reveal where prior setups have validity issues. For instance, on Wikipedia OLMo 2 32B reproduces non-training 10-token suffixes roughly 24% as often as training ones: that share of the training generation rate reflects false positives, not memorization. For Llama 3.1 70B on books, the thresholds we calibrate are as low as 1e-27, supporting memorization claims for sequences that no feasible sampling budget would extract. Based on these results, we refine "extractable memorization" to require a valid memorization claim and near-certain generation within a realistic budget.

</details>


### [119] [OOD-RL-Bench: A Benchmark Framework for Out-of-Distribution Detection in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12523)
*Emil Mittag,Richard Dazeley,Peter Vamplew*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OOD-RL-Bench is a framework for benchmarking out-of-distribution detectors on RL trajectories, evaluating multiple detectors against diverse anomaly types (observation perturbations, regime switches, delays, action-conditioned dynamics) using a DQN policy in LunarLander-v3. Results show high detection accuracy for some anomalies but persistent difficulty with observation delays and action-conditioned dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing OOD detection benchmarks focus on image classifiers or static low-dimensional datasets and fail to capture the action-dependent temporal structure of RL trajectories. Reliable RL agents need OOD detection to maintain integrity amid sensor faults, disturbances, and distribution shifts, but no adequate evaluation framework exists for this setting.

Method: The authors introduce OOD-RL-Bench, a modular, extensible framework with shared interfaces for detectors and anomaly injectors. Anomalies span categories including observation perturbations, regime switches, observation delays, and action-conditioned dynamics. Evaluation uses a DQN policy in LunarLander-v3 with metrics: matched-time AUROC, matched-time AUPRC, matched-time FPR, detection delay, and segmented-onset metrics.

Result: Significant performance variance across anomaly types was observed. Observation perturbations and regime switches were detected with high accuracy by several methods. Observation delay and action-conditioned dynamics remained challenging, even when comparing post-onset anomaly scores against clean scores from identical timesteps.

Conclusion: OOD-RL-Bench provides a reproducible, extensible benchmark that reveals which anomaly types are tractable for current OOD detectors and which remain open challenges. The framework, policy checkpoint, and results are publicly released to facilitate further research.

Abstract: Reliable reinforcement learning (RL) agents must maintain operational integrity amidst sensor malfunctions, dynamic disturbances, and slow environmental shifts. The detection of out-of-distribution conditions is pivotal to determining when an agent's observations, transitions, or trajectory dynamics deviate from the assumptions underpinning its policy training. Current out-of-distribution (OOD) detection benchmarks typically evaluate image classifiers or static low-dimensional datasets, failing to account for the complex, action-dependent temporal structure inherent in RL trajectories. To address this gap, we present OOD-RL-Bench, a comprehensive and extensible framework designed to evaluate OOD detectors against categories of anomalies injected into RL trajectories. Detectors and anomaly injectors are integrated through shared interfaces and configuration, which allows new scoring methods and perturbation families to be evaluated without modification of the core benchmark loop. We evaluate the utility of the framework using a Deep Q-Network policy within the LunarLander-v3 environment. We assess the performance of each detector across a suite of anomaly types using matched-time AUROC, matched-time AUPRC, matched-time false-positive rate, detection delay, and segmented-onset metrics. Our analysis reveals significant performance variance across anomaly types: observation perturbations and regime switches are identified with high accuracy by several methods, while observation delay and action-conditioned dynamics remain difficult even when post-onset anomaly scores are compared against clean scores from the same timesteps. We make the framework, trained policy checkpoint, and complete results publicly available as a reproducible artefact.

</details>


### [120] [From Preimage Search To Source-Grounded Feature Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12526)
*Kaixiang Shu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes source-grounded feature inversion, a method that reconstructs what a specific neural network feature extracts from a given input by conditioning on the local network geometry at that input, using closed-form Wiener maps to repair backpropagated signals in a single reverse pass, enabling interpretable, sample-specific feature visualization across diverse architectures without per-query optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional feature inversion methods (e.g., iterative optimization) search for any input whose encoded representation matches a target feature, ignoring the specific source sample that generated it. This underspecification means the inverse is not uniquely tied to the original input. The authors aim to ground inversion in the source sample by exploiting the local computational graph geometry at the point where the feature was produced.

Method: The method formulates source-grounded inversion by conditioning on the local network geometry at the target-generating input. It uses backpropagation to obtain reverse dependencies (adjoint signals) but repairs them using two closed-form matrix Wiener maps: (1) a map from a mean-seed VJP (vector-Jacobian product) to the upstream state, and (2) a second Wiener map for the JVP (Jacobian-vector product) forward-consistency residual. The repaired states are composed through the same computational DAG in a single finite reverse pass. A calibrated zero-intercept map family generalizes across inputs, depths, channels, channel groups, CNN/Transformer architectures, tensor components, and visual distributions without query-specific optimization.

Result: Matched target and source control experiments confirm that each inverse depends on both the selected feature and the local operators of the specific sample being explained, not on a target-independent image template. Prediction-conditioned feature atlases align these visualizations with independent interventions on internal features, validating the approach.

Conclusion: Source-grounded feature inversion opens the hidden feature hierarchy of neural networks to inspection at the level of individual layers and channels, linking what the network extracts from a given input to the internal evidence that shapes its decision. This enables faithful, sample-specific interpretation of internal representations across diverse architectures.

Abstract: Interpreting a neural network requires understanding what its internal features extract from a particular input. Feature inversion seeks to express a selected feature in the input domain, but canonical iterative methods search for an input whose re-encoded representation matches the target. Because many inputs can satisfy this constraint, target matching alone does not specify the inverse associated with the sample that generated the feature. We formulate source-grounded feature inversion by conditioning the inverse on the source-local network geometry at the target-generating input. At each boundary of the computational DAG, backpropagation provides the correct reverse dependencies but transports an adjoint signal rather than an upstream-state estimate. We locally repair this signal with a closed-form matrix Wiener map from a mean-seed VJP to the upstream state, followed by a second Wiener map for the JVP forward-consistency residual, and compose the repaired states through the same DAG in one finite reverse pass. One calibrated zero-intercept map family supports new inputs, depths, channels, and channel groups across diverse CNN and Transformer architectures, tensor components, and visual distributions without query-specific optimisation. Matched target and source controls verify that each inverse depends on the selected feature and the local operators of the sample being explained, rather than a target-independent image template. Prediction-conditioned feature atlases align these visualisations with independent interventions on the corresponding internal features. Together, source-grounded feature inversion opens the model's hidden feature hierarchy to inspection at the level of individual layers and channels, linking what the network extracts from an input to the internal evidence that shapes its decision.

</details>


### [121] [Learning Mechanistic Reasoning for Chemical Reactions with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12771)
*Xingyu Dang,Haocheng Tang,Junmei Wang,Yanjun Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a large-scale reaction mechanism reasoning dataset and the FukuyamaBench benchmark, showing that mechanism-aware fine-tuning enables LLMs (Qwen3-30B-A3B) to outperform specialized models like FlowER on hierarchical mechanism reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current chemical LLMs focus on coarse-grained product prediction and retrosynthesis, leading to physical inconsistencies and hallucinations. Meanwhile, specialized small generative models for mechanism inference lack generalization across diverse chemical spaces. A gap exists for models that combine broad chemical knowledge with rigorous, stepwise mechanistic reasoning.

Method: The authors constructed a large-scale reasoning dataset of reaction mechanisms and developed FukuyamaBench, a challenging benchmark derived from Fukuyama's Advanced Organic Reaction Mechanism book, to evaluate hierarchical mechanism reasoning. They fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B with mechanism-aware training and compared performance against the specialized FlowER model.

Result: The fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B achieved 8.3% exact pathway match on FukuyamaBench Set A, surpassing the specialized FlowER model's 5.1%, demonstrating that mechanism-aware training significantly enhances chemical reasoning in LLMs.

Conclusion: Mechanism-aware training substantially improves the fundamental chemical intelligence of LLMs. Integrating stepwise mechanistic reasoning into LLM training bridges the gap between broad-coverage language models and specialized mechanism inference tools, yielding more physically consistent and generalizable chemical reasoning.

Abstract: Reaction mechanisms consist of the step-by-step sequences of elementary reactions that explain chemical transformations. Learning the mechanism logic is therefore essential for enhancing the fundamental chemical intelligence of large language models (LLMs). The stepwise deduction of reaction mechanism aligns naturally with the reasoning paradigms of reasoning LLMs. However, current chemical LLMs primarily emphasize coarse-grained name reactions for product prediction and retrosynthesis, often leading to physical inconsistencies and hallucinations. In contrast, specialized small-scale generative models for mechanism inference typically suffer from restricted generalization capacity across diverse chemical spaces. To overcome these limitations, we built a novel, large-scale reasoning dataset of reaction mechanisms. Furthermore, we established the FukuyamaBench, a difficult benchmark derived from Fukuyama's Advanced Organic Reaction Mechanism book, to rigorously evaluate model performance on hierarchical mechanism reasoning. Our fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B achieves 8.3% exact pathway match on FukuyamaBench Set~A, surpassing the specialized FlowER model (5.1%), demonstrating that mechanism-aware training substantially enhances chemical reasoning in language models.

</details>


### [122] [Lightweight Multi-Scale Anomaly Detection for Resource-Constrained Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12599)
*Raheen Junaid Wani,Smruti R. Sarangi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes LMSAE, a lightweight multi-scale autoencoder using Discrete Wavelet Transform for efficient univariate time-series anomaly detection, achieving competitive accuracy with a model under 500KB and demonstrating significant latency and power reductions on edge hardware (NVIDIA Jetson Nano).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-series anomaly detection in IoT and edge environments requires models that are both accurate and resource-efficient. Current deep-learning methods are often computationally expensive, too large for edge deployment, and lack multi-scale sensitivity to detect subtle anomalies. There is a need for a compact, low-latency, low-power solution that maintains competitive detection performance.

Method: LMSAE is a lightweight autoencoder architecture that uses Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT) to decompose input signals into multi-scale frequency components, enabling the model to capture features at different temporal resolutions. A multi-scale loss function is applied across these decomposed components to enhance sensitivity to subtle anomalies. The entire model is designed for compactness, with fewer than 500KB total size.

Result: The model achieves competitive or superior anomaly detection performance on benchmark datasets compared to existing methods, despite using significantly fewer parameters. On an NVIDIA Jetson Nano, LMSAE demonstrates a 9x reduction in inference latency and a 2x reduction in power consumption, confirming its suitability for edge deployment.

Conclusion: LMSAE effectively balances accuracy and efficiency for univariate time-series anomaly detection, making it a practical solution for resource-constrained edge and IoT environments. The combination of DWT-based multi-scale feature extraction and a compact autoencoder design proves to be a viable strategy.

Abstract: Time-series anomaly detection is increasingly important in IoT systems, sensor networks, and edge monitoring applications, where models must operate under strict constraints on memory, latency, and power consumption. While recent deep-learning approaches have improved detection accuracy, many remain computationally expensive and often fail to capture subtle anomalies due to limited multi-scale sensitivity. Autoencoders are widely used for anomaly detection because they reconstruct normal patterns well, leading to elevated reconstruction errors for anomalous inputs. Their simplicity and efficiency also make them suitable lightweight backbones for handling multi-scale inputs. To address these challenges, we propose a Lightweight MultiScale AutoEncoder (LMSAE) network for univariate time-series anomaly detection, designed to be compact and computationally efficient. LMSAE leverages the Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT) to extract multi-scale features and employs a multi-scale loss function to improve sensitivity to subtle or hidden anomalies. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate competitive or superior detection performance despite using significantly fewer parameters and a model size of less than 500 KB. LMSAE also achieves low-latency, low-power inference on the NVIDIA Jetson Nano, with 9x reduction in inference latency and 2x reduction in power consumption, making it ideal for edge deployment.

</details>


### [123] [The Geometry of Memorization: Finite-Time Spectral Sensitivity as a Diagnostic for Flow Matching Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12616)
*Shuchan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Finite-Time Spectral Sensitivity (FTSS), a gradient-free metric that tracks the singular values of the state-transition matrix in continuous-time generative models. It reveals that overfitting manifests as spectral collapse—a sharp drop in stable rank—while well-generalizing models maintain stable effective dimensions. This enables internal detection of generative memorization without external data or baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous-time generative models (e.g., flow matching, diffusion) theoretically favor straight trajectories, but empirical networks develop complex path deformations. Under data scarcity, models can overfit and memorize training samples, yet detecting this typically requires external membership inference queries or baseline comparisons. The authors seek an internal, purely geometric signal of overfitting derived from the model's own trajectory dynamics.

Method: The authors propose FTSS g(t), defined as the root-mean-square singular value of the state-transition matrix (Jacobian of the flow) along generated trajectories. This serves as a continuous proxy for stable rank. They compute g(t) via forward passes only (gradient-free), analyze its behavior across training regimes, and use observed spectral collapse — a sudden dimensionality reduction in the effective rank — as a marker of memorization. They then construct an internal geometric audit framework that flags overfitting solely from trajectory geometry.

Result: Generalizing models exhibit stable effective dimensions (stable g(t)), while overfitting models display a distinct spectral collapse. The FTSS metric reliably detects generative memorization using only internal trajectory dynamics, matching or surpassing the need for external membership inference methods or baseline data comparison.

Conclusion: Spectral sensitivity analysis via FTSS provides a principled, self-contained diagnostic for generative overfitting. The geometric pathology of spectral collapse emerges as a robust internal signal of memorization in continuous-time generative models, enabling audit without external references.

Abstract: Continuous-time generative frameworks construct probability paths between base and target domains by optimizing time-dependent velocity fields. While theoretical targets favor straight trajectories, empirical networks develop complex path deformations. This paper presents the Finite-Time Spectral Sensitivity (FTSS) g(t), a gradient-free, forward-pass metric that exposes flow geometry by tracking the root-mean-square singular value of the state-transition matrix. Serving as a continuous proxy for stable rank, g(t) reveals a distinct geometric pathology under data scarcity: while generalizing models maintain stable effective dimensions, overfitting causes a spectral collapse. We leverage this structural phenomenon to develop an internal geometric audit based on g(t). Our framework detects generative memorization using purely internal trajectory dynamics, removing the need for external membership queries or baseline data comparison.

</details>


### [124] [Accelerating Masked Diffusion Large Language Models: A Survey of Efficient Inference Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12829)
*Daehoon Gwak,Minhyung Lee,Junwoo Park,Jaegul Choo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey proposes a unified latency decomposition framework for diffusion large language models (dLLMs) to disentangle algorithmic, architectural, and system-level factors affecting inference speed, and systematically categorizes acceleration techniques along three axes while providing guidelines for reproducible benchmarking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While diffusion LLMs promise parallel generation advantages over autoregressive models, practical speedups remain elusive. The field lacks rigorous comparisons because end-to-end latency depends on conflated algorithmic, architectural, and system-level factors. A structured framework is needed to disentangle these factors and systematically evaluate acceleration techniques.

Method: The authors introduce a unified latency decomposition framework that breaks down end-to-end inference latency into distinct, disentangled components. Using this framework as an organizing principle, they taxonomize existing acceleration techniques along three axes: (1) algorithmic innovations, (2) architectural and system optimizations, and (3) inference-time scaling strategies.

Result: The survey provides a structured categorization of dLLM acceleration research, revealing how different techniques map to specific latency components. It also yields guidelines for reproducible benchmarking and identifies open challenges that currently prevent diffusion LLMs from achieving their theoretical parallel-generation speedups in practice.

Conclusion: Achieving practical speedups from dLLMs requires co-optimization across algorithms, architectures, and systems rather than isolated improvements. The proposed latency decomposition framework enables principled comparison and highlights that reproducible benchmarking and addressing open challenges are essential for realizing the full potential of parallel generation.

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) offer a theoretical advantage in parallel generation over standard autoregressive models. However, parallel generation alone does not guarantee practical speedups. Realizing this efficiency requires specialized inference mechanisms, such as diffusion-aware caching and reuse. Consequently, as inference efficiency becomes a prerequisite for practical deployment, recent research has actively explored acceleration techniques across algorithms, architectures, and systems. However, rigorous comparisons remain difficult, as end-to-end latency stems from intricate trade-offs between algorithmic, architectural, and system-level factors that are often conflated in existing benchmarks. In this survey, we introduce a unified latency decomposition framework for dLLMs to disentangle these factors and analyze their impact on inference speed in real deployments. Guided by this framework, we categorize acceleration techniques along three axes covering algorithmic innovations, architectural and system optimizations, and inference-time scaling. Finally, we provide guidelines for reproducible benchmarking and highlight open challenges for realizing the full potential of parallel generation.

</details>


### [125] [Learning Forced Multibody Dynamics on Lie Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12627)
*Martine Dyring Hansen,Marta Ghirardelli,Elena Celledoni,David Martin de Diego,Brynjulf Owren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Lie group-based learning architecture that infers mechanical system dynamics from position-only data using discrete forced Euler-Lagrange equations, preserving geometric structure and conservation laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning dynamical models of mechanical systems typically requires velocity data, which is often noisy or unavailable. Additionally, standard learning methods fail to respect the underlying geometric structure (manifold constraints, symmetries, conservation laws). The authors aim to address both limitations simultaneously by leveraging Lie group formulations and position-only measurements.

Method: The method formulates dynamics directly on Lie group-valued configuration spaces using discrete forced Euler-Lagrange equations. By working intrinsically on manifolds, geometric invariants and conservation laws are automatically preserved. The architecture learns from position data alone, without requiring velocity measurements. The framework naturally extends to multibody systems and incorporates external control inputs.

Result: The approach demonstrates strong performance on both synthetic and real-world datasets, showing effective dynamics learning while preserving geometric properties.

Conclusion: Learning dynamics on Lie groups from position-only data is feasible and advantageous, combining geometric structure preservation with practical applicability in velocity-scarce settings. The method generalizes to multibody systems with control inputs.

Abstract: We propose an architecture for learning the dynamics of mechanical systems based on discrete forced Euler-Lagrange equations on Lie groups using only position data. By formulating the dynamics directly on manifold-valued configuration spaces, the method naturally respects the geometric structure of the systems and preserves geometric invariants and conservation laws. The reliance on position measurements alone makes the framework applicable in settings where velocity data are unavailable or noisy. The approach extends naturally to multibody systems, accommodates external control inputs, and demonstrates strong performance on both synthetic and real-world datasets.

</details>


### [126] [AdaPCLA: Adaptive Prior-Calibrated Logit Adjustment for Long-Tailed Longitudinal EHR Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12645)
*Shuai Cui,Chen Wenxuan,Wenjie Du,Jian Lou,Dan Li,Wenjie Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaPCLA is an adaptive training framework for generative modeling of longitudinal EHR data that uses simulated annealing and zero-shot distribution control to improve representation of rare (tail) disease co-occurrences, achieving over 114% improvement in TailPairSeen over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard autoregressive generative models for longitudinal EHR data systematically underrepresent tail events (rare diseases, symptoms, and their co-occurrences), compromising fidelity for rare subpopulations and limiting utility for privacy-preserving research.

Method: AdaPCLA introduces a data distribution-aware training strategy with three components: (1) internalizing data knowledge parameters via simulated annealing training, (2) training-free zero-shot distribution control for adapting to diverse clinical populations, and (3) theoretical analysis using label-wise empirical NTK to characterize rare-code logit updates and derive a prior-internalization bound relating annealing speed and NTK conditioning.

Result: On MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, AdaPCLA improves TailPairSeen by 114.2% and 65.1% respectively over HALO, and achieves 3.5% F1 improvement over GPT-style generation for zero-shot cross-population adaptation, with consistent gains in tail plausibility, downstream utility, and zero-shot control.

Conclusion: AdaPCLA effectively addresses the tail-event underrepresentation problem in EHR generative modeling through adaptive distribution-aware training and zero-shot control, with both strong empirical performance and theoretical grounding via NTK-based analysis.

Abstract: Generative modeling of longitudinal Electronic Health Records is increasingly important for privacy-preserving research, yet standard autoregressive models tend to underrepresent the co-occurrence structure of tail events (i.e., diseases, symptoms), reducing the fidelity and faithfulness of generated data for rare subpopulations. To this end, we propose AdaPCLA framework, which enables generative models to adaptively fit and generate EHR data through a data distribution-aware training strategy; this is achieved by internalizing data knowledge parameters by simulated annealing training. It also supports training-free adaptation to a diverse clinical population for generation through zero-shot distribution control. Moreover, our theoretical analysis characterizes rare-code logit updates through the label-wise empirical NTK and derives a prior-internalization bound for how annealing speed and NTK conditioning affect retained prior signals. Experiments on real-world data show that AdaPCLA achieves consistent gains in tail plausibility, downstream utility, and zero-shot control; in particular, it improves TailPairSeen over HALO by 114.2% on MIMIC-III and 65.1% on MIMIC-IV, outperforms GPT-style generation by 3.5% F1 for zero-shot cross-population adaptation.

</details>


### [127] [Learning-based Probabilistic Load Forecasting with Post-hoc and In-model Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12730)
*Sarah Al-Shareeda,Gulcihan Ozdemir,Heung Seok Jeon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Probabilistic load forecasting with reconstructed inputs reveals that integrated quantile learning (especially with TFT) outperforms post-hoc residual quantiles; reconstruction errors inflate quantile scores ~106% without widening intervals, exposing limits of modular uncertainty methods under feature-limited deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smart-building load forecasters trained on dense, multivariate data often face deployment scenarios with sparse (e.g., hourly), feature-limited inputs. Missing features must be reconstructed, introducing errors that propagate and miscalibrate prediction intervals, undermining demand-response scheduling. The paper investigates where uncertainty should be placed when inference relies on reconstructed inputs.

Method: A unified one-day-ahead probabilistic forecasting framework is developed that aligns temporal resolution, reconstructs unavailable inputs, and derives causal features. Two uncertainty placement strategies are compared: (1) modular post-hoc residual-quantile estimation and (2) integrated in-model quantile learning. Three mid-scale DL backbones — recurrent, hybrid recurrent, and attention-based Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) — are evaluated under identical inputs, horizon, preprocessing, and training budgets. Evaluation uses MAPE, RMSE, interval width, coverage, quantile score, Diebold-Mariano tests, reconstruction-sensitivity analysis, and robustness checks against non-DL baselines and seasonal hold-out weeks.

Result: Uncertainty placement is backbone-dependent. TFT with integrated quantile learning achieves 2.2–3.6% MAPE and 28–83W RMSE, with intervals ~5× narrower than modular intervals at nominal coverage. Diebold-Mariano tests confirm TFT superiority and mixed recurrent backbone behavior. Reconstruction-sensitivity test shows a 106% QS increase with nearly unchanged interval width, indicating the model does not absorb reconstruction-induced uncertainty. Robustness checks against non-DL baselines and seasonal hold-outs support the ranking.

Conclusion: Post-hoc residual quantiles are unreliable when inference depends on reconstructed inputs. Integrated quantile learning with TFT provides the most reliable probabilistic forecasts, and reconstruction error must be explicitly accounted for rather than relying on models to absorb it automatically.

Abstract: Smart-building load forecasters are often trained offline on dense, multivariate, high-frequency data, but deployment may provide only hourly, feature-limited inputs. Missing features must then be reconstructed, and their errors can propagate through the model. If this input uncertainty is not reflected, prediction intervals may become miscalibrated, affecting demand-response scheduling. Our work examines where uncertainty should be placed once inference inputs are reconstructed. We develop a unified one-day-ahead probabilistic forecasting framework that aligns temporal resolution, reconstructs the unavailable inputs, and derives causal features, and we compare a modular post-hoc residual-quantile scheme with an integrated in-model quantile-learning scheme. The comparison uses three mid-scale Deep Learning (DL) backbones: recurrent, hybrid recurrent, and attention-based Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) models, under identical inputs, forecasting horizon, preprocessing rules, and training budgets. Results show that uncertainty placement is backbone-dependent. Integrated quantile learning is most reliable with the TFT, yielding 2.2-3.6% MAPE and 28-83W RMSE on the labeled test window, while producing intervals about 5x narrower than the modular intervals at the closest-to-nominal coverage level. Diebold-Mariano tests support the TFT ranking and the mixed behavior of the recurrent backbones. A reconstruction-sensitivity test shows that reconstructed inputs increase the Quantile Score (QS) by 106% while interval width remains nearly unchanged, indicating that the model does not automatically absorb reconstruction-induced uncertainty. Robustness checks against non-DL baselines and seasonal hold-out weeks support this ranking. Our results expose the limits of post-hoc residual quantiles when inference depends on reconstructed inputs.

</details>


### [128] [What Makes a Representational Prior Work? Feature Families, Label-Free Invariances, and Critical Windows in Grokking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12735)
*Gunner Levi Howe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper characterizes what makes contrastive priors effective for accelerating grokking, showing that feature-family alignment is critical, label-free invariance priors suffice, early application captures nearly all benefit, and structure injection dramatically flattens the weight-norm delay-law exponent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work established that grokking delay is causally the time needed to form task-structured representations, which can be injected via a contrastive prior. This paper systematically investigates what properties make such a prior effective.

Method: 188 new experimental runs across four axes: (1) Content — testing priors built from correct vs. wrong feature families (magnitude bands vs. task-relevant features); (2) Supervision — comparing label-supervised priors against a fully label-free invariance prior using only commuted pairs (a,b)~(b,a); (3) Timing — applying the prior only during early windows (first 2000 epochs, 4% of budget) vs. continuous or later windows; (4) Setting — replicating across modular multiplication, depths, normalization variants, and conducting clamp sweeps to quantify weight-norm delay-law exponents.

Result: A prior from the wrong feature family blocks generalization (1/15 grok), indistinguishable from random partition (0/20; p=0.43). The label-free invariance prior generalizes in 15/15 runs with 2.7× median speedup, more reliably than label-supervised priors (p=0.038); combined with weight-norm clamp, it achieves 17× median speedup (5/5). Early-only application (first 2000 epochs) yields 2.7× speedup (10/10), outperforming both continuous (8/10, 1.25×) and later-window application (2.1×). The clamp sweep reveals structure injection flattens the weight-norm delay-law exponent ~17-fold (31× vs. 1.22× slowdown per +10 norm units).

Conclusion: Feature-family alignment determines whether a prior permits generalization at all; invariance content alone (without labels) suffices for reliable acceleration; a brief early window captures nearly all benefit. These findings refine understanding of how and when contrastive priors accelerate grokking.

Abstract: Companion work showed the grokking delay is causally the time to form task-structured representations, injectable via a contrastive prior. Here we characterize what makes such a prior work, across four axes, in 188 new runs. Content: a coherent, learnable prior built from the wrong feature family (magnitude bands) blocks generalization like a random partition (1/15 vs 0/20 grok; $p=0.43$ between them), confirming the companion's prediction that priors act at the level of the circuit's features. Supervision: a fully label-free invariance prior -- positives are commuted pairs $(a,b)\sim(b,a)$ only -- generalizes in 15/15 runs at a median $2.7\times$ speedup, more reliably than the label-supervised prior itself ($p=0.038$), and combined with a weight-norm clamp yields the strongest method we test (median $17\times$, 5/5) -- strongest meaning reliably fast: plain cross-entropy with a clamp matches this speed only at the exact critical norm, while the prior keeps it fast across the entire clamp range. Timing: the prior is only needed early -- applied solely during the first 2000 epochs (4% of budget) it generalizes 10/10 at $2.7\times$, beating continuous application (8/10, $1.25\times$) and a duration-matched later window ($2.1\times$). Setting: the dissociation replicates on modular multiplication and across depths and normalization variants, and a clamp sweep quantifies the companion's central claim: structure injection flattens the weight-norm delay-law exponent about 17-fold (plain cross-entropy slows $31\times$ per +10 norm units, a lower bound as higher cells are censored, versus $1.22\times$ with the prior). Honest boundary: tasks that generalize before memorizing have no delay to control. Feature-family alignment decides whether a prior permits generalization; invariance content suffices for acceleration without labels; a brief early window captures nearly all of the benefit.

</details>


### [129] [Constraint-Aware Aggregation for Federated Reinforcement Learning in Microgrid Energy Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12763)
*Usman Haider,Karl Mason*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes lightweight, constraint-aware aggregation rules for Federated RL that incorporate penalty-based weighting to reduce system-level constraint violations in distributed energy coordination, demonstrating improved safety and reward over FedAvg across synthetic and real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard FedAvg aggregation in Federated RL ignores system-level constraints (e.g., shared grid capacity), leading to unsafe global policies in distributed energy resource coordination, where local data privacy must be preserved.

Method: The authors propose server-side aggregation rules that weight client contributions by both local performance and estimated constraint violation. The primary approach is a penalty-based rule: w_i ∝ R_i − αV_i, where R_i is local reward and V_i is constraint violation. A combined reward-violation scheme with a tunable λ parameter is also explored. No dual optimization or local training modifications are required.

Result: Evaluated on DairyGridEnv (synthetic) and real load-driven demand profiles from Finland and the German FIELD dataset. Across multiple seeds, penalty-based aggregation substantially reduces constraint violations while improving reward relative to FedAvg. The λ-based combined scheme exposes a tunable trade-off but is less stable.

Conclusion: Lightweight, server-side aggregation strategies can substantially improve empirical safety in Federated RL without altering local training or communication protocols, with the simple penalty-based rule providing the most reliable reward-safety trade-off.

Abstract: Federated Reinforcement Learning (FedRL) enables coordination of distributed energy resources without sharing raw local data, but standard aggregation methods such as FedAvg do not account for system-level constraints, often leading to unsafe global behavior. In this work, we study constraint-aware aggregation for federated reinforcement learning in distributed energy coordination. We propose aggregation rules that incorporate both local performance and estimated constraint violation into the server-side update. Among these, a simple penalty-based rule, $w_i \propto R_i - αV_i$, consistently provides the most reliable trade-off between reward and safety, without requiring dual optimization or modifications to local training. \textcolor{black}{We evaluate our approach on DairyGridEnv, a benchmark modeling multiple farms coordinating battery storage under stochastic demand and a shared grid capacity constraint, and further assess robustness using real load-driven demand profiles from Finland and the German FIELD dataset. Across multiple seeds, penalty-based aggregation substantially reduces violations while improving reward relative to FedAvg in both synthetic and real load-driven settings.} A combined reward-violation scheme exposes a tunable trade-off via $λ$, but is less stable. These results demonstrate that lightweight aggregation strategies can substantially improve empirical safety in federated reinforcement learning while preserving standard communication protocols.

</details>


### [130] [AVQ-Attention: Adaptive Vector-Quantized Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12789)
*Winfried van den dool,Patrick Forré,Amir Habibian,Yuki M. Asano,Max Welling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AVQ Attention adaptively allocates VQ codebook capacity based on attention importance, refining high-importance keys with child codewords while keeping low-importance regions coarse — all implemented efficiently within Flash Attention's tiled paradigm, preserving O(MN) complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard VQ attention uses a fixed-size codebook uniformly across all key tokens, which is wasteful: high-attention regions get the same coarse representation as low-attention regions, leading to suboptimal accuracy-efficiency trade-offs. The key insight is that attention mass is highly non-uniform, so codebook capacity should be allocated adaptively.

Method: Starting from a small base codebook, AVQ Attention performs adaptive refinement during the forward pass: (1) identifies the most important codewords via attention-based importance scoring, (2) replaces them with pre-learned child codewords that provide finer-grained quantization, and (3) implements this entire process using custom Triton kernels within Flash Attention's tiled computation to minimize overhead while maintaining O(MN) complexity.

Result: The method achieves superior accuracy-efficiency trade-offs compared to fixed-codebook VQ attention baselines while maintaining the same asymptotic O(MN) complexity.

Conclusion: Adaptive capacity allocation in VQ attention is both feasible and beneficial — by concentrating fine-grained quantization where attention mass concentrates, AVQ Attention improves efficiency without sacrificing accuracy, and can be integrated into Flash Attention with minimal overhead.

Abstract: The $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ complexity of attention over $N$ tokens remains a computational bottleneck in transformer models. Vector-Quantized (VQ) attention reduces this to $\mathcal{O}(MN)$ by representing keys with $M$ codewords, but applies uniform codebook capacity regardless of where attention mass concentrates: high-attention regions of key space may be coarsely approximated while low-attention regions waste representational capacity. We propose Adaptive Vector-Quantized (AVQ) Attention, which adaptively allocates codebook capacity based on attention importance. Starting from a small set of codewords, our method identifies the most important codes during the forward pass and refines them with pre-learned child codewords, achieving fine-grained quantization where it matters most while maintaining coarse quantization elsewhere. We develop an implementation using custom Triton kernels that enables the full adaptive refinement process, including importance scoring, child codeword insertion, and parent contribution replacement, to be carried out within the tiled computation paradigm of Flash Attention with minimal overhead.
