Our approach maintains $\mathcal{O}(MN)$ complexity while achieving improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs compared to fixed-codebook VQ-attention.

</details>


### [131] [Verifier-Based Reinforcement Fine-Tuning of Reasoning Models for Thermal Energy Storage Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12856)
*Takumi Shioda,Kohei Terashima,Tatsuo Nagai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Open-weight reasoning LLMs fine-tuned via RLVR using DP-derived dense rewards can schedule TES setpoints near-optimally, with performance driven by stabilized planning patterns rather than new strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MPC and RL for building TES scheduling are difficult to scale across heterogeneous buildings. The authors explore whether open-weight reasoning LLMs, adapted via reinforcement fine-tuning with verifiable rewards, can serve as scalable, near-optimal schedulers using only text-based states and forecasts.

Method: The authors convert exact offline DP action values into dense per-action rewards (RLVR). An open-weight reasoning model is fine-tuned with only 30 training prompts to output hourly heat-pump setpoints. Evaluation uses a simple office-building TES benchmark where the DP optimum is known. Comparisons include GPT-5 (no fine-tuning), GPT-4o (non-reasoning), MPC, and a no-storage baseline. Trace analysis examines planning patterns; robustness tests cover forecast errors, unseen TES conditions, and a battery task.

Result: RFT reduces the open-weight model's emissions from 70.5 to 61.2 kg-CO₂, close to the DP optimum (60.8 kg-CO₂). GPT-5 nearly matches DP/MPC without task-specific training. GPT-4o performs worse than the no-storage baseline. Trace analysis reveals RFT stabilizes candidate comparison, look-ahead, and feasibility checking rather than creating new strategies. Reinforced planning patterns persist under forecast errors and an unseen TES condition, and carry over partially to a battery task.

Conclusion: DP-based verifiable rewards offer a practical path for adapting open-weight reasoning models to building storage scheduling. Results motivate higher-fidelity whole-building control tests and scalable verifiers for city-scale energy management.

Abstract: Buildings are expected to shift cooling loads in response to grid conditions. Thermal energy storage (TES) enables this shift, but scheduling it well requires planning hours ahead under storage constraints. Model predictive control (MPC) and reinforcement learning are difficult to scale across buildings. This study instead adapts an open-weight reasoning model through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We convert exact offline dynamic-programming (DP) action values into dense rewards for every candidate action. Using only 30 training prompts, reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) trains the model as an upper-level scheduler that outputs hourly heat-pump setpoints from text-based states and forecasts. Evaluation uses a deliberately simple office-building TES benchmark where exact DP is tractable and the optimum is known. RFT reduces the open-weight model's emissions from 70.5 to 61.2 kg-CO2, close to the DP optimum of 60.8 kg-CO2. GPT-5 nearly matches DP and MPC without task-specific training, while GPT-4o, a non-reasoning LLM, produces higher emissions than the no-storage baseline, so inference-time reasoning appears important. Trace analysis shows that RFT mainly stabilizes observable planning patterns (candidate comparison, look-ahead, and feasibility checking) rather than creating a new strategy. Robustness and generalization tests clarify what transfers: the reinforced planning patterns persist under forecast errors and an unseen TES condition and carry over to a battery task, but its different structure limits the gains. DP-based verifiable rewards offer a practical way to adapt open-weight reasoning models to building storage scheduling. These results motivate higher-fidelity tests of whole-building control and scalable verifiers for city-scale energy management.

</details>


### [132] [Energy-Based Physics-Informed Form Finding for Clustered Tensegrity Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12888)
*Jing Qin,Muhao Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an energy-based learning framework for clustered tensegrity structures that simultaneously predicts equilibrium nodal configurations and physical properties (member forces, force densities) by embedding total potential energy minimization and constitutive relations into the training objective, improving physical consistency, robustness, and data efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tensegrity form-finding and physical property prediction are fundamentally challenging inverse problems due to strong nonlinearity from geometry-force coupling, structural stability requirements, constraint enforcement (boundary conditions, symmetry), and the susceptibility of traditional methods to noise and outliers. A learning-based approach that respects physical laws is needed.

Method: An energy-based learning framework that integrates total potential energy minimization and constitutive relations directly into the training loss function. This allows the model to jointly predict equilibrium nodal configurations and associated physical quantities such as member forces and force densities, while physical consistency is enforced through energy-based losses during training.

Result: Numerical experiments on tensegrity prism and lander systems demonstrate the framework's capability for scalable form-finding and accurate prediction of structural properties, showing strong potential compared to traditional approaches.

Conclusion: Incorporating energy-based physical losses into the learning process significantly enhances physical consistency, robustness to noise/outliers, and data efficiency, making the approach promising for scalable tensegrity form-finding and property prediction tasks.

Abstract: Tensegrity form-finding and physical property prediction are fundamental inverse problems in structural mechanics, which aim to determine equilibrium configurations and internal force distributions. These problems are challenging due to strong nonlinearity arising from the coupling between geometry and forces, the need to ensure structural stability, and the enforcement of constraints such as boundary conditions and symmetry. Moreover, traditional methods often lack robustness to noise and outliers. This paper proposes an energy-based learning framework for clustered tensegrity form finding and physical property prediction. The proposed approach incorporates total potential energy minimization and constitutive relations into the training objective, enabling the simultaneous prediction of equilibrium nodal configurations and associated physical quantities, including member forces and force densities. By incorporating energy-based physical losses directly into the learning process, the framework improves physical consistency, robustness, and data efficiency. Numerical experiments on tensegrity structures, including prism and lander systems, show the great potential of the proposed approach and demonstrate its capability for scalable form finding and accurate prediction of structural properties.

</details>


### [133] [Contrastive-Collapsed Loss for Flexible and Geometrically Optimal Embeddings and Faster Convergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12916)
*Blanca Cano-Camarero,Ángela Fernández-Pascual,José R. Dorronsoro*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoCo is a novel loss function for representation learning that enforces intra-class collapse and inter-class separation with large angular margins. It offers theoretical advantages over dot regression and cross-entropy, including better initialization proximity, more informative gradients, and stronger collapse incentives. Experiments on OpenML-CC18 tabular datasets demonstrate competitive performance against SOTA methods, tighter class clustering, and faster convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing loss functions like cross-entropy and dot regression do not sufficiently encourage well-structured, normalized representations with clear intra-class compactness and inter-class angular separation. The authors aim to design a loss that explicitly drives class-wise collapse and angular contrast while maintaining flexibility for neural networks to learn geometrically optimal embeddings.

Method: The authors propose CoCo (Collapse and Contrast), a loss function that jointly optimizes for intra-class collapse (pulling same-class representations together) and inter-class contrast (pushing different-class representations apart with large angular margins). They provide a theoretical analysis comparing CoCo to dot regression and cross-entropy, examining properties such as distance to optimal initialization, gradient informativeness, and collapse incentives. Empirical evaluation is conducted on tabular datasets from the OpenML-CC18 benchmark against kernel SVM, Random Forest, dot regression, and cross-entropy-based neural networks.

Result: CoCo achieves competitive predictive performance with state-of-the-art methods on OpenML-CC18 tabular benchmarks. Both theoretical analysis and empirical results confirm that CoCo promotes tighter class clustering and faster convergence compared to alternative loss functions.

Conclusion: CoCo is an effective loss function for learning discriminative, well-structured representations. It combines strong theoretical properties with competitive empirical performance, making it a compelling alternative to cross-entropy and dot regression for representation learning tasks.

Abstract: In this work, we introduce CoCo, a loss function aimed at learning normalized and well-structured representations. The proposed loss encourages intra-class collapse and inter-class contrast while preserving sufficient flexibility for neural networks to approximate geometrically optimal embeddings with large angular separation between classes. We provide a theoretical analysis positioning CoCo with respect to related objectives such as dot regression and cross-entropy, showing that the new proposed loss benefits from closer initialization to the optimal configuration, more informative gradients, and stronger incentives for class-wise representation collapse. Extensive experiments on diverse tabular datasets from the OpenML-CC18 benchmark show that CoCo achieves competitive performance with state-of-the-art methods, including kernel SVM, Random Forest, dot regression, and cross-entropy-based neural networks. In addition, both theoretical arguments and empirical analyses demonstrate that the proposal promotes tighter class clustering and faster convergence. These results highlight CoCo loss as an effective objective for learning discriminative representations while maintaining competitive predictive performance.

</details>


### [134] [Efficient Sequential Calibration with $O(T^{2/3-ε})$ Error Bound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12928)
*Zihan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents an efficient randomized online forecaster that achieves expected calibration error of O(T^{2/3-ε}), improving upon the classical T^{2/3} barrier, by combining SPR-Calibration with an outer Blackwell-style correction layer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcoming the classical T^{2/3} barrier for calibration error in the online binary sequential calibration problem, building upon the recent breakthrough by Dagan et al. (2024).

Method: The forecaster combines two components: (1) the SPR-Calibration procedure from Dagan et al. (2024) that controls calibration with respect to a surrogate sequence of conditional-mean estimates, and (2) an outer Blackwell-style correction layer that controls the additional error when these surrogates approximate true outcomes. The analysis decomposes total calibration error into surrogate calibration error (bounded by SPR-Calibration guarantees) and residual discrepancy (controlled via a quadratic potential argument leveraging the sparsity of SPR-Calibration).

Result: An efficient randomized forecaster achieving expected calibration error of O(T^{2/3-ε}) for some constant ε > 0, breaking the classical T^{2/3} barrier.

Conclusion: The proposed method successfully breaks the T^{2/3} calibration error barrier through a novel two-layer architecture that separates surrogate calibration from outcome approximation, with the sparsity of SPR-Calibration enabling effective error control.

Abstract: We study the online binary sequential calibration problem. A recent breakthrough by \citet{dagan2024breaking} overcomes the classical \(T^{2/3}\) barrier for calibration error. Building on this result, we present an efficient randomized forecaster that achieves an expected calibration error \(O(T^{2/3-\varepsilon})\) for some constant \(\varepsilon>0\).
  Our forecaster combines the \textsc{SPR-Calibration} procedure \citep{dagan2024breaking} with an outer Blackwell-style correction layer. The \textsc{SPR-Calibration} procedure controls calibration with respect to a surrogate sequence of conditional-mean estimates, while the correction layer controls the additional error incurred when these surrogates are used to approximate the true outcomes. The analysis decomposes the total calibration error into the surrogate calibration error and the residual discrepancy between the surrogate sequence and the true outcomes. The former is bounded by the \textsc{SPR-Calibration} guarantee in \citet{dagan2024breaking}, and the latter is controlled using a quadratic potential argument together with the sparsity of the \textsc{SPR-Calibration} forecaster.

</details>


### [135] [The Spectrum Is Not Enough: When Context Helps Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13006)
*Mert Onur Cakiroglu,Mehmet Dalkilic,Hasan Kurban*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Spectral predictability indices (e.g., from power spectrum) cannot answer whether adding context (longer lookback, retrieval, foundation models) will improve forecasting, because such indices are phase-invariant while beyond-second-order structure (exploited by retrieval/foundation models) is phase-sensitive. The authors prove this impossibility, introduce the "coverage deficit" diagnostic to measure beyond-spectrum structure, and demonstrate empirically that retrieval gains vanish under phase-randomized surrogates while linear-window gains persist.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners misuse spectral predictability indices — designed to measure how predictable a series is from its spectrum — to decide whether adding context (longer lookback, retrieval, pretrained models) will help. These are fundamentally different questions: context value depends on the operating point, not just the series. Spectral indices are phase-invariant (asymptotically Gaussian), but retrieval and foundation models exploit beyond-second-order structure that is phase-sensitive. No prior work formalizes this distinction or provides a diagnostic for it.

Method: The authors: (1) prove an impossibility result showing spectral indices cannot capture beyond-spectrum structure; (2) construct surrogate pairs that fix both the spectrum and marginal distribution via phase randomization (producing asymptotically Gaussian series); (3) introduce the "coverage deficit" — a label-free, configuration-level diagnostic whose principal term measures beyond-spectrum structure as the gain of analog prediction over linear prediction; (4) evaluate on seven benchmarks using window-keyed retrieval, foundation models, and linear windows, with leave-one-dataset-out validation.

Result: On seven benchmarks: retrieval's value collapses across surrogate pairs (ECL median +33% → -35%, p < 10⁻⁴⁰) while every spectral index remains frozen; foundation model value splits into a surviving second-order part and a small beyond-linear margin that collapses; longer linear window value survives. Leave-one-dataset-out, the structure term predicts the sign of beyond-spectrum value where spectral indices fail, and the reverse holds for second-order mechanisms.

Conclusion: The key contribution is not a new forecaster but a conceptual distinction between spectral predictability and context value, a controlled comparison methodology using surrogate pairs, and the coverage deficit diagnostic to guide deployment decisions about whether retrieval or foundation models will add value beyond linear second-order methods.

Abstract: A growing family of indices scores how predictable a series is from its spectrum. Practitioners increasingly read these scores as answering a different question: whether \emph{adding context}, a longer lookback, a retrieval plug-in, or a pretrained model, will help. These are not the same question. The value of context is a property of the operating point, not of the series. Any index built from the power spectrum is invariant under phase randomization, whereas the beyond-second-order value that retrieval and foundation models supply is not, because a phase-randomized series is asymptotically Gaussian. We state this as an impossibility result and isolate it with surrogate pairs that fix the spectrum and the marginal by construction. We then give a label-free, configuration-level diagnostic, the coverage deficit, whose principal term measures beyond-spectrum structure as the gain of analog over linear prediction. On seven benchmarks the prediction holds: window-keyed retrieval's value collapses across surrogate pairs (ECL median $+33\%\!\to\!-35\%$, $p{<}10^{-40}$) while every spectral index stays frozen; a foundation model's value splits into a surviving second-order part and a small beyond-linear margin that collapses; a longer linear window's value survives. Leave-one-dataset-out, the structure term predicts the sign of beyond-spectrum value where the spectral indices trail it, and the reverse holds for the second-order mechanism. We introduce no new forecaster; the contribution is the distinction, a controlled comparison, and a diagnostic for the deployment decision. Code: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/SINE.

</details>


### [136] [TerraZero: Procedural Driving Simulation for Zero-Demonstration Self-Play at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13028)
*Zhouchonghao Wu,Akshay Rangesh,Weixin Li,Wei-Jer Chang,Zachary Lee,Tim Wang,Wei Zhan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TerraZero is a procedural driving simulator and self-play training stack that achieves 1.3M agent-steps/sec on a single GPU, trains RL policies from scratch with zero human demonstrations, and achieves state-of-the-art results on long-tail benchmarks while generalizing zero-shot across cities and driving conventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing driving simulators suffer from a speed-realism-diversity trade-off: fast simulators lack heterogeneous agents and traffic rules, while realistic ones are too slow for RL at scale. Logged data lacks safety-critical long-tail scenarios. TerraZero addresses all three requirements simultaneously — speed for scalable RL, realism from real-world map geometry, and unbounded diversity through procedural randomization.

Method: TerraZero uses a configurable C engine with CPU simulation and GPU policy inference over a zero-copy path (1.3M agent-steps/sec). It sources only map geometry from logged data, populating maps with randomized rule-based road users, signal controllers, and per-episode randomization of agent dynamics, rewards, and sizes. Training uses a compute-efficient self-play RL recipe across GPUs with no human demonstrations, no fallback planners, and no reference trajectories at inference.

Result: TerraZero is the first fully learned policy to top the InterPlan long-tail benchmark, ranks among the best on val14 with the best collision and TTC scores, outperforms other demonstration-free methods on Waymo Open Sim Agents realism, and is competitive with the strongest reference-anchored self-play method. Policies generalize zero-shot across cities and datasets, including emergent left-hand-traffic driving.

Conclusion: A single unified stack — combining a fast procedural simulator with pure self-play RL — can produce driving policies and sim agents that simultaneously achieve top-tier long-tail safety, routine-driving performance, and traffic realism, all without human demonstrations or hand-crafted planners.

Abstract: Training robust autonomous driving agents requires a simulator that is fast enough for reinforcement learning at scale, realistic enough to ground behavior in real-world map structure, and diverse enough to cover the safety-critical long tail that logged data rarely contains. We present TerraZero, a procedural driving simulator and self-play training stack. A configurable C engine runs simulation on the CPU and policy inference on the GPU over a zero-copy path, sustaining 1.3M agent-steps per second on a single server-grade GPU, far faster than existing object-level simulators, while keeping fidelity lighter single-agent systems omit: heterogeneous agents, multiple dynamics models, and full traffic-rule enforcement. TerraZero treats logged data only as a source of real-world map geometry, populating each map with randomized rule-based road users and signal controllers and randomizing agent dynamics, rewards, and sizes per episode, so a map yields an unbounded set of scenarios. Every reported policy trains from scratch by reinforcement learning alone on a compute-efficient self-play recipe across GPUs, with zero human demonstrations and no fallback planner at inference. Policies generalize zero-shot across cities and datasets, including emergent left-hand-traffic driving without explicit supervision. As an ego policy, TerraZero is the first fully learned policy to top the InterPlan long-tail benchmark, ahead of larger learned planners; on routine-driving val14 it ranks among the best approaches and is the safest, posting the best collision and time-to-collision scores. On Waymo Open Sim Agents realism the same recipe outperforms other demonstration-free methods and is competitive with the strongest reference-anchored self-play method. One stack serves both roles: driving policies across dynamics for cars and trucks, and sim agents that jointly control vehicles, pedestrians, and cyclists.

</details>


### [137] [The Seriality Gap in Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13031)
*Jorge Diaz Chao,Konpat Preechakul,Yuxi Liu,Yutong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Video diffusion models exhibit a "seriality gap" — performance degrades on multi-ball collision dynamics as causal chains lengthen, not due to video length but because denoising steps fail to provide scalable serial computation. Autoregressive generation and deeper architectures mitigate this, and the authors prove denoising adds no serial compute beyond the backbone for deterministic prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard video diffusion models struggle with tasks requiring sequential causal reasoning (e.g., multi-ball collisions), but the root cause — video length vs. dependent-event structure — was unclear. The authors aim to isolate whether the degradation stems from longer videos or from the growing serial dependency chain itself.

Method: The authors design controlled experiments using multi-ball hard-sphere dynamics. They compare: (1) multi-ball sequences (growing causal chain) vs. length-matched single-ball controls (no ball-ball interactions), (2) standard bidirectional diffusion vs. autoregressive/blockwise generation and deeper architectures (which increase effective serial computation), and (3) provide a theoretical proof analyzing whether denoising steps contribute serial computation beyond the backbone for deterministic video prediction.

Result: Performance degradation scales with causal chain length, not video length (single-ball control shows no degradation). Interventions increasing effective serial computation — autoregressive generation, blockwise generation, and architectural depth — disproportionately improve performance. The proof shows denoising steps add no serial computation beyond the backbone for deterministic prediction, revealing a structural limitation.

Conclusion: There exists a fundamental "seriality gap" in standard video diffusion: the denoising loop does not provide scalable serial compute, creating a structural obstacle for tasks requiring sequential reasoning and simulation. Increasing serial computation through architecture or generation strategy is necessary to bridge this gap.

Abstract: When one ball strikes another, then another, video models should predict the consequences of each bounce. In controlled experiments on multi-ball hard-sphere dynamics, we find that the performance of standard bidirectional video diffusion degrades as the causal chain lengthens, even when provided more denoising steps. In a length-matched single-ball control, where ball-ball interactions are absent, the degradation largely disappears, isolating dependent-event structure rather than video length as the cause. Across intervention studies, methods that increase effective serial computation improve performance disproportionately, including autoregressive/blockwise generation and architectural depth. We identify this pattern as the seriality gap: a mismatch between tasks requiring growing serial computation and video diffusion models whose denoising loop does not provide scalable serial compute. We then prove that, for deterministic video prediction, denoising steps do not add serial computation beyond the backbone, indicating a structural obstacle for video diffusion on serial reasoning and simulation tasks.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [138] [Predicting Acceptance and Review Effort in Human and Agent Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12057)
*Kartik Ghanshyambhai Pansuriya,Ehsan Ghorbani,Deepak Singh,Eman Abdullah AlOmar*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether pull request (PR) outcomes — specifically acceptance likelihood and review effort — can be predicted at submission time using only pre-review features. Using the AIDev dataset with leakage-aware modeling, tree-based classifiers achieve F1 > 0.95 for acceptance prediction, while review-effort metrics (comment count, time-to-merge) remain harder to forecast, limited by factors beyond the PR itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI coding agents increasingly contribute PRs alongside humans, maintainers need scalable ways to triage incoming submissions. The key question is whether a PR's fate can be estimated at the moment it is opened — before any reviewer discussion, CI/CD feedback, or merge decisions — enabling proactive prioritization and resource allocation.

Method: The authors construct a leakage-aware prediction pipeline on the AIDev dataset, strictly limiting features to submission-time information: PR textual characteristics, metadata, repository context, temporal signals, and lightweight diff statistics. They evaluate classical ML models (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, Gradient Boosting, Extra Trees, MLPs) under multiple data views: pooled (human + agent), human-only, agent-only, and balanced contributor splits.

Result: Acceptance prediction is highly feasible: tree-based models achieve F1 scores above 0.95, with textual clarity and metadata emerging as top predictors. Review-effort prediction (comment counts, time-to-merge) is substantially harder — submission-time features explain only a modest portion of variance, indicating that reviewer availability, project workflows, and team-specific practices dominate these outcomes.

Conclusion: Early PR outcome models can serve as effective advisory tools for triage and reviewer prioritization, but should not replace human judgment in automated decision-making, particularly for review-effort estimation where contextual factors outside the PR are decisive.

Abstract: Pull requests (PRs) are a central mechanism for reviewing and integrating code changes in modern software repositories. As AI coding agents begin to submit more code changes alongside human developers, maintainers face a new challenge: deciding which PRs are likely to be accepted and which ones may require substantial review effort. This paper studies whether such outcomes can be estimated at the time a PR is opened, before reviewer discussion, CI feedback, or merge decisions are available. Using the AIDev dataset, we construct a leakage-aware prediction pipeline for human- and agent-authored PRs. The feature set is limited to submission-time information, including PR text characteristics, metadata, repository context, temporal signals, and lightweight diff statistics. We evaluate classical machine-learning models, including Logistic Regression, Random Forests, Gradient Boosting, Extra Trees, and MLPs, across pooled, human-only, agent-only, and balanced contributor views. Our results show that acceptance prediction is feasible from early signals: tree-based models achieve F1 scores above 0.95, with textual clarity and metadata among the most influential predictors. Review-effort prediction is more difficult. Comment counts and time-to-merge are only modestly explained by submission-time features, suggesting that reviewer availability, project workflow, and team-specific review practices play a major role. These findings indicate that early PR models can support triage and reviewer prioritization, but should be used as advisory tools rather than automated decision-makers.

</details>


### [139] [AutoTrace: From Patches to Triggers via Agentic Interprocedural Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12058)
*Arastoo Zibaeirad,Marco Vieira,Thomas Zimmermann*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AutoTrace is an agentic pipeline that localizes vulnerability triggers (the specific statement turning a vulnerable state into an unsafe operation) by exploring code property graphs with LLM agents and deterministic evidence gates. It achieves 75% VulnHit on InterPVD, and the authors also contribute SinkTrace-Bench, a balanced source-to-sink dataset of 1,542 verifier-confirmed samples exposing the causal-reasoning gap in frontier LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vulnerability trigger localization is harder than binary vulnerability detection because it requires interprocedural, causal reasoning — the triggering statement often lies several call layers outside the patched function, beyond static rule sets and pattern-matching LMs. Existing approaches fail on such cases.

Method: AutoTrace uses an agentic pipeline that explores a Code Property Graph (CPG) layer by layer. LLM agents decide navigation, but deterministic admissibility gates control what evidence is required before reporting a trigger. Every trigger must be backed by explicit graph-derived evidence, never by ungrounded model judgment alone.

Result: AutoTrace achieves 75.0% VulnHit and 80.8% FuncHit on the InterPVD benchmark, surpassing prior SOTA. The authors also build SinkTrace-Bench (1,542 verifier-confirmed, perfectly balanced samples) and show that frontier LLMs struggle to separate matched vulnerable/safe pairs.

Conclusion: Combining LLM-guided exploration with deterministic evidence gates effectively bridges the interprocedural causal-reasoning gap in vulnerability trigger localization. The SinkTrace-Bench dataset reveals that even the strongest LLMs lack robust causal reasoning for source-to-sink vulnerability analysis.

Abstract: Given a vulnerability-fixing commit, trigger localization asks which specific statement turns the vulnerable program state into a concrete unsafe operation. This question is harder than binary vulnerability detection because the answer demands interprocedural, causal reasoning: in a substantial fraction of real-world CVEs the triggering statement lies several call layers outside the patched function, beyond the reach of static rule sets and pattern-matching language models alike. We present AutoTrace, an agentic pipeline that localizes vulnerability triggers by exploring a code property graph layer by layer, with LLM agents deciding where to look next and deterministic admissibility gates deciding what evidence is required before a trigger can be reported. Agents never accept a trigger on their own authority; every reported trigger is backed by explicit evidence drawn from the graph, so the pipeline covers both intra- and interprocedural vulnerabilities without relying on ungrounded model judgment. On the full InterPVD benchmark, AutoTrace reaches 75.0% VulnHit and 80.8% FuncHit, surpassing the prior state of the art on the same corpus. Building on the same machinery, we construct SinkTrace-Bench, a dataset that exposes each vulnerability as a source-to-sink (S2S) causal chain from attacker-controlled input through propagation to the dangerous operation, drawn from matched vulnerable and patched program states. It comprises 1,542 verifier-confirmed, perfectly balanced vulnerable/safe samples whose label fidelity we audit against expert annotations. Benchmarking frontier LLMs on it, we find that even the strongest struggle to separate the matched pairs, exposing the causal-reasoning gap that trigger localization targets. Artifact available at https://github.com/Erroristotle/AutoTrace.

</details>


### [140] [Beyond Test Presence: Assessing the Quality and Robustness of Agent-Generated Tests in Open-Source Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12068)
*Preet Jhanglani,Zeel Kaushal Desai,Vidhi Kansara,Eman Abdullah AlOmar*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: An empirical study of 204,673 test artifacts reveals AI coding agents surpass humans in edge-case coverage but produce flakier tests, while humans maintain a slight edge in assertion strength — exposing a "stealth technical debt" where agent-generated tests pass yet lack holistic quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation benchmarks (e.g., SWE-bench) judge AI-generated tests solely by pass-rates, ignoring intrinsic quality dimensions like coverage and semantic value. This creates a risk of "stealth technical debt" — tests that execute successfully but provide inadequate verification. The authors aim to fill this methodological gap by comparing human-authored and AI-generated tests across multiple quality dimensions.

Method: The authors conducted a large-scale empirical comparison of 204,673 test artifacts (24,941 human-authored, 179,732 AI-generated) from the AIDev dataset. They implemented a "white-box" static analysis framework using AST parsing with Python's `ast` module to evaluate three quality dimensions: Assertion Strength (RQ1), Edge-Case Coverage (RQ2), and Flakiness Potential (RQ3).

Result: AI agents outperformed humans in Edge-Case Coverage with nearly double the boundary-check variety (Variety Score: 0.62 vs 0.32) and higher null-safety testing rates (13.40% vs 8.3%). Humans held a slight advantage in Assertion Strength (88.1% vs 85.37% strong assertions). However, agent-generated tests showed higher flakiness risk (Candidate Rate: 0.41 vs 0.30), largely due to file I/O and non-deterministic logic dependencies.

Conclusion: AI agents excel at rigorous boundary testing but lack the "environmental awareness" required for stable, hermetic test design. The findings caution against relying solely on pass-rate metrics and advocate for multi-dimensional quality assessment of AI-generated test suites to mitigate stealth technical debt.

Abstract: The integration of AI-powered coding agents into Continuous Integration/Continuous Delivery (CI/CD) pipelines has fundamentally altered how software verification is conducted. While these agents successfully automate the test generation, current evaluation benchmarks (e.g., SWE-bench) largely focus on pass-rates rather than the intrinsic quality of the generated tests. This raises the possibility of "stealth technical debt", in which test suites pass execution but do not offer comprehensive coverage or semantic value. We address this methodological gap through a large-scale, empirical comparison of 204,673 test artifacts which comprises of 24,941 human-authored files and 179,732 agent-generated files; sourced from the AIDev dataset. Using the Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) parsing with Python's naive ast module, we implemented a "white-box" static analysis framework to evaluate three quality dimensions: Assertion Strength (RQ1), Edge-Case Coverage (RQ2), and Flakiness Potential (RQ3). Our results present a nuanced inversion of traditional assumptions. AI agents performed better than humans in Edge-Case Coverage, with almost twice the variety of boundary checks (Variety Score: 0.62 vs 0.32) and a higher frequency of null-safety testing (13.40% vs. 8.3%), even though human developers had a slight advantage in Assertion Strength (88.1% strong assertions vs. 85.37% for agents). But this thoroughness comes at a price: due mostly to their reliance on file I/O and non-deterministic logic, agent-generated tests exhibited a higher risk of flakiness (Candidate Rate: 0.41 vs. 0.30). These findings suggest that while AI agents excel at rigorous boundary testing, they lack the "environmental awareness" needed to write stable, hermetic tests.

</details>


### [141] [Fault Injection in OpenAPI Specifications for Evaluating Black-Box Testing Effectiveness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12101)
*Hamza Bin Mazhar,Yuqing Wang,Mika V. Mäntylä*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces a taxonomy of six OpenAPI specification fault classes, injects them at five severity levels into two microservice benchmarks, and evaluates their impact on three black-box API testing tools using four metric categories. Results show specification faults cause heterogeneous degradation, with some faults being "hidden" (undetected by coverage metrics but degrading request/response quality), demonstrating that specification quality critically shapes testing effectiveness and that code/specification coverage alone is insufficient for evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work on OpenAPI-based black-box testing tools for microservice systems assumes correct specifications and focuses on tool performance evaluation. However, real-world OpenAPI specifications are often incomplete, inconsistent, or incorrect. There is a gap in understanding how specification faults impact the effectiveness of these testing tools, which this paper aims to systematically address.

Method: The authors (1) develop a literature-grounded taxonomy of six OpenAPI specification fault classes; (2) inject these faults at five severity levels into OpenAPI specifications; (3) apply the mutated specifications to two microservice benchmarks (TrainTicket and SocialNetwork); (4) evaluate three black-box testing tools (EvoMaster, RESTler, and Schemathesis); and (5) measure impact using four metric categories: code coverage, specification coverage, request/response quality, and behavioral diversity.

Result: Specification faults cause strong and heterogeneous degradation patterns across tools and systems. Faults in method semantics cause broad degradation across all metrics. Modifications to response codes have weak impact. Relaxations of schema constraints produce "hidden degradation": no impact on code and specification coverage, but significant negative impact on request/response quality. These findings show that coverage-only evaluations understate specification fault impacts.

Conclusion: Specification quality directly shapes black-box API testing effectiveness in microservice systems. Evaluations relying solely on code and specification coverage can understate the impact of specification faults and should be complemented by request/response quality and behavioral diversity metrics.

Abstract: OpenAPI specifications are the primary input for black-box testing tools in microservice systems (MSS), yet prior work shows these specifications are often incomplete, inconsistent, or incorrect. Despite this, most studies on OpenAPI-based black-box testing assume correct specifications and evaluate tool performance. We address this gap by introducing a literature-grounded taxonomy of six OpenAPI specification fault classes. We inject faults at five severity levels, and evaluate the resulting mutated specifications on two microservice benchmarks, TrainTicket and SocialNetwork, using three testing tools: EvoMaster, RESTler, and Schemathesis. We measure the impact of these faults using code coverage, specification coverage, request/response quality, and behavioral diversity. Our results show that specification faults cause strong and heterogeneous degradation patterns across testing tools and systems. Faults in method semantics cause broad degradation across all metrics, while others, such as modifications to response codes, remain weak. Relaxations of schema constraints cause hidden degradation, with no impact on code and specification coverage but a large impact on request/response quality. These findings demonstrate that specification quality directly shapes black-box API testing effectiveness. Also, code and specification coverage-only evaluations can understate the impact of specification faults on black-box testing in MSS and should be complemented by request/response quality and behavioral diversity.

</details>


### [142] [TraceSynth: Generating Production-Quality Kernel Traces with Constraint-Guided Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12104)
*Yuvraj Sehgal,Sneh Patel,Mahsa Panahandeh,Naser Ezzati-Jivan,Francois Tetreault*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TraceSynth is a diffusion-based framework that generates synthetic kernel execution traces to augment limited real data for ML diagnostics. Using a Transformer-based denoising diffusion process with constraint-guided repair, it models traces as multi-channel sequences. Results show strong workload dependence, with context length (L=4096) as the dominant quality factor and lightweight 2-channel models retaining 97-99% of full-model performance at half the cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collecting production kernel execution traces in industrial systems is prohibitively expensive due to runtime overhead, storage requirements, and privacy constraints. This scarcity of real trace data limits the effectiveness of ML-based system diagnostics, creating a need for high-quality synthetic trace generation that can augment limited real datasets.

Method: TraceSynth employs a Transformer-based denoising diffusion probabilistic model that treats kernel traces as multi-channel sequences comprising event types, timestamps, CPU affinity, thread identifiers, and process metadata. The generation process is augmented with constraint-guided repair to enforce system invariants and ensure synthetic traces respect realistic kernel behavior. The framework supports variable context lengths and channel configurations, evaluated via ablation studies comparing full 6-channel versus lightweight 2-channel variants.

Result: On deterministic, compute-heavy workloads (scimark2), synthetic augmentation achieved 87.2% F1-Macro at context length L=4096, within 2.6 percentage points of real-only baselines. Context length proved the dominant quality factor: L=4096 yielded a 104% relative improvement over L=256. Constraint-guided repair improved synthetic data quality by up to 4.3%. Lightweight 2-channel models retained 97-99% of full 6-channel model performance at roughly half the computational cost.

Conclusion: TraceSynth provides cost-effective synthetic augmentation for kernel execution traces in production observability pipelines. The framework helps practitioners determine when synthetic data can viably substitute for scarce real traces, with context length and constraint enforcement being critical to output quality. The strong performance of lightweight channel configurations suggests practical deployment is feasible even under computational constraints.

Abstract: Machine learning models for system diagnostics rely on kernel execution traces to capture fine-grained system behavior, but collecting production traces in industrial systems is costly due to runtime overhead, storage demands, and privacy constraints. We present TraceSynth, a diffusion-based framework for generating synthetic kernel traces that augment limited real data for downstream ML tasks. TraceSynth models traces as multi-channel sequences (event types, timestamps, CPU affinity, thread identifiers, and process metadata) using a Transformer-based denoising diffusion process with constraint-guided repair to enforce system invariants. Across six benchmarks, results show strong workload dependence. For deterministic, compute-heavy workloads (scimark2), synthetic augmentation achieves 87.2% F1-Macro at context length L=4096, only 2.6 percentage points below real-only baselines. Context length is the dominant quality factor, with L=4096 yielding a +104% relative improvement over L=256, while constraint-guided repair improves synthetic data quality by up to 4.3%. Ablation studies show that lightweight 2-channel models retain 97-99% of the performance of full 6-channel models at roughly half the computational cost. TraceSynth supports cost-effective augmentation of kernel execution traces in production observability pipelines and helps identify when synthetic data can substitute for limited real traces.

</details>


### [143] [TestMiner: Software Testing Analysis for GitHub Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12223)
*Andre Hora,José Miguel Rojas,Romain Robbes*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TestMiner is a tool that helps users explore and understand software testing practices in GitHub repositories by providing test statistics, location, metrics across releases, and testing-related dependencies, validated through use in an undergraduate Software Testing course with positive student feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software testing practices vary significantly across projects and programming ecosystems — some emphasize unit tests, others focus on end-to-end testing, and test file organization differs (dedicated folders in Python/Java vs. co-location in Go/Rust). These variations make it difficult to understand how a project approaches testing, motivating the need for a dedicated exploration tool.

Method: The authors developed TestMiner, a tool that automatically extracts and presents testing-related information from GitHub repositories, including: (1) test statistics, (2) test file location patterns, (3) test metrics tracked across software releases, and (4) dependencies related to testing. The tool was evaluated through deployment in an undergraduate Software Testing course with 50 students who used it to explore real-world repositories.

Result: Students expressed positive feedback overall. They successfully used TestMiner to critically explore diverse testing practices including test organization, test evolution over releases, test fixtures, mocking strategies, and edge-case testing across real-world GitHub projects.

Conclusion: TestMiner effectively supports both educational and exploratory use cases for understanding software testing practices. It makes the testing landscape of GitHub repositories more accessible and interpretable, and the positive student reception suggests its value as a pedagogical tool in software testing education.

Abstract: Software systems have unique testing characteristics. Some projects can emphasize unit tests, while others may focus on end-to-end testing. Test organization may vary across ecosystems: in languages like Python and Java, tests are typically placed in dedicated folders, whereas Go and Rust projects commonly co-locate tests with source code. These distinctions make it harder to understand how a project approaches testing. In this paper, we present TestMiner, a tool for exploring software testing in GitHub repositories. TestMiner provides an overview of a project's testing practices, including test statistics, test location, test metrics across releases, and dependencies related to testing. We used TestMiner in an undergraduate Software Testing course, where 50 students explored the testing practices of real-world GitHub repositories. Overall, students expressed positive feedback regarding TestMiner. They were able to critically explore a variety of testing practices, including test organization, test evolution, test fixtures, mocking, and edge-case testing. TestMiner is available at: https://andrehora.github.io/testminer. Screencast: https://youtu.be/w1sBgLTq-7Y.

</details>


### [144] [WIP: Software Engineering Competencies in the Age of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12259)
*Lynn Vonderhaar,Massood Towhidnejad,Omar Ochoa*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes adding two new AI-related competencies — AI Literacy and AI Development — to the Software Engineering Competency Model (SWECOM), motivated by a literature review of industry AI needs, and analyzes those needs to define the necessary skill areas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: University curricula, guided by accreditation bodies and competency models like SWECOM, often lag behind rapidly evolving industry needs. The rise of AI in industry has outpaced current competency models, creating a gap between what universities teach and what graduates need to succeed in an AI-driven workforce.

Method: The authors conducted a literature review to identify AI-related needs within industry, then used these findings to motivate and justify the addition of two new competencies (AI Literacy and AI Development) to SWECOM. They further analyzed the industry needs to propose specific skill areas that should constitute these new competencies.

Result: Two new competencies are proposed for SWECOM: (1) AI Literacy, and (2) AI Development. The paper identifies and outlines the necessary skill areas for each competency based on the industry needs surfaced in the literature review.

Conclusion: Competency models like SWECOM must evolve to incorporate AI competencies so that university programs can properly prepare graduates. The proposed AI Literacy and AI Development competencies, along with their skill areas, offer a concrete step toward aligning software engineering education with contemporary industry demands.

Abstract: A university education is meant to fully prepare graduates to enter and succeed in the workforce in their field. Accreditation bodies and professional organizations help to achieve this by publishing curriculum content, skills and competency models to outline the basics that universities must teach within a degree program. Although these curriculum guidelines and competency models are periodically updated to align university curricula with current industry needs, the needs of industry evolve rapidly and are often not properly reflected within the model, a situation which is occurring now with the rise of Artificial Intelligence (AI) use in industry. This paper reviews literature describing the AI needs within industry to motivate two new AI competencies, AI Literacy and AI development, within the Software Engineering Competency Model (SWECOM). This work then analyzes the industry needs described in the literature to propose the necessary skill areas for these new competencies.

</details>


### [145] [Code-MUE: Measuring Code LLMs' Uncertainty through Execution-based Semantic Interaction Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12273)
*Xiaoning Ren,Yinxing Xue,Lei Ma,Yuheng Huang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Code-MUE introduces a black-box uncertainty estimation framework for Code LLMs that uses execution-based Semantic Interaction Graphs and Von Neumann entropy to quantify semantic diversity, achieving up to -0.98 Spearman correlation with functional correctness, significantly outperforming text-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code LLMs are inherently stochastic, and minor errors can cause severe functional/security failures. Existing uncertainty methods fail for closed-source models (white/grey-box inapplicable) or rely on surface-level text similarity that fails to capture code's syntax-semantics gap (syntactic variation ≠ semantic divergence).

Method: Code-MUE is a purely black-box framework that constructs execution-based Semantic Interaction Graphs from sampled code solutions, then computes Von Neumann entropy over the solution space to quantify global semantic diversity as an uncertainty measure.

Result: Across 8 state-of-the-art LLMs, Code-MUE achieves Spearman's correlation up to -0.98 with functional correctness, significantly outperforming lexical and embedding-based baselines, and enables robust risk detection and selective prediction.

Conclusion: Execution-grounded semantic uncertainty estimation effectively bridges the syntax-semantics gap for Code LLMs, providing reliable risk detection in practical black-box workflows where model internals are inaccessible.

Abstract: As Code Large Language Models (LLMs) become central to modern software engineering, their inherent stochasticity poses significant real-world risks, where even minor errors can lead to severe functional, security, or safety consequences. Reliable automation, therefore, demands the ability to distinguish between confident, well-supported predictions and stochastic guessing. However, existing uncertainty estimation methods face a critical gap: white and grey-box techniques are often inapplicable to closed-source models, while standard "black-box" text metrics fail to capture the unique fragility of code, where syntactic variation does not always imply semantic divergence. To bridge this syntax-semantics gap, we introduce Code-MUE, a purely black-box framework that measures uncertainty through execution-based Semantic Interaction Graphs. Unlike prior approaches that rely on superficial textual similarity, Code-MUE grounds uncertainty in observable runtime behavior, calculating the Von Neumann entropy of the solution space to quantify global semantic diversity. A large-scale empirical study across eight state-of-the-art LLMs demonstrates that Code-MUE achieves a strong negative correlation with functional correctness (Spearman's correlation up to -0.98), significantly outperforming lexical and embedding-based baselines while enabling robust risk detection and selective prediction in practical workflows.

</details>


### [146] [XScientist: A Git-Like Research Protocol for Long-Running Autonomous Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12301)
*Jixiang Luo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: XScientist introduces a git-like research operating system that treats each autonomous research run as a portable, auditable artifact (ARA) rather than just a PDF, enabling continuous observable pipelines with provenance, re-execution hooks, and integrity forensics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autonomous research systems are evaluated as one-shot paper generators, which obscures the reality that research is long-running, branching, failure-prone, and requires auditable human-agent handoffs. This framing undermines trust and reproducibility.

Method: XScientist implements a git-like protocol and operating system that orchestrates the full research lifecycle—idea generation, experiment execution, drafting, self-review, repair, quality gating, and daemon scheduling—as one continuously observable pipeline. Its core innovation is the Agent-Native Research Artifact (ARA) protocol, which records an exploration DAG, per-node code/outputs, claim-to-evidence anchors, content hashes, provenance, and re-execution hooks, making each paper inspectable as a science exploration tree. Additional safeguards include deterministic integrity forensics, sample gates, truth contracts, and reviewer-oriented repair loops.

Result: The paper presents the XScientist architecture, the ARA protocol surface, and practical safeguards aimed at transitioning autonomous science from single-run demos to reproducible, reviewable, and forkable research infrastructure. Implementation and manuscript source are publicly available on GitHub.

Conclusion: By shifting from PDF-centric output to artifact-centric, continuously observable pipelines with strong provenance and re-execution guarantees, XScientist provides the foundational infrastructure needed for trustworthy, auditable autonomous research systems.

Abstract: Autonomous research systems are often evaluated as one-shot paper generators: given a topic, they produce a manuscript and a small set of experiment logs. This framing hides the operational problem that makes such systems difficult to trust: research is long-running, branching, failure-prone, and dependent on auditable handoffs between agents and humans. XScientist is a git-like research protocol and operating system for this setting. It orchestrates idea generation, experiment execution, manuscript drafting, self-review, repair, quality gating, daemon scheduling, and reproducibility artifacts as one continuously observable pipeline. The central design choice is to treat each run as a portable research artifact rather than only as a PDF. XScientist exports an Agent-Native Research Artifact (ARA), a protocol that records an exploration DAG, per-node code and outputs, claim-to-evidence anchors, content hashes, provenance, and re-execution hooks. This makes each generated paper inspectable as a science exploration tree: failed branches, repaired experiments, ablations, and manuscript claims remain connected to the nodes that produced them. The system also includes deterministic integrity forensics, sample gates, truth contracts, reviewer-oriented repair loops, and long-running daemon controls. This paper describes the current XScientist architecture, the ARA protocol surface, and the practical safeguards needed to move autonomous science from single-run demos toward reproducible, reviewable, and forkable research infrastructure. The implementation and manuscript source are maintained in the public GitHub repository at https://github.com/smileformylove/XScientist.

</details>


### [147] [How Many Interviews Are Enough in a Software Engineering Study? Preliminary Findings on Sample Size and Saturation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12326)
*Ronnie de Souza Santos,Italo Santos,Mauricio Rodrigues,Cleyton Magalhaes*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper systematically reviews how interview-based empirical software engineering studies report and justify sample adequacy and saturation. Analyzing publications from 2016–2025, the authors find wide variation in sample sizes, with 13–24 interviewees being the most common range, and heterogeneous, often implicit, saturation justifications. The study offers empirical insights into qualitative methodological reporting practices and calls for greater transparency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interview-based studies are prevalent in empirical software engineering for investigating human and socio-technical phenomena, yet the reporting of sample adequacy and saturation is inconsistent and lacks standardized practice. The authors aim to empirically characterize how these methodological concepts are operationalized across the field.

Method: A systematic literature analysis of papers published between 2016 and 2025 in major software engineering venues. The analysis focused on three dimensions: interview sample sizes, how saturation is discussed, and how sample adequacy is justified. Both quantitative patterns (sample size distributions) and qualitative dimensions (justification types) were examined.

Result: Sample sizes varied substantially. Studies with fewer than 12 interviewees were common and tied to specialized industrial or constrained-access contexts. The modal range was 13–24 interviewees. Saturation and adequacy justifications were heterogeneous; many studies relied on implicit or contextual reasoning rather than explicit methodological discussion, indicating a lack of standardized reporting practices.

Conclusion: The paper provides initial empirical evidence on methodological reporting practices in interview-based software engineering research. It highlights the predominance of moderate sample sizes, the variability in saturation discourse, and the need for greater rigor and transparency in qualitative research reporting within the field.

Abstract: Background. Interview based studies are widely used in empirical software engineering to investigate human, organizational, and socio technical phenomena, yet interview sample adequacy and saturation are reported inconsistently across the literature. Aims. This paper investigates how interview sample adequacy and saturation are operationalized in empirical software engineering research. Method. We analyzed papers published between 2016 and 2025 across major software engineering venues, focusing on interview sample sizes, saturation discussions, and sample adequacy justifications. Results. Preliminary findings indicate substantial variation in sample sizes, from highly specialized small sample studies to broader investigations involving large interview datasets. Studies involving fewer than 12 interviewees were common and frequently associated with specialized industrial contexts or constrained organizational access. However, the most recurrent range was 13 to 24 interviewees, suggesting that moderate sized samples represent the most common configuration in empirical software engineering research. Saturation and sample adequacy justification were heterogeneous, with many studies relying on implicit or contextual reasoning rather than explicit methodological discussion. Conclusions. Our findings provide initial empirical insights into methodological reporting practices in interview based software engineering research and contribute to ongoing discussions regarding qualitative rigor and transparency.

</details>


### [148] [Skills That Don't Exist: A Large-Scale Study of Hallucinated Skill Recommendation in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12340)
*Weifeng Yuan,Wenbo Guo,Feng Dong,Haoyu Wang,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LLM agents hallucinate skill names when recommending packages from open registries, enabling supply-chain attacks where adversaries pre-register malicious skills under those hallucinated names. A large-scale study (15K prompts, 12 configurations) reveals systemic hallucination rates averaging ~36%, with names that repeat reliably across prompts and models. Retrieval grounding reduces hallucination but cripples usefulness, demanding ecosystem-wide structural fixes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents increasingly download skills from open registries, but developers rely on agents to recommend skills by name — a process vulnerable to "skill name hallucination," where agents invent non-existent skill names. This opens a low-effort supply-chain attack vector: adversaries can monitor hallucinated names, pre-register them with malicious payloads, and wait for victims to install. No prior large-scale measurement of this vulnerability existed.

Method: The authors conducted the first large-scale measurement of skill name hallucination using 15,000 prompts across 12 configurations (4 standalone LLMs and 8 agent setups). A conservative counting method was applied: a name was classified as hallucinated only if absent from all live registries and GitHub. They also analyzed name repetition across prompts/models, tested four model-level defenses (including retrieval grounding), and evaluated the security–usability trade-off.

Result: Hallucination rates averaged 36.0% for standalone LLMs and 36.9% for agents, rising to 43.1% on real-world developer queries. In total, 5,669 distinct hallucinated names were generated. These names were not random — agents consistently repeated the same fake names across prompts and models, providing reliable attack targets. Retrieval grounding cut hallucination from 40.8% to 3.2% but severely degraded usefulness: the best-defended system recommended the correct skill only ~1/6 of the time.

Conclusion: Skill name hallucination is a systemic, highly exploitable vulnerability requiring minimal attacker effort. Prompt engineering and model-level tuning alone cannot fix it. The authors call for ecosystem-wide structural changes: registry-level name reservations and verified recommendation pipelines.

Abstract: LLM agents acquire new capabilities by downloading skills from open registries. Instead of browsing these catalogs manually, developers typically ask the agent to recommend and install a skill. This convenience hides a risk: agents frequently invent names for skills that exist in no registry. We term this flaw skill name hallucination. A fake name may seem harmless, but it opens the door to supply-chain attacks. Because registries rarely verify publishers, an adversary can prompt the agent, collect the fake names it returns, pre-register malicious skills under them, and wait for a victim to install the payload. We conducted the first large-scale measurement of skill name hallucination, evaluating 15,000 prompts across 12 configurations (4 standalone LLMs and 8 agents). We conservatively counted a name as hallucinated only if it was missing from all live registries and GitHub. The results reveal a systemic vulnerability: every configuration hallucinates. Rates average 36.0% for standalone LLMs and 36.9% for agents, rising to 43.1% on real-world developer questions. In total, the systems generated 5,669 distinct hallucinated names. Crucially, these names are not random noise. Agents repeat the same fake names across prompts and models, giving attackers highly reliable targets to hijack. Finally, we tested four model-level defenses and found a severe conflict between security and usability. The strongest, retrieval grounding, cut the hallucination rate from 40.8% to 3.2% but crippled usefulness: even the best-defended system recommended the correct skill only about one in six times. Skill name hallucination is thus a highly exploitable vulnerability requiring minimal attacker effort. Fixing it cannot rely on prompt engineering or model tuning alone. It demands ecosystem-wide structural changes: registry-level name reservations and verified recommendation pipelines.

</details>


### [149] [Detecting Rendering Bugs in Imperative Data Visualization Libraries via Equivalent Mutations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12363)
*Weiqi Lu,Yongqiang Tian*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VIZDETOUR detects rendering bugs in imperative visualization libraries by injecting semantically equivalent API call sequences into seed scripts. If the final rendered image differs from the original, a bug is flagged. It found 47 previously unknown bugs across matplotlib, bokeh, and plotly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imperative data visualization libraries (matplotlib, bokeh, plotly) suffer from rendering bugs that produce incorrect visual outputs without crashes, making automated detection difficult. The core challenge is the oracle problem: there is no specification of what an arbitrary plotting script should render. Additionally, stateful API calls can cause unintended side effects across graphic elements, leading to subtle rendering inconsistencies.

Method: VIZDETOUR transforms the oracle problem into an equivalence-checking problem. Starting from a seed plotting script, it appends endpoint-preserving mutation sequences — short series of semantically equivalent API calls that temporarily alter visualization state and then restore it. These mutations exercise different code paths but should produce identical final renderings. The approach involves: (1) constructing a render tree from the seed script, (2) identifying traceable graphic elements and mutable properties, (3) synthesizing mutation sequences that preserve the endpoint state, and (4) comparing original and mutated outputs via perceptual hashing. Any visual discrepancy indicates a rendering bug.

Result: VIZDETOUR discovered 47 previously unknown rendering bugs across matplotlib, bokeh, and plotly, using scripts from their official example galleries. Of these, 39 were confirmed by developers and 18 have been fixed.

Conclusion: Equivalence-based mutation testing is an effective strategy for automated detection of rendering bugs in imperative visualization libraries. By reframing the oracle problem as equivalence checking, VIZDETOUR bypasses the need for ground-truth specifications and successfully uncovers real, subtle bugs in widely-used libraries.

Abstract: Imperative data visualization libraries construct plots through a sequence of stateful API calls that incrementally create and update graphic elements. Rendering bugs in these libraries often manifest as incorrect visual outputs rather than crashes or exceptions, making them difficult to detect automatically. A fundamental challenge is the lack of an oracle that specifies the expected rendering of an arbitrary plotting script. Furthermore, an update to one graphic element may inadvertently affect other elements or properties, leading to subtle inconsistencies in the final rendered image.
  This paper presents VIZDETOUR, an automated testing approach for detecting rendering bugs in imperative data visualization libraries via equivalent mutations. The key idea is to transform the oracle problem into an equivalence-checking problem. Starting from a seed plotting script, VIZDETOUR appends a short sequence of semantically equivalent API calls that temporarily modify the visualization state and then restore it to its original state. Although these mutations exercise different execution paths, they should preserve the final rendering. Any visual discrepancy between the original and mutated scripts therefore indicates a rendering bug. To generate such mutations, VIZDETOUR constructs a render tree from the seed script, identifies traceable graphic elements and mutable properties, and synthesizes endpoint-preserving mutation sequences. It then compares the rendered outputs using perceptual hashing. We evaluate VIZDETOUR on matplotlib, bokeh, and plotly using scripts collected from their official example galleries. VIZDETOUR discovers 47 previously unknown bugs, of which 39 are confirmed and 18 are fixed.

</details>


### [150] [Automatic Testing of Interacting Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12452)
*Fabio Cavaleri,Alessio Gambi,Paolo Arcaini,Dejan Ničković,Mauro Pezzè*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: EVITA uses multi-objective optimization to generate diverse, minimal-complexity scenarios for testing multiple interacting autonomous vehicles, outperforming existing approaches in triggering safety-critical behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current scenario-based AV testing methods test a single AV and miss critical emergent behaviors arising from multi-AV interactions. Testing n-way interactions faces a combinatorial explosion problem that existing approaches do not adequately address.

Method: EVITA employs multi-objective optimization to simultaneously maximize the diversity of triggered AV interactions and minimize scenario complexity, enabling efficient generation of test scenarios for multiple interacting AVs.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that EVITA triggers a higher variety of AV interactions compared to state-of-the-art approaches, increasing the likelihood of revealing safety-critical behaviors.

Conclusion: EVITA effectively addresses the combinatorial explosion challenge in multi-AV scenario-based testing by using multi-objective optimization, revealing safety-critical scenarios that single-AV testing approaches overlook.

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles (AVs) must be thoroughly tested to meet high safety standards and avoid endangering both AV passengers and road users. Scenario-based testing implements driving scenarios in virtual simulation environments as a cost-effective alternative to field testing. Common scenario-based testing approaches set the environment and the surrounding traffic and test a single AV. Recent studies show that the approaches that test single AVs miss critical behaviors that emerge from interactions among multiple AVs. Effective approaches to test scenarios that emerge from n-way interactions must address the combinatorial explosion that the presence of multiple AVs further exacerbates. In this paper, we propose EVITA, an approach that leverages multi-objective optimization to generate scenarios that trigger multiple and diverse AVs interactions, while minimizing the complexity of the generated scenarios, to effectively test multiple interacting AVs and reveal safety-critical scenarios that current approaches overlook. The experimental results that we discuss in this paper confirm that EVITA triggers a higher variety of AVs interactions than state-of-the-art approaches, thus improving the likelihood to reveal safety-critical behaviors.

</details>


### [151] [CodeOwl: Automatic Generation of Tiered Parsons Problems for Introductory Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12460)
*Luca Cisternino,Florian Obermüller,Gordon Fraser*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CodeOwl is an AI-driven tool that automatically generates tiered Parsons problems from sample tasks or programming concepts, addressing learner heterogeneity. Evaluation across 297 sequences showed 98.7% achieved positive complexity progression, with strong expert ratings for clarity, though teachers desired more curriculum control and students wanted better feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manually creating differentiated instructional resources like tiered Parsons problems is labor-intensive, yet necessary to accommodate variations in student speed, prior knowledge, and motivation in programming education.

Method: CodeOwl automatically generates tiered Parsons problem sequences from input tasks or concepts. Evaluation used a mixed-method framework: automated complexity analysis of 297 three-tier sequences, expert ratings of problem clarity, and user studies with teachers and students.

Result: 98.7% of generated sequences exhibited a positive complexity increase from Tier 1 to Tier 3. Experts rated problem statements as highly clear. Teachers praised utility but wanted more curriculum alignment control; students requested enhanced feedback mechanisms and alternative interaction modes.

Conclusion: CodeOwl demonstrates that AI-driven automation of tiered Parsons problems is feasible and effective, though further work is needed on curriculum alignment controls and learner-facing interaction features.

Abstract: Addressing learner heterogeneity in programming education is challenging due to variations in student speed, prior knowledge, and motivation. While differentiated instruction, such as tiered sequences, allows students to engage at appropriate difficulty levels, manually creating these resources is labour-intensive. This paper introduces CodeOwl, an AI-driven tool that automates the generation of tiered Parsons problems. Starting from a sample task or specific programming concepts, CodeOwl produces tiered sequences of Parsons problems automatically. We evaluated CodeOwl with a mixed-method framework comprising complexity analysis, expert ratings, and user studies. Analysis of 297 tiered sequences (three tiers each) revealed that 98.7% achieved a positive complexity increase, successfully rising in difficulty from Tier 1 to Tier 3. Experts rated the generated problem statements as highly clear. While teachers praised the tool's utility, they identified a need for greater control over curriculum alignment. Similarly, students reported positively but requested enhanced feedback mechanisms and alternative interaction modes.

</details>


### [152] [Understanding before Naming! Enhancing LLM-based Method Name Prediction with Code Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12467)
*Wei Liu,Weisong Sun,Tingting Xu,Hanwei Qian,Yi Zhao,Chunrong Fang,Xia Feng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses two challenges in Method Name Prediction (MNP): the inadequacy of token-similarity metrics for evaluating semantic quality, and the limitation of direct code-to-name generation. Through empirical studies comparing evaluators and generation strategies, the authors propose SMNP — an approach combining MNP-oriented summarization with chain-of-thought enhanced refinement — demonstrating improved semantic quality and robustness across multiple LLMs and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MNP research suffers from two key gaps: (1) evaluation relies on token-similarity metrics (e.g., BLEU, ROUGE) that poorly correlate with human judgment of semantic quality, and (2) LLM-based methods use direct code-to-name generation, which diverges from how humans first understand functionality and then derive meaningful names.

Method: The authors conduct two empirical studies: (1) comparing 6 metric-based evaluators, 5 LLM-based evaluators, and 6 human evaluators to assess alignment with human judgment; (2) comparing direct generation versus summarization-and-refinement strategies for MNP. Based on findings (DeepSeek-based evaluators align best with humans; summarization+refinement improves semantic quality but suffers from inaccurate summaries, semantic misalignment, and close semantic scores), they propose SMNP, which integrates MNP-oriented summarization with chain-of-thought enhanced refinement.

Result: LLM-based evaluators, particularly DeepSeek-based ones, show stronger consistency with human judgments than traditional token-similarity metrics. The summarization-and-refinement strategy generally yields better semantic quality than direct generation. SMNP demonstrates effectiveness and robustness across 5 LLMs and 2 datasets.

Conclusion: LLM-based evaluation is a more reliable alternative to traditional metrics for MNP, and incorporating summarization with chain-of-thought refinement better emulates human naming processes, leading to semantically superior method name generation.

Abstract: Method names are critical to software quality, affecting code comprehensibility, maintainability, and developer collaboration. However, manually designing meaningful method names is challenging. Method Name Prediction (MNP), which automatically generates method names from code snippets, has recently attracted attention. Although large language models (LLMs) show promising performance for MNP, two challenges remain. First, existing evaluations mainly rely on token similarity metrics, which often fail to reflect human judgments of semantic quality. Second, current LLM-based MNP methods usually generate names through direct code-to-name mapping, which differs from the human process of understanding functionality before naming. To address these challenges, we conduct empirical studies on LLM-based evaluation and MNP strategies. We compare 6 metric-based evaluators, 5 LLM-based evaluators, and 6 human evaluators. Results show that LLM-based evaluators, especially DeepSeek-based evaluators, are more consistent with human judgments than traditional metrics. We further compare direct generation and summarization-and-refinement strategies. Results indicate that summarization and refinement generally improve the semantic quality of generated names. Case studies reveal three limitations: inaccurate summaries, semantic misalignment, and close semantic scores.
