Based on these findings, we propose SMNP, an MNP approach combining MNP-oriented summarization and chain-of-thought enhanced refinement. Experiments on 5 LLMs and 2 datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of SMNP.

</details>


### [153] [Agent-Safety Evaluations as Load-Bearing Evidence: A Vendor-Neutral, Cross-Harness Reconstructability Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12469)
*Oleg Solozobov*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces a reconstructability metric for agent-safety evaluations, measuring whether captured evidence can reproduce the decision a claim depends on. It defines eight decision-property classes, emits per-decision Evidence Sufficiency Cards, and implements a counterfactual-replay protocol. Results show sufficiency scores (0.458–0.833) vary widely across inputs with identical surface outcomes, and replay preconditions fail in all scored traces. A synthetic release-gate demonstration shows the metric can block insufficient evidence and pass instrumented variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent-safety evaluation results lack load-bearing evidentiary quality: identical nominal outcomes (task success, attack success, monitor scores) can rest on fundamentally different underlying evidence. There is no vendor-neutral, runnable instrument that scores reconstructability — whether the captured evidence can actually reconstruct the decision a claim relies on — as an evaluation-validity metric.

Method: The authors propose (1) a property-level reconstructability metric spanning eight decision-property classes; (2) a cross-harness adapter that emits per-decision Evidence Sufficiency Cards supporting a per-run monitor-coverage release check; (3) a counterfactual-replay intervention protocol with a replayability-precondition probe; and (4) a claim-evidence overclaim gap metric. Evaluation uses public and bundled traces without new model runs.

Result: Twelve-field sufficiency scores range from 0.458 to 0.833 across four inputs sharing identical surface readings. Replay preconditions remain unmet in every scored trace. In a synthetic release-gate experiment, the sufficiency gate blocks the raw variant (score 0.542) and passes the instrumented variant (score 0.667).

Conclusion: Safety-evaluation claims should be accompanied by their reconstructability vector. The authors provide a reproducibility package that regenerates every reported number, enabling downstream adoption of evidence-sufficiency gating in agent-safety evaluation pipelines.

Abstract: Many agent-safety evaluation results are not yet load-bearing evidence: identical nominal outcomes (task success, attack success, monitor scores) may sit atop materially different evidence regimes. No vendor-neutral, runnable instrument scores reconstructability as an evaluation-validity metric: whether captured evidence can reconstruct the decision a claim depends on. This paper introduces a property-level reconstructability metric over eight decision-property classes and a cross-harness adapter emitting per-decision Evidence Sufficiency Cards backing a per-run monitor-coverage release check. It specifies a counterfactual-replay intervention protocol, implements its replayability-precondition probe, and defines a claim-evidence overclaim gap. On public and bundled traces, without new model runs, twelve-field sufficiency spans 0.458-0.833 across four inputs sharing a surface reading; replay preconditions are unmet in every scored trace. In a synthetic release-gate pair, the sufficiency gate blocks the raw variant (0.542) and passes the instrumented (0.667). Safety-evaluation claims should travel with their reconstructability vector; a reproducibility package regenerates every reported number.

</details>


### [154] [Taming the Drift: Context-aware Repair of Dockerfile Drift during Software Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12541)
*Chengjie Wang,Jingzheng Wu,Xiang Ling,Tianyue Luo,Chen Zhao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Cadre is a context-aware framework for automated Dockerfile drift repair that uses a Context-aware Dependency Graph (CDG) and a two-step workflow to generate targeted patches. It achieves a 35.22% repair rate, significantly outperforming rule-based (2.78×) and LLM-based baselines (1.24×). The paper also introduces DodeX for mining real-world drift instances and the D³ benchmark of 1,040 reproducible cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dockerfile drift—the divergence between a Dockerfile and its evolving source code—causes CI/CD build failures. Existing rule-based and retrieval-based repair methods analyze Dockerfiles in isolation and fail to handle context-dependent failures, motivating the need for a context-aware approach that models file-level and inter-instruction dependencies.

Method: Cadre uses static analysis to construct a Context-aware Dependency Graph (CDG) mapping each Dockerfile instruction to its file-level and inter-instruction dependencies. Guided by the CDG, a two-step workflow first selects causally relevant context for a failure and then generates a targeted patch. DodeX, a mining pipeline, extracts real-world Dockerfile drift instances from GitHub Actions CI logs while preserving complete build configurations.

Result: On the D³ benchmark (1,040 reproducible drift instances), Cadre achieves a 35.22% repair rate—2.78× the rule-based baseline and 1.24× the best LLM-based baseline. Its two-step workflow keeps 95.25% of prompts under 30k tokens, avoiding prompt-overflow failures that plagued competing methods in 41–58 cases. Ablations confirm both the CDG and two-step workflow improve performance, and Cadre's advantage grows as drift ages across commits.

Conclusion: Explicit dependency modeling via the CDG and context-aware patch generation significantly advances automated Dockerfile drift repair. The results support context-aware infrastructure-as-code maintenance and demonstrate the value of the DodeX pipeline and D³ benchmark for future research.

Abstract: Docker is widely used to create reproducible build environments, but Dockerfile drift, the divergence between a Dockerfile and its evolving source code, can cause CI/CD builds to fail. Existing rule-based and retrieval-based repair approaches analyze Dockerfiles in isolation and therefore struggle with context-dependent failures. We present Cadre, a context-aware framework for automated Dockerfile drift repair. Cadre uses static analysis to construct a Context-aware Dependency Graph (CDG), which maps each Dockerfile instruction to its file-level and inter-instruction dependencies. Guided by the CDG, Cadre first selects the context causally relevant to a failure and then generates a targeted patch from that context. We also introduce DodeX, a pipeline that mines real-world Dockerfile drift instances from GitHub Actions CI logs while preserving the complete build configurations omitted by static-snapshot datasets. Using DodeX, we construct $D^3$, a benchmark of 1,040 drift instances reproducible locally with the original CI parameters. Across $D^3$, Cadre achieves a 35.22\% repair rate, 2.78$\times$ that of the rule-based baseline and 1.24$\times$ that of the best LLM-based baseline. Its two-step workflow keeps 95.25\% of prompts below 30k tokens and avoids the prompt-overflow failures that prevent competing LLM-based methods from producing patches in 41 to 58 cases per method. Ablation results confirm that both the CDG and the two-step workflow improve repair performance. Cadre's advantage over diff-only approaches also increases as drift ages across commits, supporting explicit dependency modeling for context-aware infrastructure-as-code maintenance. Code and data are available at https://github.com/dw763j/Cadre.

</details>


### [155] [Evaluation Bias and Epistemic Inequality in Global Software Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12563)
*Sam Khosravi,Amir H. Payberah*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A mixed-methods study of 48 software engineers across East Africa and Northwestern Europe reveals systematic underestimation of East African engineers' competence by European peers, despite comparable measured performance among senior engineers. Communication and organizational practice differences reflect distinct but complementary work styles across unequal regional contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite rapid growth of African technology ecosystems, empirical evidence on everyday engineering practice and evaluation in these contexts remains limited. The authors aim to examine how competence is assessed and valued across unequal regional contexts—specifically between East Africa (Rwanda, Uganda) and Northwestern Europe (Sweden, Netherlands)—to understand fairness and accountability in global software development.

Method: On-site mixed-methods study with 48 software engineers across four countries (Rwanda, Uganda, Sweden, Netherlands). The study combined three task types—programming, system design, and code review—with semi-structured interviews to capture both measured performance and perceived competence.

Result: Two key findings: (1) Consistent gaps between measured performance and perceived competence—senior Rwandan engineers often performed comparably to European peers, yet European participants systematically underestimated East African engineers' competence. (2) Observable differences in communication styles and organizational practices across regions, reflecting distinct but complementary ways of working rather than deficits.

Conclusion: Global software development is marked by asymmetric evaluation practices that disadvantage engineers from the Global South. The systematic underestimation of East African engineers despite comparable objective performance points to structural biases in competence assessment, with implications for fairness and accountability in distributed engineering work.

Abstract: This paper examines fairness and accountability in global software development by focusing on how competence is assessed and valued across unequal regional contexts. We compare software engineers from East Africa (Rwanda and Uganda) and Northwestern Europe (Sweden and the Netherlands), regions that are increasingly connected but embedded in asymmetric technological, economic, and institutional structures. Despite the rapid growth of African technology ecosystems, empirical evidence on everyday engineering practice and evaluation in these contexts remains limited. We present findings from an on-site mixed-methods study with 48 software engineers across four countries. The study combines programming, system design, and code review tasks with semi-structured interviews. Our results reveal consistent gaps between measured performance and perceived competence. Senior engineers in Rwanda often performed at a level comparable to that of their European peers, yet European participants systematically underestimated the competence of East African engineers. We also observed differences in communication styles and organizational practices across regions, reflecting distinct but complementary ways of working.

</details>


### [156] [Hierarchical Fault Localization for Autonomous Driving Systems with Hypothesis Validation and Intent Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12598)
*Rui Zheng,Changwen Li,Yi Ji,Rongjie Yan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: HINT is a two-phase hierarchical fault localization framework for Autonomous Driving Systems that uses hypothesis validation and intent analysis to pinpoint bugs from system-level failures down to source code, achieving 77.8% end-to-end Class@5 accuracy on real-world Apollo bugs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ADS testing techniques can detect system-level failures or attribute them to coarse-grained modules, but fall short of localizing root causes at the source-code level. This forces developers into labor-intensive debugging to manually connect behavioral violations with complex implementation logic.

Method: HINT operates in two phases: Phase I transforms failure-triggering execution recordings into multi-modal abstractions and applies causal reasoning to identify the responsible module. Phase II reconstructs both design-side intent and implementation-side behavior, then localizes suspicious code via reliability-aware consistency checking, without requiring costly re-simulation.

Result: Evaluated on the Apollo ADS across diverse failure modes and modules, HINT achieved the strongest overall performance across both module-level diagnosis and code-level localization metrics, with 77.8% end-to-end Class@5 accuracy on real-world bugs.

Conclusion: HINT demonstrates that hierarchical fault localization combining causal reasoning with intent-behavior consistency checking can effectively bridge the gap from system-level failures to root-cause code, significantly reducing debugging effort for ADS developers.

Abstract: Comprehensive testing is essential for the safety and reliability of Autonomous Driving Systems (ADS). Existing techniques can detect system-level failures or attribute them to coarse-grained modules, but they often fall short of localizing the root cause in source code. As a result, debugging remains labor-intensive, requiring developers to connect behavioral violations with complex implementation logic. To address this gap, we present HINT, a two-phase framework for hierarchical ADS fault localization based on hypothesis validation and intent analysis. In Phase I, HINT transforms failure-triggering execution recordings into multi-modal abstractions and uses causal reasoning to identify the responsible module. In Phase II, it reconstructs design-side intent and implementation-side behavior, then localizes suspicious code through reliability-aware consistency checking, without costly re-simulation. We evaluate HINT on Apollo across diverse failure modes and modules. The results show that HINT achieves the strongest overall performance across module-level diagnosis and code-level localization metrics, with 77.8% end-to-end Class@5 accuracy on real-world bugs.

</details>


### [157] [Multi-Perspective Agentic Program Repair via Code Property Graphs and Temporal Execution Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12605)
*Zhili Huang,Ling Xu,Hongyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CT-Repair is an agentic APR framework that uses queryable Code Property Graphs and compact Temporal Execution Graphs, combined with three FSM-guided agents analyzing bugs from static, dynamic, and hybrid perspectives, to generate evidence-grounded repair strategies. It repairs 489/854 Defects4J bugs (mixed-model) and outperforms ReinFix (+19) and RepairAgent (+30) under controlled settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based APR methods suffer from (1) raw execution traces being too large and repetitive for effective context, and (2) repeated patch sampling yielding different implementations without distinct root-cause hypotheses or repair strategies.

Method: CT-Repair introduces: (1) Code Property Graphs (CPG) and Temporal Execution Graphs (TEG) as queryable representations of static/dynamic evidence; (2) a three-stage filtering pipeline to construct compact TEGs; (3) three finite-state-machine-guided agents that analyze bugs from static, dynamic, and hybrid perspectives to produce evidence-grounded repair strategies; and (4) a strategy-guided generation procedure that instantiates strategies as candidate patches, using validation feedback to refine the most promising one.

Result: On 854 Defects4J v3.0 Java bugs: mixed-model config repairs 489 bugs; controlled GPT-5.4-mini config repairs 388 bugs (19 more than ReinFix, 30 more than RepairAgent); union of three perspectives repairs 99 more than the strongest single perspective; filtering pipeline narrows candidate method scope by 94.85% (execution filtering) and reduces retained runtime records by 55.97% (behavior filtering).

Conclusion: Structured runtime evidence (TEG) and multi-perspective reasoning significantly improve repair effectiveness without requiring larger patch-generation budgets, demonstrating that evidence quality and diversity of analysis perspectives matter more than sampling volume.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have improved automated program repair (APR), but two limitations remain. First, raw execution traces are often too large and repetitive to serve as effective model context. Second, repeated patch sampling may produce different implementations without yielding distinct root-cause hypotheses or repair strategies. We present CT-Repair, an agentic APR framework representing static and dynamic evidence as queryable Code Property Graph (CPG) and Temporal Execution Graph (TEG). CT-Repair applies a three-stage filtering pipeline to construct compact TEGs. Three finite-state-machine-guided agents analyze each bug from static, dynamic, and hybrid perspectives and independently produce evidence-grounded repair strategies. A strategy-guided generation procedure instantiates these strategies as candidate patches and uses validation feedback to refine the most promising strategy.
  We evaluate CT-Repair on 854 Java bugs from Defects4J v3.0. In the mixed-model configuration, CT-Repair correctly repairs 489 bugs. Under a controlled GPT-5.4-mini configuration, it repairs 388 bugs, 19 and 30 more than ReinFix and RepairAgent, respectively. The union of the three evidence perspectives repairs 99 more bugs than the strongest individual perspective. The filtering pipeline also compacts runtime evidence, with execution filtering narrowing the candidate method scope by 94.85% on average and behavior filtering further reducing retained runtime records by 55.97%. These results show that structured runtime evidence and multi-perspective reasoning can improve repair effectiveness without relying solely on a larger patch-generation budget.

</details>


### [158] [Line-Anchored Feedback Cuts Token Costs and Improves Correctness in AI Code Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12713)
*William Franz Lamberti*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Line-anchored feedback via a tool called FileMark reduces generated token counts by 22–58% in LLM-based code editing while improving correctness, especially for weaker local models. Token savings and correctness gains increase with file size, and correctness triples for local models on 100+ line files when the system (not the model) applies edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generated tokens directly drive the cost, latency, and energy consumption of generative AI code editing. The authors hypothesize that the format in which feedback is delivered to an LLM is a controllable lever affecting all three, yet this had not been systematically studied.

Method: A paired experiment compares two delivery formats for the same code-change requests: a holistic prompt (control) versus structured, line-anchored export from FileMark, a VSCodium extension for inline comments. Seven models (including Claude Opus, Claude Sonnet, and five local models) are evaluated on token generation and correctness. A secondary exploratory experiment offloads edit application from the model to the harness, applying function-level patches.

Result: Line anchoring reduced generated tokens by 22% (Claude Opus) and 58% (Claude Sonnet), reaching 24–80% on files ≥100 lines; four of seven models showed significant reductions after multiple-testing correction. Pooled correctness rose +2.0 points, with +5 to +7 point gains for three of five local models. When the harness applies edits, local-model correctness on 100+ line files roughly triples.

Conclusion: Line-anchored feedback is a low-cost intervention that simultaneously reduces spending (for stronger models) and improves accuracy (for weaker models). Offloading edit application to the harness amplifies correctness gains, suggesting architectural choices in tooling can compound the benefits of structured feedback.

Abstract: Generated tokens are a direct driver of the cost, latency, and energy of generative AI (GAI) code editing. We show the format of feedback is a lever on all three. We compare two deliveries of the same requested changes: a holistic prompt (control) versus the structured, line-anchored export of FileMark (treatment). FileMark is a VSCodium extension for inline comments on any file. In a paired experiment line anchoring cut generated tokens by 22% (Claude Opus) and 58% (Claude Sonnet), reaching 24%-80% on files of 100 lines or more, with four of seven models generating significantly fewer tokens after multiple-testing correction. Correctness rose where models had headroom: +2.0 points pooled and +5 to +7 points for three of five local models. An exploratory experiment in which the harness, not the GAI model, applies function-level patches shows the correctness benefit grows further when the edit-application burden is lifted: local-model correctness on 100+ line files roughly triples under anchoring. Line-anchored feedback reduces what stronger models spend and improves what weaker models get right.

</details>


### [159] [Toward Localizing and Repairing Bias in Transformer Attention Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12863)
*Sigma Jahan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ROBIN is a white-box inference-time fairness intervention that ranks attention heads by sensitivity to fairness probes and removes a small bias subspace from selected heads, reducing WinoBias gaps while preserving language modeling quality better than whole-head zeroing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fairness testing/repair methods operate at input-output or retraining levels, but bias-related behavior can concentrate in specific attention heads. The paper addresses the gap in localized, inference-time bias repair at the attention-head level.

Method: ROBIN: (1) ranks attention heads using sensitivity to fairness probes that predict bias from head outputs; (2) applies a targeted subspace removal — subtracting a small bias-relevant subspace from selected heads' output representations — rather than zeroing entire heads.

Result: In a four-model pilot study, ROBIN reduced the WinoBias gap across all models tested and preserved language modeling quality better than the baseline of whole-head zeroing.

Conclusion: Head-level bias repair requires careful consideration of both which heads to select and how to modify them; subspace removal is more effective and less destructive than head ablation.

Abstract: Transformer language models are increasingly used as software components, yet biased outputs remain difficult to localize and repair inside the model. Existing fairness testing and repair methods largely operate at the input-output or retraining level, while recent work suggests that bias-related behavior can concentrate in a small set of attention heads. This paper studies whether attention heads can be localized and repaired through a targeted inference-time intervention. We introduce ROBIN, a white-box head-level fairness debugging method that ranks attention heads using sensitivity to fairness probes and removes a small bias subspace from selected head outputs. In a four-model pilot study, ROBIN reduces the measured WinoBias gap across all models while preserving language-modeling quality better than whole-head zeroing. These preliminary results suggest that head-level bias repair should consider not only which heads are selected, but also how selected heads are modified.

</details>


### [160] [Deep4ge: DNN Training Trajectories for Fault Detection and Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12868)
*Sigma Jahan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Deep4ge is a benchmark dataset of 14,227 per-epoch training runs (9,845 faulty, 4,382 correct) from 59 DNN programs, generated via 27 fault-injection transformations across 7 categories, enabling fault detection, diagnosis, and early prediction research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The software engineering community lacks a public dataset of per-epoch deep learning training runs with documented fault history, extracted features, and clear support for fault detection and diagnosis tasks. Existing work recognizes that subtle implementation faults alter training behavior, but no standardized benchmark exists for systematic evaluation.

Method: The authors collected 59 TensorFlow/Keras DNN programs from Stack Overflow, then applied 27 source-code transformations spanning seven fault categories to generate faulty variants. Each of the 14,227 training runs records 4 evaluation metrics and 26 per-epoch features (weights, gradients, activations, accuracy/loss trends, learning rate, hardware usage), producing a controlled benchmark with both faulty (9,845) and correct (4,382) runs.

Result: Deep4ge delivers 14,227 training runs (9,845 faulty, 4,382 baseline) across 59 DNN programs and 7 fault categories. The dataset supports three key tasks: binary fault detection, multi-class fault diagnosis, and early fault prediction from partial training runs. The benchmark and fault-injection framework are publicly released.

Conclusion: Deep4ge fills a critical gap by providing the first public, systematically constructed benchmark for per-epoch fault detection and diagnosis in deep learning training pipelines, with comprehensive metadata and a reusable fault-injection framework to facilitate reproducible research.

Abstract: Deep learning systems often fail due to subtle implementation faults that alter training behavior. Recent work has studied how to detect and diagnose such failures from changes observed across training epochs. However, the software engineering community still lacks a public dataset of per-epoch training runs with documented fault history, feature extraction details, and clear reuse support for fault detection and diagnosis tasks. We present Deep4ge, a controlled benchmark of 14,227 training runs generated from 59 adapted TensorFlow/Keras deep neural network (DNN) programs collected from Stack Overflow. We generated faulty variants using 27 source-code transformations that introduce known faults across seven categories. The dataset contains 9,845 faulty runs and 4,382 correct baseline runs. For each run, we record 4 evaluation metrics and 26 features that measure training behavior at every epoch. These features capture weights, gradients, activations, accuracy and loss trends, learning rate, and hardware use. Deep4ge supports binary fault detection, multi-class fault diagnosis, and early fault prediction from partial training runs. We release the dataset and fault-injection framework at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.20337241.

</details>


### [161] [Salesforce Messaging Architecture: Platform Events, Async Sends, and Multi-Tenancy at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12943)
*Devam Gupta*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes embedding the entire enterprise messaging system natively within a CRM platform, treating messages as first-class CRM records to eliminate fragile external integrations, sync delays, and data silos. It evaluates production deployments across multiple industries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional enterprise messaging integrations operate as external connectors outside the CRM, causing data isolation, fragile synchronization jobs, rigid field mappings, and eventual consistency gaps. Messages and CRM records remain in separate silos, preventing seamless automation and unified reporting.

Method: The authors propose and evaluate a "platform-native" architectural paradigm where the entire messaging core is built inside the CRM. Messages are native CRM records, outbound paths are platform transactions, and all state (delivery status, opt-in metrics, conversation history, record ownership) is stored as standard database objects. The study draws from a production managed package deployed across independent enterprise organizations in healthcare, sales operations, field services, and customer support.

Result: The paper examines core design patterns, platform scaling mechanics, multi-tenant decoupling constraints, and the boundaries of platform-native architecture based on real-world production deployments.

Conclusion: A platform-native messaging architecture enables seamless use of standard reporting pipelines, native workflow builders, and platform automation triggers, offering a more coherent and tightly integrated alternative to external connector-based messaging in enterprise CRM environments.

Abstract: Most enterprise messaging integrations function as external connectors. They reside outside the Customer Relationship Management (CRM) platform, receive webhooks, write peripheral logs, and consider the process integrated. Consequently, messages are isolated within the messaging platform while CRM records remain strictly inside the CRM, bridged only by fragile sync jobs, rigid field mappings, and eventual consistency windows. This paper outlines an alternative architectural paradigm: constructing the entire messaging core natively inside the CRM. Within this model, every message is treated as a native CRM record,every outbound path is a platform-native transaction, and all state parameters - including delivery status, opt-in metrics, conversation history, and record ownership - are stored as standard database objects. This structural native alignment allows the system to utilize standard reporting pipelines, operate inside native workflow builders, and trigger platform automation seamlessly. Drawing from a production managed package deployed across independent enterprise organizations spanning healthcare, sales operations, field services, and customer support, this study evaluates the core design patterns, platform scaling mechanics, multi-tenant decoupling constraints, and the boundaries of the platform-native architectural design.

</details>


### [162] [Form, Not Content? A Preregistered, Placebo-Controlled Evaluation of Learned Error-Conditioned Self-Repair Through Prompts and Weights in Frozen Small Code Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12962)
*Mehmet Iscan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PoPE introduces a placebo-controlled methodology to test whether frozen small code LLMs can operationally use execution feedback to self-repair. Across both prompt and weight channels, content-attributable improvement over placebos was not confirmed, suggesting that what appears as self-repair may be conditioning rather than genuine testing of new conjectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The self-repair literature lacks placebo controls, making it unclear whether improvements after a failed code generation attempt are truly due to the model using falsifying evidence or are merely artifacts of the scaffolding process. The authors aim to fill this gap by introducing a methodologically rigorous, falsification-based evaluation grounded in Popperian epistemology.

Method: PoPE pairs error content with channel-specific placebos: a content-ablated form placebo (prompt channel) and a SHA-deranged placebo (weight channel via small-data adapter training). Frozen code LLMs (0.5–1.5B parameters) are evaluated under preregistered rules over a 40-unit resistant band, with four generations per arm-unit pair. The primary endpoint is public-tier screening (unlocked programs), with hidden-tier confirmation deferred by design.

Result: Prompt channel: 12 unlocks under the content-ablated placebo vs. 10 under the live error-pattern arm — recorded as mechanism-null. Weight channel: an 8–8 tie between the error-content adapter and the intervention-free baseline (p=1.0), while the SHA-deranged placebo led with 10 unlocks. Content-attributable superiority was not confirmed in either channel. Equivalence and non-inferiority were not separately tested.

Conclusion: The results do not support the hypothesis that frozen small code LLMs operationally use execution feedback for self-repair. The authors interpret this as compiled criticism losing its external role in testing new conjectures: when oracle-derived representations are written back into the generation state, testing is replaced by conditioning. PoPE is offered as a placebo-controlled, retestable measurement standard, not as a claim of a working JEPA-RL controller.

Abstract: Frozen small code LLMs are deployed locally, yet the information guiding a retry after a failed attempt is still measured without placebo controls in the self-repair literature. We treat a failed program as a conjecture and an execution counterexample as an oracle-relative refutation, and introduce PoPE (Popperian Placebo-controlled Evaluation): a methodology for measuring whether evidence that falsifies LLM-generated code can be used operationally by that same model. In PoPE, error content is paired with channel-specific placebos that keep the predeclared scaffold while ablating task-relevant content or deranging the task-error assignment. Frozen small code models (0.5-1.5B) are evaluated under preregistered rules through a prompt channel and a weight channel (small-data adapter training), with four generations per arm-unit pair. In the prompt channel, public-tier screening unlocked 12 units under the content-ablated form placebo versus 10 under the live error-pattern arm on a 40-unit resistant band; the result was recorded as mechanism-null. In the weight channel, an 8-8 tie was observed between the error-content adapter and the intervention-free baseline (p=1.0), while the SHA-deranged placebo adapter stayed ahead with 10 unlocks; content-attributable superiority was not confirmed. These results do not constitute evidence of equivalence or non-inferiority. Equivalence was not tested separately. Findings are restricted to the public-tier screening endpoint; hidden-tier confirmation was deferred by design. We read this not as compiled criticism disappearing as information, but as the loss of its external role in testing a new conjecture: when a representation learned from the oracle is written back into the generation state, testing is replaced by conditioning. No working JEPA-RL controller is claimed. PoPE is presented as a placebo-controlled, retestable measurement standard.

</details>


### [163] [Software Supply Chains are Dead: Use-Case-Oriented Regeneration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13021)
*Tanmay Singla,James C. Davis*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes "use-case-oriented regeneration," an agentic workflow that synthesizes only the specific dependency functionality exercised by a repository. Across 180 repository-dependency pairs, replacements preserved 99.8% of observed behavior and reduced exported API surface by 93%. The paradigm shifts software sourcing from external trust to local verification, motivated by rising supply chain attack risks and the lowered cost of AI-driven code generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional software development assumes that the up-front savings of reusing dependencies outweigh long-term maintenance costs. Two emerging trends challenge this: (1) software supply chain attacks have increased the security risk and cost of external reliance, and (2) generative AI has dramatically lowered the cost of implementing functionality locally. These shifts demand revisiting the build-vs.-reuse calculus, motivating a new paradigm where only the actually-used portions of dependencies are regenerated and locally verified rather than blindly trusted.

Method: The authors propose an agentic workflow for "use-case-oriented regeneration." Instead of importing an entire dependency, the system analyzes a target repository to identify precisely which slices of a dependency's functionality are exercised. It then synthesizes a minimal, repository-specific replacement that covers only that exercised functionality. The evaluation spans 180 repository-dependency pairs, measuring behavioral preservation (via baseline validation checks) and reduction in exported API surface area.

Result: Across 180 repository-dependency pairs, the synthesized replacements preserved 99.8% of repository-observed behavior as measured by baseline validation checks. Additionally, the exported API surface was reduced by 93% compared to the original dependencies, demonstrating that minimal, use-case-specific code synthesis is both functional and substantially leaner.

Conclusion: Use-case-oriented regeneration is feasible and represents a viable new software sourcing paradigm. The approach is particularly promising when the required dependency functionality is narrow, stable, and well-tested. Software sourcing may evolve toward verifiable, repository-specific code synthesis, shifting the trust model from external supply chain dependence to local verification.

Abstract: Modern software development relies on an increasingly doubtful premise: that the up-front implementation savings from adopting a dependency outweighs the maintenance costs. Two changes are reshaping the build-vs.-reuse calculus: software supply chain attacks have raised the cost of external reliance, while generative AI has lowered the cost of local implementation. We envision use-case-oriented regeneration as a new software sourcing paradigm that shifts the supply chain from external trust to local verification. We evaluate an agentic workflow that synthesizes only the specific slice of dependency functionality that a repository exercises. Our measurements across 180 repository-dependency pairs suggest that this approach is feasible: the replacements preserve 99.8% of repository-observed behavior across baseline validation checks and reduce the exported API surface by 93%. Software sourcing may evolve toward verifiable repository-specific code synthesis, especially when the required functionality is narrow, stable, and well tested.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [164] [MetaInfer: A Knowledge Only LLM Inference Engine Generator SKILL Toolbox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12875)
*Zhenwen Miao,Honglin Wang,Mingheng Mi*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: MetaInfer proposes an "LLM-as-Compiler" approach where an LLM-driven multi-agent system with a contract knowledge base automatically generates compact, customized inference frameworks from user-specified runtime constraints, reducing code complexity and maintenance cost in the expanding LLM inference landscape.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapidly expanding space of model families, compute hardware, quantization schemes, parallelization strategies, and specialized kernels has sharply increased code complexity and maintenance cost in general-purpose inference frameworks. While conventional software engineering uses abstraction layers to manage diversity, these abstractions add system complexity and may incur performance overhead.

Method: MetaInfer introduces an "LLM-as-Compiler" paradigm. Users specify only runtime constraints for an inference program. A multi-agent collaboration system driven by LLMs, coupled with a contract knowledge base (CKB), automatically generates a compact, customized inference framework satisfying those constraints. The system forms a closed loop of generation constraints, validation feedback, and knowledge consolidation.

Result: Evaluation covers three perspectives: (1) the effect of source-code reference, (2) runtime behavior and performance of engines generated under zero-reference constraints on CKB-covered targets, and (3) knowledge-base evolution for new model and platform scenarios. Results demonstrate that the system produces runnable customized inference solutions from explicit knowledge via the continuous closed-loop process.

Conclusion: MetaInfer demonstrates that an LLM-driven closed-loop generation system can produce functional, customized inference frameworks from explicit knowledge, offering a viable alternative to hand-engineered general-purpose frameworks. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/MetaInfer/MetaInfer.

Abstract: As LLM technology advances, the space of model families, compute hardware, quantization schemes, parallelization strategies, and specialized optimization kernels continues to expand, sharply increasing the code complexity and maintenance cost of general-purpose inference frameworks. Conventional software engineering uses multiple layers of abstraction to support diverse application scenarios, but these abstractions also increase system complexity and may introduce additional performance overhead. This paper presents metainfer, an 'LLM-as-Compiler' approach in which users specify only the runtime constraints of an inference program. An LLM-driven multi-agent collaboration system, coupled with a contract knowledge base, then automatically generates a compact customized inference framework that satisfies these constraints. We evaluate metainfer from three perspectives: the effect of source-code reference, the runtime behavior and performance profile of engines generated under the zero-reference constraint on CKB-covered targets, and knowledge-base evolution for new model and platform scenarios. The results show that metainfer organizes generation constraints, validation feedback, and knowledge consolidation into a continuous closed loop, enabling runnable customized inference solutions to be generated from explicit knowledge. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/MetaInfer/MetaInfer.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [165] [Optimal Adaptive Market Making: A Theoretical Framework for High-Yield Liquidity Provision in Perpetual Futures Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11888)
*Minmin Zeng,Yi Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a rigorous stochastic control framework for optimal market making in perpetual futures markets with zero maker fees, unifying and extending classical market-making paradigms. It presents ten key theoretical contributions including a PnL decomposition theorem, HJB equation with verification, High-APY Regime Theorems with a Master APY Formula, and results on hedging, robustness, drawdown bounds, ergodic inventory, Kelly leverage, and portfolio allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rise of decentralized perpetual futures exchanges with zero maker fees creates a fundamentally new market-making environment that existing models (Avellaneda-Stoikov, Gueant-Lehalle-Fernandez-Tapia, Glosten-Milgrom) do not adequately address. The authors aim to fill this gap by providing a unified theoretical framework that accounts for the unique features of these venues: zero maker fees, funding rate dynamics, cross-exchange hedging, and the distinct economic trade-offs they create.

Method: The problem is formulated as a stochastic optimal control problem on a filtered probability space, with adaptive bid-ask spreads and inventory hedging decisions across two exchanges as control variables. The methodology employs CARA (constant absolute risk aversion) utility, dynamic programming via the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation with a verification theorem, dimensional analysis yielding five dimensionless parameters, and Bayesian adaptive estimation. Numerical analysis with twenty-three figures illustrates phase transitions between regimes.

Result: Key results include: (1) a PnL decomposition into spread income, adverse selection loss, inventory carrying cost, hedging friction, and funding rate exposure; (2) characterization of profitable regimes via the Master APY Formula; (3) optimal entry-exit thresholds for zero-fee DEX markets; (4) a hedge regime trichotomy for cross-exchange policies; (5) a robustness margin quantifying parameter uncertainty tolerance; (6) exponential drawdown probability bounds and a universal APY-VaR identity; (7) ergodic inventory distribution under optimal control; (8) Kelly-optimal leverage with ruin boundaries; and (9) diversification saturation in multi-pair portfolio allocation.

Conclusion: The framework successfully unifies three classical market-making paradigms and extends them to modern decentralized venues with zero maker fees. The Master APY Formula and dimensionless parameter characterization provide practical tools for determining when market making is profitable. The analysis demonstrates clear phase transitions between profitable and unprofitable regimes, offering actionable insights for market makers operating on decentralized perpetual futures exchanges.

Abstract: We develop a rigorous theoretical framework for optimal market making in perpetual futures markets with zero maker fees. We model the market maker's problem as a stochastic optimal control problem on a filtered probability space, where the controls are adaptive bid-ask spreads and inventory hedging decisions across two exchanges. Our contributions include: (i) a PnL decomposition theorem separating revenue into spread income, adverse selection loss, inventory carrying cost, hedging friction, and funding rate exposure; (ii) the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation for the joint spread-inventory-hedging control problem under CARA utility with a verification theorem; (iii) High-APY Regime Theorems characterizing profitable regions via five dimensionless parameters, culminating in a Master APY Formula; (iv) analysis of zero-fee economics on decentralized perpetual exchanges with optimal entry-exit thresholds; (v) optimal cross-exchange hedging policies with funding rate dynamics and a hedge regime trichotomy; (vi) a robustness margin quantifying parameter uncertainty tolerance; (vii) exponential drawdown probability bounds and a universal APY-VaR identity; (viii) ergodic inventory distribution under optimal control with Bayesian adaptive estimation; (ix) Kelly-optimal leverage with ruin boundaries; and (x) multi-pair portfolio allocation with diversification saturation results. Numerical analysis with twenty-three figures reveals phase transitions between profitable and unprofitable regimes. Our framework unifies and extends the Avellaneda-Stoikov, Gueant-Lehalle-Fernandez-Tapia, and Glosten-Milgrom paradigms for modern decentralized venue microstructure.

</details>


### [166] [Ontology-Amplified Distillation and Contextuality Auditing for Sovereign Enterprise Language Models: A Combined Proof-of-Mechanism and Negative-Results Method Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11948)
*Thanh Luong Tuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper combines two FAOS studies: (1) a proof-of-mechanism for ontology-amplified distillation of a Qwen3.6-27B student using supervised fine-tuning and ontology-grounded DPO on 47 synthetic preference pairs, achieving grounded rates matching GPT-5 on Vietnamese financial tasks but underpowered for equivalence; (2) a contextuality-audit method for enterprise-agent routing yielding null results (zero Contextuality-by-Default degree). Neither study provides evidence for deployability, safety, superiority, equivalence, or a positive routing rule.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regulated financial institutions under data-residency constraints require tenant-owned language models that operate within institutional perimeters. The work addresses two needs: (1) a mechanism to distill frontier-model capabilities into locally-deployable, ontology-grounded student models, and (2) a governance diagnostic for enterprise-agent routing decisions when models disagree.

Method: Two studies: (1) Ontology-amplified distillation — a Qwen3.6-27B student is fine-tuned on frontier-teacher trajectories and ontology-grounded DPO using 47 synthetic cross-domain preference pairs, trained on a single Apple M5 Max; evaluated on 40 held-out Vietnamese financial tasks using grounded rate and ontology term-coverage (r_onto, floored at 0.50). (2) Contextuality-by-Default audit — applied to both local-Qwen and Gemma replication runs, measuring corrected canonical contextuality degree across Phase 1.3 groups, plus direct influence and construct coupling analysis.

Result: The distilled student grounds 36/40 tasks (0.90 grounded rate; r_onto = 0.95), identical to GPT-5's 36/40. The 95% CI for paired differences spans ±4 tasks, making the study underpowered for equivalence. The pre-registered amplification prediction (student exceeding frontier) was not tested or shown. The contextuality audit produced null results: corrected Contextuality-by-Default degree is zero for all groups; only direct influence and construct coupling provide signal.

Conclusion: The evidence supports neither deployability, safety, superiority, statistical equivalence, nor a contextuality-positive routing rule. The paper pairs an ontology-grounded model-building mechanism with a governance diagnostic but yields no positive actionable findings.

Abstract: Regulated financial institutions operating under data-residency rules need tenant-owned language models that can run inside the institution's perimeter. This paper combines two related FAOS studies into one mechanism-and-control article. First, it reports a reduced-power proof-of-mechanism study of ontology-amplified distillation: a Qwen3.6-27B student is adapted to the Foundation AgenticOS ontology through supervised fine-tuning on frontier-teacher trajectories and ontology-grounded direct preference optimization (DPO), trained locally on a single Apple M5 Max from 47 synthetic, English-language, cross-domain preference pairs. On 40 held-out Vietnamese financial-domain tasks, the distilled student grounds 36 of 40 tasks (grounded rate 0.90; mean ontology term-coverage r_onto = 0.95 on a metric floored at 0.50), equal to the GPT-5 frontier baseline, which also grounds 36 of 40. The outcome is underpowered to establish equivalence: the paired-difference 95% confidence interval spans +/-4 tasks, and the run does not test or show the pre-registered amplification prediction that the student should exceed the frontier. Second, the paper consolidates a contextuality-audit method for enterprise-agent routing. In a separate negative-results pilot, the corrected canonical Contextuality-by-Default degree is zero for all Phase 1.3 groups in both the local-Qwen run and an explicitly labeled Gemma replication check; the useful signal is direct influence and construct coupling, not surviving residual contextuality. Together, the studies pair an ontology-grounded model-building mechanism with a governance diagnostic for deciding when apparent disagreement should trigger prompt standardization, multi-agent synthesis, or human review. The evidence supports neither deployability, safety, superiority, statistical equivalence, nor a contextuality-positive routing rule.

</details>


### [167] [In-Context Reinforcement Learning under Non-Stationarity: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11906)
*A Run,Ziluo Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper surveys non-stationary in-context reinforcement learning (ICRL), where a pretrained decision model with frozen parameters must adapt to changing environments using only its context window. The authors organize the literature around three key questions: what changes (reward, dynamics, observations, etc.), how the change unfolds (abrupt, gradual, recurrent), and how observable the change is to the agent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ICRL surveys focus on pretraining objectives, architectures, context formats, evaluation protocols, and theoretical mechanisms, but the non-stationary setting remains underexamined. In changing environments, accumulated context can become stale, misleading, or cyclically relevant, posing a distinct challenge from stationary ICRL where more context simply means more evidence about a fixed task.

Method: The authors present a conceptual survey and taxonomy. They define non-stationary ICRL, relate it to adjacent fields (meta-RL, decision sequence modeling, retrieval-augmented RL, value- and model-aware ICRL, reward-feedback agents), and organize the literature along three organizing axes: (1) what changes — reward specification, transition kernel, observation channel, action interface, constraint model, or demonstration/memory distribution; (2) how the change unfolds — abrupt shifts, gradual drift, or recurrent/cyclical regimes; and (3) how observable the change is to the agent — fully observable regime indicators versus latent/hidden changes that must be inferred from outcomes.

Result: The survey identifies that non-stationary ICRL forces agents to solve a dual inference problem: inferring the current decision rule while simultaneously determining which parts of accumulated context remain valid. It highlights that previously useful context can become stale, misleading, or regain relevance when old regimes return, creating qualitatively different demands compared to stationary ICRL.

Conclusion: Non-stationary ICRL represents a significant and underexplored frontier. The taxonomy of what changes, how change unfolds, and change observability provides a structured framework for future research. Key open challenges include detecting regime shifts from within the context window, managing stale evidence, and exploiting recurring structure across regimes — all while keeping deployed policy parameters fixed.

Abstract: The development of decision-pretrained transformers, algorithm distillation, long-context meta-RL, and retrieval-augmented agents has renewed interest in in-context reinforcement learning (ICRL): the ability of a pretrained or fine-tuned decision model to infer latent task rules and improve future behavior from interaction context, without test-time parameter updates. This line of work asks when trial-and-error evidence, rewards, transitions, demonstrations, feedback, or retrieved experience can make learning-like computation happen inside the context window. However, existing surveys of ICRL mainly organize the field around pretraining objectives, architectures, context formats, evaluation protocols, and theoretical mechanisms, while the non-stationary setting remains comparatively underexamined. In changing environments, accumulated context is not merely more evidence about a fixed task: the reward specification, transition kernel, observation channel, action interface, constraint model, or demonstration and memory distribution can fall out of alignment with the current regime. Previously useful context can therefore become stale, misleading, or useful again when an old regime returns. We survey non-stationary ICRL as the problem of adapting through context while deployed policy parameters remain fixed: the policy must infer both the current decision rule and which parts of its accumulated evidence still support that rule. We define non-stationary ICRL, relate it to meta-RL, decision sequence modeling, retrieval-augmented RL, value- and model-aware ICRL, and reward-feedback agents, and organize the literature along three questions: what changes, how the change unfolds, and how observable the change is to the agent.

</details>


### [168] [Graph Feedback Controls Consensus and Clique Formation in Open-Weight Language-Model Populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12077)
*Samer Saab,Chaouki Abdallah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how routing strategies in multi-agent LM populations affect convention formation. Using naming-game experiments across models from 1.1B to 32B parameters, the authors show that threshold-similarity routing causes fragmentation by eliminating cross-basin exposure, while bridge-seeking routing and retained interaction history promote behavioral and latent state-space consensus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LM systems increasingly use interaction graphs for routing, yet the runtime interaction graph is typically treated as an implementation detail rather than a design variable. The authors aim to understand how different routing strategies — specifically homophilous (similarity-based) vs. bridge-seeking — affect the emergence of shared conventions and whether consensus at the behavioral (label) level aligns with consensus in the models' latent state spaces.

Method: The study employs a naming-game protocol with open-weight LMs (1.1B–32B parameters). Key methodological innovations include: (1) restricted first-token scores over tokenizer-safe labels to measure prompt-conditioned score-state distributions, (2) construction of state-similarity graphs from these distributions, (3) separation of sampled-label agreement from latent state-space consensus, and (4) controlled comparisons of threshold-similarity routing vs. bridge-seeking routing (state-component and label-disagreement bridges), with and without retained interaction memory, across homogeneous and mixed-model populations under varying population sizes, vocabulary sizes, and random seeds.

Result: Threshold-similarity routing consistently fails to produce consensus: in a three-seed mixed four-model grid, 0/189 runs achieve final behavioral or state consensus; homogeneous populations fare similarly. Bridge-seeking routing with retained memory succeeds: 14/18 mixed-model runs and 18/18 Qwen2.5-32B well-mixed runs reach behavioral and state consensus. Retained partner-label evidence is necessary but not sufficient — history alone helps but does not guarantee consensus without bridge-seeking routing. Graph-energy features from early interaction windows provide diagnostic signals for within-grid outcomes. Results are robust across state thresholds, population sizes, and vocabulary sizes.

Conclusion: The interaction graph is not a mere implementation detail but a critical design choice that determines whether multi-agent LM populations converge to shared conventions or fragment. Homophilous routing amplifies fragmentation by deleting cross-basin exposure; bridge-seeking routing with memory repairs it. The dissociation between behavioral agreement and latent state consensus underscores the need to evaluate both levels, and early graph-energy metrics offer practical diagnostics for predicting collective outcomes.

Abstract: Multi-agent language-model systems increasingly route local interactions, yet the runtime interaction graph is often treated as an implementation detail. We study convention formation in open-weight LM populations spanning 1.1B-32B parameters with a naming-game protocol. Restricted first-token scores over tokenizer-safe labels let us measure prompt-conditioned score-state distributions, construct state-similarity graphs, and separate sampled-label agreement from latent state-space consensus. Across controlled interventions, in the main open-weight repair grids, retained partner-label evidence is necessary but not sufficient: homophilous threshold-similarity routing deletes cross-basin exposure and amplifies fragmentation, while bridge-seeking routing often repairs fragmentation when memory is available. In a three-seed mixed four-model grid, threshold-similarity produces no final behavioral or state consensus in 189 setting-seed runs, whereas state-component and label-disagreement bridges recover final behavioral consensus in 14/18 retained-memory runs. Across homogeneous model populations, retained history generally shifts fragmented dynamics toward consensus; the clearest case is Qwen2.5-32B, which reaches stable behavioral and final state consensus in all 18 retained-history well-mixed settings, while threshold-similarity reaches neither form of consensus in 189 settings. Robustness over state thresholds, population size, and vocabulary size preserves the qualitative ordering, and early-window graph-energy features provide useful within-grid diagnostics.

</details>


### [169] [GRID: Grammar-Railed Decoding for Enterprise SQL Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11951)
*Mohsen Arjmandi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRID is a grammar-constrained decoding engine for LLM SQL generation that uses LALR(1) parser state (not token sequences) to compute exact next-token masks, compiles role-based access control into the grammar itself, provides provable guarantees (soundness, completeness, termination, constant per-token cost), achieves 3.6–6.7 μs median per-token masking, and delivers significant execution-accuracy gains on Spider with a verifiable audit trail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise SQL generation from LLMs requires more than plausible text: outputs must be syntactically valid, respect per-role and per-schema access policies, carry provable guarantees (not best-effort), maintain constant-time performance regardless of generation length, and produce a compliance-grade audit trail. Existing constrained decoding approaches fall short on one or more of these dimensions.

Method: GRID uses the LALR(1) parser's internal configuration (lexer scan state × LALR(1) stack) as the key for computing exact next-token masks, treating the parser itself as a viable-prefix oracle. LLM tokens are bridged to grammar terminals via a byte-level trie walk with a context-independent/context-dependent split that makes cache-key soundness hold by construction. Role-based access control is compiled directly into the grammar: role projections subset productions, and schema lexicons restrict identifier terminals, making forbidden verbs/identifiers unreachable at the mask level. A hash-chained per-token audit trail enables bit-identical replay with 100% tamper detection.

Result: Per-token mask computation achieves 3.6–6.7 μs median (Rust), ahead of llguidance at p50 and p90 on two tokenizers with zero false rejects. Per-token guard cost remains position-flat at n=16,000. On Spider, constrained decoding yields +13 execution-accuracy points at 0.5B scale; one checker-guided repair pass over mask-unenforceable column-level policy lifts a 7B model to 94.5% executable. The audit trail replays bit-identically with 100% tamper detection.

Conclusion: GRID demonstrates that grammar-constrained decoding with provable guarantees, compiled RBAC, constant-time per-token cost, and a verifiable audit trail is feasible for enterprise SQL generation. The authors also transparently acknowledge limitations: the mask cannot enforce distribution faithfulness, column-level RBAC, or non-LALR(1) languages, and certain costs remain measurable.

Abstract: Large language models can write SQL, but enterprise deployment demands more than plausible text: outputs must be syntactically valid, must respect per-role and per-schema policy, must carry provable (not best-effort) guarantees, must not slow down as generations grow, and must leave a compliance-grade record of every decision. We present GRID (Grammar-Railed Decoding), a grammar-constrained decoding engine that keys exact next-token masks on parser configurations (lexer scan state x LALR(1) stack) rather than on token sequences, and uses the incrementally advanced LALR(1) parser itself as a viable-prefix oracle. LLM tokens are bridged to grammar terminals by a byte-level trie walk with a context-independent/context-dependent split that makes cache-key soundness hold by construction. Role-based access control is compiled into the language: role projections subset the grammar's productions and schema lexicons restrict identifier terminals, so forbidden verbs and identifiers are unreachable at mask level. Four guarantees (soundness, completeness, termination, and near-constant per-token cost) are stated with explicit preconditions and each paired with a test or benchmark. Rust kernels bring the per-token mask to a 3.6-6.7 us median, ahead of llguidance at p50 and p90 on two tokenizers with zero false rejects; per-token guard cost is position-flat at n=16,000. On Spider, constrained decoding is worth +13 execution-accuracy points at 0.5B, and one checker-guided repair pass over the provably mask-unenforceable residue (column-level policy) lifts a 7B model to 94.5% executable. A hash-chained per-token audit trail replays bit-identically with 100% tamper detection. We state plainly what the mask cannot do (distribution faithfulness, column-level RBAC, non-LALR(1) languages) and where measured cost remains.

</details>


### [170] [Calibration-First Reward-Component Auditing for Reinforcement Learning Control in Smart Greenhouses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11959)
*Yuhui Bie,Guowei Xu,Yaojun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a reproducible calibration-first reward audit framework for greenhouse control that decomposes scalar reinforcement learning rewards into interpretable, comparable components (temperature, CO2, humidity/VPD, screen, actuation) across simulation, real rollouts, and logged competition data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A single scalar simulator return is insufficient for practical smart-greenhouse control — growers and engineers need to understand when and why the policy activates specific actuators (heating, CO2 enrichment, ventilation, humidification, screens, lamps). Existing RL approaches lack interpretability and cross-context comparability.

Method: The authors introduce GreenLight-Gym, implementing a calibration-first reward audit framework that: (1) decomposes the scalar reward into named, interpretable components (temperature, CO2, humidity/VPD, screen, actuation-proxy terms); (2) adapts the GreenLight simulator to logged climate traces from the second Autonomous Greenhouse Challenge; (3) scores identical reward components on both simulator data and real logged greenhouse data, enabling direct cross-context comparison; and (4) supports actuator-rule distillation for interpretable policy extraction.

Result: The framework enables reproducible comparison of reward components across simulator training, facility-adapted rollouts, and logged competition records, providing actionable transparency into when and how control policies use each actuator.

Conclusion: Decomposing greenhouse RL rewards into interpretable, named components and calibrating them against real logged data bridges the gap between simulation-based training and practical deployment, offering growers and engineers the interpretability needed for real-world adoption.

Abstract: Greenhouse reinforcement learning can test climate-control ideas at a speed and scale that is difficult to achieve with crop experiments alone. For smart-greenhouse control, however, a single simulator return is not enough: a grower or control engineer also needs to know when the policy heats, enriches CO2, vents, manages humidity, deploys screens, or uses lamps.We propose a reproducible calibration-first reward audit framework that keeps named greenhouse-control reward components comparable across simulator training, facility-adapted rollouts, logged Autonomous Greenhouse Challenge records, and actuator-rule distillation. In GreenLight-Gym, the framework decomposes the scalar reward into conditional temperature, CO2, humidity and vapor-pressure-deficit, screen, and actuation-proxy terms; adapts GreenLight to the second Autonomous Greenhouse Challenge logged climate traces; and scores the same components on logged greenhouse data.

</details>


### [171] [Optimization Is Not All You Need](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11977)
*Minh Hua,Rita Raley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reframes the alignment of large language models not as a technical achievement but as an expression of "optimization culture" — the belief that measurable improvement along predefined axes fully captures value. Tracing this logic from pretraining through interface design and back to the audit society, the authors argue that optimization procedures can measure improbability but cannot distinguish error from invention, yet have nonetheless inherited the centuries-old cultural authority to define legitimate language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To critique the cultural and epistemic authority that optimization-driven language models have assumed, and to expose a fundamental limit: that loss functions and reward models execute judgment without the capacity to truly judge — they cannot distinguish between ungrammatical error and creative invention.

Method: Genealogical analysis tracing optimization logic through the full LLM stack (pretraining, decoding, preference tuning, benchmarking, interfaces) and situating it within the broader history of the "audit society" and optimization culture that predates the technology itself.

Result: The analysis reveals that optimization procedures can only measure statistical improbability of generated text, not whether that unlikelihood constitutes error or invention. Despite this incapacity, within five years these systems have assumed authority over legitimate language — authority previously held by academies, grammars, and examiners.

Conclusion: The apparatus of loss functions, reward models, benchmarks, and system prompts has displaced traditional arbiters of language without inheriting their capacity for genuine judgment. Optimization culture's conviction that measurable improvement exhausts the question of value is exposed as fundamentally inadequate for linguistic authority.

Abstract: In 2019, OpenAI released two million GPT-2 outputs-ungrammatical, half broken-to aid the detection of machine-generated text. The alignment that produced their more fluent successors is usually regarded as an engineering achievement; we read it instead as the newest expression of optimization culture: the conviction, older than the technology, that measurable improvement along predefined axes exhausts the question of value. Tracing that conviction through the stack-pretraining, decoding, preference tuning, benchmarking, interface-and back through its genealogy in the audit society, we arrive at the limit: an optimization procedure can measure how improbable a piece of generated text is; it cannot tell whether that unlikelihood is error or invention. A procedure that cannot make that distinction has nonetheless, within half a decade, assumed the authority to set the protocols of legitimate language. Held for centuries by academies and schoolrooms, grammars and examiners, this authority has been given over to loss functions, reward models, benchmarks, and system prompts: an apparatus that executes the office of judgment with no capacity for judging.

</details>


### [172] [LP Mining with LP2Graph: A Use Case for Railway Rescheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11980)
*Jörn Maurischat,Nikola Bešinović,Michael Färber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LP2Graph mines MILP formulations from published railway rescheduling papers into a typed variable–equation graph, enabling deterministic clustering into an objective, reproducible taxonomy of variables, constraints, and model types — validated by regenerating and re-solving cluster representatives across multiple solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Railway rescheduling relies heavily on MILP, but modeling knowledge is fragmented across hundreds of papers using incompatible notations. Existing narrative surveys classify models subjectively by vocabulary rather than structural properties and do not reproduce the formulations. There is a need for an objective, reproducible, and structural taxonomy grounded directly in the mathematical formulations themselves.

Method: LP2Graph parses each formulation through a canonical grammar into a single canonical model represented as a typed variable–equation graph. Sources are extracted, homologized, and clustered bottom-up at three levels: variables, then constraints/objective, then whole-model structure. Parallel clustering occurs by application domain and solution approach. Groups are labeled via a rule-seeded, self-updating classifier. Validation regenerates per-cluster representatives as independent LaTeX and re-solves them across CBC, HiGHS, and Gurobi, comparing results against the original paper's reported optimum.

Result: The method produces a deterministic, repeatable taxonomy of MILP structures in railway rescheduling. Cluster representatives can be regenerated as compilable LaTeX and verified by re-solving across multiple solvers (CBC, HiGHS, Gurobi) with results matching reported optima, confirming representational fidelity.

Conclusion: LP2Graph provides the first objective, structurally grounded taxonomy of MILP formulations in railway rescheduling, forming the principled foundation for the authors' raiLPminer line of automated model development. The approach generalizes beyond railway rescheduling to any optimization-driven domain with published MILP formulations.

Abstract: Like many optimization-driven domains, railway rescheduling relies on Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP), yet the field's modeling knowledge is scattered across hundreds of papers in incompatible notations, and narrative surveys organize it subjectively: they classify models by vocabulary rather than by structure, and reproduce neither. We present LP Mining with LP2Graph, a method that mines the structure of published LP and MILP formulations into a reproducible dataset and an induced taxonomy. Its core, LP2Graph, represents each formulation admitted by its canonical grammar as a typed variable--equation graph derived from a single canonical model; once a source is extracted into that model, everything downstream is deterministic. Each source is parsed into this model, homologized, and clustered bottom-up (over variables, then constraints and the objective, then whole-model structure) and, separately, by application domain and solution approach; the resulting groups are labeled by a rule-seeded, self-updating classifier. We validate the representation rather than assume it: per-cluster representatives are regenerated as independent LaTeX and re-solved across CBC, HiGHS and Gurobi against the optimum reported in the source paper. The outcome is an objective, repeatable taxonomy of variables, constraints and model types: the principled foundation on which our raiLPminer line of automated railway-rescheduling model development builds.

</details>


### [173] [Designing Agent-Ready Websites for AI Web Agents: A Framework for Machine Readability, Actionability, and Decision Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12056)
*Said Elnaffar,Farzad Rashidi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes the "agent-ready website" design framework to optimize e-commerce platforms for AI agent interaction, structured around agent interpretability, executability, and decision reliability. A controlled experiment with three browser-agent models across 300 runs showed the framework nearly doubled strict success rates (89.3% vs. 49.3%) and significantly reduced errors and step counts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online shopping is increasingly mediated by AI agents that search, compare, and execute purchases autonomously. Existing web design, SEO, and generative engine optimization (GEO) metrics fail to assess how well a website supports agent-mediated interaction, creating a gap that this paper addresses.

Method: The authors propose a three-dimensional framework: (1) agent interpretability (machine readability, semantic clarity), (2) agent executability (agent actionability), and (3) agent decision reliability (contextual decision-reliability signals). A controlled experiment compared a human-oriented baseline website against an agent-ready version with identical catalogs, pricing, stock, and workflows. Evaluation involved five tasks, three browser-agent models (GPT-4.1, Gemini-2.5 Flash, Grok-4 Fast), and 300 total runs, measuring PASS/PARTIAL/FAIL outcomes, success rates, error patterns, step counts, and token consumption.

Result: The agent-ready website achieved 134/150 PASS runs versus 74/150 for the baseline (strict success rate 89.3% vs. 49.3%). PARTIAL outcomes dropped from 43 to 3, and average step count decreased from 9.31 to 6.49. The largest gains were in product detail extraction, comparison, and multi-constraint selection tasks.

Conclusion: Enhanced structural clarity, action cues, evidence signals, and temporal validity indicators can substantially improve both the reliability and efficiency of AI browser agents. The agent-ready framework provides preliminary evidence that deliberate website design for agent interpretability and executability yields measurable performance gains in agent-mediated e-commerce.

Abstract: Online shopping is increasingly shifting toward a model in which AI agents independently search for products, compare options, evaluate constraints, and carry out parts of the purchasing process for users. Website design must now support both human and agent-mediated interaction. This paper introduces the agent-ready website, a design framework for enhancing the readability, interpretability, verifiability, and actionability of e-commerce platforms for AI agents. Existing web design, SEO, and generative engine optimization (GEO) metrics do not fully assess a website's capacity for agent-mediated interaction. The proposed framework is structured around three dimensions agent interpretability, agent executability, and agent decision reliability supported by features such as machine readability, semantic clarity, agent actionability, and contextual decision-reliability signals. The framework is evaluated through a controlled experiment comparing a human-oriented baseline and an agent-ready version of an identical website prototype, with identical catalogs, pricing, stock, and shopping workflows. The evaluation involved five tasks, three browser-agent models (GPT-4.1, Gemini-2.5 Flash, and Grok-4 Fast), and 300 runs, measuring PASS,PARTIAL,FAIL outcomes, strict and functional success rates, error patterns, step counts, and token consumption. The agent-ready website achieved 134 PASS runs out of 150 versus 74 out of 150 for the baseline (strict success rates of 89.3% vs. 49.3%), with the largest gains in product detail extraction, comparison, and multi-constraint selection. It also reduced PARTIAL outcomes from 43 to 3 and lowered the average step count from 9.31 to 6.49. These results provide preliminary evidence that enhanced structural clarity, action cues, evidence signals, and temporal validity indicators can substantially improve the reliability and efficiency of AI browser agents.

</details>


### [174] [Internet of Agentic Things: Networked AI Agents for Closed-Loop IoT Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12662)
*Quanyan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Internet of Agentic Things (IoAT), a layered architecture integrating agentic AI with IoT, cyber-physical systems, Physical AI, edge computing, and digital twins. It formalizes IoAT as a coupled workflow-control problem using hylomorphic dynamic programming and demonstrates smart-building orchestration as a use case, while discussing safety, security, and trustworthiness challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IoT and cyber-physical systems lack unified closed-loop orchestration that seamlessly integrates high-level AI reasoning and planning with low-level physical sensing and actuation. The authors seek to bridge this gap by embedding autonomous AI agents across cloud, edge, and physical layers, enabling coordinated perception, reasoning, and actuation in distributed environments.

Method: The paper introduces the IoAT architectural framework comprising three layers (cloud, edge/fog, physical IoT) interconnected by autonomous AI agents.
