It formalizes the system as a coupled workflow-control problem with nested strategic and tactical decision-making, and proposes a hylomorphic dynamic programming framework — combining form (hyle) and function (morphe) — to link agentic planning with physical execution.

Result: A smart-building orchestration use case is presented to illustrate the IoAT framework's applicability, demonstrating how autonomous agents can coordinate across layers for building management. The paper also identifies and discusses key open challenges including safety, security, governance, resilience, and trustworthy deployment.

Conclusion: The IoAT framework offers a promising path toward fully autonomous, closed-loop cyber-physical systems by tightly coupling agentic AI planning with physical-world execution. However, significant research remains in ensuring safe, secure, resilient, and trustworthy deployment before real-world adoption can be realized.

Abstract: The paper introduces the Internet of Agentic Things (IoAT), an architectural framework that integrates agentic AI, IoT, cyber-physical systems, Physical AI, edge computing, and digital twins into a unified closed-loop orchestration framework. The proposed architecture consists of cloud, edge/fog, and physical IoT layers connected through autonomous AI agents that perceive, reason, coordinate, and actuate across distributed cyber-physical environments. The paper formalizes IoAT as a coupled workflow-control problem with nested strategic and tactical decision making using a hylomorphic dynamic programming framework that links agentic planning with physical execution. Smart-building orchestration is presented as a representative use case, and key research challenges related to safety, security, governance, resilience, and trustworthy deployment are discussed.

</details>


### [175] [Operationalising Multi-Dimensional Evaluation for Conversational Agents: A Scalable, Governed Pipeline with Selective Re-evaluation and Model Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12085)
*Niranjan Kumar M,Balaji Nagarajan,Karthik Nair,Faysal Satter,Nithin Surendran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A governed, configuration-driven LLM evaluation pipeline for retail conversational agents that processes ~50K records/day, achieving macro F1 of 0.93 and 89% human-acceptability accuracy for translation across 2M+ evaluated interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retail conversational agent evaluation requires assessing qualitative dimensions (helpfulness, truthfulness, clarity, tone, intent alignment) beyond simple lexical-overlap metrics. While LLM-as-a-judge offers scalable alternatives to human evaluation, production deployment faces challenges in governance, reproducibility, cost, schema consistency, traceability, and reliability that existing solutions do not adequately address.

Method: The GenAI Evaluation framework operates as a governed, configuration-driven pipeline: (1) production chatbot logs undergo normalization and sharding, (2) asynchronous execution with schema-constrained LLM scoring evaluates helpfulness, truthfulness, clarity, tone alignment, and translation-specific dimensions, (3) selective re-evaluation targets only incomplete, malformed, or schema-invalid records, and (4) governance mechanisms include schema locking, versioned configurations, validation logs, and record-level provenance. Validation used 12,980 stratified-random human-labeled records from four trained annotators spanning 14 intents, 156 sub-intents, 18 major domains, and 129 sub-domains.

Result: The pipeline processes approximately 50,000 records daily and has evaluated over two million interactions. It achieved a macro F1 score of 0.93 and 89% human-acceptability accuracy for translation.

Conclusion: A governed, schema-constrained LLM evaluation pipeline can deliver scalable, reproducible, and auditable quality assessment of retail conversational agents at production scale, with strong alignment to human judgment across multiple qualitative dimensions.

Abstract: Evaluating retail conversational agents requires methods beyond lexical-overlap metrics to assess intent alignment, factuality, helpfulness, clarity, tone, and overall response quality. Although LLM-as-a-judge methods provide scalable alternatives to human evaluation, production deployment introduces challenges in governance, reproducibility, cost, schema consistency, traceability, and reliability. We present GenAI Evaluation, a governed, configuration-driven pipeline for large-scale evaluation of retail conversational systems. It processes production chatbot logs through normalization, sharding, asynchronous execution, and schema-constrained LLM scoring. The framework evaluates helpfulness, truthfulness, clarity, tone alignment, and translation-specific dimensions. Selective re-evaluation processes only incomplete, malformed, or schema-invalid records, while schema locking, versioned configurations, validation logs, and record-level provenance support auditability. The framework processes approximately 50,000 records daily and has evaluated more than two million interactions. Validation used 12,980 stratified-random human-labeled records from four trained annotators. Classification covered 14 intents, 156 sub-intents, 18 major domains, and 129 sub-domains. The pipeline achieved a macro F1 score of 0.93 and 89% human-acceptability accuracy for translation.

</details>


### [176] [Who Grades the Grader? Co-Evolving Evaluation Metrics and Skills for Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12790)
*Xing Zhang,Guanghui Wang,Yanwei Cui,Ziyuan Li,Wei Qiu,Bing Zhu,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes co-evolving evaluation metrics and agent skills simultaneously when no reliable metric exists. Using a "metric loop" that composes small drawback detectors, anchored by a small reference set and regularized by consensus, the metric co-evolves with a skill loop (Double Ratchet), retaining 88–110% of ground-truth-driven lift across code generation, text-to-SQL, and report generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving agent systems implicitly assume a reliable evaluation metric already exists, but in many real-world applications, no such metric is available. Building a trustworthy metric from scratch is the core unsolved problem this paper addresses.

Method: Two co-evolving loops: (1) A metric loop that searches compositions of small drawback detectors under a full evolutionary lifecycle, trained on a ten-item anchored reference set, regularized by consensus over unlabeled outputs, and audited against a held-out anchor it never sees. (2) Double Ratchet — co-evolution of this metric with a lifecycle-managed skill loop, where metric and skills ratchet each other forward without access to ground truth.

Result: Across code generation (MBPP+), enterprise text-to-SQL (Spider 2.0-Snow), and reference-free report generation, Double Ratchet retains 88–110% of the held-out lift achieved by the same skill loop driven by ground truth or the best available rubric. Ablation: removing anchor guards collapses the metric into a vacuous detector; removing the lifecycle does not. When evolved skills gamed the report rubric, an independent judge caught it, one detector repaired it, and a task-aware judge preferred evolved outputs in 77% of decided pairs.

Conclusion: Co-evolving metrics alongside skills with anchor discipline and outer audits provides a failure-expecting architecture that should be the default wherever no reliable automatic verifier exists.

Abstract: Self-evolving agent systems improve by creating, revising, and retiring their own skills, but every such loop rests on a hidden assumption: a reliable evaluation metric already exists. In many real applications it does not. We make three claims. First, metrics can be \emph{evolved}: our metric loop searches compositions of small drawback detectors under a full evolutionary lifecycle, trained to agree with a ten-item anchored reference set, regularized by consensus over unlabeled outputs, and audited against a held-out anchor it never reads, yielding a transparent, inspectable metric rather than an opaque judge. Second, since no metric exists to beat, the yardstick is recovering what an accurate metric would have enabled, and \emph{Double Ratchet}, our co-evolution of the metric with a lifecycle-managed skill loop, does so: across code generation (MBPP+), enterprise text-to-SQL (Spider~2.0-Snow), and reference-free report generation, it retains 88--110\% of the held-out lift achieved by the same skill loop driven by ground truth or the best available rubric. Third, safety comes from anchor discipline plus outer audits: removing anchor guards collapses the metric into a vacuous detector while removing the lifecycle does not; and when evolved skills gamed the report rubric, an independent judge caught it, one detector repaired it, and a task-aware judge then preferred the evolved outputs over the pre-evolution baseline in 77\% of decided pairs. We argue this failure-expecting architecture is the right default wherever no reliable automatic verifier exists.

</details>


### [177] [Representing and Generating Levels Over Time through Playtrace Reconstructive Partitioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12097)
*Emily Halina,Matthew Guzdial*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces "cake," a domain-independent representation for game levels that implicitly encodes their dynamic, temporally-experienced nature. Paired with a novel level generation algorithm called Playtrace Reconstructive Partitioning (PRP), the approach generates valid Sokoban levels without sacrificing solution diversity, outperforming six state-of-the-art PCG methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Procedural Content Generation (PCG) approaches for video game levels typically use representations that abstract away the inherently dynamic, time-based experience of gameplay. The authors argue this is a critical gap, as games are fundamentally experienced over time, not as static snapshots.

Method: The authors propose (1) the "cake" representation, a domain-independent formalism that implicitly encodes dynamic information about how a level unfolds over time, and (2) Playtrace Reconstructive Partitioning (PRP), a level generation algorithm designed to exploit this representation. Evaluation is conducted in the Sokoban domain, benchmarking against six state-of-the-art PCG approaches.

Result: The PRP approach, using the cake representation, generates valid Sokoban levels while maintaining solution diversity, matching or exceeding the performance of six state-of-the-art PCG methods.

Conclusion: The cake representation more neatly encodes the implicit dynamic nature of games compared to existing representations, and this representational strength enables a domain-agnostic level generation algorithm (PRP) that is both effective and preserves diversity.

Abstract: Video games are a dynamic medium experienced over time. While there are many Procedural Content Generation (PCG) approaches for generating video game levels, they often use representations that abstract away this dynamic nature. In this paper, we introduce a novel, domain-independent ``cake'' representation for game levels over time which implicitly encodes dynamic information. We present a novel level generation approach Playtrace Reconstructive Partitioning (PRP) specifically developed for this cake representation. We compare against six state-of-the-art PCG approaches in the game domain of \textit{Sokoban}, and find that our approach can generate valid levels without sacrificing solution diversity. We believe our cake representation more neatly encodes the implicit dynamic nature of games compared to existing representations, which allows for our domain-agnostic level generation algorithm PRP.

</details>


### [178] [FormalAnalyticGeo: A Neural-Symbolic Based Framework for Multimodal Analytic Geometry Problem Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12982)
*Ruoran Xu,Wending Gao,Qiufeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FormalAnalyticGeo is a fully automatic, closed-loop framework that generates multimodal analytic geometry problems by combining formal language (CDL), SDF-based diagram rendering, and four specialized LLM components, producing the verified AnalyticGeo7K dataset of 7K+ problems with 82.3% of answers within 5% relative error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analytic geometry is underexplored in multimodal math reasoning due to scarcity of annotated samples. Existing diagram generation approaches fail: template methods cannot handle constraint-driven layouts, and generative models lack geometric precision for conic curves.

Method: The framework uses CDL (Condition Description Language) as a formal intermediate representation bridging problem text and SDF-based diagram rendering. Four LLM components form a pipeline: (1) Generator creates diverse problems, (2) Formalizer converts them to CDL, (3) Measurer extracts ground-truth via vision-based measurement, (4) Quality Verifier checks outputs at three stages with structured feedback driving automatic retry in a closed loop, eliminating human annotation.

Result: The framework produced AnalyticGeo7K (7K+ verified multimodal problems with aligned text, diagram, formal annotation, and ground truth). Generated problems achieve a median ground-truth relative error of 0.70%, with 82.3% of answers falling within 5% of the exact symbolic solution.

Conclusion: FormalAnalyticGeo successfully enables scalable, high-quality automatic generation of analytic geometry problems with precise diagrams and accurate ground-truth annotations, filling a gap in multimodal math reasoning datasets.

Abstract: Math reasoning has achieved significant progress with the rapid advancement of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), however analytic geometry remains largely underexplored, primarily due to the scarcity of annotated samples. Existing diagram generation approaches struggle with analytic geometry: template methods cannot handle constraint-driven layouts, and generative models lack the geometric precision to render annotated conic curves correctly. We present FormalAnalyticGeo, a scalable framework for fully automatic generation of multimodal analytic geometry problems. Leveraging the rigor of formal languages, we design the framework around CDL (Condition Description Language), a formal intermediate representation that bridges free-form problem text with precise diagram rendering via a Signed Distance Field (SDF) engine. The framework employs four specialized LLM components in sequence: a Generator that produces diverse analytic geometry problems, a Formalizer that converts each problem into CDL for SDF-based rendering, a Measurer that extracts ground-truth answers through vision-based measurement on the rendered diagrams, and a Quality Verifier that checks outputs at three stages. Structured feedback from the Quality Verifier drives automatic retry, forming a closed loop that eliminates any need for human annotation. Applying FormalAnalyticGeo at scale yields AnalyticGeo7K, a dataset of over 7K verified multimodal problems, each with aligned text, diagram, formal annotation, and ground truth.Experiments show that the generated problems achieve a median ground-truth relative error of 0.70\%, with 82.3\% of answers falling within 5\% of the exact symbolic solution. Our framework and dataset will be publicly released.

</details>


### [179] [Connected by Construction: Learning Tractable Near-Tour Marginals for Traveling Salesman Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12127)
*Ke Sun,Xinyuan Zhang,Xinwu Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: C2TSP is an end-to-end unsupervised learning pipeline for TSP that learns structurally meaningful latent representations (rooted 1-tree Gibbs distributions) rather than opaque surrogates. It uses residual edge perturbations trained via implicit differentiation with a smoothed Held-Karp layer and certificate-guided sharpening to produce interpretable, tour-like structures that decode into high-quality solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learning-based TSP methods evaluate via decoded tours but obscure what Hamiltonian structure is actually learned, since the learned object lives in surrogate spaces (heatmaps, policies, scores). The authors aim to directly answer what structure is learned by designing a method where the latent object itself is structurally meaningful and interpretable.

Method: The method, C2TSP, uses a connected-by-construction rooted 1-tree Gibbs family as the latent representation. It learns residual edge perturbations from unbiased TSP cost via implicit differentiation. A smoothed Held-Karp layer restores expected degree balance, and certificate-guided sharpening pushes the distribution toward tour-like structures. The pipeline is end-to-end and unsupervised.

Result: C2TSP achieves strong decoding (tour quality) while preserving interpretable structural information in the latent space. Ablation studies confirm that both edge perturbation and certificate-guided sharpening jointly improve tour cost and tour-like structure.

Conclusion: Learning TSP through a structurally meaningful latent object (rooted 1-tree Gibbs family) is effective: it yields competitive tours while making the learned Hamiltonian structure transparent and interpretable, bridging the gap between learning and combinatorial structure.

Abstract: Learning-based methods for the traveling salesman problem (TSP) are often evaluated through the tours produced after decoding or search, but the learned object itself frequently lives in a surrogate space such as heatmaps, assignments, construction policies, or search-guidance scores. This hides the fundamental question: what Hamiltonian structure has actually been learned before decoding? In this study, we directly answer this question by learning TSP through a structurally meaningful latent object, rather than leaving most of the Hamiltonian structure to the final decoding stage. Based on a connected-by-construction rooted $1$-tree Gibbs family, we propose an end-to-end unsupervised learning pipeline called \emph{C2TSP}. The pipeline learns residual edge perturbations from unbiased TSP cost through implicit differentiation. For structural correction, a smoothed Held--Karp layer restores expected degree balance, while certificate-guided sharpening further pushes the connected distribution toward more tour-like structures. Experiments show that C2TSP yields strong decoding performance while preserving interpretable structural information. Ablations further verify that edge perturbation and certificate-guided sharpening jointly improve both tour cost and tour-like structure.

</details>


### [180] [Cost-Governed RAG: Unified Per-Tenant Cost Attribution Across Retrieval and Generation in Multi-Tenant LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12188)
*Navnit Shukla*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cost-Governed RAG introduces an architecture combining a codebook-oblivious vector index (TurboVec) with multi-tenant LLM governance to achieve joint per-tenant attribution of embedding, retrieval, and generation costs, achieving 99.96% accuracy with negligible telemetry overhead and 3.1-9.0x infrastructure cost reduction versus managed vector databases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise multi-tenant RAG deployments suffer from a governance gap: LLM generation is metered per-token, but retrieval costs (vector memory, similarity compute, embedding APIs) are shared and unattributed, enabling invisible cross-subsidization among tenants. Existing graph-based vector indexes have non-linear memory overhead that prevents deterministic per-tenant cost attribution.

Method: The authors propose Cost-Governed RAG, which integrates TurboVec (a codebook-oblivious vector index with a deterministic, closed-form memory formula) with a multi-tenant LLM governance gateway. This creates a unified observability stack where all three cost layers — embedding, retrieval, and generation — are jointly attributable per tenant. The system is deployed on Snowpark Container Services within a cloud data platform's governance boundary. A formal three-layer cost model is developed, and TurboVec's deterministic properties enable near-exact per-tenant retrieval cost calculation while also removing the shared-codebook leakage surface inherent in trained quantizers.

Result: Across 100 simulated tenants with 10M vectors following a log-normal size distribution, the system achieves 99.96% end-to-end cost attribution accuracy with telemetry overhead under 0.04% of query latency. Retrieval infrastructure costs are reduced by 3.1–9.0x compared to managed vector database services under stated pricing assumptions.

Conclusion: Codebook-oblivious quantization enables deterministic per-tenant cost attribution for retrieval, closing the governance gap in multi-tenant RAG. The removal of the shared-codebook leakage surface is an exploratory finding requiring further investigation. The architecture provides a practical path toward fully accountable, tenant-fair RAG infrastructure.

Abstract: Enterprise Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) deployments face a critical governance gap: while LLM generation cost is metered per token, the retrieval layer - vector memory, similarity compute, and embedding API calls - remains an unattributed shared cost, enabling invisible cross-subsidization among tenants. We present Cost-Governed RAG, an architecture that integrates a codebook-oblivious vector index (TurboVec) with a multi-tenant LLM governance gateway, creating a unified observability stack where embedding, retrieval, and generation costs are jointly attributable per tenant. The architecture exploits TurboVec's deterministic, closed-form memory formula to enable near-exact per-tenant retrieval cost calculation - a property unavailable in graph-based indexes with non-linear memory overhead. Deployed on Snowpark Container Services within a cloud data platform's governance boundary, the system achieves 99.96% end-to-end cost attribution accuracy across 100 simulated tenants (10M vectors, log-normal size distribution) with telemetry overhead below 0.04% of query latency. The architecture reduces retrieval infrastructure cost by 3.1-9.0x compared to managed vector database services under the pricing assumptions detailed in Section IV. We formalize a three-layer cost model and demonstrate that codebook-oblivious quantization enables deterministic per-tenant cost attribution while also removing the shared-codebook leakage surface present in trained quantizers - the latter observation being exploratory and subject to the limitations described in Section VII.

</details>


### [181] [The Emerging Paradigm of Geospatial Foundation Models: From Pre-Training to Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12177)
*Shelley Cazares*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Geospatial Foundation Models (GeoFMs) — AI/ML models pre-trained on massive geospatial data — enabling a paradigm shift where computationally heavy pretraining is separated from task-specific fine-tuning. It distinguishes between finetunable vision models and zero-shot vision-language models, provides a taxonomy of adaptation strategies with a cost-effectiveness framework, and envisions agentic geospatial reasoning where LLMs orchestrate GeoFMs for automated analytical workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The advent of foundation models in geospatial analysis demands a structured understanding of how these models shift the operational paradigm. The authors aim to (1) articulate the separation-of-duties paradigm that democratizes access to state-of-the-art AI/ML while preserving downstream task security, (2) clarify the distinct capabilities of different GeoFM types, and (3) provide practical guidance for operationalization, addressing the gap between raw model availability and mission-effective deployment by domain experts.

Method: This is a conceptual/position paper that synthesizes and taxonomizes approaches rather than conducting new experiments. It categorizes GeoFMs into two types: (1) finetunable vision models trained via self-supervised methods like masked auto-encoding, and (2) vision-language models trained via contrastive learning for zero-shot, open-vocabulary tasks. It then introduces a taxonomy of model adaptation strategies and a cost-effectiveness framework to guide domain experts in selecting appropriate adaptation approaches for specific missions.

Result: The paper yields a structured framework comprising: (1) a clear articulation of the separation-of-duties paradigm enabled by GeoFMs, (2) a functional taxonomy distinguishing finetunable vision models from contrastively learned vision-language models with zero-shot capabilities, (3) a cost-effectiveness decision framework for model adaptation strategies, and (4) a forward-looking architecture for Agentic Geospatial Reasoning where LLMs serve as orchestrators leveraging GeoFMs as tools, moving the field from perception to cognition.

Conclusion: GeoFMs represent a transformative shift in geospatial intelligence, decoupling expensive pretraining from accessible downstream adaptation. The paper argues that the future lies in Agentic Geospatial Reasoning, where LLM-based orchestrators integrate GeoFMs to handle natural language queries and automate complex analytical pipelines, transitioning geospatial AI from purely perceptual tasks to higher-order cognitive reasoning. Practical adoption hinges on informed selection of adaptation strategies balancing performance, cost, and mission requirements.

Abstract: The analysis of satellite and aerial imagery has entered a new era with the advent of foundation models. This paper describes the concept of Geospatial Foundation Models (GeoFMs), which are artificial intelligence/machine learning (AI/ML) models pre-trained on massive geospatial datasets through varied methodologies. We first articulate the core paradigm shift that GeoFMs enable: a separation of duties, where large-scale model providers perform the computationally intensive pretraining, allowing domain experts to rapidly fine-tune or prompt these models for specific, mission-critical tasks. This approach democratizes access to state-of-the-art AI/ML while maintaining the security and confidentiality of the downstream task. We then explore the novel capabilities unlocked by different types of GeoFMs, distinguishing between the finetunable vision models produced by self-supervised techniques like masked auto-encoding, and the vision-language models produced by contrastive learning which enable zero-shot tasks like open-vocabulary image analysis. Next, we discuss the practical considerations for operationalizing GeoFMs, from performance-cost analysis to the broader MLOps ecosystem. To that end, we introduce a taxonomy of model adaptation strategies and propose a framework for domain experts to select the most cost-effective adaptation approach for their particular mission set. Finally, we present a forward-looking vision of Agentic Geospatial Reasoning, where Large Language Models act as intelligent orchestrators, leveraging GeoFMs as tools to answer high-level user queries in natural language and automate complex analytical workflows, moving the field from perception to cognition.

</details>


### [182] [On-Device Deep Research at 4B: Exposure Bounds Faithfulness, Retrieval Bounds Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12257)
*Vinay Kumar Chaganti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study using a 4B model on a 24GB laptop finds that faithful citations depend primarily on per-source exposure (character length shown to the generator), not source quality, while trustworthy coverage depends on retrieval recall. Increasing exposure from 400 to 1500 characters lifts faithfulness from ~0.45 to 0.58 regardless of whether sources are gold or retrieved, at a cost of ~235 extra output tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device research agents that run locally on consumer laptops are underexplored. Prior work conflates citation faithfulness and source coverage into a single metric, and no study has measured these separately for a deployable small model, nor identified which levers control each outcome and at what cost.

Method: The study fixes a single 4B-parameter generator model on a 24GB laptop and disentangles two metrics: (1) cited claim faithfulness — whether a cited source actually supports the claim, and (2) trustworthy coverage — whether the agent cites the correct sources. It crosses two experimental factors: per-source exposure (400 vs. 1500 characters shown to the generator) and source quality (gold papers vs. retrieved papers). Results are validated with a primary judge and a second independent judge.

Result: Faithfulness is governed by exposure: increasing from 400 to 1500 characters raises faithfulness from 0.45→0.58 (retrieved) and 0.37→0.58 (gold), with the two source-quality settings converging, indicating exposure — not source correctness — is the binding constraint. This lift is robust across both judges (tight convergence under primary, approximate under second). Coverage is governed by retrieval: trustworthy coverage remains near 0.22 regardless of exposure, because retrieval recall is fixed near 0.40, so exposure cannot compensate for which sources are cited. The added exposure costs ~235 output tokens.

Conclusion: The practical recipe for on-device research agents is to first raise per-source exposure (cheaply gains faithfulness), then treat retrieval recall as the sole remaining lever for improving coverage, since exposure alone cannot fix source selection.

Abstract: On-device research agents search a corpus, read sources, and write a cited brief on a personal laptop. Whether their citations are faithful, and at what cost, is unmeasured for a deployable small model. This study fixes one 4B generator on a 24 GB laptop and asks what makes its citations faithful. It separates two quantities usually reported as one number. Cited claim faithfulness asks whether the cited source supports the claim. Trustworthy coverage asks whether the agent also cites the right sources. The study crosses how much of each source the generator sees, 400 against 1500 characters, with the quality of the sources supplied, gold papers against retrieved papers. Two levers fall out, and they act on different outcomes. Exposure sets faithfulness. More of each source lifts faithfulness from 0.45 to 0.58 on retrieved sources and from 0.37 to 0.58 on gold sources, and the two settings converge, so faithfulness is bound by exposure, not by whether the source is correct. The exposure lift is robust to a second, independent judge; the exact convergence is tight under the primary judge and only approximate under the second. Retrieval sets coverage. Trustworthy coverage stays near 0.22 on retrieved sources at any exposure, because recall is held near 0.40, so exposure cannot fix which sources are cited. The extra exposure costs about 235 output tokens. The practical recipe is to raise per source exposure first, cheaply, and then treat retrieval recall as the only remaining lever.

</details>


### [183] [A Threshold Exceedance Framework for CBRN Uplift Evaluation in Frontier Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12200)
*Rahul Gupta,Abhinav Mohanty,Payal Motwani,Venkatesh Saligrama,Satyapriya Krishna,Connor Harris,Gary Anthony Ackerman,Brandon Behlendorf,Tom Hobson,Theodore Wilson,Spyros Matsoukas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Threshold Exceedance Criteria (TEC) framework for evaluating whether language model access materially increases non-expert ability to plan CBRN misuse. A large-scale empirical study found that while model-assisted plans sometimes achieved expert-equivalent ratings, confirmed material uplift was limited to the radiological domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CBRN uplift evaluations suffer from inconsistent definitions of non-experts, threat scope, baselines, scoring rubrics, and decision rules, making cross-study comparisons unreliable. A standardized, decomposable framework is needed to rigorously assess whether model access materially increases CBRN misuse planning capabilities beyond publicly available tools.

Method: The authors propose the Threshold Exceedance Criteria (TEC) framework, which decomposes uplift studies into three independent components: (1) determining non-expert participant eligibility, (2) defining the CBRN threat scope, and (3) statistically estimating material uplift. They operationalize this in a large-scale empirical study distinguishing two uplift types: generative uplift (model assists plan creation from scratch) and revisionist uplift (model assists refinement of existing plans). Attack plans across CBRN domains were evaluated via subject-matter-expert review.

Result: Under controlled pre-release evaluation, model-assisted plans sometimes received expert-equivalent instructional ratings, but confirmed material uplift was statistically significant only in the radiological domain. The findings revealed domain heterogeneity and directly informed mitigation and deployment-governance decisions.

Conclusion: The authors recommend future CBRN uplift evaluations adopt prespecified criteria, explicit baselines, separation of generative and revisionist uplift estimates, and careful distinction between preliminary screening signals and confirmed risk determinations to improve rigor and comparability.

Abstract: As frontier language models advance, policymakers and model developers need methods for assessing whether model access materially increases a non-expert actor's ability to plan high-consequence Chemical, Biological, Radiological, or Nuclear (CBRN) misuse relative to public tools alone. Existing CBRN evaluations differ in non-expert definitions, threat scope, baselines, scoring rubrics, and decision rules, making results difficult to compare across studies. We introduce a Threshold Exceedance Criteria (TEC) framework that decomposes an uplift study into independently executable components: determining non-expert participant eligibility, defining the CBRN threat scope for the study, and statistically estimating material uplift. We then operationalize the TEC framework in a large-scale empirical study using a design that determines two forms of uplift: generative (where a model assists plan creation from scratch) and revisionist (where a model assists refinement of an existing plan). The study produced attack plans across the CBRN domains, which we evaluated through subject-matter-expert review to estimate generative and revisionist uplift. Applying the framework, our empirical study revealed domain heterogeneity: under this controlled pre-release evaluation, model-assisted plans sometimes received expert-equivalent instructional ratings, but confirmed material uplift was limited to the radiological domain. These findings informed mitigation and deployment-governance decisions rather than characterizing deployed model behavior. We conclude with methodological lessons for future CBRN uplift evaluations, emphasizing prespecified criteria, explicit baselines, separation of generative and revisionist estimates, and careful distinction between preliminary screening signals and confirmed risk determinations.

</details>


### [184] [Good Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12217)
*Ivan Bercovich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The abstract defines criteria for high-quality tasks: they should be correct, solvable, verifiable, well-specified, and genuinely challenging, framed as real-world problems with outcome-based verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a normative standard for what constitutes a "good task" in machine learning and AI evaluation, addressing the gap between artificial benchmarks and problems practitioners actually face.

Method: The authors propose a set of five defining criteria (correctness, solvability, verifiability, well-specifiedness, and interesting hardness) plus a framing principle: tasks should mirror real practitioner problems using authentic language and outcome-based tests.

Result: A concise, principled taxonomy emerges: good tasks are those that resist trivial solution while remaining objectively evaluable, grounded in practitioner reality rather than synthetic abstraction.

Conclusion: Task quality hinges on alignment with real-world problem-solving: correctness, verifiability via outcome tests (not method tests), and hardness stemming from substantive rather than artificial obstacles.

Abstract: Good tasks are correct, solvable, verifiable, well-specified, and hard for interesting reasons. The best tasks describe a real problem an experienced practitioner would recognize, in language a practitioner would use, with tests that verify the outcome rather than the approach.

</details>


### [185] [Rethinking the Evaluation of Harness Evolution for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12227)
*Yike Wang,Huaisheng Zhu,Zhengyu Hu,Yige Yuan,Zhengyu Chen,Shakti Senthil,Hannaneh Hajishirzi,Yulia Tsvetkov,Pradeep Dasigi,Teng Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper challenges automatic harness evolution for LLM agents, arguing that existing evaluations conflate harness design improvements with test-time search gains and risk overfitting. Experiments show harness evolution fails to consistently outperform simple test-time scaling baselines and generalizes poorly to held-out tasks, calling for fairer evaluation protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automatic harness evolution methods use the same public benchmark for both iterative search/tuning and final evaluation, creating two problems: (1) it's unclear whether performance gains come from better harness design or merely from additional search compute, and (2) reported improvements risk overfitting to the specific benchmark. A rigorous, controlled comparison under matched feedback and inference budgets is needed.

Method: The authors conduct a controlled evaluation comparing automatic harness evolution against (1) simple test-time scaling baselines and (2) discovery baselines, all under matched feedback and inference budgets. They also evaluate evolved harnesses on held-out tasks to test generalization. Experiments use Terminal-Bench 2.1 with GPT-5.4 and Claude Opus 4.6.

Result: Automatic harness evolution does not consistently outperform simple test-time scaling methods. The evolved harnesses exhibit limited generalization when evaluated on held-out tasks, suggesting that reported gains in prior work may stem from additional search compute and benchmark overfitting rather than genuine harness design improvements.

Conclusion: The findings question the effectiveness of automatic harness evolution and underscore the need for fairer evaluation protocols — specifically, decoupling search from final evaluation, matching inference budgets across methods, and using held-out benchmarks to assess generalization.

Abstract: We revisit the evaluation of automatic harness evolution for LLM agents. Existing harness evolution methods use unit test cases to search for harness configurations and then report final performance on the same public benchmark. This protocol raises two fundamental concerns. First, harness evolution is itself an iterative search procedure that repeatedly evaluates and revises candidate harnesses using task feedback. As in agentic test-time scaling, it should therefore be compared with simple task-level search baselines under matched feedback and inference budgets to determine whether its gains arise from improved harness design or from additional search alone. Second, because the search and the final evaluation share the same benchmark, the reported gains risk overfitting to that specific task set. To address these concerns, we conduct an extensive evaluation comparing harness evolution with simple test-time scaling and discovery baselines under comparable feedback and inference budgets, and also evaluate evolved harnesses on held-out tasks to assess whether the discovered improvements generalize. Experiments on Terminal-Bench 2.1 with GPT-5.4 and Claude Opus 4.6 show that automatic harness evolution does not consistently outperform simple test-time scaling methods and exhibits limited generalization. Our results raise important questions about the effectiveness of automatic harness evolution and highlight the need for fairer evaluation protocols and benchmarks for automatic harness design. Our code is available at https://github.com/rethinking-harness-evolution.

</details>


### [186] [How Many Tasks Are Enough for Agent Benchmark Decisions? A Replay Analysis of Public LLM Agent Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12338)
*Wei-Jung Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper examines whether partial benchmark runs can reliably reproduce the pairwise agent comparisons of full benchmarks. By replaying completed task-level records from SWE-bench, AppWorld, and tau-bench, the authors find that the required task fraction varies dramatically — from 15% for AppWorld to 90% for SWE-bench Verified — and propose reporting standards for partial evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full agent benchmark evaluations are costly, creating a temptation to draw conclusions from partial runs. However, a task completion fraction alone provides no guarantee that the partial run supports the same pairwise ranking as the full benchmark. The paper addresses the gap between the practical desire for early stopping and the statistical reliability needed for valid comparisons.

Method: The authors replay completed public task-level records from three benchmarks (SWE-bench, AppWorld, tau-bench). A partial budget is considered "enough" only when it satisfies three criteria: (1) it supports the same pairwise decision as the completed benchmark, (2) it covers required task groups under a coverage rule, and (3) it leaves no more than a target fraction of comparisons unresolved. The analysis uses a 5 percentage point budget grid with a strict 0% unresolved threshold.

Result: The required task fraction varies sharply across benchmarks. At a strict 0% unresolved threshold, AppWorld reaches all targets at 15% of tasks, tau-bench at 25%, and SWE-bench Verified at 90%. SWE-bench Lite fails to meet all targets even at 95% under the primary coverage rule. These results demonstrate that no single rule of thumb for partial evaluation suffices across benchmarks.

Conclusion: Partial evaluation reports should explicitly state: (1) the performance margin required between agents, (2) task selection methodology, (3) the coverage rule, (4) the decision rule, and (5) the allowable fraction of unresolved comparisons. Standardized reporting along these dimensions would make partial benchmark results more interpretable and trustworthy.

Abstract: Agent benchmarks often compare two agents after all tasks have run, but costly evaluations make partial runs tempting. A task fraction alone does not show whether a partial run supports the same pairwise conclusion as the completed benchmark. We study this question by replaying completed public task-level records from SWE-bench, AppWorld, and tau-bench. A partial budget counts as enough only when it supports the completed benchmark's decision, covers required task groups, and leaves no more than a target fraction of comparisons unresolved. The required task fraction varies sharply. At the strict 0 percentage point threshold on a 5 percentage point budget grid, AppWorld first meets all targets at 15 percent, tau-bench at 25 percent, and SWE-bench Verified at 90 percent; SWE-bench Lite does not meet all targets by 95 percent under the primary coverage rule. Partial-evaluation reports should state how much one agent must outperform another, how tasks are selected, what coverage rule is required, what decision rule is used, and how many comparisons may remain unresolved.

</details>


### [187] [PM-Bench: Evaluating Prospective Memory in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12385)
*Genglin Liu,Saadia Gabriel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PM-Bench is a text-based benchmark inspired by the Virtual Week paradigm that evaluates prospective memory in LLM agents—their ability to remember and execute delayed intentions at appropriate future cues while juggling ongoing activities. Across a simulated seven-day week, the best agent (GPT-5.4) achieves only 65.1% F1, and no single strategy dominates across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prospective memory—executing an intended action at a specific future cue or state amid ongoing tasks—is a critical yet underexplored capability in agentic AI. Existing benchmarks do not systematically evaluate whether LLM agents can maintain deferred intentions over extended time horizons, recognize latent environmental triggers, and appropriately interleave scheduled tasks with ongoing activity. This gap motivates a controlled, standardized testbed for diagnosing prospective memory failures.

Method: The authors introduce PM-Bench, a text-based benchmark modeled on the Virtual Week paradigm from cognitive science. Agents are placed in a simulated seven-day week where they must carry out a continuous primary activity while monitoring for time- or event-based cues that signal when a previously stored intention should be executed. The benchmark tests three core facets: (1) maintaining user intentions, (2) executing delayed intentions, and (3) monitoring latent environment changes. Eight state-of-the-art LLMs are evaluated under eight distinct agent configurations, covering variations in prompting, memory architecture, and planning strategies.

Result: PM-Bench proves challenging for all models and configurations. The top-performing system—a GPT-5.4-based agent—achieves only a 65.1% F1 score. Notably, no single strategy for improving prospective memory (e.g., memory augmentation, rehearsal, or planning) consistently outperforms others across all models, indicating that prospective memory failures are multifaceted and not trivially addressed by any one intervention.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents exhibit substantial deficits in prospective memory, as evidenced by uniformly modest performance on PM-Bench. The benchmark is released as a controlled testbed to facilitate systematic diagnosis of these failures and to spur development of training and inference-time interventions that enable more reliable prospective behavior in agentic AI systems.

Abstract: A significant challenge in agentic AI is prospective memory: the ability to execute an intention at a specific future cue or state while other activities are ongoing. We introduce PM-Bench, a text-based benchmark for measuring prospective memory capabilities in modern LLM agents. Inspired by the Virtual Week paradigm from cognitive science, PM-Bench evaluates how well LLM agents maintain user intentions, execute delayed intentions, and monitor latent environment changes. Over the course of a simulated seven-day week, agents must continue an ongoing activity while deciding whether any deferred task is due. We compare eight state-of-the-art LLMs on PM-Bench under eight different agent configurations. PM-Bench proves challenging across all settings: the best method, a GPT-5.4 agent, reaches only 65.1\% F1 score under our evaluation. Furthermore, no single strategy for improving prospective memory dominates across models. We release PM-Bench as a controlled testbed for diagnosing these failures and developing training or inference-time interventions that support reliable prospective behavior.

</details>


### [188] [Critic Experience Bank: Self-Evolving Step-Level Confidence Estimation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12397)
*Yaopei Zeng,Congchao Wang,JianHang Chen,Nan Wang,Yurui Chang,Lu Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AMBER is a self-evolving critic framework for LLM agents that provides calibrated step-level confidence estimates before action execution by accumulating past judgments and their observed consequences, without requiring training or ground-truth step labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents need reliable step-level confidence estimation to avoid wasting interaction budgets or triggering irreversible side effects, but existing confidence estimators only score responses from a given prompt without considering execution consequences in the environment.

Method: AMBER uses a critic LLM that retrieves similar productive and unproductive experiences from a memory bank. After each trajectory, a hindsight LLM with access to full execution feedback votes on whether each step was productive. These pseudo-labels populate the memory bank, enabling the critic to self-evolve by accumulating evidence. The framework is training-free and requires no ground-truth step labels.

Result: Across three agent benchmarks and three critic backbones, AMBER achieves the best calibration (ECE and Brier) and ranking (AUC) in every dataset-critic combination, reducing ECE by up to 54% relative to the strongest training-free baseline.

Conclusion: AMBER demonstrates that inferring step quality from past experiences with known outcomes is a simple yet effective strategy for agent confidence estimation, significantly outperforming training-free baselines across diverse benchmarks and critic models.

Abstract: LLM agents act in external environments where each action changes the state that later decisions condition on, and where a single wrong step can waste interaction budget or trigger irreversible side effects long before the final failure is observed. Reliable deployment therefore requires \emph{step-level confidence estimation}: a calibrated probability that each proposed action is productive, available \emph{before} the action is executed. Existing LLM confidence estimators are designed to score a response from the given prompt, but agent confidence also depends on execution consequences: whether similar actions in similar situations actually advanced the task after the environment responded. We introduce the \method (\methodshort), a self-evolving critic framework in which an LLM critic accumulates evidence from its own past judgments and their observed consequences. After each trajectory, a hindsight LLM that sees the full execution feedback votes on whether each step was productive. The resulting pseudo-labels populate a memory bank from which related productive and unproductive experiences are retrieved into the critic's prompt whenever a similar step recurs. \methodshort requires no training and uses no ground truth step labels. Across three agent benchmarks and three critic backbones, \methodshort attains the best calibration (ECE and Brier) and ranking (AUC) in every dataset--critic combination, reducing ECE by up to $54\%$ relative to the strongest training-free baseline.

</details>


### [189] [Isolation as a First-Class Principle for LLM-Agent System Safety: Concepts, Taxonomy, Challenges and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12406)
*Huihao Jing,Wenbin Hu,Shaojin Chen,Haochen Shi,Sirui Zhang,Hanyu Yang,Changxuan Fan,Zhongwei Xie,Hongyu Luo,Wun Yu Chan,Wei Fan,Haoran Li,Yangqiu Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey proposes isolation as a first-class safety principle for LLM-agent systems, organizing the literature through a boundary-centric taxonomy of five boundaries (user-agent, agent-tool, agent-execution, agent-agent, system-environment) to explain how structural causes like prompt injection, tool misuse, and memory poisoning share common roots and propagate through agent workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety literature for LLM agents is fragmented across attack types, applications, and benchmarks, making it difficult to understand why diverse failures (prompt injection, tool misuse, memory poisoning) often share the same structural cause and how they propagate through agent workflows. Existing content-alignment perspectives are insufficient because LLM agents now function as the "brain" of systems, expanding safety concerns beyond input-output alignment to system behavior and real-world execution outcomes.

Method: The authors employ a boundary-centric taxonomy organized around five isolation boundaries: user-agent, agent-tool, agent-execution, agent-agent, and system-environment. They survey and classify existing attack vectors, defense mechanisms, and failure modes according to which boundary's isolation is violated, trace cross-boundary failure propagation paths, and identify where loss of isolation first occurs in typical agent architectures.

Result: The taxonomy reveals that many superficially distinct attacks (prompt injection, tool misuse, memory poisoning) share the same structural root cause: violation of isolation at specific boundaries. The paper maps how compromise propagates across boundaries, catalogs which defenses are relevant at each interface, identifies cross-boundary failure paths, and provides a structured foundation for reasoning about LLM-agent safety holistically.

Conclusion: Isolation should be treated as a foundational design principle for LLM-agent system safety. The boundary-centric taxonomy not only clarifies the shared structural causes of diverse attacks but also points toward an "isolation-by-construction" research agenda for building safer future agent systems. Open challenges remain in achieving robust isolation without sacrificing agent capability.

Abstract: The capability of LLM agents to function as the ``brain'' of a system fundamentally expands the scope of analysis beyond a standalone model. Consequently, safety is no longer only about input--output content alignment. It also concerns system behavior and real-world execution outcomes. However, the current literature is fragmented across attack types, applications, and benchmarks. This makes it hard to explain why failures such as prompt injection, tool misuse, and memory poisoning often share the same structural cause, and how they spread through an agent workflow. In this survey, we treat isolation as a first-class principle for LLM-agent system safety. By isolation, we refer to the separation of user inputs, tool access, execution channels, inter-agent communication, and environment-originated context. We organize the literature with a boundary-centric taxonomy of five boundaries: user-agent, agent-tool, agent-execution, agent-agent, and system-environment. This view helps identify where the loss of isolation first occurs, how compromise propagates across boundaries, and which defenses are most relevant at each interface. We also summarize cross-boundary failure paths, discuss open challenges, and outline a research agenda for isolation-by-construction in future agent systems.

</details>


### [190] [Accepted Prefixes Are Not All You Need: A Negative Result on PEFT-Based Block-Diffusion Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12422)
*Abdurrahman Javat,Allan Kazakov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PEFT-BD adds a LoRA-like adapter to the same backbone for block-diffusion drafting, but fails to achieve practical speedups because drafting still requires a full backbone forward pass, making the drafter parameter-efficient but not compute-efficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve speculative decoding draft quality while reducing auxiliary parameters and system overhead. PEFT-BD is motivated by attractive properties: no tokenizer mismatch, no separate draft model, few trainable parameters, and parallel block proposal via a denoising objective.

Method: PEFT-BD uses a LoRA-like adapter on the same backbone model. The adapter acts as a block-diffusion drafter (BD3LM-style denoising) to propose multiple tokens in parallel, while the unadapted backbone serves as the autoregressive verifier. Each step requires an adapter-enabled draft pass and an adapter-disabled verification pass.

Result: On Qwen3-0.6B, PEFT-BD achieves nontrivial accepted prefixes but no practical speedup. Profiling reveals the drafter is parameter-efficient but not compute-efficient, as drafting costs are near-identical to verification costs.

Conclusion: A necessary condition for successful speculative decoding is that the drafter must be substantially cheaper to execute than the verifier. Longer accepted prefixes cannot compensate when draft computation remains at verifier-scale.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive language model inference by using a cheap drafter to propose multiple future tokens and a target model to verify them. A common design goal is therefore to improve draft quality while reducing auxiliary parameters and systems overhead. We study a negative result for this direction through PEFT-BD, a same-backbone speculative decoding method in which a LoRA-like adapter acts as a block-diffusion drafter for an autoregressive verifier. PEFT-BD is motivated by several attractive properties: it avoids tokenizer mismatch, avoids loading a separate draft model, adds only a small number of trainable parameters, and uses a BD3LM-style denoising objective to propose a block of tokens in parallel. Despite these advantages, PEFT-BD does not yield a practical speedup in our Qwen3-0.6B experiments. Although the method obtains nontrivial accepted prefixes, profiling shows that each speculative step requires an adapter-enabled full-backbone draft pass followed by an adapter-disabled full-backbone verification pass. Thus, the drafter is parameter-efficient but not compute-efficient. Our results isolate a simple but important condition for successful speculative decoding: the drafter must be substantially cheaper to execute than the verifier. Longer accepted prefixes alone cannot compensate when draft computation remains verifier-scale.

</details>


### [191] [EVOQUANT: Self-Evolving Verifier-Guided Strategy Optimization for Robust Quantitative Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12455)
*Jie Mao,Changlun Li,Xiang Li,Qiqi Duan,Jinhui Yuan,Xiang Liu,Yuyu Luo,Jing Tang,Xiaowen Chu,Nan Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EVOQUANT proposes a self-evolving, verifier-guided LLM framework that automates quantitative trading strategy optimization by diagnosing performance bottlenecks, generating semantically controlled edits, and selecting improvements through multi-stage verification, achieving significant Sharpe ratio gains across A-share and crypto market strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantitative strategy optimization is labor-intensive and manual, requiring domain experts to iteratively identify weak signals, tune risk controls, and validate revisions. While LLMs could accelerate this, directly using them to rewrite strategies risks hallucinated edits, strategy drift, and backtest overfitting — necessitating a more controlled, verifiable approach.

Method: EVOQUANT employs LLMs to: (1) deeply diagnose strategy performance bottlenecks, (2) generate semantically controlled candidate edits, (3) select the optimal strategy via a multi-stage verification pipeline, and (4) distill optimization experience into reusable knowledge for continual self-improvement. Evaluated on seven strategies (four A-share, three Crypto).

Result: Average test Sharpe ratio improved from -0.298 to 0.538 across all strategies. The best-performing strategy achieved a 199% relative improvement. Ablation studies and stress tests under stricter conditions validated framework effectiveness and robustness.

Conclusion: EVOQUANT transforms quantitative strategy optimization from costly manual trial-and-error into an automated, verifiable iterative paradigm, offering a promising new direction for applying LLMs to financial strategy research.

Abstract: Quantitative strategy optimization remains largely manual, requiring domain experts to identify weak signals, tune risk-control rules, and repeatedly validate iterative revisions. Large language models can accelerate this process, but directly relying on them to rewrite trading strategies often introduces hallucinated edits, strategy drift, and backtest overfitting. We propose EVOQUANT, a self-Evolving Verifier-guided framework for strategy Optimization in Quantitative trading. Our method utilizes LLMs to deeply diagnose performance bottlenecks, generates semantically controlled candidate edits, selects the best strategy through a multi-stage verification pipeline, and distills optimization experience into reusable knowledge for continual self-improvement. We evaluate our method using seven representative strategies: four from the A-share market and three from the Crypto market. Experimental results show that our method significantly improves the Sharpe ratio across all tested strategies: the average test Sharpe increases from -0.298 to 0.538, and the best-performing strategy achieves a 199% relative improvement. Ablation studies and stress tests under stricter conditions further validate the effectiveness and robustness of the framework. Overall, this work transforms quantitative strategy optimization from costly manual trial and error into an automated and verifiable iterative paradigm, offering a new path for applying large language models to financial strategy research.

</details>


### [192] [Do We Really Need Transformers for Global Spatial Information Extraction in Traffic Forecasting?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12462)
*Qihang Zhang,Siyao Zhang,Letao Kang,Wenzhe Liang,Miao Zhang,Zhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether complex attention-based global spatial mixing is necessary for traffic forecasting, finding that a simple uniform global aggregation operator achieves comparable performance to standard spatial attention (only 0.14% mean MAE difference across six benchmarks) while reducing complexity from O(N²) to O(N). The authors further decompose spatial attention into a row-uniform global background and a non-uniform residual, showing the residual's value is dataset-dependent and marginal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The underlying mechanism by which global spatial information is modeled and extracted in traffic forecasting remains insufficiently investigated. It is unclear whether high-degree-of-freedom adaptive attention is truly necessary, or whether a simple global aggregation operator can suffice for capturing global spatial dependencies.

Method: The authors design a controlled ablation framework that isolates and replaces only the spatial mixing module, enabling direct comparison between attention-based global interaction and uniform full-range mixing. They further decompose spatial attention into two components: a row-uniform global background and a non-uniform residual, to analyze where the value of attention mechanisms originates.

Result: Across six traffic benchmarks, uniform full-range mixing and standard spatial attention each achieve lower MAE on three datasets, with only a 0.14% difference in mean MAE. The uniform approach reduces node-scale spatial mixing complexity from O(N²) to O(N). Mechanism analysis reveals the non-uniform residual in spatial attention provides only dataset-dependent marginal value.

Conclusion: Spatial attention in traffic forecasting should be justified by stable gains beyond a simple row-uniform global background. The marginal benefit of complex attention mechanisms suggests that practitioners should carefully evaluate whether such complexity is warranted. Source code is publicly available.

Abstract: Existing traffic forecasting models commonly focus on extracting spatial dependencies, particularly global spatial information, which characterizes the representations obtained through interactions between each individual node and all nodes across the traffic network. However, the underlying mechanism by which such global information is modeled and extracted remains insufficiently investigated. Whether global information must be extracted by high-degree-of-freedom adaptive attention or can be captured by a simple global aggregation operator remains unclear. For this purpose, we design a controlled ablation framework that replaces only the spatial mixing module to test attention-based global interaction. Across six traffic benchmarks, uniform full-range mixing and standard spatial attention each achieve lower MAE on three datasets, with only a 0.14% difference in mean MAE, while the former reduces node-scale spatial mixing complexity from O(N2) to O(N). Mechanism analysis further decomposes spatial attention into a row-uniform global background and a non-uniform residual. The residual shows dataset-dependent marginal value, suggesting that spatial attention should be justified by stable gains beyond a row-uniform global background. The corresponding source code is publicly available at: https://github.com/uuesti/U-Trans

</details>


### [193] [Function-Aware Fill-in-the-Middle as Mid-Training for Coding Agent Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12463)
*Yubo Wang,Jiarong Liang,Yuxuan Zhang,Xuye Liu,Cong Wei,Yuyu Zhang,Ping Nie,Wenhu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes function-aware fill-in-the-middle (FIM) mid-training for coding agents, exploiting the structural isomorphism between agent action-observation loops and function call sites. Using program dependency graph analysis to mask functions, mid-training on a 2.6B-token code corpus yields consistent SWE-Bench gains (+2.8–5.4 points) across multiple models and post-training pipelines, while also mitigating capability erosion on non-agent coding and tool-use benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard left-to-right pretraining on code only captures the forward direction of reasoning, but coding agents need to integrate external tool returns into ongoing reasoning — a bidirectional conditioning pattern. The authors observe that the agent's action-observation-continuation loop mirrors the structure of a function call site (caller → callee return → downstream consumption), which exists at scale in ordinary code. They aim to exploit this untapped structural signal through self-supervised mid-training to improve agentic coding capabilities.

Method: Function-aware fill-in-the-middle (FIM) mid-training: (1) Functions are selected for masking using program dependency graph (PDG) analysis combined with a complexity-inferability double criterion; (2) Selected functions are masked and the model must predict them given surrounding context (caller + downstream consumer); (3) Mid-training applied to Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct (7B/14B) and Qwen3-8B on a decontaminated 2.6B-token corpus from 968 GitHub repositories; (4) Existing agentic post-training pipelines (R2E-Gym, SWE-Smith, SWE-Lego) are then applied on top.

Result: SWE-Bench-Verified improvements: +2.8 (7B), +3.0 (14B), +3.2 (Qwen3-8B). SWE-Bench-Lite improvements: +3.7 (7B), +4.0 (14B), +5.4 (Qwen3-8B). Gains hold across two post-training pipelines (R2E-Gym, SWE-Smith) and on non-Qwen2.5 base (Qwen3-8B with SWE-Lego). Mid-training mitigates capability erosion from agentic post-training on non-agent coding benchmarks (LiveCodeBench) and non-coding tool-use benchmarks (tau-bench, BFCL), despite the mid-training corpus containing only Python code.

Conclusion: Function-aware FIM mid-training provides a principled, self-supervised way to instill the bidirectional conditioning pattern needed for coding agents. The function-call inductive bias transfers beyond in-domain agentic coding, suggesting it captures a fundamental reasoning primitive that generalizes across tasks and survives subsequent fine-tuning stages.

Abstract: Coding agents must integrate external tool returns into ongoing reasoning - a capability that standard left-to-right pretraining on code exposes only in its forward direction. We observe that the action-observation-continuation loop of a coding agent is structurally isomorphic to a function call site, where a caller binds arguments, a callee returns a value computed elsewhere, and downstream code consumes that value. This conditioning structure exists at internet scale in ordinary code. We exploit it through function-aware fill-in-the-middle (FIM) mid-training: a self-supervised objective that masks functions selected via program dependency graph analysis and a complexity-inferability double criterion. We mid-train Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct (7B/14B) and Qwen3-8B on a 2.6B-token decontaminated corpus drawn from 968 GitHub repositories, then apply existing agentic post-training pipelines. Mid-training improves SWE-Bench-Verified by +2.8/+3.0 at 7B/14B and by +3.2 on Qwen3-8B; SWE-Bench-Lite gains are +3.7/+4.0/+5.4 on the same models. The improvement holds across two post-training pipelines (R2E-Gym, SWE-Smith) and on a non-Qwen2.5 base (Qwen3-8B with SWE-Lego). Beyond in-domain gains, mid-training also mitigates the capability erosion that agentic post-training otherwise inflicts on non-agent coding (e.g., LiveCodeBench) and non-coding tool-use benchmarks (tau-bench, BFCL): although the mid-training corpus contains Python code only, the function-call inductive bias survives post-training and yields consistent gains.

</details>


### [194] [From Observation to Insight: Mechanistic World Models and the Quest for Autonomous Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12474)
*Ingmar Posner,Anson Lei,Bernhard Schölkopf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that scientific discovery requires explanatory mechanisms, not just prediction. It introduces Mechanistic World Models — a paradigm placing reusable causal mechanisms at the core of representation, computation, and learning — as a unified conceptual and computational blueprint for moving AI from forecasting toward autonomous discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI for Science excels at predictive tasks (protein folding, weather forecasting) but fundamentally lacks explanatory power. Scientific understanding depends on uncovering reusable mechanisms that generate observations, whereas contemporary ML remains organized around predictive mappings. There exists no unified framework that integrates mechanistic interpretability, causal representation learning, equation discovery, and modular architectures toward genuine discovery.

Method: The authors (1) draw on philosophy of science to characterize scientific understanding as knowledge organization around mechanisms, (2) derive necessary computational capabilities for mechanistic discovery, (3) identify design principles and inductive pressures that promote the emergence of explanatory knowledge, (4) formalize the anatomy of a mechanism-centric world model, and (5) map existing research directions (mechanistic interpretability, causal representation learning, equation discovery, modular architectures) onto this paradigm, highlighting their complementary roles and current fragmentation.

Result: The paper identifies that diverse research threads each capture partial ingredients of mechanism-centric discovery but lack a unifying framework. Mechanistic World Models are proposed as that conceptual foundation, specifying how reusable mechanisms should be represented, how computation should operate over them, and what learning objectives encourage their emergence.

Conclusion: Prediction alone is insufficient for scientific discovery. Mechanistic World Models offer a coherent computational blueprint for building AI systems that can organize knowledge around reusable explanatory mechanisms, enabling a transition from predictive forecasting to autonomous scientific discovery.

Abstract: Recent advances in foundation models have transformed AI for Science, enabling remarkably accurate predictive performance across domains ranging from protein folding to weather forecasting. Yet prediction alone does not constitute scientific discovery. Scientific understanding depends on uncovering the reusable explanatory mechanisms that generate observations, whereas contemporary machine learning remains fundamentally organised around predictive mappings rather than explanatory structure. In this paper, we argue that scientific discovery is fundamentally a problem of knowledge organisation. To this end, we introduce Mechanistic World Models, a new design paradigm that places reusable mechanisms at the centre of representation, computation and learning. Drawing on insights from the philosophy of science, we derive the computational capabilities required for discovery, identify the design principles and inductive pressures that encourage explanatory knowledge to emerge, and formalise the anatomy of a mechanism-centric world model. Finally, we show how diverse research directions including mechanistic interpretability, causal representation learning, equation discovery and modular architectures capture complementary ingredients of this paradigm while lacking a unified framework. We propose Mechanistic World Models as a conceptual foundation and computational blueprint for moving AI beyond predictive forecasting towards autonomous scientific discovery.

</details>


### [195] [TRACE: An Operational Reasoning Schema for Auditable Agentic Commitments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12480)
*Edward Y. Chang,Emily J. Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a typed, versioned schema for recording reasoning traces with a binding operational contract: no durable state change without a record. It argues that language models natively compute association, not reasoning, and provides a structured recording framework with fields, tests, a reference writer, gate-first measurement, and consumer contracts to make AI decisions auditable and verifiable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the claim that current language models — including those using chain-of-thought and reinforcement learning — lack the formal machinery of genuine reasoning (e.g., Socratic procedure, Pearl's ladder of causation). The autoregressive mechanism computes only association, and training techniques inherit this fundamental limitation. Without structured, verifiable records of reasoning, AI systems make unaccountable state changes. TRACE aims to fill this gap by providing an operational interface that makes reasoning traces auditable, testable, and gated.

Method: The paper proposes TRACE: (1) a typed, versioned schema centered on the TraceRecord and its causal specialization; (2) an eight-stage reference writer procedure for producing records; (3) a gate-first measurement regime; (4) the TRACE-Bench protocol for evaluation; (5) consumer contracts specifying what a record guarantees and what consumers must honor (memory admission, plan gating, temporal regret, verdict reuse); and (6) two worked illustrative examples — a music-lessons argument and a flood search-and-rescue vignette — demonstrating association/intervention/prescription separation and record-based commitment deferral with append-only revision.

Result: The paper presents no empirical results. Two illustrative vignettes are worked through: the music-lessons example demonstrates tracing an argument from sentence to typed verdict, separating association, intervention, and prescription; the flood rescue vignette shows a predictive world model whose own support and out-of-distribution scores contradict its confident plan, so the record defers commitment, requests bounded observation, revises append-only, and clears a different branch. The contribution is the schema and contract, not performance claims.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that reasoning is not in the language model — it is in the record. TRACE provides the schema, writer, measurement regime, and consumer contract to operationalize this principle. The framework makes AI decisions auditable by requiring no durable state change without a record, with consumers (memory, planning, regret, verdict reuse) bound by a contract that makes the schema an operational interface. Closed-loop evaluation is left to future work.

Abstract: This paper defines TRACE (Typed Reasoning And Commitment Evidence): a typed, versioned schema for recording reasoning traces, a reference procedure for writing records against it, and one operating discipline, no durable state change without a record. The paper argues in three layers that reasoning is not in the language model: the autoregressive mechanism natively computes association; chain-of-thought and reinforcement learning inherit its limits; and the formal constructs of reasoning theory, from Socratic procedure to Pearl's ladder, are absent as machinery. The schema answers the absence with fields and tests: the TraceRecord and its causal specialization, an eight-stage reference writer, a gate-first measurement regime, the TRACE-Bench protocol, and the consumers, memory admission, plan gating, temporal regret, and verdict reuse, whose more auditable decisions are the measure of the record. A record-consumer contract states what a record guarantees and what a consumer must honor in return, making the schema an operational interface rather than a passive document. Two worked examples run in the main text: a music-lessons argument traced from sentence to typed verdict, separating association, intervention, and prescription; and a flood search-and-rescue vignette in which a predictive world model reports confident plan success that its own support and out-of-distribution scores contradict, so the record defers the commitment, requests a bounded observation, revises append-only, and clears a different branch. The vignette is illustrative, not empirical; closed-loop evaluation is left to future work, so the contribution is the schema and its contract, not a performance claim. Appendices carry the full schema, writer algorithms and cost model, clinical and policy illustrations, the benchmark protocol, convergence metrics, and usage scenarios.

</details>


### [196] [Win by Silence: Deletion Non-Monotonicity, Autonomous Exploitation, and Typed-State Gating in LLM Plan Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12986)
*Aleh Manchuliantsau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Evaluators can inadvertently reward plans that omit necessary details. This paper formalizes an "omission incentive" in LLM-generated venture route scoring, proves a mathematical identity for score gains from deleting interior transitions, demonstrates that a score-seeking optimizer exploits this vulnerability, and proposes PCSC/GATE as a structured countermeasure that constrains search toward covered (non-omitted) plans without claiming semantic completeness verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strategic plan evaluators using expected-value scoring can perversely reward plans for becoming less explicit — i.e., omitting necessary intermediate steps can increase the score. This creates a systematic "omission incentive" where a plan appears better not because it is actually improved, but because it has hidden work. The authors seek to formally characterize this failure mode in LLM-generated venture planning and develop a detection/neutralization mechanism.

Method: The paper develops a formal framework using staged expected-value scoring over typed-state transition graphs.
