The CRC clamp is a proof-of-concept certification tool under a constructible reference, not a deployed solution, demonstrating that causal intervention on report coordinates can simultaneously satisfy the competing demands of resisting forbidden pressure and updating on licensed evidence.

Abstract: Aligned language models routinely misreport under non-evidential incentive pressure: they agree with a confident user or overstate certainty even when their internal belief is unchanged. We cast this as a failure of internal incentive-compatibility (IC) and present a method for learning and certifying counterfactual report mediators that hold a model's reports to a causal contract: invariant to forbidden influences (pressure, prestige, restyling) and responsive to licensed ones (genuine evidence). These two demands, resist and update, pull in opposite directions. We study them on a Bayesian-witness benchmark with known posteriors, in which the same user disagreement is licensed evidence or forbidden pressure purely by stated source reliability. We (i) causally identify, by interchange interventions rather than probe accuracy, low-rank report coordinates for answer, confidence, and caveat that are near-orthogonal and independently controllable, and (ii) introduce a training-free counterfactual report-coordinate (CRC) clamp that references the model's own report under a counterfactually incentive-neutralized context. On the witness benchmark the two-pass clamp attains resist and update of 1.00 jointly (Wilson 95% CI [0.99,1.00]), a causal certificate under a constructible reference, not a deployed solution. Global decoding and steering show a single-parameter tradeoff; output-level fine-tuning matches both objectives only when both are enumerated; resist-only training loses evidence-responsiveness. The deployable single-pass compilation is lossy (0.73/0.97). The mechanism and clamp reproduce across three model families and transfer to a natural sycophancy benchmark (SycophancyEval). Our contribution is the interface and certification method: activation-level counterfactual incentive-invariance as a structural primitive for internal IC.

</details>


### [216] [Dynamic Resource Allocation for Ensemble Determinization MCTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13007)
*Jakub Kowalski,Adam Ciężkowski,Artur Krzyżyński,Mark H. M. Winands*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes two dynamic resource allocation enhancements for Ensemble Determinization MCTS — Dynamic Number of Determinizations and Dynamic Simulation Allocation — and demonstrates statistically significant strength improvements on tabletop games Jaipur, Lost Cities, and Splendor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MCTS variants like Ensemble Determinization are effective in adversarial games with randomness and hidden information, but static resource allocation (fixed number of determinization trees, uniform simulation budgets) is suboptimal. The authors aim to improve performance by dynamically allocating computational resources during search.

Method: Two axes of dynamic resource allocation are introduced: (1) Dynamic Number of Determinizations, which adaptively adjusts how many determinization trees are maintained based on search progress; and (2) Dynamic Simulation Allocation, which non-uniformly distributes the simulation budget across trees using per-simulation decisions to favor trees with the highest expected knowledge gain. Evaluation is conducted on three tabletop games (Jaipur, Lost Cities, Splendor) under both iteration-based and time-based constraints.

Result: Specific configurations of the proposed enhancements yielded statistically significant improvements in playing strength compared to baselines across all three benchmark domains, in both iteration- and time-based settings.

Conclusion: Dynamic resource allocation within Ensemble Determinization MCTS is beneficial; selectively adjusting the number of determinization trees and non-uniformly allocating simulations can significantly boost algorithm performance in imperfect-information games.

Abstract: Simulation-based algorithms are especially suited for high-uncertainty environments such as adversarial board games with significant elements of randomness and hidden information. In particular, several Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) variants are commonly used in such domains. In this paper, we propose a series of enhancements for Ensemble Determinization MCTS, introducing two axes for dynamic resource allocation. First, Dynamic Number of Determinizations, increases or decreases the number of currently used determinization trees depending on the behavior of so-far search. Second, Dynamic Simulation Allocation, splits the simulation budget nonuniformly across the determinization trees, using simulation-to-simulation decisions to choose the tree with potentially the best knowledge gain. As benchmark domains, we used three popular tabletop games: Jaipur, Lost Cities, and Splendor. Testing our proposed enhancements in iteration- and time-based settings showed that particular configurations yield a statistically significant increase in the algorithm's strength.

</details>


### [217] [Audio-Native Speech Recognition with a Frozen Discrete-Diffusion Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13013)
*Harsha Vardhan Khurdula,Abhinav Kumar Singh,Yoeven D Khemlani,Vineet Agarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper trains a discrete diffusion language model (DiffusionGemma, 26B MoE) for speech recognition by adding a lightweight audio interface (~42M params, 0.16% of backbone). A frozen Whisper encoder + projector + LoRA adapters enable parallel transcript generation in ~8 denoising steps. A key finding is that standard training objectives fail due to a gradient deadlock, resolved by adding a CTC loss. The model achieves 6.6% WER on LibriSpeech test-clean and supports multilingual transcription with a single adapter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive decoders dominate ASR, emitting tokens sequentially. The authors investigate whether discrete diffusion language models can instead transcribe speech by refining entire transcripts in parallel over few denoising steps, potentially enabling faster, non-autoregressive generation.

Method: The approach uses a frozen DiffusionGemma 26B mixture-of-experts backbone with uniform random-token discrete diffusion (not absorbing-mask). A frozen Whisper encoder extracts acoustic features, a lightweight projector maps them into the model's embedding space, and low-rank adapters (LoRA) allow the frozen backbone to attend to audio. Only ~42M parameters are trained. Critically, a CTC loss applied through the frozen output head is added because standard training objectives fail — the gradient reaches the projector only through attention that has already dismissed the audio, creating a deadlock that CTC breaks.

Result: The model achieves 6.6% WER on LibriSpeech test-clean. Transcription runs in approximately 8 parallel denoising steps regardless of utterance length. A single adapter trained on six languages works for English, Hindi, and Mandarin (evaluated).

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion language models can serve as effective, parallel ASR decoders when properly grounded to audio. The CTC auxiliary loss is essential to overcome the gradient deadlock between the acoustic projector and the diffusion backbone. The approach is highly parameter-efficient and supports multilingual transcription with a single adapter.

Abstract: Automatic speech recognition is dominated by autoregressive decoders that emit one token at a time. We ask whether a discrete diffusion language model can transcribe speech instead, refining a whole transcript in parallel over a small number of denoising steps. We train an audio-native interface for DiffusionGemma, a 26B mixture-of-experts model that generates text by uniform, random-token discrete diffusion rather than the absorbing-mask scheme common to recent diffusion language models. A frozen Whisper encoder supplies acoustic features, a lightweight projector maps them into the model embedding space, and low-rank adapters let the frozen backbone attend to the new modality.
  About 42M parameters are trained, which is 0.16 percent of the backbone. We find that the natural training objectives fail to ground the audio because their gradient reaches the projector only through attention that has already dismissed it. A connectionist temporal classification loss applied through the frozen output head breaks this deadlock. The resulting model reaches 6.6 percent word error rate on LibriSpeech test-clean, transcribes in roughly eight parallel steps regardless of utterance length, and uses a single adapter trained on six languages, which we evaluate here on English, Hindi, and Mandarin.

</details>


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [218] [Reading the Eyes in VR: Multimodal Modeling of Social Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11931)
*Mohammad Fahim Abrar,Shayla Sharmin,Roghayeh Leila Barmaki*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This study examined how presentation medium (VR vs. desktop) and immediate trial-level feedback affect performance and engagement on the Reading the Mind in the Eyes Test (RMET). While VR received higher usability ratings and was perceived as more effortful, no reliable medium effects emerged on objective measures. Feedback increased fixation durations and EEG-based engagement but did not alter accuracy or task completion time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RMET is widely used to assess social intelligence, but its standard administration on paper/desktop introduces uncontrolled variability in viewing geometry (distance, field of view) and rarely provides immediate feedback. The authors aimed to test whether standardizing presentation via VR and adding trial-level feedback would influence behavioral, eye-tracking, and neural (EEG) metrics, thereby evaluating RMET's viability as a process-aware assessment.

Method: A 2×2 mixed design with 20 participants: presentation medium (VR vs. desktop, between-subjects) and feedback (immediate correctness cue vs. none, within-subjects). RMET was implemented in Unity; VR held stimulus distance and field of view constant. Synchronized multimodal data were collected: eye-tracking (fixation-based gaze), EEG, response time, accuracy, and subjective usability/effort ratings.

Result: Immediate feedback significantly increased fixation durations and EEG-based engagement indices. No significant differences emerged in total correct answers or task completion time. Presentation medium yielded no reliable differences in objective measures, but VR was rated significantly higher on usability and perceived effort.

Conclusion: RMET can be effectively deployed as a process-aware assessment in controlled VR and desktop environments. The dissociation between subjective experience and objective performance highlights the value of multimodal measurement. Feedback influences attentional and engagement processes without altering accuracy, suggesting it modulates how participants approach the task rather than their ultimate inferential success.

Abstract: Social intelligence, the ability to interpret others' emotions, beliefs, and intentions, is often assessed with the Reading the Mind in the Eyes Test (RMET), in which participants infer mental states from images of the eye region. Yet RMET is typically presented on paper or desktop displays, where viewing geometry can vary across participants, and it rarely includes immediate feedback. We investigated whether presentation medium and brief trial-level feedback influence RMET behavior. We implemented RMET in Unity for both desktop and Virtual Reality (VR), using VR to hold stimulus distance and field of view constant without changing the items. We conducted a 2x2 mixed study with 20 participants, with device (VR vs. desktop) manipulated between subjects and feedback (immediate correctness cue vs. none) manipulated within subjects. Eye-tracking and EEG data were recorded and synchronized with behavioral logs. We analyzed fixation-based gaze measures, EEG signals, response time, accuracy, and subjective measures. Immediate feedback was associated with longer fixation durations and higher EEG-based engagement, while no significant differences were observed in task completion time or total correct answers. Presentation medium did not produce reliable differences in the objective measures, but VR received higher usability ratings and was also rated as more effortful. These results provide initial evidence that RMET can be studied as a process-aware assessment task in controlled VR and desktop settings.

</details>


### [219] [Thought Experiments for Conceptual Work: A New Application of a (Very) Old Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12092)
*Leah Hope Ajmani,Mo Houtti,Eric P. S. Baumer,Stevie Chancellor*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper proposes thought experiments as a rigorous conceptual method for HCI research, demonstrating how hypothetical scenarios with logical progression can expose normative implications of conceptual commitments, illustrated through Value-Sensitive Design critique and Nissenbaum's contextual integrity framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HCI, as an interdisciplinary field, relies on concepts as building blocks for theory, yet the normative consequences of conceptual commitments often go unexamined. There is a need for a structured method to interrogate these commitments and anticipate technological harms, aligning with growing calls for heightened research ethics and implications scrutiny.

Method: The authors adapt thought experiments from philosophy — defined as hypothetical but tractable scenarios that logically progress to a conclusion — and recast them as an interrogative method for HCI. Through logical moves, TEs trace conceptualizations to their normative implications. Two illustrative cases are presented: original TEs critiquing stakeholders in Value-Sensitive Design, and an analysis of Helen Nissenbaum's deployment of TEs to derive the theory of contextual integrity.

Result: Thought experiments enable precise anticipation of potential technology harms. The two examples demonstrate TEs' capacity to reveal hidden normative stakes in conceptual frameworks and to generate robust theoretical constructs like contextual integrity.

Conclusion: Thought experiments equip HCI researchers with a method to operationalize ethical scrutiny, making conceptual and normative analysis systematic rather than ad hoc. This strengthens both the theoretical rigor and the anticipatory ethical capacity of the field.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose thought experiments (TEs) as a crucial method for Human-Computer Interaction (HCI) researchers to engage in conceptual work. As an interdisciplinary field, HCI often uses concepts as the fundamental building blocks for larger theories. However, the conceptual commitments we make in this process carry normative consequences. TEs are a well-established philosophical method, whereby a hypothetical but tractable scenario logically progresses to a conclusion. We outline TEs as an interrogative method that brings conceptualizations to their normative implications through logical moves. We illustrate the value of thought experiments through two examples: (1) original thought experiments to critique stakeholders in Value-Sensitive Design and (2) Helen Nissenbaum's use of thought experiments to generate contextual integrity. We discuss how TEs precisely anticipate the potential harms of technologies, allowing HCI to operationalize current calls for increased scrutiny of research ethics and broader implications.

</details>


### [220] [Faster AI, Uneven Frontier: Rapid Crossings, a Jagged Frontier, and the Repositioning of Human Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12125)
*Ancuta Margondai,Julie Rader,Emma Rader,Sara Willox,Mustapha Mouloua*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Frontier AI systems have surpassed human expert baselines on many cognitive benchmarks between 2023-2026, with task length capability doubling every ~7 months. However, the frontier is "jagged": humans retain key advantages, and benchmarks overstate real-world capability. Evidence suggests naive human-AI collaboration underperforms, requiring a redesigned human role focused on specification, verification, and oversight rather than direct task execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To synthesize and assess the current state of frontier AI capabilities relative to human expertise, examine the evidence on human-AI collaboration effectiveness, and articulate the implications for how the human role must be redesigned — not merely defended — in light of rapid but uneven AI progress.

Method: This is a position paper that synthesizes and triangulates multiple lines of evidence: (1) documented AI benchmark crossings over human expert baselines, (2) literature on cognitive offloading and skill atrophy, (3) experimental records on human-AI collaboration outcomes, and (4) labor-market field data. It critically evaluates benchmark validity issues (contamination, construct validity, vendor self-evaluation, reliability gaps) and integrates findings across these disparate research threads to state a coherent position.

Result: Key findings include: (a) frontier AI crossed human expert baselines on graduate science, competition math, software engineering, and diagnostic reasoning benchmarks, with 50%-reliability task length doubling every ~7 months; (b) humans retain advantages in long-horizon reliability, novel problems, calibrated self-knowledge, sample-efficient learning, and embodied action; (c) benchmarks overstate deployed capability due to contamination, construct validity issues, vendor self-evaluation, and the gap between 50% reliability and economically required reliability; (d) naive human-AI collaboration often underperforms the stronger partner alone; (e) early field evidence is consistent with offloading-induced skill costs, though the largest meta-analyses on prior technologies point the opposite way; (f) the necessary human role shift toward specification, verification, and oversight is visible in experiments but barely visible in labor-market data.

Conclusion: The central thesis is that frontier AI progress is real, rapid, and broad, but crucially jagged — requiring a fundamental redesign of the human role toward specification, verification, and oversight, rather than attempting to defend traditional human task domains. This carries theoretical implications for understanding intelligence and practical implications for labor markets, training, and system design.

Abstract: Between 2023 and 2026, frontier AI systems crossed documented human expert baselines on a growing set of bounded, well-specified, evaluable cognitive tasks, including graduate-level science questions, competition mathematics, software-engineering benchmarks, and structured diagnostic reasoning, while the length of tasks such systems can complete at 50% reliability doubled roughly every seven months. These crossings are rapid and broad, but the frontier is jagged: humans retain decisive advantages in long-horizon reliability, genuinely novel problems, calibrated self-knowledge, sample-efficient learning, and embodied action, and benchmark results overstate deployed capability for reasons that are themselves now documented, namely contamination, construct validity, vendor self-evaluation, and the gap between 50% reliability and the reliability that economic work requires. Concurrently, humans increasingly use these systems as cognitive extensions. The offloading literature predicts costs to unaided skill, and early field evidence is consistent with such costs, though the largest meta-analytic evidence on prior technologies points the other way, and the question of whether generative AI differs is open. Finally, the experimental record on human-AI collaboration shows that naive combination often underperforms the stronger partner, implying that the human contribution must be repositioned toward specification, verification, and oversight, a shift visible in experiments but, so far, barely visible in field labor-market data. This paper states the resulting position, rapid crossings on a jagged frontier with a human role that must be redesigned rather than defended, and draws out its theoretical and practical implications.

</details>


### [221] [TRAIL: A Platform for Configurable Human--AI Teaming Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12180)
*Mohammad Amin Samadi,Pedro Martins De Bastos,Jaeyoon Choi,Spencer JaQuay,Seehee Park,Nia Nixon*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: TRAIL is a web platform for configuring and studying AI teammates in sustained, real-time human-AI collaboration. It enables reproducible manipulation of AI design properties (persona, communication style, participation level) and was validated in a six-session classroom study showing that different Big Five–based personas produce distinct, predictable effects on team dynamics, linguistic alignment, and trust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Studying how an AI teammate's design properties (personality, communication style, when it speaks) affect team trust, coordination, and decision-making requires infrastructure for reproducible, instrumented, longitudinal human-AI collaboration—something no existing tool provides.

Method: The authors built TRAIL, a web platform featuring: (1) a configurable AI teammate with Big Five persona traits, (2) a selective-participation message pipeline, (3) dual memory architecture, (4) chained longitudinal experiment support, and (5) export-ready analytics. Validation involved a six-session classroom deployment (~51 students) with a single-blind persona manipulation comparing a cognitive-scaffolding agent versus a socially-supportive agent.

Result: TRAIL successfully sustained longitudinal chaining across sessions and kept the AI to a stable minority of conversation. The persona manipulation produced a design-consistent double dissociation: the cognitive-scaffolding agent elicited stronger contribution ratings and closer linguistic alignment, while the socially-supportive agent produced a warmer team climate and lower over-reliance.

Conclusion: TRAIL demonstrates that an AI teammate can be treated as a reproducible design object, and that even a single-blind persona change yields measurable, divergent effects on team dynamics—advancing rigorous empirical study of human-AI collaboration.

Abstract: An AI teammate's design properties (personality, communication style, when it speaks) can shape a team's trust, coordination, and decisions. Studying this rigorously demands infrastructure no existing tool provides: reproducible configuration of an AI teammate embedded in instrumented, real-time collaboration sustained over time. We present the Team Research and AI Integration Lab (TRAIL), a web platform that makes the AI teammate a configurable, reproducible design object, pairing a Big Five persona with a selective-participation message pipeline, dual memory, chained longitudinal experiments, and export-ready analytics. In a real six-session classroom deployment (about 51 students), TRAIL sustained longitudinal chaining, held the AI to a stable minority of the conversation, and enabled export-driven AI-human text-similarity analysis. A single blind persona change produced a design-consistent double dissociation: a cognitive-scaffolding agent drew stronger contribution ratings and closer linguistic alignment; a socially-supportive agent, a warmer team climate and lower over-reliance.

</details>


### [222] [Compos3D: Interactive Part-Based Composition for Creative Control in Generative 3D Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12193)
*Faraz Faruqi,Sean J. Liu,George Fitzmaurice,Justin Matejka*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Compos3D introduces a compositional remixing workflow for generative 3D modeling, where users select parts from multiple candidate models and assemble them into a refined final model, offering greater creative control than repeated regeneration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative AI workflows for 3D content creation rely on multiple regenerations, which yield limited user control and unpredictable outcomes. There is a need for a more controllable, intent-aligned approach to 3D modeling.

Method: Compos3D enables a compositional workflow: users generate multiple 3D candidates from text or image prompts, select desired parts via 2D image regions or 3D mesh segments, and assemble them. The system then synthesizes the composition into a coherent 3D model that preserves high-level intent while resolving low-level geometry conflicts. The approach was evaluated through a controlled user study comparing remixing vs. regeneration workflows in both 2D and 3D modalities.

Result: The user study demonstrated that the remixing workflow provides participants with significantly greater creative control, stronger alignment with their design intent, and higher overall satisfaction compared to the regeneration workflow.

Conclusion: The compositional remixing paradigm offers a superior alternative to iterative regeneration for AI-assisted 3D modeling. The authors provide design recommendations to guide future development of such workflows.

Abstract: While generative AI has unlocked new opportunities for 3D content creation, current workflows often rely on multiple regenerations, which provides limited control and unpredictable outcomes. We present Compos3D, a system that introduces a compositional workflow for generative 3D modeling through remixing. Instead of repeatedly regenerating models, users generate multiple candidates from text or image prompts, select parts of interest via 2D image regions or 3D mesh segments, and assemble them into a coherent design. The system synthesizes these compositions into a refined 3D model, preserving high-level intent while resolving low-level geometry. To evaluate this approach, we conducted a controlled user study comparing remixing and regeneration workflows across both 2D and 3D modalities. Results show that the remixing workflow provides participants with greater creative control, stronger alignment with their intent, and higher satisfaction. We conclude with design recommendations for future AI-assisted 3D modeling workflows.

</details>


### [223] [From Chaos to Clarity: A Framework for Program-Level AI Learning Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12221)
*Grace Barkhuff,Ian Pruitt,William Gregory Johnson,Rodrigo Borela,Ben Rydal Shapiro,Anu G. Bourgeois*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper proposes Program-Level AI Learning Outcomes (PLAI-LOs) as a structured framework to align institutional GenAI priorities with curriculum design, defining what graduates should know and be able to do with, without, and about GenAI, and illustrates implementation through computing and music examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher education lacks a coherent structure for defining AI readiness across disciplines, programs, courses, and assignments. Current approaches oscillate between broad institutional policies and ad hoc course-level decisions, leading to mixed messages for students, fragmented expectations, and limited strategic visibility for university leaders.

Method: The authors introduce the PLAI-LOs framework, which nests within existing program-level learning outcomes and cascades down through course-level learning outcomes to assignment-level objectives. The framework is illustrated with disciplinary examples from computing and music and operationalized through artifact-level GenAI policies that specify where GenAI should be taught/used versus where students should work without it.

Result: The PLAI-LOs framework provides a concrete, measurable, and adaptable structure that connects institutional priorities to curriculum-level action, enabling programs to make deliberate decisions about GenAI integration while maintaining learning-centered alignment across all levels.

Conclusion: PLAI-LOs offer higher education a path away from scattered, inconsistent GenAI rules toward a strategy with clear, learning-centered alignment, supporting coherent institutional messaging and program-level accountability for AI readiness.

Abstract: Industry is leaning into generative artificial intelligence (GenAI), and higher education is under pressure to prepare graduates for a GenAI-augmented workforce. Yet, there is still no clear structure for defining AI readiness across disciplines, programs, courses, and assignments. Current approaches often rely on broad institutional policies or individual course-level decisions, which can also create mixed messages for students, fragmented expectations across programs, and limited visibility for university leaders. In this paper, we argue that higher education needs a more coherent way to connect institutional priorities to curriculum-level action. We propose Program-Level AI Learning Outcomes (PLAI-LOs) as a framework for defining what students graduating from a program should know and be able to do with, without, and about GenAI in a given discipline. The PLAI-LOs framework complements existing program-level learning outcomes and supports alignment across institutional priorities, program-level AI learning outcomes, course-level learning outcomes, and assignment-level objectives. We illustrate the framework with examples from computing and music and show how PLAI-LOs can be implemented through artifact-level GenAI policies, helping programs decide where GenAI should be taught and used, and when students should be expected to work without GenAI. We offer PLAI-LOs as a concrete, measurable, and adaptable path for moving higher education from scattered GenAI rules toward a strategy with clear, learning-centered alignment.

</details>


### [224] [Towards Knitted Textile Electromechanical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12237)
*John Martins,Abigail Hou,Brandon Tendilla,Noah Tannas,Rishit Garg,Wenchi Liu,Ben Kim,Michael Miller,Alejandro Goldstein,Elizabeth McLaughlin,Nivedita Arora*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A dip-coating process based on fluid dynamics modeling is developed to fabricate TPU-insulated conductive yarns optimized for machine knitting. The resulting yarns (~630 μm coating) maintain electromechanical properties after knitting and washing, enabling reliable multi-layer knitted capacitive pressure sensor arrays for wearable textiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine knitting offers scalable, mechanically tunable structures for capacitive sensing textiles, yet few studies develop or characterize insulated conductive yarns specifically engineered to withstand knitting's complex structural geometry and high flexure strain.

Method: A yarn dip-coating process driven by an adjusted dip-coating fluid dynamics model, using TPU dissolved in DMF, is optimized to produce ~630 μm insulated coatings. The fabricated yarns are machine-knitted into multi-layered capacitive pressure sensing arrays and evaluated for electromechanical stability after knitting and washing.

Result: Optimal dip-coating parameters and TPU/DMF concentrations yield knitting-optimized coatings. The insulated yarns exhibit minimal deviation in electromechanical characteristics post-knitting and post-washing, confirming their robustness.

Conclusion: Machine knitting with dip-coated insulated yarns is a viable, reliable manufacturing approach for integrating capacitive tactile pressure sensing into wearable textiles.

Abstract: E-textiles and wearable sensing technologies enable flexible, customizable interfaces for human-computer interaction, with capacitive sensing offering precise touch and pressure detection. While machine knitting provides scalable, mechanically tunable structures ideal for such sensors, few studies develop or characterize insulated conductive yarns engineered for knitting's complex structural geometry and high flexure strain. In this work, we present a yarn dip-coating process, driven by an adjusted dip-coating fluid dynamics model, that enables scalable, machine knittable fabrication of capacitive tactile pressure sensing arrays. We establish optimal dip-coating parameters and concentrations of thermoplastic polyurethane (TPU) dissolved in dimethylformamide (DMF) to create knitting-optimized coatings (~630 um thickness). These fabricated yarns are shown to maintain electromechanical characteristics with minimal deviation after knitting and washing, thus allowing the creation of knitted pressure sensors through multi-layered structures. This process demonstrates that machine knitting with insulated yarns is a viable and reliable manufacturing approach to integrate sensing functionality into wearable textiles.

</details>


### [225] [Real-time Generation of Listener Nodding via Prediction of Kinematic Parameters for Avatar Dialogue Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12329)
*Kazushi Kato,Koji Inoue,Taiga Mori,Divesh Lala,Tatsuya Kawahara*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A real-time model for predicting both the timing and kinematic features of listener nodding in conversational avatars, using dyadic attention networks and Voice Activity Projection, outperforming stochastic and fixed-motion baselines in subjective evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human dialogue relies on precisely timed nonverbal cues like nodding to signal active listening and encourage speaker continuation. Current conversational avatar systems lack the ability to generate contextually appropriate nodding with both accurate timing and natural motion characteristics in real time.

Method: A two-module architecture: (1) a timing prediction module and (2) a kinematic parameter prediction module, both employing dyadic attention networks over speaker and listener channels based on Voice Activity Projection (VAP). The kinematic module is fine-tuned from the pre-trained timing module. The model is lightweight, runs in real time, and is integrated into an avatar dialogue system.

Result: The proposed method significantly outperforms a baseline with stochastic timing and another baseline with fixed-motion nodding in subjective evaluation experiments.

Conclusion: Real-time, context-specific prediction of both nodding timing and kinematic parameters is feasible and yields more natural avatar interactions than prior approaches. Fine-tuning the kinematic module from the timing module improves performance, and the lightweight design enables practical deployment in dialogue systems.

Abstract: In human dialogue, we achieve smooth communication by expressing nonverbal cues such as eye contact, nodding, and facial expressions with precise timing. It is expected for conversational avatars to express these cues appropriately to realize natural and human-like interactions. This study focuses on nodding, which is crucial for demonstrating active listening and encouraging further user utterances. We propose a model that predicts both timing and kinematic parameters representing the motion features of listener nodding in real time. The proposed model consists of a timing prediction module and a kinematic parameter prediction module. Each implements a dyadic attention network over the speaker and listener channels based on the technique of Voice Activity Projection (VAP). Unlike conventional models, this approach enables real-time prediction of kinematic parameters based on the specific context of the dialogue rather than just predicting the timing. Furthermore, we demonstrate the effectiveness of fine-tuning the kinematic parameter prediction module initialized from the trained timing prediction module. The proposed model is lightweight and capable of real-time operation, and it has been integrated into an avatar dialogue system. Subjective evaluation experiments shows that our proposed method significantly outperforms both a baseline with stochastic timing and another with fixed-motion nodding. The code and trained models are available at https://github.com/MaAI-Kyoto/MaAI.

</details>


### [226] [Aïra: Rethinking AI Research Assistants for Interdisciplinary Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12736)
*Diya Mirji,Tiffany Degbotse,Sharmil Nanjappa,Jiayi Zhou,Vishnu Manoj,Vihaan Nama,Boyuan Chen,Lee Tiedrich,Christopher Bail,David W. Johnston,Walter Sinnott-Armstrong,Brinnae Bent*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper proposes shifting AI research assistants from individual productivity tools to systems supporting interdisciplinary team collaboration, and introduces aïra, a system that identifies disciplinary perspectives, translates terminology, highlights assumptions, and synthesizes collaborative research opportunities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI research assistants optimize individual workflows (literature review, writing, coding) but fail to support the collaborative reasoning needed for interdisciplinary research, where teams must integrate distinct expertise, vocabularies, assumptions, and evidentiary standards.

Method: The authors present aïra, an AI research assistant built on design principles for interdisciplinary collaboration. The system architecture supports: (1) identification of disciplinary perspectives, (2) terminology translation across fields, (3) assumption highlighting, and (4) synthesis of collaborative research opportunities. Outputs are demonstrated through interdisciplinary research meetings.

Result: The paper presents aïra's system architecture and illustrative outputs from interdisciplinary research meetings, demonstrating how the system surfaces cross-disciplinary gaps and opportunities that individual-focused tools miss.

Conclusion: AI research assistants should evolve toward systems designed for collaborative, interdisciplinary scholarship. The authors outline future research directions for building AI that supports the integrative reasoning demands of team science.

Abstract: Scientific discovery increasingly depends on interdisciplinary teams whose members contribute distinct expertise, conceptual frameworks, vocabularies, assumptions, and standards of evidence. Today's AI research assistants are largely designed to support individual researchers through literature review, writing assistance, coding, and data analysis. While these capabilities improve personal productivity, they provide little support for the collaborative reasoning required to integrate knowledge across disciplines. We argue that AI research assistants should evolve from tools that optimize individual workflows to systems designed for interdisciplinary teams. We introduce aïra, an AI research assistant built around this idea. Rather than focusing solely on summarization or question answering, aïra identifies disciplinary perspectives, translates terminology, highlights assumptions, and synthesizes collaborative research opportunities. We describe the design principles underlying aïra, present its system architecture, illustrate its outputs through interdisciplinary research meetings, and outline future research directions for AI systems that support collaborative scholarship.

</details>


### [227] [Practical Judgment, Virtue, and Intuition in the Use of Opaque AI-Enabled Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12755)
*Nathan G. Wood,Andrew P. Rebera*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper argues that concerns about opaque, autonomous AI systems can be mitigated through human practical judgment, virtue, and intuition rather than purely technical transparency solutions. It emphasizes that these non-quantifiable human traits require training regimens grounded in humanistic values, using the military domain as a primary case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Widespread deployment of "black box" AI systems that couple opacity with autonomy raises serious concerns about reliability, predictability, human control, and compliance with ethical and legal norms. The authors seek to address how these concerns can be managed without relying solely on technical transparency.

Method: The authors employ philosophical argumentation, drawing on virtue ethics and practical reasoning traditions. They analyze how distinctly human capabilities — practical judgment (phronesis), virtue, and intuition — can bridge the gap between opaque AI systems and the ethical, legal, and social norms of deployment domains. The military domain serves as an exemplar for illustration.

Result: The analysis yields that many positive human traits essential for responsible AI deployment are inherently non-quantifiable. Therefore, effective mitigation of opacity-related risks requires developing training regimens and deployment guidelines anchored in humanistic values, rather than attempting to quantify or automate these safeguards.

Conclusion: Focusing on practical judgment, virtue, and intuition offers a viable path for ethically sound deployment of opaque AI systems across all domains. While the military context illustrates the argument most vividly, the framework generalizes to any domain where opaque, autonomous systems are deployed, subject to domain-specific adaptations.

Abstract: AI-enabled systems are seeing increasing deployment across numerous domains, with many being "black boxes" with respect to core functions and capabilities. I.e., many systems take inputs and give outputs, but without users having any ability to see how the former lead to the latter. AI-enabled systems are also being used to augment autonomy in systems, and autonomy coupled with opacity raises numerous concerns surrounding, e.g., the reliability of systems, their regularity in functioning, human ability to control them, or whether deploying opaque and potentially autonomous systems is in compliance with ethical and legal norms. In this article, we argue that many of these worries can be mitigated by leveraging practical judgment, virtue, and intuition in the deployment and use of opaque AI-enabled systems. We show that focusing on these distinctly human capabilities provides a means for bridging between the practical challenges created by opacity and the ethical, legal, and social norms underpinning particular domains. We argue that a core element in doing this is a recognition that many positive human traits are not quantifiable and we therefore must develop training regimen and guidelines on AI deployment anchored in humanistic but non-quantifiable values. Throughout the article, we focus on the military domain as an exemplar of the importance of practical judgment, virtue, and intuition as drivers for ethical and effective human decision-making surrounding AI deployments, but the underlying arguments apply to all domains where opaque and potentially autonomous systems are being deployed (subject to domain-specific alterations).

</details>


### [228] [GraphPolaris: A System for Query, Analysis, and Visualization of Graph Databases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12845)
*Michael Behrisch,Sjoerd Vink,Leonardo Christino,Remco Chang*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: GraphPolaris is a no-code Visual Analytics system for graph databases, featuring the GPQL query language that bridges user interactions with graph databases, enabling interactive graph exploration without programming skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing BI tools lack adequate support for interactive graph analysis: tabular BI systems cannot reason over nodes/edges, while graph databases demand specialized query languages and fragmented workflows that hinder accessibility for non-programmers.

Method: The authors developed GraphPolaris with GPQL (GraphPolaris Query Language), a formal query grammar serving as an intermediary between user interactions and the database. The interface was designed based on a formative user study. Evaluation included two real-world case studies (telecommunications and supply-chain) and a 22-month formative mixed-method study with MILC-based assessment.

Result: GPQL enables no-code query construction, database-agnostic query generation, and guarantees valid executable queries. The system lowers technical barriers and supports iterative, collaborative exploration of complex networks. Case studies and the 22-month study demonstrated fit to analysts' graph analytics workflows.

Conclusion: GraphPolaris successfully bridges the gap between graph databases and accessible visual analytics, providing a formal, no-code solution that empowers analysts to explore complex network relationships without programming expertise.

Abstract: Graph databases are increasingly adopted as alternatives to tabular, aggregation-focused data models used in business intelligence (BI) systems such as Tableau, Power BI, and Looker. They capture complex relationships between entities, processes, and events, enabling analysis of information propagation in networks. As a result, graph analysis is central to applications such as fraud detection, social influence analysis, and supply chain resilience. Despite these advantages, existing tools do not adequately support interactive analysis of graph databases. Tabular BI systems lack mechanisms for reasoning over nodes and edges, while graph databases require specialized query languages and fragmented workflows that hinder accessibility. We present GraphPolaris, a no-code Visual Analytics system that enables users to explore, analyze, and visualize graph databases without programming skills. At its core, GraphPolaris features the GRAPHPOLARIS QUERY LANGUAGE (GPQL), a formal query grammar that facilitates flexible and composable graph queries, providing a formal foundation for analyzing relationships and graph patterns. GPQL serves as an intermediary between user interactions and the underlying database. Its formal foundation enables no-code query construction, database-agnostic query generation, and guarantees that every interaction produces a valid executable query. Informed by a formative user study, we designed GraphPolaris' interface and visualizations to lower technical barriers and foster iterative, collaborative exploration of complex networks. We evaluate GraphPolaris through two real-world case studies in telecommunications and supply-chain analysis and a 22-month-long formative mixed-method study, including a MILC-based assessment of its fit to analysts' graph analytics workflows.

</details>


### [229] [CD-MED: Cross-Domain Multimodal Emotion Descriptor for Visual Comparison of Digital Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12958)
*Elnara Kadyrgali,Muragul Muratbekova,Pakizar Shamoi*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: CD-MED is a cross-domain multimodal emotion descriptor that maps heterogeneous digital objects (movies, songs, images, books) into a unified valence-arousal space, enabling emotion-based comparison and retrieval across modalities and domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing emotion recognition models are modality-specific, making it impossible to directly compare the emotional content of digital objects that express emotions through different modalities (e.g., movies via visuals/audio/dialogue vs. songs via melody/rhythm/lyrics). A unified representation is needed for cross-domain emotion-based tasks.

Method: CD-MED processes each modality through its own dedicated emotion recognition model, then transforms the resulting emotional outputs into a shared descriptor. The descriptor is visualized in the valence-arousal space, where position encodes affective coordinates, color denotes emotion category, size indicates intensity, and shape represents the modality.

Result: The unified CD-MED representation preserves information from individual modalities while providing an integrated emotional profile. It supports emotion-based comparison, retrieval, recommendation, and visualization across heterogeneous domains including movies, songs, images, and books.

Conclusion: CD-MED successfully bridges modality-specific emotion recognition systems by providing a common emotional descriptor space, enabling practical cross-domain applications in multimedia retrieval, recommendation, and visualization.

Abstract: Digital objects express emotions through different modalities. For example, a movie may include visual scenes, audio, dialogue, and facial expressions, while a song may contain melody, rhythm, lyrics, and vocal tone. Because existing emotion recognition models are usually modality-specific, it is difficult to compare such objects directly. This paper proposes CD-MED, a Cross-Domain Multimodal Emotion Descriptor for representing heterogeneous digital objects in a common emotional space. Each modality can be processed by its own emotion recognition model, and the resulting emotional outputs are transformed into a shared descriptor. The descriptor preserves information from individual modalities while also allowing an integrated emotional profile of the object. For interpretation, CD-MED is visualized in the valence-arousal space: position represents affective coordinates, color denotes emotion category, size indicates intensity, and shape shows the modality. This unified representation enables emotion-based comparison, retrieval, recommendation, and visualization across different domains such as movies, songs, images, and books.

</details>


### [230] [Sensing the properties of virtual objects without physical feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12978)
*Rhoslyn Roebuck Williams,Harry J. Stroud,Luis E. Toledo,Mark D. Wonnacott,David R. Glowacki*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper investigates the subjective experience of "feeling" virtual molecular objects in XR without haptic feedback, using psychophysical methods to quantify just-noticeable differences (JNDs) in perceived molecular rigidity. Direct interaction yielded finer discrimination (11.5% JND) than observation-only (18.5% JND), and prior interaction improved subsequent observational sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Users of interactive molecular dynamics in extended reality (iMD-XR) report a subjective sense of "feeling" simulated objects despite the absence of haptic feedback. The authors aim to empirically quantify this phenomenon and understand whether direct interaction enhances perceptual sensitivity to virtual object properties compared to passive observation.

Method: The study employs a psychophysics approach measuring just-noticeable differences (JNDs) in perceived rigidity of simulated C₆₀ fullerene molecules under two conditions: (1) direct real-time interaction with the simulation, and (2) observation-only (watching another person interact). A crossover design also examined whether interaction experience transfers to improved observation-based discrimination.

Result: Direct interaction enabled participants to detect rigidity differences as small as 11.5%, significantly better than the 18.5% threshold in the observation-only condition. Additionally, participants who experienced the interaction condition first demonstrated improved discrimination in the subsequent observation-only condition, suggesting a training/transfer effect.

Conclusion: The findings demonstrate that direct interaction without haptic feedback can still produce quantifiable perceptual sensitivity to virtual object properties. This supports iMD-XR as a viable sensing paradigm for molecular research and education, with implications for designing XR interfaces that leverage multimodal perceptual learning.

Abstract: People who have interacted with simulated worlds and simulated objects in extended reality (XR) often have a sense that they can 'feel' the objects being simulated despite them not being physical. Our sense of touch is essential for how we 'feel' the physical world, however, there is an open question as to what it means to 'feel' virtual objects when interacting with them in immersive digital environments. In prior research, we have reported that participants often describe a subjective experience of 'feeling' the properties of simulated molecular objects while using interactive molecular dynamics in extended reality (iMD-XR), a field-based interaction paradigm for manipulating real-time simulations of molecular objects without haptic feedback. To better understand these subjective reports of 'feeling', we used a psychophysics approach to quantify the threshold at which participants perceive differences in the rigidity of simulated molecular objects (C$_{60}$ molecules) in iMD-XR. To evaluate this, we carried out experiments to compare the just-noticeable differences (JNDs) in two conditions: (1) via direct interaction with a real-time C$_{60}$ simulation, and (2) via observation-only$\unicode{x2013}$i.e. watching another person interacting with the simulations. Our findings show that direct interaction enabled participants to perceive more subtle rigidity differences of 11.5%, compared to 18.5% for observation-only. Furthermore, participants who undertook interaction first were better able to distinguish rigidity differences in the subsequent observation-only condition, suggesting that interaction trained participants to better perceive differences in molecular properties. These findings demonstrate a novel and flexible approach for sensing the properties of virtual objects in XR, and offer new insights into iMD-XR's potential in molecular research and education.

</details>


### [231] [From Words to Widgets for Controllable LLM Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10925)
*Chao Zhang,Yiren Liu,Lunyiu Nie,Jeffrey M. Rzeszotarski,Yun Huang,Tal August*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Malleable Prompting transforms subjective natural language preference expressions into interactive GUI widgets (sliders, dropdowns, toggles) that users can directly manipulate to control LLM generation, with a decoding algorithm that modulates token probabilities based on widget values, improving precision, controllability, and transparency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Users struggle to precisely express and control subjective preferences (tone, style, emphasis) through natural language prompting alone, as natural language is imprecise for conveying continuous or fine-grained preferences.

Method: The authors propose Malleable Prompting, which reifies preference expressions into GUI widgets. An LLM decoding algorithm modulates the token probability distribution during generation based on preference expressions and their corresponding widget values. The interface also visualizes each control's influence on output to support attribution and cross-iteration comparison.

Result: A user study demonstrated that Malleable Prompting helps participants achieve target preferences more precisely and was perceived as more controllable and transparent compared to natural language prompting alone.

Conclusion: Malleable Prompting enhances controllable LLM generation by bridging the gap between subjective user intent and model behavior through interactive, visual, and directly manipulable preference controls.

Abstract: Natural language remains the predominant way people interact with large language models (LLMs). However, users often struggle to precisely express and control subjective preferences (e.g., tone, style, and emphasis) through prompting. We propose Malleable Prompting, a new interactive prompting technique for controllable LLM generation. It reifies preference expressions in natural language prompts into GUI widgets (e.g., sliders, dropdowns, and toggles) that users can directly configure to steer generation, while visualizing each control's influence on the output to support attribution and comparison across iterations. To enable this interaction, we introduce an LLM decoding algorithm that modulates the token probability distribution during generation based on preference expressions and their widget values. Through a user study, we show that Malleable Prompting helps participants achieve target preferences more precisely and is perceived as more controllable and transparent than natural language prompting alone.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [232] [Cross-Cutting Security Analysis of LLM-Generated Code via Metamorphic Testing and Association Rule Mining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12089)
*Zedong Peng,Chenggang Wang,Shangyue Zhu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: LLMs generate code with interconnected security vulnerabilities. This paper introduces a framework using Security-oriented Metamorphic Relations (MRs) and Association Rule (AR) mining to detect cross-cutting vulnerabilities in LLM-generated code, revealing structured co-violation patterns and linking them to prompt-level risk factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-generated code frequently contains security vulnerabilities that are not isolated but span multiple concern areas. Existing approaches treat vulnerabilities independently, overlooking their cross-cutting, co-occurring nature and the prompt-level factors that drive them.

Method: Nine security-oriented Metamorphic Relations (MRs) covering CWE categories (SQL injection, XSS, command injection, path traversal, hard-coded credentials, weak cryptography, memory-safety) are defined. An LLM-based judge applies these MRs to 3,700 code snippets from five open models (LLMSecEval benchmark). Association Rule mining is then used to discover co-violation patterns, followed by prompt-level risk analysis to link violation structures back to prompt characteristics.

Result: 68.8% of snippets violate ≥1 MR. Hard-coded credentials (79.1%) and command injection (74.4%) are most prevalent. AR mining reveals XSS + weak cryptography co-violations predict hard-coded credentials with 82.5% confidence (lift=3.23). Tightly coupled clusters link authentication/credential/cryptographic weaknesses, and input-handling/memory-safety failures. Database- and authentication-related prompts are strong predictors of cross-cutting insecurity. 65.5% of prompts yield consistent violation outcomes across all five models.

Conclusion: Insecure code generation is a structured, prompt-conditioned phenomenon rather than a set of independent defects. This motivates cluster-aware verification strategies and prompt-level interventions to improve LLM-assisted programming safety.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) frequently generate code with security vulnerabilities, yet these weaknesses are rarely isolated: they often span multiple concern areas simultaneously, reflecting the cross-cutting nature of security in software. We present a framework that combines security-oriented Metamorphic Relations (MRs) with Association Rule (AR) mining to detect vulnerabilities in LLM-generated code, uncover their co-violation structure, and trace that structure back to prompt-level risk factors. We define nine MRs covering major CWE categories, including SQL injection, XSS, command injection, path traversal, hard-coded credentials, weak cryptography, and memory-safety errors, and apply them using an LLM-based judge to 3,700 code snippets generated by five open models from the LLMSecEval benchmark. The results show that 68.8% of snippets violate at least one MR, with hard-coded credentials (79.1%) and command injection (74.4%) among the most prevalent applicable failures. AR mining reveals strong cross-cutting co-violation patterns, notably that XSS and weak cryptography co-violations predict hard-coded credentials with 82.5% confidence (lift = 3.23), along with tightly coupled clusters linking authentication, credential handling, and cryptographic weakness, as well as input-handling and memory-safety failures. We then perform prompt-level risk analysis and find that database- and authentication-related prompts are strong predictors of broad cross-cutting insecurity, while 65.5% of prompts yield consistent violation outcomes across all five models. These findings show that insecure code generation is not merely a collection of independent defects, but a structured and prompt-conditioned phenomenon, motivating cluster-aware verification and prompt-level intervention for safer LLM-assisted programming.

</details>


### [233] [MindReader: Using LLMs to Encourage Memorable and Secure Password Replacement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12148)
*Anna Gerchanovsky,Lujo Bauer,Michael K. Reiter*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MindReader is an LLM-based tool that helps users replace passwords by suggesting semantically related but syntactically unrelated variations, making them memorable for users but harder for attackers to predict — even with full knowledge of the original password and tool implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Users tend to create predictable variations when forced to change passwords, weakening security. There is a need for a tool that generates replacement passwords that are both memorable and resistant to targeted guessing attacks by adversaries who know the original password.

Method: MindReader uses LLMs to infer the semantic meaning behind components of the original password and then generates new components that are semantically related (aiding memorability) but syntactically unrelated (resisting predictable-variation attacks). A user study evaluated security (resistance to online guessing) and memorability (login success after one week).

Result: Passwords created with MindReader were more secure than both user-created replacement passwords and original passwords. They resisted online attacks even when the attacker knew the original password and the full tool implementation. Memorability was comparable to both alternative replacement passwords and original passwords.

Conclusion: MindReader effectively balances security and usability in the password-replacement context, leveraging LLMs to break the predictable-variation pattern while preserving memorability through semantic relatedness.

Abstract: We report on the design and evaluation of MindReader, a tool that helps a user replace her password when she is required to do so. Left to their own devices, users tend to replace their previous passwords with predictable variations of the original ones. MindReader leverages LLMs to suggest password variations that are chosen to be easy for the user to remember but harder for an attacker to predict. To do this, MindReader infers the meaning behind original password components and then suggests semantically related (yet syntactically unrelated) components for the new password. In a user study, passwords created using MindReader were more secure than both replacement passwords created without using MindReader and original passwords. In particular, MindReader replacement passwords were harder to guess in an online attack than alternative replacement passwords even by an attacker with knowledge of the original password and full knowledge of the tool implementation. Passwords created with MindReader were also comparably memorable to alternative replacement passwords and original passwords, as measured by the ability of users to successfully log in a week after creating their password.

</details>


### [234] [MTD-Playground: An Attacker-Aware Evaluation Framework for Network Moving Target Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12199)
*Mohammad Farhad,Mohoshin Ara Tahera,Padam Jung Thapa,Shuvalaxmi Dass,Bhupendra Acharya*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MTD-Playground is an attacker-aware benchmarking framework for evaluating SDN-enabled path-randomization MTD techniques under realistic multi-stage attacks. It introduces composite metrics that assess deployment effectiveness, mutation-interval trade-offs, and defender-attacker balance. Evaluation shows aggressive mutation intervals reduce attack success rates to 4–20%, increase attack completion time to 160–311s, and improve throughput by up to 30.9%, demonstrating practical deployability in enterprise environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of SDN-enabled path-randomization MTD techniques are fragmented due to inconsistent attacker assumptions, attack scenarios, and metrics, limiting reproducibility and meaningful deployment-oriented comparison. A unified, realistic evaluation framework is needed.

Method: The authors propose MTD-Playground, an attacker-aware evaluation framework that benchmarks SDN-based path-randomization MTD under enterprise-style multi-stage attack scenarios. It employs a composite evaluation methodology capturing deployment effectiveness, mutation-interval trade-offs, and defender-attacker operational balance. Periodic path randomization serves as the representative PR-MTD strategy for evaluation.

Result: Aggressive mutation intervals reduce attack success rates to 4–20% and extend attack completion time to 160–311 seconds. PR-MTD improves throughput by up to 30.9%, reduces internal-path latency without service interruption, and shorter mutation intervals consistently achieve the highest deployment effectiveness and positive defender advantage.

Conclusion: SDN-based path-randomization MTD can substantially disrupt multi-stage attack progression while remaining practically deployable in enterprise environments, with shorter mutation intervals yielding the best composite defense outcomes.

Abstract: Moving Target Defense (MTD) has emerged as a proactive network cyber defense paradigm that increases attacker uncertainty through dynamic network reconfiguration techniques such as Software-Defined Networking (SDN)-enabled path randomization. However, existing evaluations remain fragmented due to inconsistent attacker assumptions, attack scenarios, and evaluation metrics, limiting reproducibility and deployment-oriented comparison. In this paper, we present MTD-Playground, an attacker-aware evaluation framework for benchmarking SDN-enabled path-randomization (PR) MTD techniques under realistic enterprise-style multi-stage attack scenarios. Beyond isolated security and performance metrics, MTD-Playground introduces a composite evaluation methodology for analyzing deployment effectiveness, mutation-interval trade-offs, and defender-attacker operational balance. Using periodic path randomization as a representative PR-MTD strategy, our evaluation shows that aggressive mutation intervals reduce attack success rates to 4-20% while increasing attack completion time to 160-311s across evaluated attack scenarios. At the same time, PR-MTD improves throughput by up to 30.9% and reduces internal-path latency without service interruption. Composite analysis further shows that shorter mutation intervals consistently achieve the highest deployment effectiveness and positive defender advantage. These results demonstrate that SDN-based PR-MTD can substantially disrupt multi-stage attack progression while remaining practically deployable in enterprise environments.

</details>


### [235] [Explaining Intrusion Alert Decisions of Deep Learning-based Network Intrusion Detection Systems for Security Analysts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12203)
*Ayush Kumar,Vrizlynn L. L. Thing*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: EXP-SEC is a novel framework that explains DL-based NIDS alerts to SOC analysts through three modules: forensic isolation of suspect packets, group-aware explanation handling complex feature dependencies, and multi-stage mapping to domain-specific analyst-friendly explanations. It outperforms xNIDS on group-level and overlap-aware metrics while matching conventional feature-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explanation methods for DL-based NIDS cannot adequately handle complex feature dependencies in network traffic (overlapping groups with varying importance) and fail to produce explanations aligned with SOC analysts' domain knowledge. There is a gap between raw feature/group explanations and actionable, analyst-friendly interpretations.

Method: EXP-SEC comprises three modules: (1) a forensic module that isolates suspect packets/flows causing alerts; (2) an explanation module that handles complex feature dependencies by modeling features as overlapping groups with hierarchical importance; and (3) a multi-stage mapping module that translates feature/group-based explanations into domain-specific, analyst-consumable explanations.

Result: EXP-SEC outperforms xNIDS on group-level and overlap-aware explanation utility metrics while performing comparably on conventional feature-level metrics: descriptive accuracy, sparsity, and stability. A case study demonstrates the analyst-friendly explanation format produced by the framework.

Conclusion: EXP-SEC successfully bridges the gap between DL-based NIDS alert generation and SOC analyst interpretation by providing domain-aligned, group-aware explanations with forensic traceability, advancing beyond existing frameworks like xNIDS.

Abstract: In this paper, we present EXP-SEC, a novel framework which can explain the intrusion detection decisions of DL-based NIDS (which lead to security alerts) in a way that is aligned with the domain knowledge of analysts working in Security Operations Center (SOC). We highlight the following features of our framework: (1) a forensic module that isolates the suspect packets/flow which likely caused an alert (2) an explanation module which can handle much more complex feature dependencies in network traffic than existing methods (features can be divided into overlapping groups and some groups are more important than others), and (3) a multi-stage mapping module which translates the feature/group-based explanations generated by explanation module to domain-specific explanations suitable for processing by security analysts. We evaluate EXP-SEC with state-of-the-art DL-based NIDS and our evaluation results show that EXP-SEC outperforms xNIDS (existing best performing explanation framework) in terms of group-level and overlap-aware explanation utility metrics while performing similarly in terms of conventional feature-level metrics such as descriptive accuracy, sparsity and stability. Moreover, taking the case of a state-of-the-art DL-based NIDS, we demonstrate the security analyst-friendly explanation format generated by EXP-SEC.

</details>


### [236] [$\mathrm{P}^{3}$CDA: Privacy-Preserving and Provably Secure Cross Domain Authentication Scheme for Internet of Drones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12288)
*Chengqi Hou,Beibei Li,Ziqing Zhu,Yang You,Licheng Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proposes P³CDA, a privacy-preserving cross-domain authentication scheme for the Internet of Drones (IoD) that combines an adaptive pseudonym management mechanism, a structurally enhanced Merkle Hash Tree for batch pseudonym updates, and a cryptographic accumulator-based protocol to achieve anonymous, traceable, and efficient authentication with provable security under the CK adversary model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cross-domain authentication schemes for IoD fail to simultaneously satisfy strong security, high efficiency, and identity privacy — a critical gap given the highly dynamic, resource-constrained nature of drone environments where cross-domain interactions are inevitable.

Method: (1) An adaptive pseudonym management mechanism supporting batch registration, verification, and revocation. (2) A structurally enhanced Merkle Hash Tree (MHT) enabling batch pseudonym updates to reduce drone storage overhead. (3) A cryptographic accumulator-based cross-domain authentication protocol providing anonymous authentication with authorized pseudonyms, traceability, and efficient revocation. Security is formally proven under the Canetti-Krawczyk (CK) adversary model.

Result: Extensive experiments show that P³CDA achieves lower computational, communication, and storage overhead compared to state-of-the-art cross-domain authentication schemes.

Conclusion: P³CDA successfully balances strong security, privacy preservation, and efficiency, making it well-suited for highly dynamic and resource-constrained IoD environments.

Abstract: With the rapid expansion of the Internet of Drones (IoD) and the increasing mobility of drones, cross-domain interactions among geographically distributed domains have become inevitable. Cross-domain authentication is therefore a fundamental security requirement for IoD. However, existing authentication schemes often struggle to simultaneously achieve strong security, high efficiency, and identity privacy, making them unsuitable for the stringent requirements of highly dynamic and resource-constrained IoD environments. To address this challenge, we propose $\mathrm{P}^{3}$CDA, a privacy-preserving and provably secure cross-domain authentication scheme. First, we design an efficient pseudonym management mechanism that supports adaptive pseudonym generation as well as batch registration, verification, and revocation. Second, we propose a structurally enhanced Merkle Hash Tree (MHT) that supports batch pseudonym updates, thereby reducing the pseudonym storage overhead of drones. Building on these components, we develop a cryptographic accumulator-based cross-domain authentication protocol that enables anonymous authentication with authorized pseudonyms while preserving the traceability and efficient revocation of malicious drones. We rigorously analyze the security of $\mathrm{P}^{3}$CDA and formally prove its security under the Canetti--Krawczyk (CK) adversary model. Extensive experiments demonstrate that $\mathrm{P}^{3}$CDA achieves lower computational, communication, and storage overhead than state-of-the-art schemes.

</details>


### [237] [Antiproof: Synthesizing Vulnerability Detectors and Proofs of Exploitability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12316)
*Alon Shakevsky,Corban Villa,Ion Stoica,Raluca Ada Popa*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Antiproof is a vulnerability discovery system combining neuro-symbolic detector synthesis with proof-of-exploitability oracles, achieving high recall (~97%) and uncovering hundreds of zero-days including critical RCEs in LLM infrastructure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vulnerability discovery methods face a trade-off between high recall and reliable automatic validation, with neither static analysis nor learning-based approaches achieving both at acceptable cost. The authors aim to bridge this gap.

Method: Antiproof uses a neuro-symbolic approach that learns and iteratively refines static detectors from vulnerability datasets (candidate generation), then validates candidates through proof-of-exploitability oracles that verify whether executable proofs demonstrate concrete attacker capabilities.

Result: On BountyBench and KEVBench, Antiproof detected 64/66 vulnerabilities (>60pp recall improvement over baselines). A scan of 50 widely deployed systems uncovered several hundred previously unknown vulnerabilities, yielding 12 CVEs including RCEs in Ray, SGLang, vLLM, and LiteLLM.

Conclusion: Neuro-symbolic detector synthesis paired with exploitability proofs enables practical, high-recall vulnerability discovery with automatic validation at scale, as demonstrated by real-world zero-day discoveries in critical AI infrastructure.

Abstract: Discovering vulnerabilities before attackers exploit them requires high recall and reliable automatic validation, but existing approaches struggle to achieve both without prohibitive cost. We present Antiproof, an end-to-end vulnerability discovery system that combines neuro-symbolic detector synthesis for high-recall discovery with proof-of-exploitability oracles for automatic validation. Antiproof learns and iteratively refines static detectors from vulnerability datasets, then validates candidates by verifying whether executable proofs demonstrate concrete attacker capabilities. Evaluated on BountyBench and our curated KEVBench dataset, Antiproof detects 64 of 66 vulnerabilities, improving recall by more than 60 percentage points over static-analysis and neuro-symbolic baselines. In a scan of 50 widely deployed systems, Antiproof uncovered several hundred previously unknown vulnerabilities. We are responsibly disclosing all confirmed zero-days and have received 12 CVE assignments to date, including remote code execution vulnerabilities in Ray, SGLang, vLLM, and LiteLLM that could allow attackers to take over LLM training and inference systems.

</details>


### [238] [Trust but Verify? Uncovering the Security Debt of Autonomous Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12428)
*A H M Nazmus Sakib,Dipayan Banik,Murtuza Jadliwala*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: An empirical study of 4,022 agent-generated pull requests reveals 38.9% contain security smells, with supply chain integrity issues dominating (82.3%) and hard-coded credentials comprising 99.6% of critical-severity smells. Human collaborators introduced 67.6% of genuine leaked secrets, yet 81.1% of these credentials evaded both automated and human review before integration, highlighting urgent needs for context-aware security guardrails in human-AI collaborative workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous coding agents are increasingly adopted, accelerating development but introducing scoped security risks in high-impact file paths that outpace traditional human review. Prior work has focused on functional correctness and productivity, leaving the security properties of agent-generated code — particularly security code smells in pull requests — systematically uncharacterized.

Method: The authors conduct a large-scale empirical study using the AIDev dataset, analyzing 16,112 file changes across 4,022 agent-generated pull requests. They employ a validated LLM-as-a-judge framework for automated detection combined with manual qualitative analysis to identify and classify security misconfigurations. Security smells are categorized by type (e.g., supply chain integrity, hard-coded credentials) and severity level.

Result: Key findings: (1) 38.9% of agent-generated PRs contain at least one security smell; (2) supply chain integrity issues account for 82.3% of all detected smells; (3) hard-coded credentials represent 99.6% of critical-severity smells; (4) human collaborators are responsible for 67.6% of genuine leaked secrets; (5) 81.1% of these credentials go undetected by existing review processes before integration.

Conclusion: The findings suggest that agent-assisted development workflows introduce substantial security risks and may reduce developer vigilance. The authors underscore the urgent need for context-aware security guardrails implemented directly at the point of human-AI collaboration, rather than relying solely on downstream review processes which currently fail to catch the majority of introduced vulnerabilities.

Abstract: The increasing adoption of autonomous coding agents accelerates software development but also introduces scoped security risks within high-impact file paths that can outpace traditional human review capacity. While prior research has primarily evaluated these systems in terms of functional correctness and productivity, this paper presents a large-scale empirical study using the AIDev dataset to systematically characterize security code smells in agent-generated pull requests (PRs). Through a combination of a validated LLM-as-a-judge framework and manual qualitative analysis, we identify and classify security misconfigurations across 16,112 file changes spanning 4,022 pull requests.
