Our results reveal that 38.9% of agent-generated PRs contain at least one security smell, with supply chain integrity issues accounting for 82.3% of all detected security smells. Furthermore, hard-coded credentials constitute 99.6% of all critical-severity security smells. Crucially, we find that human collaborators are responsible for introducing 67.6% of genuine leaked secrets within these agent-assisted workflows, while existing automated and human review processes fail to detect 81.1% of these credentials prior to integration. These findings highlight substantial security risks in agent-assisted software development workflows and suggest a potential reduction in developer vigilance. They also underscore the urgent need for context-aware security guardrails implemented directly at the point of human-AI collaboration.

</details>


### [239] [On the Security Implications of PQC in TLS: Handshake Exhaustion and IDS Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12504)
*Lin-Fa Lee,Yi-Yu Chang,Chia-Mu Yu,Kuo-Hui Yeh*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: PQC integration in TLS 1.3 amplifies CPU exhaustion during DDoS handshake attacks (up to 88× longer), while state-of-the-art deep learning IDS suffer severe detection degradation (recall ~50%, AU-ROC near 0.49). The authors release a PQC-DDoS dataset, attack timestamps, resource monitoring data, and reproducible AWS deployment scripts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PQC is being adopted in TLS 1.3 to counter quantum threats, but the heavier computational/communication overhead of PQC handshakes may exacerbate TLS handshake exhaustion DDoS attacks. Existing IDS may fail to detect such attacks under PQC traffic conditions — a gap not yet systematically studied.

Method: An empirical testbed is built with one PQC-TLS server and ten attacking nodes, generating >16.5 GB of mixed legitimate and attack traffic. CPU utilization under attack is measured for both classical and PQC TLS. Two state-of-the-art deep learning IDS (exosphere and HyperVision) are evaluated on detection performance under PQC traffic, using recall and AU-ROC metrics.

Result: PQC-TLS sustains high server CPU utilization up to 88× longer than classical TLS during handshake exhaustion attacks. exosphere recall drops to ~50%; HyperVision AU-ROC degrades to near-random (0.49), indicating critical detection blind spots.

Conclusion: PQC amplifies TLS handshake DDoS severity and breaks existing deep learning IDS. The work contributes root-cause analysis of detection blind spots, a public PQC-DDoS dataset with ground-truth labels and server metrics, and fully reproducible open-source cloud testbed artifacts to spur development of PQC-aware IDS.

Abstract: Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) is increasingly being integrated into TLS 1.3 to enhance resilience against quantum-enabled attacks. However, the additional computational and communication overhead introduced by PQC primitives during the handshake phase may also amplify the impact of TLS handshake exhaustion attacks, leading to more severe Distributed Denial-of-Service (DDoS) threats.
  In this study, we establish an empirical testbed consisting of one PQC-enabled TLS server and ten attacking nodes, generating over 16.5 GB of mixed traffic data that includes both legitimate browsing behavior and high-intensity handshake exhaustion attacks.
  Experimental results show that PQC-TLS can prolong periods of sustained high CPU utilization on the server by up to 88 times, significantly amplifying the effectiveness of such attacks. Furthermore, we evaluate state-of-the-art deep learning-based Intrusion Detection Systems (IDS) and observe a substantial decline in attack detection performance under PQC traffic conditions.
  In particular, exosphere achieves only around 50% recall, while HyperVision's AU-ROC degrades to near-random levels (0.49), revealing critical detection blind spots in existing IDS when operating in PQC environments.
  The main contributions of this work are threefold: (1) we systematically quantify and analyze the root causes of IDS detection blind spots in PQC settings; (2) we publicly release a comprehensive PQC-DDoS hybrid traffic dataset, including precise attack timestamps and server-side resource monitoring data; and (3) we open-source all experimental code and AWS deployment scripts, enabling a fully reproducible cloud-based testing environment.
  These resources aim to support both academia and industry in developing next-generation PQC-aware intrusion detection systems.

</details>


### [240] [When Binaries Talk Back: Representation-Confusion Attacks on LLM-Assisted Reverse Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12507)
*Igor Santos-Grueiro*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: RARE-Bench identifies Representation-Confusion Attacks in Reverse Engineering, where attacker-crafted binaries cause LLM-assisted RE pipelines to misinterpret data as instructions or treat single-origin evidence as independent. RARE-Guard mitigates these attacks through Data-Only rendering, Tool Authorization, Support Gate, and Provenance Gate controls, preventing unsafe proposals from binary-derived content while preserving legitimate analyst requests across multiple RE tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-assisted reverse engineering systems are vulnerable to adversarial binaries that can manipulate the analysis pipeline — making data appear as instructions or causing records from a single origin to masquerade as independent corroborating evidence. Existing systems lack mechanisms to distinguish between properly authorized observations and attacker-planted misrepresentations, creating a critical security gap in automated RE workflows.

Method: The authors introduce RARE-Bench, a benchmark measuring representation-confusion failures using behavior-checked clean and adversarial binaries across an 11,520-call exploratory study. They propose RARE-Guard with four defense mechanisms: (1) Data-Only rendering — binary-derived content displayed strictly as data without instruction authority; (2) Tool Authorization — denying unsafe proposals not matched to analyst requests; (3) Support Gate — validating claims by counting evidential records; and (4) Provenance Gate — grouping records by origin before counting support to prevent single-source evidence from appearing as independent confirmation. The system is evaluated on 20 programs with two models, then instrumented on Ghidra, r2pipe, and angr across 16 additional programs, with both single-tool and fused-draft scenarios.

Result: Without controls, models proposed planted unsafe actions in 35/40 adversarial cases and 0/40 clean cases. Data-Only rendering alone left 15 unsafe proposals. Tool Authorization denied all 15 while authorizing all 40 matched analyst requests. Support Gate validated 23/40 false claims by counting single-origin records separately; Provenance Gate prevented all 40 false claims while retaining all 40 supported claims. In the 16-program instrumented study, no single-tool draft in an 8-program subset reached the validation threshold for false claims. In fused drafts across all 16 programs, Support Gate validated 32/32 false claims; Provenance Gate prevented all 32 and retained all 32 supported claims.

Conclusion: Binary-derived content can safely guide RE analysis without gaining authority over tools, and views from multiple tools do not constitute independent evidence. RARE-Guard's provenance-aware authorization and evidence controls effectively prevent representation-confusion attacks while preserving legitimate analyst workflows, demonstrating that proper access control and evidence provenance tracking are essential for trustworthy LLM-assisted reverse engineering.

Abstract: LLM-assisted reverse-engineering (RE) systems analyze strings, decompiler output, and tool reports derived from ttacker-controlled binaries. A binary can make data look like instructions or records from one origin look like independent evidence. We call such failures Representation-Confusion Attacks in Reverse Engineering (RARE): the pipeline promotes a correctly extracted observation to instruction authority, claim-validating evidence, or trusted analysis state without the authority or support that role requires. RARE-Bench measures these failures with behavior-checked clean and adversarial binaries. After an exploratory 11,520-call study, we test RARE-Guard's authorization and evidence controls on 20 new programs and two models. Without runtime controls, the models propose a planted unsafe action in 35/40 adversarial cases and 0/40 clean cases. When binary-derived content is shown only as data (Data-Only rendering), they still make 15 unsafe proposals. Tool Authorization denies all 15 and authorizes all 40 matched analyst requests. On identical report drafts, Support Gate validates 23/40 false claims by counting records from one origin separately. Provenance Gate groups those records before counting support, validates 0/40 false claims, and retains all 40 supported claims. We then instrument Ghidra, r2pipe, and angr on 16 further programs. In a preselected eight-program subset, no single-tool draft reaches Support Gate's validation threshold for the false claim. In fused drafts across all 16 programs, Support Gate validates 32/32 false claims. Provenance Gate prevents validation of all 32 and retains all 32 supported claims. A deterministic renderer prevents downgraded claims from reappearing in the final report. Binary-derived content may therefore guide analysis without gaining authority over tools, and views from several tools do not necessarily provide independent evidence.

</details>


### [241] [Open-Source Intelligence and Music Information Retrieval for Geographic Attribution of Musical Affect and the Ecological Limits of Population Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12517)
*Mohammadreza Rashidi*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Folk melodies across 16 countries show significant structural differences, but none of the extracted musical-affect features correlate with national happiness or individualism indices, rejecting the intuition that a region's music mirrors its population's temperament.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically test the widespread intuition that a population's emotional disposition is reflected in its folk music — specifically, whether measurable features of folk melodies predict national levels of happiness and individualism.

Method: Using 2,393 deduplicated melodies from the Essen Folksong Collection spanning 16 countries and 7 regions, the authors extracted 8 melodic and affect-related features from symbolic scores (not audio). Mode was computed via a key-finding algorithm rather than relying on unreliable metadata. Features were tested for cross-country differences and then correlated against two validated national indices: the World Happiness Report ladder score and Hofstede's individualism index.

Result: All 8 melodic features differed significantly across countries (p<0.001), with leap-related features reaching p<10⁻⁹⁰. China exhibited a distinctive wide-leap, high-activity signature. However, none of the 6 correlations between regional musical-affect measures and national happiness or individualism indices were significant (0 of 6).

Conclusion: The geography of musical affect is real and measurable, but it does not predict population-level happiness or individualism. Any claim to the contrary constitutes an ecological fallacy. The full pipeline and a fail-closed checker are publicly released for reproducibility.

Abstract: A common intuition holds that a region's music mirrors the temperament of its people, so that melancholic melodies mark melancholic populations. We test the measurable half of that intuition and reject the inferential half. Using the Essen Folksong Collection, a corpus of thousands of notated folk melodies, we extract real melodic and affect-related features from 2393 deduplicated melodies spanning 16 countries and 7 geographic regions, with the analysis performed on symbolic scores rather than audio. The mode of each melody is computed with a key-finding algorithm rather than read from the file, because the collection's own documentation warns its major and minor labels are unreliable. Cross-country differences in melodic structure are large and highly significant. All 8 tested features differ across countries at p<0.001, with the leap-related features reaching p<10^-90, and China carries a distinctive wide-leap, high-activity signature (arousal composite +1.24 standard deviations, mean absolute interval 2.77 semitones against Germany's 2.17). We then test the inferential half. We correlate the regional musical-affect measures with two published, validated national indices, the World Happiness Report ladder score and the Hofstede individualism index. None of the 6 correlations is significant (0 of 6). The geography of musical affect is real and measurable, but it does not predict how happy or how individualist a population is, and any claim that it does is an ecological fallacy. We release the full extraction and analysis pipeline, and a fail-closed checker re-derives every number in this paper from the data.

</details>


### [242] [Open-Source Intelligence for Code Provenance and the Security Patterns that Separate Human and Large-Language-Model Implementations of Common Programming Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12524)
*Mohammadreza Rashidi*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether code provenance (human-written vs. LLM-generated) can be determined from code itself, and whether the two sources differ in security patterns for identical tasks. Using a reproducible pipeline covering 31 security-sensitive tasks across 9 LLMs and Stack Overflow in 4 languages (Python, JavaScript, Go, Java), the authors achieve 93% accuracy in binary provenance classification and 48% in 7-way model attribution. They find systematic security divergences between human and model code across all languages, and a 77% vulnerability repair rate with a recurring partial-fix failure mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developers increasingly draw code from two distinct sources — human-curated answers (e.g., Stack Overflow) and large language models — yet it is unknown whether the provenance of a code snippet can be inferred from the code itself, and whether these sources systematically differ in the security patterns they employ for the same programming tasks. Understanding these differences is critical for assessing downstream security risks in modern software development.

Method: The authors build a fully reproducible, data-driven pipeline using only open sources: the public Stack Overflow API and open-weight LLMs via a public gateway. They collect real implementations of 31 security-sensitive tasks (OAuth with PKCE, JWT verification, password hashing, SQL access, etc.) from 9 language models and human answers, scoring each sample with deterministic security and style detectors. On 528 samples, they train a cross-validated classifier for binary provenance (human vs. model) and a 7-way model-specific classifier. They also conduct a vulnerability repair case study across 21 seeds and 12 weakness classes, and evaluate across Python, JavaScript, Go, and Java. The pipeline is specification-driven: new tasks or languages are added as single entries, and a fail-closed checker re-derives all reported numbers from stored data.

Result: The binary classifier recovers provenance with 93% accuracy (baseline 78%). The 7-way model attribution classifier achieves 48% accuracy. Security divergences between human and model code hold across all four languages, while provenance boundary transfer is partly language-specific and asymmetric. In the vulnerability repair case study, models achieve 77% repair rate across 21 seeds and 12 weakness classes, but exhibit a recurring partial-fix failure: removing the insecure pattern without adding the correct defense.

Conclusion: Code provenance is recoverable with high accuracy, and LLMs systematically differ from human-written code in security-relevant patterns across multiple languages. The partial-fix failure mode in vulnerability repair highlights a dangerous blind spot: models can make code look "less insecure" without actually making it secure. The reproducible, extensible pipeline design enables ongoing monitoring as both human and model codebases evolve.

Abstract: Developers now draw code from two very different sources, the accumulated human answers on sites such as Stack Overflow and the output of large language models. We ask two questions about that split. First, can the provenance of a code snippet be recovered from the code itself, and second, do the two sources differ in the security patterns they adopt for the same task. Using only open sources, a public gateway of open-weight language models and the public Stack Overflow API, we build a fully reproducible pipeline that collects real implementations of 31 security-sensitive programming tasks, among them OAuth with PKCE, JWT verification, password hashing, and SQL access, from 9 language models and from human answers, and scores every sample with deterministic security and style detectors. On 528 real samples we train a cross-validated classifier that recovers human versus model provenance with 93 percent accuracy against a 78 percent baseline, and a 7-way classifier that attributes a sample to the specific model that wrote it at 48 percent. We then report where the sources diverge on security, which patterns models adopt more often than the human corpus and which they inherit from it. Running the same tasks in Python, JavaScript, Go, and Java, we find the security divergence holds in every language while the provenance boundary is partly language-specific and does not transfer symmetrically between them. A vulnerability repair case study, in which models are handed insecure code and asked to fix it, finds a 77 percent repair rate across 21 seeds and 12 weakness classes, but a recurring partial-fix failure in which the model removes the insecure pattern without adding the correct defense. The pipeline is data driven, so any new task or language is added as a single specification entry, and a fail-closed checker re-derives every number in this paper from the stored data.

</details>


### [243] [VanillaBench: The Hidden Accuracy Cost of Adversarial Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12545)
*Niklas Bunzel*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: VanillaBench systematically quantifies the accuracy gap between adversarially robust models and their vanilla (non-robust) counterparts, revealing that the robustness-accuracy trade-off is substantially larger than typically reported in individual papers, with mean gaps ranging from -3.5 to -29.5 percentage points depending on the comparison methodology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarial robustness papers almost universally report results in isolation—showing clean and adversarial accuracy of robust models without quantifying the accuracy gap relative to corresponding vanilla models. This omission obscures the true cost of adversarial defenses and leaves practitioners unable to assess the business impact of deploying robust models.

Method: The authors introduce VanillaBench, a benchmark that computes the accuracy delta between every adversarially-trained model catalogued in RobustBench (186 models across four threat models) and multiple vanilla reference baselines: all Papers with Code entries, no-extra-data entries, the best vanilla model from the robust model's publication year, and an architecture-matched baseline (both exact and approximate).

Result: Mean clean accuracy deltas range from -7.7 to -29.5 pp against the best vanilla model; even the single most robust model per track trails by 4.0–21.0 pp. Architecture-matched gaps are -3.5 to -17.5 pp (mean), narrowing to -4.0 to -14.0 pp when restricted to exact-architecture matches. These gaps substantially exceed what individual papers typically convey.

Conclusion: The robustness-accuracy trade-off is significantly larger than commonly reported. The authors argue that vanilla-referenced accuracy gaps should become a standard reporting component in future robustness evaluations to provide practitioners and decision-makers with the information needed to assess real-world deployment trade-offs.

Abstract: Adversarial robustness research has produced hundreds of defended models over the past decade, yet the literature almost universally reports robustness results in isolation: standard (clean) accuracy and adversarial accuracy of the robust model are shown, but the gap to the corresponding vanilla model is rarely quantified. We introduce VanillaBench, a systematic benchmark that makes this gap explicit. For every adversarially-trained model catalogued by RobustBench across four threat models, we compute the accuracy difference against multiple vanilla references from Papers with Code, computed over both all entries and no-extra-data entries, the best vanilla model as of the robust model's publication year, and an architecture-matched baseline. Across all 186 robust models, the mean delta clean relative to the best vanilla model ranges from -7.7 to -29.5 percentage points, and even the single most robust model per track still trails its temporal vanilla counterpart by 4.0-21.0 points. The architecture-matched comparison, which isolates the effect of adversarial training from architectural differences, reveals a mean gap of -3.5 to -17.5 points. Restricting this architecture-matched comparison to models whose vanilla accuracy is known for the exact same architecture, rather than approximated from a related one, narrows the gap to -4.0 to -14.0 points. These results demonstrate that the robustness-accuracy trade-off is substantially larger than what is typically conveyed by individual papers. This information is critical for practitioners and decision-makers. When deploying models in real-world settings, the accuracy cost of robustness directly affects business outcomes, yet current publications do not provide the vanilla baseline needed to assess it. We argue that future robustness evaluations should report vanilla-referenced accuracy gaps as a standard component.

</details>


### [244] [How Agentic Is Agentic Commerce? A Population-Scale Measurement of x402 Adoption and Authenticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12575)
*Shengchen Ling,Yajin Zhou,Lei Wu,Cong Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents the first population-scale measurement of the x402 protocol on Base blockchain, revealing that its widely-cited hundreds of millions of settlements are largely manufactured rather than indicative of genuine AI agent adoption. Over 280 days, 136.7M settlements ($44.1M) were recorded, but 21.2% are fictitious and 63.8% are internal settlements within linked clusters. The authors argue settlement count measures manufacturability, not adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The x402 protocol is widely deployed for AI agent payments and its settlement count is cited as proof of an emerging AI agent economy. However, the authors identify a fundamental measurement validity problem: settlement count is the one metric an interested party can manufacture almost for free, since the facilitator sponsors gas fees and nothing on-chain marks who controls a payment. This motivates the first rigorous, population-scale empirical analysis to distinguish genuine adoption from manufactured activity.

Method: The authors conduct a population-scale measurement of x402 on the Base blockchain over a 280-day window, supplemented with a coarser census on Solana. They identify settlements via on-chain events and resolve the true payer through the meta-transaction layer. Each settlement is sorted by what its trace can prove via a payment graph, categorizing transactions as fictitious, internal settlements within linked clusters, or genuinely independent payments reaching nameable services.

Result: Over 280 days, Base records 136,708,672 settlements worth $44,121,383.81, with extreme concentration (payer, recipient, and value Gini coefficients all above 0.98). Critically, 21.20% of settlements are fictitious and 63.78% are internal settlements within linked clusters. Genuinely independent activity is bounded between $187,861.35 (demonstrably reaching nameable services) and $20,258,746.09 (45.92% of value, not provably manufactured). The manufacturable component forms a coherent operator-driven economy that is star-shaped, machine-timed, and gas-subsidized.

Conclusion: Settlement count measures manufacturability, not adoption. The widely-cited x402 settlement figures cannot be interpreted as evidence of a genuine AI agent economy, as the metric is trivially gamed by interested parties exploiting gas subsidies and the absence of on-chain payer attribution. The paper establishes a methodology for distinguishing real economic activity from manufactured metrics in blockchain protocols.

Abstract: AI agents are said to be forming an economy in which they pay, on their own, for the data, APIs, and compute they consume. x402, which settles a stablecoin payment on-chain for each purchase, is the most widely deployed protocol for this, and its hundreds of millions of settlements are read as proof that the economy has arrived. We show the count cannot be read as adoption: it is the one metric an interested party can manufacture almost for free, since the facilitator sponsors the gas and nothing on-chain marks who controls a payment. We give the first population-scale measurement of x402 on Base, supplemented with a coarser Solana census. Identifying settlements from their on-chain event and resolving the true payer through the meta-transaction layer, we sort each by what its trace can prove via a payment graph. Over a 280-day window Base carries 136{,}708{,}672 settlements worth \$44{,}121{,}383.81, concentrated on every axis we measure (payer, recipient, and value Gini all above 0.98), yet 21.20\% are fictitious and 63.78\% internal settlement within a linked cluster. What is genuinely independent is bounded: it lies between the \$187{,}861.35 that demonstrably reaches a nameable service and the \$20{,}258{,}746.09 (45.92\% of value) not provably manufactured. Finally, we resolve the count's manufacturable component, a coherent operator-driven economy, star-shaped, machine-timed, and gas-subsidized. Settlement count measures manufacturability, not adoption.

</details>


### [245] [When Security Meets Usability: An Empirical Investigation of Post-Quantum Cryptography APIs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14539)
*Marthin Toruan,R. D. N. Shakya,Samuel Tseitkin,Raymond K. Zhao,Nalin Arachchilage*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic empirical evaluation of Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) API usability, observing how developers interact with PQC APIs and documentation during software development tasks, and identifying cognitive factors affecting performance with minimal onboarding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum computing poses a growing threat to current public-key cryptosystems. While NIST has standardized PQC algorithms and plans to deprecate RSA/ECDSA by 2035, PQC adoption remains slow due to limited developer expertise. Cryptographic APIs are meant to bridge this gap, but poor usability can lead developers to introduce vulnerabilities — a risk amplified by PQC's novelty and complexity. Crucially, no prior study has systematically examined PQC API usability.

Method: The authors conducted an empirical usability evaluation of PQC APIs through a developer study, observing participants as they performed software development tasks using PQC APIs and associated documentation, with minimal onboarding.

Result: The study identified specific cognitive factors that influence developer performance when working with PQC primitives. Findings reveal opportunities across the PQC ecosystem to improve developer-facing guidance, terminology alignment, and workflow examples to better support non-specialist developers.

Conclusion: PQC API usability is currently lacking and poses a risk to secure adoption. The PQC ecosystem needs targeted improvements in documentation, consistent terminology, and practical workflow examples to lower the barrier for non-specialist developers and reduce the likelihood of vulnerabilities introduced through API misuse.

Abstract: Advances in quantum computing increasingly threaten the security and privacy of data protected by current cryptosystems, particularly those relying on public-key cryptography. In response, the international cybersecurity community has prioritized the implementation of Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC), a new cryptographic standard designed to resist quantum attacks while operating on classical computers. The National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) has already standardized several PQC algorithms and plans to deprecate classical asymmetric schemes, such as RSA and ECDSA, by 2035. Despite this urgency, PQC adoption remains slow, often due to limited developer expertise. Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) are intended to bridge this gap, yet prior research on classical security APIs demonstrates that poor usability of cryptographic APIs can lead developers to introduce vulnerabilities during implementation of the applications, a risk amplified by the novelty and complexity of PQC. To date, the usability of PQC APIs has not been systematically studied. This research presents an empirical evaluation of the usability of the PQC APIs, observing how developers interact with APIs and documentation during software development tasks. The study identifies cognitive factors that influence the developer's performance when working with PQC primitives with minimal onboarding. The findings highlight opportunities across the PQC ecosystem to improve developer-facing guidance, terminology alignment, and workflow examples to better support non-specialists.

</details>


### [246] [PVDetector: Detecting Prompt Injection Attacks on Purpose-Specific LLM Agents through Policy-Violation Concept Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12624)
*Junhui Wang,Hangtao Zhang,Zhirun Zheng,Li Zeng,Jiejun Xiao,Xi Luo,Lihua Yin,Saiqin Long*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: PVDetector is a training-free framework that detects prompt injection attacks by measuring how well a model's hidden states align with latent "policy-violation" concepts derived offline from contrastive prompt pairs, achieving <1% false negative rate with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents deployed for domain-specific tasks face expanded attack surfaces from prompt injection due to purpose-specific restrictions beyond generic safety guardrails. Existing detection methods relying on input-output analysis show limited effectiveness, motivating an investigation of the hidden activation space for more robust detection.

Method: The method first discovers that LLMs encode latent policy-violation (PV) concepts in hidden states when processing requests that conflict with predefined restrictions. PVDetector then detects PI attacks by measuring the alignment (cosine similarity) between a query's hidden-state representation and pre-computed PV direction vectors. These PV directions are derived offline using contrastive pairs of policy-violating and policy-compliant prompts, requiring no additional training.

Result: Experiments across multiple LLMs and datasets demonstrate PVDetector achieves a false negative rate below 1% with minimal auxiliary computational overhead, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art detection methods.

Conclusion: Leveraging latent PV concepts in the hidden activation space provides a highly effective, training-free approach to detecting prompt injection attacks against purpose-specific LLM agents, offering both strong detection performance and practical efficiency.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as purpose-specific agents to handle domain-specific tasks such as customer service and code generation. These agents are expected to comply with not only generic safety guardrails but also purpose-specific restrictions tailored to their designated roles. Such additional restrictions enlarge the attack surface, particularly to prompt injection (PI) attacks. To defend against such attacks, existing detection methods primarily rely on analyzing input-output patterns, yet yield limited effectiveness. To address this limitation, we turn to analyzing the hidden activation space and discover that LLMs inherently retain latent policy-violation (PV) concepts when prompted with requests beyond their designated purpose. Particularly, PV concepts capture the semantics of conflicts between user queries and predefined restrictions, implicitly reflecting LLMs' intrinsic awareness of recognizing policy violations. Building on this insight, we propose PVDetector, a training-free framework that detects PI attacks during LLM inference by measuring hidden-state alignment with PV concepts, which are derived offline from the contrastive pairs of policy-violating and policy-compliant prompts. Experiments across multiple LLMs and datasets show that PVDetector achieves <1\% false negative rate with minimal auxiliary overhead, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/Claresigle/PVDetector .

</details>


### [247] [Auditable and Transparent Fully Authenticated Disk Encryption via USB Storage Interposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12716)
*Enrique Soriano-Salvador,Gorka Guardiola Múzquiz*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper presents CC (Cryptographic Companion), a Raspberry Pi-based inline USB device that transparently performs Fully Authenticated Disk Encryption (FADE) between a host and an external hard disk, offering a low-cost, flexible, and open-source alternative to software-based FDE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional FDE relies on OS-level drivers, which are platform-dependent and vulnerable to malicious disk firmware. The authors seek an alternative inline approach using generic, low-cost hardware that provides transparency, flexibility, and hardware-level isolation of cryptographic operations.

Method: The authors design and implement CC using a general-purpose single-board computer (e.g., Raspberry Pi) with USB OTG support running Linux. The device sits inline between the host and the external USB hard disk, performing encryption/decryption and authentication while presenting a standard USB mass-storage interface to the host. The host remains unaware of the encryption layer.

Result: An experimental evaluation of the research prototype demonstrates that the CC system is sufficiently efficient for most common use cases, validating the feasibility of the inline cryptographic companion approach.

Conclusion: The CC design offers a viable, transparent, low-cost FADE solution using free and open-source software on generic hardware, with advantages including adaptability to new cryptographic schemes, flexibility, and mitigation against malicious disk firmware.

Abstract: Full Disk Encryption (FDE) has become increasingly important in the last decades due to the evident confidentiality concerns. In most systems, encryption is provided by an operating system driver, through which the user can transparently access the encrypted disk after supplying the required keys (or the credentials from which those keys are derived). In this work, we explore an alternative approach: the use of an intermediate USB device placed between the host system and the external hard disk, where the encrypted data are stored. Although there are existing devices that follow this inline approach (such as RAID controllers and USB enclosures with built-in encryption), we explore the use of a general-purpose single-board computer running Linux with USB On-The-Go support (e.g. a Raspberry Pi), to provide FADE (Fully Authenticated Disk Encryption). Our system, named CC (Cryptographic Companion), performs the cryptographic operations (encryption/decryption and authentication), providing a standard USB mass-storage interface to the host (which is entirely unaware of the presence of encryption) while relying on the external USB hard disk to store the corresponding encrypted blocks. Our design provides several key advantages: flexibility, low cost, transparency, the use of generic hardware and free and open-source software, adaptability to emerging cryptographic schemes, and mitigations against malicious disk firmware. This paper presents the design and implementation of the CC and an experimental evaluation of our current research prototype, which indicates that it is sufficiently efficient for most common use cases.

</details>


### [248] [Bulkhead: Automated Semantic Detection and Remediation of Container Escape Vulnerabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12723)
*Qiyuan Fan,Zhi Li,Junjie Li,XiaoFeng Wang,Bin Yuan,Deqing Zou*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Bulkhead is an automated framework combining LLMs with formal methods to detect and fix path traversal (PaTra) vulnerabilities in container ecosystems. It uses a multi-agent system with knowledge patterns from known cases to locate cross-boundary interaction points, analyze call chains, detect missing security checks and TOCTOU flaws, generate PoC exploits, and produce verified patches via assertion-driven model checking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Container filesystem isolation is undermined by path traversal vulnerabilities from insecure host-container interactions, exacerbated by mounting shared resources (GPUs, agent workspaces) for AI workloads. Existing defenses fail: kernel-level protections are too intrusive for mainline Linux, static rules produce excessive false positives by missing semantic context, and manual auditing is costly and unscalable.

Method: Bulkhead integrates LLMs with formal methods in a multi-agent framework. It leverages multi-dimensional knowledge patterns (high-risk functional patterns to locate cross-boundary entry points; call-chain patterns to recover execution paths). The Detection pipeline analyzes call chains against application scenarios and threat models to identify vulnerabilities (missing security checks, TOCTOU flaws) and generates PoC exploits. The Patch pipeline performs assertion-driven verification using predefined model-checking templates to ensure remediation correctness.

Result: The paper reports successful identification of path traversal vulnerabilities through cross-boundary interaction analysis, automated PoC generation for validation, and verified patch generation via formal model checking.

Conclusion: Bulkhead demonstrates that combining LLM-based semantic understanding with formal verification provides an effective, scalable solution for detecting and remediating PaTra vulnerabilities in container ecosystems, overcoming limitations of both kernel-level defenses and static/manual analysis approaches.

Abstract: Filesystem isolation in container ecosystems is often weakened by cross-boundary path misresolution, causing path traversal (PaTra) vulnerabilities. These vulnerabilities stem from insecure host-container interactions and have become increasingly pervasive as cloud systems mount shared resources, such as GPUs and agent workspaces, into containers to support AI workloads. Existing defenses remain inadequate. Kernel-level protections are intrusive, can destabilize system calls, and have therefore not been accepted into the Linux mainline. Detection methods rely on static rule matching or manual code auditing. Static rules can flag path-related functions but fail to capture the semantics needed to determine whether a host-container interaction exists, causing many false positives. Manual review requires domain expertise, making it costly, inefficient, and difficult to scale.
  To address this threat, we present Bulkhead, an automated framework that integrates large language models (LLMs) with formal methods for semantic vulnerability discovery and remediation. Bulkhead uses a multi-agent system to identify and repair PaTra vulnerabilities through multi-dimensional knowledge patterns generalized from known cases. It first applies high-risk functional patterns to locate entry points for cross-boundary interactions in containerized code, then uses call-chain patterns to recover the corresponding execution paths at suitable depth. The Detection pipeline analyzes these call chains against the application scenarios and threat model, identifying vulnerabilities such as missing security checks and TOCTOU flaws in cross-boundary interactions, and generating proof-of-concept (PoC) exploits for validation. These PoCs then guide patch generation. To ensure remediation correctness, the Patch pipeline performs assertion-driven verification using predefined model-checking templates.

</details>


### [249] [Stability Buys Time: A Re-Keying Game for Encrypted Multi-Agent Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12742)
*Sai Sandeep Damera,John S. Baras*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the security of CKKS-based encrypted control systems under advanced persistent threats (APT) as a two-phase dynamic game. The passive phase involves reconnaissance via encryption noise leakage; the active phase is a detection-evasion timing game where the defender uses re-keying (not bootstrapping) to reset accumulated leakage. The authors derive a Stackelberg equilibrium determining optimal re-keying cadence, revealing a three-way tension between FHE precision, control accuracy, and re-keying frequency bounded by a securability floor and a static-suffices ceiling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing security analyses of approximate FHE (CKKS) are static and do not account for the unavoidable encryption noise leakage that occurs when encrypted control loops must repeatedly decrypt to actuate. Traditional threat models assume honest-but-curious clouds, while APT games never reach inside the cryptosystem. There is a need to model and defend against an adversary who can observe accumulated noise over time (passive reconnaissance) and then launch stealthy manipulation attacks that evade residual detectors.

Method: The authors model security as a two-phase game: (1) a passive reconnaissance phase reduced to the known flooding tradeoff of CKKS, and (2) an active detection-evasion timing game where the adversary manipulates while evading a measured residual detector. The defender's move is re-keying (refreshing the secret key) to reset accumulated leakage noise. They solve for the Stackelberg equilibrium to determine the optimal re-keying cadence, which depends on the control-theoretic fragility of the graph topology (marginally-stable vs. well-connected). The analysis bounds the operating regime between a securability floor and a static-suffices ceiling.

Result: A three-way tension emerges among FHE precision, control accuracy, and re-keying cadence. Marginally-stable graph topologies require significantly more frequent re-keying than well-connected ones. The efficient secure operating point lies within a window bounded below by a securability floor (minimum re-keying to deny the adversary) and above by a static-suffices ceiling (where static security is adequate). Re-keying is characterized as the price of precision efficiency in encrypted control.

Conclusion: Security for approximate cryptosystems in feedback loops is fundamentally a dynamic game where the defender's move is the scheme's own refresh (re-keying). This framework generalizes beyond encrypted control to any system that must repeatedly decrypt to act, reframing FHE security as an online, control-aware, game-theoretic problem rather than a static cryptographic one.

Abstract: Encrypted control lets a cloud coordinate a fleet of agents on fully homomorphically encrypted state, keeping their positions and commands private. The approximate scheme for real-valued control, CKKS, returns decryptions that carry the encryption noise, a key-recovery leak; the loop must decrypt to actuate, so the leak is unavoidable. Yet the security of approximate FHE is studied statically, encrypted control assumes an honest-but-curious cloud, and persistent-threat games never reach inside the cryptosystem. We model the loop's security under an advanced persistent threat as a two-phase game, passive reconnaissance then active manipulation, separated by a measured residual detector that sees only the manipulation. The passive phase reduces to the known flooding tradeoff; the active defense is re-keying, not bootstrapping, since only re-keying resets accumulated leakage. The active phase is a detection-evasion timing game: overt manipulation is caught, so the rational adversary stays stealthy, and at its Stackelberg equilibrium the defender re-keys on the laziest cadence that denies it, set by the control-theoretic fragility of the graph topology. The marginally-stable graph must re-key far more often than the well-connected one. A three-way tension among FHE precision, control accuracy, and re-key cadence sets where this game lives, between a securability floor and a static-suffices ceiling. The efficient secure point is that window, where re-keying is the price of precision efficiency. More broadly, security for an approximate cryptosystem in a feedback loop is a dynamic game whose defender's move is the scheme's own refresh, applying beyond control to any system that must repeatedly decrypt to act.

</details>


### [250] [A Scalable Cloud-Orchestrated and Service-Oriented Multi-Domain QKD Network with PQC Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12765)
*Konstantinos Krilakis,Antonia Tsili,Aikaterini Mandilara,Dimitris Syvridis*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A multi-domain quantum-secure network architecture combining vendor-agnostic QKD, SDN orchestration, and Zero Trust access with PQC-based authentication, validated on a real-world testbed with 3 QKD vendors, demonstrating low PQC/SDN overhead and key retrieval as the main bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing QKD networks face scalability challenges across heterogeneous infrastructures and administrative domains due to vendor-specific interfaces, trusted-node limitations, and poor interoperability. There is a need for a flexible framework that extends quantum-safe key transport beyond native QKD boundaries while maintaining compatibility with existing infrastructure.

Method: The authors propose a multi-domain, multi-site architecture integrating: (1) vendor-agnostic QKD to abstract heterogeneous QKD hardware; (2) SDN-based orchestration for dynamic key routing and network control; (3) cloud-managed trust services; (4) Zero Trust Network Access with multi-level authentication based on PQC signature and key encapsulation algorithms. The system is deployed on a real-world testbed spanning domains with QKD nodes from three different vendors and domains lacking QKD infrastructure entirely.

Result: Experimental evaluation shows that PQC computational overhead and SDN control-plane overhead remain relatively low, even on resource-constrained devices. The primary performance bottleneck is identified as QKD key retrieval latency and vendor-specific key streaming throughput limitations, rather than the orchestration or cryptographic layers.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully extends quantum-safe key transport beyond native QKD boundaries, enabling heterogeneous multi-vendor QKD deployments with strong interoperability, flexibility, and backward compatibility with existing network infrastructures.

Abstract: Quantum key distribution (QKD) offers unconditional security but existing QKD networks remain difficult to scale across heterogeneous infrastructures and administrative domains due to vendor-specific interfaces, trusted-node constraints, and limited interoperability. This work presents a flexible multi-domain and multi-site quantum-secure network architecture integrating vendor-agnostic QKD, SDN orchestration, and cloud-managed trust services. Communication is based on Zero Trust Network Access protocols featuring multi-level authentication mechanisms building upon post-quantum cryptography (PQC) signature and key encapsulation algorithms. The system is deployed on a real-world testbed with domains incorporating QKD nodes from 3 vendors, as well as domains without QKD infrastructure elements. Experimental results show that PQC and SDN overhead remain relatively low even on constrained devices, with the main bottleneck being QKD key retrieval and vendor-specific key streaming limitations. The proposed framework extends quantum-safe key transport beyond native QKD boundaries while preserving flexibility, interoperability, and compatibility with existing infrastructures.

</details>


### [251] [Silent Alarm: A J-Space Protocol for Comparing Danger Recognition Across Models and Quantization Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12792)
*Roman Prosvirnin,Victor Minchenkov,Alexey Soldatov,Vladimir Bashun*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: JADR is a novel jailbreak-robustness evaluation protocol that probes a model's internal safety representations via Jacobian space (J-space) before response generation, using a local, judge-free SafetyAUC metric to compare models and quantization regimes with statistical rigor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-as-judge evaluations for jailbreak robustness are sensitive to grading procedures and only capture surface-level behavior on specific attacks, failing to reveal the hidden fragility of underlying safety mechanisms. There is a need for an evaluation method that directly accesses internal representations of safety before response generation begins.

Method: JADR records top-k tokens in Jacobian space (J-space) — a workspace of verbalizable concepts derived from model activations — for every prompt and layer, before the first response token. Tokens are grouped into six behavioral scenario axes and compared between danger samples (from StrongREJECT) and safe controls (from XSTest and OKTest). The comparison uses the SafetyAUC metric with bootstrap confidence intervals. No external judge is required; computation runs entirely on the evaluated model's activations.

Result: Applied across six models (Qwen3 variants and Gemma 2 9B) under BF16, INT8, and INT4 regimes, JADR separated models with strong vs. weak internal safety mechanisms with statistical significance and captured substantively different effects across quantization regimes. Results were cross-validated against an independent behavioral evaluation using StrongREJECT grader.

Conclusion: JADR provides a statistically grounded, internal-representation-based alternative to behavioral safety evaluation, enabling fair comparison across models and model modifications (quantization, fine-tuning) without reliance on external judges.

Abstract: Jailbreak-robustness research typically evaluates safety through generated responses using an LLM-as-judge approach. Such evaluations, however, are sensitive to the benchmark's grading procedure and capture only observed behavior on a given set of attacks, without directly revealing the hidden fragility of the underlying safety mechanisms. This work proposes JADR (Jacobian Assessment of Danger Recognition), a protocol that measures a model's internal representation through Jacobian space (J-space, a recently proposed workspace of verbalizable concepts) before the first response token is generated. For every prompt and layer we record the top-k J-space tokens; these are grouped into six behavioral scenario axes and compared between a danger sample based on StrongREJECT and a safe control drawn from XSTest and OKTest. The method does not call on an external judge model: the computation runs entirely locally, on the activations of the model under evaluation, which lets us compare both different models against each other and modifications of a single model -- quantization and fine-tuning in particular -- on the same terms. The final comparison rests on the proposed SafetyAUC metric, complemented with bootstrap confidence intervals. The protocol is applied to six models (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-Uncensored-4B, Qwen3-SafeRL-4B, Gemma 2 9B) across three weight-representation regimes -- BF16, INT8, and INT4 -- and checked against an independent behavioral evaluation with the StrongREJECT grader. The metric separates models with a strong versus a weak internal safety mechanism with statistical significance and captures substantively different effects across quantization regimes.

</details>


### [252] [Watermark Forensics for Generative Models: An Information-Theoretic Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13003)
*Xiaoyu Li,Zheng Gao,Xiaoyan Feng,Jiaojiao Jiang,Yulei Sui,Jiankun Hu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper establishes a theoretical framework for analyzing multi-level watermarking capabilities (detection, attribution, extraction, localization) in generative model outputs, deriving tight entropy-rate bounds on the sample length n required for each forensic task. A key insight is that an information profile ν(t) organizes all answers: detection relies on distributional distance, while attribution and extraction are paid for by total mutual information, and localization by how that information is spread. The main theorem proves that attributing to one of N users costs Θ(log N / h) tokens under stationary-ergodic sources with entropy rate h, and extracting an ℓ-bit payload costs Θ(ℓ/h), both tight to (1+o(1)).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing watermarking work typically treats detection as a binary task (machine-generated or not). The authors argue that watermarks can support a richer forensic ladder — attribution to specific users, extraction of hidden payloads, and localization of surviving content after editing — and ask what each rung costs in terms of required sample length n. Prior analyses lacked tight, general entropy-rate laws for multi-user attribution and did not unify these tasks under a single information-theoretic lens.

Method: The paper introduces the information profile ν(t) = I(S; X_t | X_{<t}), capturing how much the t-th token reveals about the secret S. Total mass of ν pays for attribution/extraction; its spread pays for localization; detection is paid by distributional distance (presence). The main theorem uses a decoder that thresholds each candidate by its own realized surprisal (rather than naive collision counting, which overcharges) to achieve the Θ(log N / h) attribution rate. A matching converse establishes two-sided tightness. The framework also reveals a Θ(log N)-token unattributable window and a footprint-resolution uncertainty principle.

Result: For statistically distortion-free schemes over stationary-ergodic sources with entropy rate h: (1) attributing to one of N users requires Θ(log N / h) tokens, tight to a (1+o(1)) factor; (2) extracting an ℓ-bit payload requires Θ(ℓ/h) tokens; (3) a Θ(log N)-token gap exists where text is provably machine-made but unattributable; (4) a footprint-resolution uncertainty principle emerges. Experiments on GPT-2, Pythia-410M, and Qwen2.5 recover the predicted constants.

Conclusion: The information profile ν(t) serves as a unifying object for watermarking's forensic ladder. The tight entropy-rate laws establish fundamental limits: attribution and extraction costs are governed by total mutual information, while detection is governed by distributional distance — two incomparable resource caps reflected in the literature's subtle-mark vs. loud-stamp dichotomy. The Θ(log N / h) attribution law is the first tight entropy-rate result for multi-user attribution via exact alignment, and the identified gaps (unattributable window, uncertainty principle) are real consequences rather than modeling artifacts.

Abstract: A watermark in a generative model's output is usually asked only whether a text is machine-made. The same mark can do more: attribute it to the user who produced it, extract a hidden payload, or localize the part that survives editing. These form a forensic ladder, and we ask what each rung costs in the sample length $n$.
  One object organizes the answers. Let $S$ be the secret the mark carries (a user's identity or payload), and let the information profile $ν(t)=I(S;X_t\mid X_{<t})$ record how much the $t$-th token reveals about $S$ given the earlier ones. Its total mass pays for attribution and extraction; how that mass is spread pays for localization; and detection alone is paid for not by information but by presence, the distance from the marked to the unmarked distribution. The literature's two quality models, a mark subtle on every token and one that stamps a few tokens loudly, are two incomparable ways of capping this profile.
  Our main theorem settles the ladder's entropy column. For statistically distortion-free schemes, attributing a text to one of $N$ users costs $Θ(\log N/h)$ tokens over every stationary-ergodic source of entropy rate $h$, sharp to a $(1+o(1))$ factor: to our knowledge the first tight entropy-rate law for multi-user attribution (via exact alignment). The natural collision-counting analysis overcharges without bound; only a decoder thresholding each candidate by its own realized surprisal attains the rate while almost never implicating an innocent user. A matching converse makes the law two-sided, and extraction of an $\ell$-bit payload costs $Θ(\ell/h)$. Two gaps are real, not modeling artifacts: a $Θ(\log N)$-token window in which a text is provably machine-made yet unattributable, and a footprint-resolution uncertainty principle. Experiments on GPT-2, Pythia-410M, and Qwen2.5 recover the predicted constants.

</details>
